HISTORY OF CITY GOVERNMENTS Colonial Period American city governments derived their initial forms largely from nglish precedent. In three of the early colonial charters (Philadelphia, Annapolis nd Norfolk) the governing bodies were close corporations, with the aldermen and councilmen holding office for life. But a strong tendency toward democratic forms was shown in the seventeen other colonial charters in which the councilmen and generally the aldermen were elected by those having the franchise; the mayors, however, were either appointed by the governor or elected by the council.
In this period legislative and executive powers were largely vested in the council which also exercised certain judicial powers. Although possessing such wide authority and responsibility the councils were nevertheless handicapped, as municipal undertakings increased, by lack of the power to tax. To this lack we may probably trace the cause of the extensive application of the fee system in the conduct of our early municipal governments.
Source of Authority The powers of colonial cities were derived from charters granted by the governors of the provinces. After the Revolution, the charter-granting power devolved upon the states. The state legislatures changed the force of charter-grants by regarding them as ordinary acts of legislation, susceptible of amendment at any time, rather than as contracts binding equally upon both parties involved.
As a result of this change much of the autonomy of local governments was sacrificed, and this result was further enhanced by the states' control of taxing function.
The precedent of the federal government was not without influence upon the forms of local government. Thus toward the end of the eighteenth century we find attempts to introduce the principle of the separation of powers by the establishment of bi-cameral councils and by entrusting the veto power to mayors.
The net result of this influence seems to have been to weaken the powers of the city council and to make the task of administering the rapidly growing municipal business even more difficult.
Expansion of Municipal Functions The middle of the eighteenth century found our city government swamped by a task of management beyond the capacity of the organization and powers with which they were endowed. Government by councilmanic committees having failed, an attempt was made to split off such committees from the council to form WCIT American Forms of Municipal Government2 separate and responsible boards of control. These boards were endowed with wide powers, being independent of the council and practically independent of the mayor even when appointed by him. Board members were appointed sometimes by the council, sometimes by the mayor, and sometimes by popular vote. Decentralization of the powers of government reached the extreme under the board system and the lines of demarcation between municipal and state authorities in some cases were almost completely obliterated.
Experience with the board system showed that the splitting up of executive responsibility among a group of separate administrative units entrusted with separate functions, might result in initial improvements but in the long run, because of the lack of central supervision and responsibility, the result would be injurious. One idea was salvaged from this experience; the plan of a single 'board of boards' cutting across functional lines and including important executive and legislative heads. Thus the informal conferences of such officials in New York City were crystallized in the new Board of Estimate and Apportionment created by the charter of 1873, and the powers of this board have been extended until today it may be said that "The Board of Estimate is, in fact, the board of directors of the Municipal Corporation of New York." The Board of Directors Idea Because of the creation of its Board of Estimate New York may be said to have been the first American city to adopt the theory of government by commission. The term "commission government," however, is of later origin, having first come into prominence when the city of Galveston, Texas, under the stress of flood conditions in 1901, appointed a "commission" of five experienced business men and gave them exceptionally broad powers and responsibility so that the city might be speedily reconstructed. Des Moines, Iowa,soon followed Galveston's lead but made certain modifications of the original form and added various new details such as the Initiative, Referendum, Recall and Protest. Many other American cities followed Des Moines' lead and for a time the spread of commission government was rapid.
In As we have previously seen, the rise of the board system about the middle of the eighteenth century divested the city councils of extensive powers.
the case of New York and one or two other cities these powers were inherited by a strong central body comparable in some respects to the board of directors of a business corporation. In a very few instances - for example, Chicago the end of this period found the powers of city councils still very strong.
Concentration of Executive Powers - The plan of council or governor appointed mayors which prevailed in colonfal days did not persist, and prior to the middle of the eighteenth century our mayors were largely chosen by popular election. When dissatisfaction with the board system brought about their abolition or curtailment the net result, in many cases, was to strengthen the hand of the mayor. Thus, in 1875 and after when a mumber of city governments were reorganized, the mayor was frequently accorded the power of making appointments; the power to remove officials on charges, and, subject to certain restrictions, the power to veto legislation. This tendency resulted in the development of the strong mayor or responsible executive form of city government, as illustrated today by the City of Detroit, Michigan, where the mayor has appointive and removal power, without council confirmation, over the 7.

erican Forms of Municipal Government - 3 ads of all departments of municipal activity, except those directly appointed popular vote. In this case we have a clear instance of a development of the yor-council form of government which approaches the more recent city-manager orm as regards the concentration of power in the hands of a single executive ief.
The latest step in the direction of concentration of municipal authority nd responsibility was taken by the city of Staunton, Virginia, whose mayor and ouncil in 1908 passed an ordinance creating the office of "general manager", nd delegated to this new official all administrative detail and responsibility.
he first city manager charter was adopted in 1912 by Sumter, South Carolina.
he city manager plan attracted general attention when it was adopted by the city f Dayton, Ohio, shortly after the disastrous flood of 1913. Dayton gained coniderable initial publicity by offering the office of city manager to General oethals, of Panama fame, at a salary of $25,000 per year. Recent developments inlude the abandonment of the plan by Akron, Ohio, hitherto, the largest city manager ity, by charter amendment vote, and the vote for the adoption of the plan in 1924 by the city of Cleveland, Ohio.
II. STATISTICS OF GOVERNMENT FORMS Summary Adequate statistics showing the totals for different forms of government represented in American cities and towns are not available. According to Census figures (1920) there are 1467 cities and towns of 5,000 or more population in the United States. According to Lamar T. Beman (see references attached to this report) there were 303 commission governed cities and 167 city manager cities to compare with this total. Thus of the cities having in 1920 a population of 5,000 or over 20.6 percent operated under the commission form and 11.3 per cent operated under the city manager form.
Commission Government The record of cities having commission government formerly kept by the National Short Ballot Organization has not been contimed in recent years.
In addition to this total of 303 commission plan cities, among cities of 5,000 or more population, Mr. Beman states that the plan is also in effect in two or three hundred villages and towns of less than 5,000 population. Pending the revised list which is to appear in a new edition of E. S. Bradford's "Commission Government in American Cities " a conservative estimate would put the total of commission governed towns and cities at somewhere near the 500 mark. The commission plan has been abandoned in some 53 cities of over 5,000 population, in 27 of these so that a change could be made to the city manager plan. Mr. Beman lists 19 other instances where commission government was abandoned in cities of less than 5,000 population, and of these cities 7 made the change in order to adopt the city manager plan.
City Manager Plan The City Managers' Association compiles currently statistics showing the number of cities which have adopted the city manager plan. These statistics, however, do not cover abandonments, and in several instances their accuracy has been questioned.

ANALYSIS OF CITY GOVERNMENT FORMS Main Types of Government Study of the history of municipal government in the United States shows that there has been developed three main types of municipal government, which may be termed the mayor-council, the commission and the city-manager forms of city government. The variation in the character of the first mentioned form, however, is so great that for the purposes of this report we will subdivide it into two forms designated the Strong Council and the Strong Mayor forms, in accordance with the relative distribution of powers and responsibility.
The Strong Council Plan In early times, as we have seen, the city councils largely controlled the exercise of the executive function because of their responsibility för the appointment of administrative officials. In some cities the city council still retains much of its former control over appointments, but in most cases this control has been limited or considerably reduced. Some city legislative bodies seem to have retained a greater degree of vitality than others, and these have fought to retain their influence in administrative matters. Sometimes they have been able to do this, to a greater or lesser extent, by means of their control of the appropriating power, Thus, under the strong-council plan of city government we find the council taking aggressive action in all legislative and policy-determining matters and also exercising, directly or indirectly, a decided influence upon administration. The essence of the strong-council plan is a one or two chambered legislative body, elected by popular vote on a geographical representation basis, which body by direct or indirect methods is able to exercise a powerful influence upon the actual administration of municipal services and undertakings.
There are many persons who advocate the strong-council form of city government on the ground of democracy. This claim is based upon the fact that the councilmen or assemblymen are directly elected to represent certain wards or districts; it ignores the statement that conflict of local interests frequently results in divided councils which defeat the ends of efficient administration.
The Strong Mayor Plan As far back as 1872 increasing attention was given to the problem of executive responsibility and it was felt that there was need for more power and more responsibility in the executive head of the municipality. Subsequently an effort was made to introduce such concentration of authority in the Brooklyn IS MEAS: bet : American Forms of Municipal Government 6.
charter but the changes did not fully justify expectations. However, the desire for concentration has persisted and today there is a strong sentiment for fewer elections and elective officers and for concentration, separately, of legislative and executive functions in a small number of persons directly responsible to the community as a whole.
As a result of feelings such as the above there has been developed in a number of American cities a strongly centralized form of government which we have termed the Strong-Mayor Plan. The essence of this form is a strong executive head elected by popular vote who possesses absolute power of appointment and removal, without council confirmation, and who may even exercise a decided influence upon legislation by the use of the veto power. The powers of the council under this plan tend to be subordinated and in some cases, as in Detroit, council members are reduced in numbers and elected at large rather than by wards.
Advocates of the strong-mayor form of city government state that this plan makes for efficiency in the conduct of the business of the city. As to the question of democracy, they state that, regardless of whether elections are by ward or at large, the real wishes of a majority of the citizens are more likely to be recognized and carried out because of the increased authority and responsibility vested in the one executive head.
The Commission Plan Developed from New York's experience, via the Galveston flood-time experiment, the commission plan of city government illustrates the tendency to concentrate both legislative and executive functions in a single governing body.
This tendency may go part way, as in the case of New York's Board of Estimate and Apportionment, or it may be completely applied as in the case of the majority of the so-called "commission government" cities where the city council and the mayor cease to exist as separate parts of the city government.
The essence of government by commission is the complete concentration of all power and responsibility in a small council or commission (3 to 5 members), elected at large, which body is vested with the complete power to appoint and to remove at will all officers or employees of the city. Additional features somtimes employed in commission governed cities include direct primaries and nonpartisan elections, civil service, and the initiative, referendum, recall and protest.
Under the commission plan, which "was devised and intended to secure the efficient management so characteristic of great business enterprises", the legislative and executive powers are concentrated in the city commissioners who act individually as the heads of the municipal departments and collectively as the deliberative body which frames the city's laws.
Commission government advocates claim that it fixes responsibility in a Small group which is thus made more responsive to the public will.
They claim also that it draws business talent into the municipal service and supplies a form of organization whereby such expert talents are allowed free play. Critics of commission government maintain that popular control is sacrificed; that it violates the principle of "the separation of powers", and that the plan results in conflicts between departmental interests which result in unbalanced municipal development and vacillating governmental policy.

: ་ The City Manager Plan When Staunton, Virginia, appointed a "general manager" of the municipal business a new form of government was born which in some respects resembles the Strong-Mayor form already described. The Lockport plan, which failed of adoption, went the Staunton plan one better by combining the "commission" and "manager" features, and most of the so-called city manager cities have since followed this precedent. Optional Charter System: Under this plan the state legislature enacts laws permitting the choice by cities of one of several different types of charters.
What the Charter Should Contain A city charter is the formulation of the basic law or constitution under which a city is organized and operates. It determines both the governmental organization and the powers and the duties of the corporation and its agencies. It is an official description of the governmental mechanism in terms of the fundamental principles upon which it is based but it should not attempt a detailed specification of the means by which these principles are to be carried out, i.e., it deals with governmental functions only in so far as these are pre-determined by governmental form.
All other regulations are proper material for inclusion in the city's administrative code.
In drafting a municipal charter it should be remembered that simplicity and clarity are first requisites.
The form of government outlined in the charter will be that form already agreed upon by popular vote. In a given city the charter itself should not be drafted until charters of similar character in use in other cities have been studied. Such surveys analyze and outline the form and functions and division of responsibility in the city government; the principles and methods governing budget, accounting system and public debt; the administration of the civil service; and the control of public property and supplies. Surveys of this character have been made in New York City, San Francisco, Norfolk, Columbus, O., Rochester, N. Y., Stroudsburg, Pa., Milford, Conn., and many other cities. As the result of investigations of this kind definite methods for securing administrative efficiency have been worked out and these methods where applied are yielding dividends in the form of increased and better municipal service at reasonable or even reduced cost.
Function as a Basis for Organization When we look upon the top-heavy administrative organization of many American cities it might almost be said that 'to the multiplication of municipal departments there is no end. According to Professor Munro, "Boston has more than thirty departments of municipal administration, although by no process of sound reasoning can one evolve a list of thirty distinct general functions to be performed." The other extreme is illustrated by the five-department plan usually found under commission government. Thus in Galveston, where the department of streets and public improvements has charge of highways, hospitals, health, and cemeteries, the commissioner is reported to have said: "My duties begin at births and end at burials." One of the first needs revealed by intensive studies of city government relates to the organization and coordination of the work of the various city departments. Of the specific faults found the following may be cited as typical: Inequitable apportionment of work among city departments.
Splitting up of single functions among different departments.
Overlapping in the work and powers of city departments.
Lack of definite provision for interdepartmental cooperation.
Departmental powers incompletely used or used inconflict with powers of other departments.
Failure to fix definitely the responsibility for the discharge of different functions.
Faults such as these have been found to exist in many American cities and through this discovery the evils of duplication and over-lapping have been thrown into strong relief.
Not only have duplication and overlapping been found as among various municipal departments, but also as among city and county jurisdictions and the numerous special districts and areas created for special administrative, taxing, or other purposes. To correct such conditions it has come to be recognized that, in so far as practicable, departments should be organized on a functional basis with due provision for inter-departmental coordination, and with clear delineation of the different lines of departmental responsibility.

 1: American Forms of Municipal Government 11 System of Financial Control Municipal financing is undoubtedly the most important single problem in the field of city administration. Fiscal methods in the past have been chaotic with few if any generally accepted standards and with wide variations in accounting methods. As a result of intensive studies of municipal financial problems in recent years there is being developed a regular procedure involving; the standardization of revenue sources and methods of assessment; the development of proper budget and accounting systems, and the formulation of improved policies 1egarding the public debt.
This new financial procedure calls for more intensive study of municipal revenue sources and the adoption of standard policies of apportionment of the tax burden among such sources. After this task has been completed it becomes necessary to develop a fair and accurate assessment system to insure equitable treatment as among individual property holders. Of interest in this connection are the general studies of city revenue sources made by the federal Census Bureau and by the National Municipal League, and the special report on "Real Estate Assessment" compiled by the National Chamber's Civic Development Department.
Framing the Budget If it is necessary to improve our methods of raising city funds, it is no less necessary to improve our methods of spending. There are two aspects to the appropriating power, i.e., the power to make estimates and the power to authorize expenditure. In past American experience both the power to plan and to make appropriations has been usually lodged exclusively in the city council. About twenty-five years ago, however, on the grounds that this plan resulted in a municipal pork barrel, a movement developed in favor of reducing the council's powers in the framing of the budget estimates. As the result of this movement some cities now place the responsibility for budget formulation upon the mayor (or city manager) or upon a board of estimate. When such body has prepared the estimates the city council, which has power to decrease but not to increase separate items, enacts these estimates into a budget law. In commission governed cities the estimates are compiled, adopted as the budget, and the moneys appropriated are expended, by the one responsible body, the city commission.
Next in importance to fixing the responsibility for budget formulation is the question as to what the budget itself, as a financial instrument, shall contain. Experience has shown that the first essential is a basic classification of objects of expenditure with careful definition of each class and grade.

J..

American Forms of Municipal Government 13 Public Debt Policy A modern budget and accounting system will do much to insure reasonable economy in the financial administration of a city, but there still remains the problem of policy as regards the public debt. The indebtedness of American cities, which in recent years has increased at a rapid rate, is the cause of widespread criticism and misgiving. Lacking a wise and definite debt policy no American city can be regarded as having successfully solved the problem of financial administration.
Historically considered our cities have passed through three periods of debt-incurring policy. In the early days no clearly-defined debt policy existed and in actual practice the general tendency was to issue bonds maturing over a long period of time. The second period came when later citizens found themselves paying for improvements which had ceased to exist. To meet this situation public policy was so changed as to provide that no bonds should run for a period longer than the estimated life of the improvement for which they were issued. The third development in municipal debt policy has been generally referred to as the "Pay-asyou-go" plan. Under this plan both capital and current expenditures, in so far as possible, are paid out of current revenues, thus affecting substantial savings in interest payments which can be devoted to future improvements.
After having adopted a sound general financial policy, the next step is to adopt some definite basis or rule by which such policy may be made practically effective. It has been suggested by students of public finance that this rule be based upon the limitation annually of the city's debt incurring power. This limitation would be equivalent to the estimated total depreciation of those assets which may be regarded as constituting the depreciable (and non-self-supporting) portion of the city's capital investment.
Guarding Against Debt Creation The limitation principle has been already recognized by state legislatures which have very generally placed definite limits upon the debt-incurring powers of local governments. There is also the question of what projects or undertakings may be legitimate cause for the creation of public debt. It is widely claimed that American cities all too frequently have extended the use of the debt incurring power beyond legitimate limits for a variety of reasons, such as artificial reduction in the tax rate. In most cities, for example, school building bonds are common but in Chicago the cost of school buildings is defrayed from current revenues. It is, then, essential that a constructive debt policy indicate as clearly as possible what objects may be regarded as legitimate causes for the creation of public debt.
There will be cases where the arguments of practical necessity, right-- fully or wrongfully, will outweigh: the dictates of sober financial judgment.
Under such conditions the most serious consideration should be given to the question of actual necessity and probable effect, before final action is decided upon. If such mature consideration is accorded and if all the facts are available in clear and understandable form by means of a proper budget and accounting system, the opportunities for unwise debt creation will be greatly reduced.

The movement for civil service reform spread from the federal to the state and city governments. New York state created a civil service commission in 1883 and similar bodies have been created in almost a dozen other states. Our municipalities have also followed suit and there are said to be today some 250 municipal governments which have civil service commissions.
Merely to establish a civil service commission, however, will not result in a proper system of personnel administration. Civil Service commission experience has demonstrated the need for basic studies of the types and varieties of positions in the government service; the duties and requirements of such positions, and the rates of compensation and salary increase. With these facts available it is possible to develop a uniform classification of positions as among various governmental departments, with standard specifications for each type of service, and maximum and minimum compensation rates.
Employee Training and Promotion If classification and standardization of public employment are necessary there is need, no less, for proper methods for the training, admission and advancement of public employees. There is no well-defined system of training to develop employees for modern public service. Some degree of training, however, is obtainable from public and private educational agencies and even from governmental departments. Thus our colleges offer advanced courses in administrative law, budgets and accounting, personnel problems, etc., and specialized vocational and private "civil service" training schools offer courses aiming generally to train more specifically for regular government positions. In some cases, notably police and fire service, municipal departments themselves have organized training schools or courses from which to recruit needed employees.
After proper organization of positions, duties and training, it is necessary to provide for the recognition of meritorious service. This will call for the development of a system of "efficiency rating "whereby the effectiveness of the work of employees can be measured. In the early days of civil service administration such ratings were obtained as the result of examinations based upon previous experience and verbal and written answers to examination questions.
strong tendency today to introduce modern methods of mental testing, based upon psychological principles, in the place of the earlier written or oral examinations.
After a rating system has been adopted it is still necessary to develop a plan or policy of employee promotion or advancement which will insure uniformity of procedure, Control of Property and Supplies A city would not leave a thousand dollars of money unguarded, but frequently a city does not hesitate to leave thousands of dollars in materials and supplies on a vacant lot. Studies of the care and use of municipal supplies have frequently revealed the waste or total disappearance of astonishingly large proportions of the original amounts purchased. Such waste and depreciation in value applies also to the larger physical assets such as vehicular and mechanical equipment and public offices and buildings. If economical administration is to American Forms of Municipal Government - 15 be achieved we must realize that public property is the tangible expression of public funds and calls for the same honesty and care in its handling.
Under this general head of public property control fall a number of specific functions such as property and office management, the standardization and purchase of materials and supplies, and the storing, transporting and keeping record of properties and supplies. In the field of office and property management and custodial care in general, standards of practice are being developed and there exists a considerable body of recorded experience which is helpful to those engaged in tasks of reorganization. It is just as essential to employ correct principles of management in handling property and materials as in handling men.
The development of standard specifications is a matter of prime importance in cities where hundreds of thousands of dollars are spent each year for physical equipment, materials and supplies. This task involves a study of the character and amounts of supplies used by different departments and the possibility of standardization of qualities and forms. Thus standard grades of paper are provided for different needs and uniformity is introduced in the printing of forms and letter-heads, with resulting simplification and economy. In certain cities considerable sums have been saved by the standardization of sizes and grades of coal used by various city departments.
The organization and centralization of purchasing has developed rapidly in business and the purchasing agent now thinks of his work in definite professional terms. Thus also professional technique and standards have been developed in the purchase of municipal supplies by the creation of central purchasing bureaus or offices. There are today some 64 cities that have centralized their purchasing in greater or less dagree. The advocates of centralized purchasing claim that it results in increased efficiency and economy in the purchase of municipal supplies.
The importance of the stores-keeping function has been realized increasingly in recent years. This has been particularly true of your larger cities where the volumes of supplies on hand reach astounding proportions.. It has been found necessary to give serious attention to the problem of storage and to the various methods for the prompt transporting or "feeing" of materials or supplies to the place of use. Then there is the record-keeping problem with the development of "continual inventory" systems whereby the amounts of stock on hand are always instantly ascertainable. It is evident that proper exercise of this function will speed up the municipal business and make for increased efficiency and economy.

APPENDIX A: SOURCES OF INFORMATION There are a number of national, state and local bodies and agencies from which information and professional advice and assistance may be obtained.
Among the more important of these are the following: A. National Agencies 1. National Municipal League, 261 Broadway, New York City. The League acts as a clearing house of information on municipal problems, issues monthly the "National Municipal Review", publishes a number of books, furnishes speakers on municipal subjects, and arranges for professional consultation service on charter problems, etc. The League is a strong advocate of the city manager plan and is critical of the commission plan. Mr. H. W. Dodds is Secretary.
2. Institute for Public Service, 1125 Amsterdam Avenue, New York City.
The Institute issues various publications and supplies public speakers. It undertakes contracts for the making of municipal government surveys at rates based upon the extent of the investigation. Dr. William H. Allen is Director of the Institute.
3. National Institute for Public Administration, 261 Broadway, New York City. Successor to New York Bureau of Municipal Research. Issues various publications and supplies public speakers. It undertakes contracts for the making of municipal government surveys at rates based upon the extent of the investigation.
Dr. Luther Gulick is Director.
4. City Managers Association, Lawrence, Kansas. This Association, supported by city managers, issues a monthly "City Manager Magazine" and an annual yearbook of statistics and convention proceedings. Acts as clearing house of city manager information. Mr. John G. Stutz, Secretary, is located at Lawrence, Kansas.
5. Government Research Conference. This is an organization of research workers in the municipal field issuing occasional reports and bulletins and contributing regularly to the "National Municipal Review." Mr. Arch M. Mandel of the Dayton Research Association, 409 Lowe Building, Dayton, Ohio, is Secretary of the Conference and his office supplies information regarding activities in the municipal research field.
6. American Political Science Association. This is an organization of students of government and political science. It issues quarterly the "American Political Science Review," Professor Frederick A. Off of the University of Wisconsin, Madison, Wisconsin, is Secretary of the Association.
The above are agencies supported in part by contributions.
In addition there are purely commercial agencies, such as: Griffenhagen and Associates, Ltd., 155 East Superior Street, Chicago, Illinois; J. L. Paul, Toledo and New Orleans.
2. Municipal Reference Libraries. A number of the larger American cities maintain municipal reference libraries and these are important agencies in the distribution of civic documents and information. The New York Municipal Reference Library, for example, has one of the most complete collections of books and data on all civic and municipal subjects. Other cities maintaining such libraries are: Baltimore, Chicago, St. Louis, Cleveland, Cincimati, Milwaukee, etc.

Chart IV Chart IV(a) Procedure-Administration, notice to employer, claim filing, settlement of claims, award effect, review and modification, appeals Coverage of Acts-Employments covered 21-25 26-29 Chart V Occupational Diseases-Coverage, medical provisions, claim filing, limitations on compensation 30-32 Chart VI Silicosis-Coverage, medical provisions, claim filing, limitations in death cases, partial disability, exposure requirements 33-37 Chart VII Medical Benefits and Waiting Period 38-39 Chart VIII Second Injury Funds 40-42 Chart IX Rehabilitation-Funds established Chart X Chart XI Methods of assessments for administration expenses Reports of Accidents-Record keeping, reporting requirements penalties Chart XII Appeal Provisions-Time for appeal, process and procedure, basis for review 46-47 Chart XIII Insurance Requirements-Type of law, insurance, self-insurance, penalties 48-49 Chart XIV Special provisions for minors JANUARY 1948 FOREWORD THIS BOOKLET IS DESIGNED to provide both employees and employers with an easy-to-read analysis of benefits allowed under state workmen's compensation laws and of the insurance requirements for complying with those laws.
All employers, no matter whether they are selfinsured or use the facilities of an insurance company, will find this information invaluable.
By use of the 14 charts, employers will be able to inform employees of the terms and conditions under which protection is afforded by law. An employer operating in more than one state can quickly determine almost any provision with which he must comply under the various laws.

I✶ History of Workmen's Compensation and Employer's Liability THE ECONOMIC THEORY underlying workmen's compensation is referred to frequently as the doctrine of occupational risk, the principle being that the risk of economic loss through personal injury in the course of employment should be borne by the industry.
The economic loss which results from such disablement is now considered a cost of production, and should be incorporated in the price of production in accord with the current price system. Therefore, workmen's compensation laws are designed to give an injured worker prompt medical care, and a reasonable part of the wages lost, at the expense of the employer; and, with a minimum of inconvenience to the worker, his dependents are provided reasonable sustenance for the loss of normal, expected wages.
Before workmen's compensation laws were enacted, an injured worker sued his employer for damages and had to prove that the injury was due to negligence by the employer. The court remedy was slow, costly and uncertain.
At the close of the nineteenth century, it was a well-established principle of common law that the master, or employer, was responsible for the injury or death of employees resulting from a negligent act by him. As industry and commercial enterprise expanded, coincident with machine production, the number of industrial accidents and the number of personal injury suits increased. It became apparent that the accepted common law defenses (of Contributory Negligence, Assumption of Risk, and Negligent Acts of Fellow Servants) were operating too harshly on the claims of injured workers. Between 1900 and 1910, many states by judicial decision adopted a rule that the employees did not assume the risk of their employment.
Consequently, there was a search for a new remedy. In 1911 the first workmen's compensation law now in effect in the United States was passed.
Since that time there have been notable developments in that field.
There no longer is a question about adopting a workmen's compensation law; the question now is the extent of the protection and employee benefits, the method of administration, and whether such protection shall be provided through private insurance carriers or state insurance funds.
History of the legislation shows that state legislators intended to enact compensation laws to cover these fundamental points: 1 Provide certain prompt and reasonable compensation to victims of work accidents and their dependents.
 3 2 Free the courts from delay, cost, and the tremendous work load ⭑ of this mass of personal injury litigation.
3 Relieve public and private charities of the financial drain caused by uncompensated industrial accidents.
4 Eliminate economic waste in payment of fees to lawyers and witnesses, and save the time consumed by trials and appeals.
5 Supplant concealment of fault in accidents by a spirit of frank study of causes, thereby eliminating accidents that are preventable and reducing cost and suffering.
⭑ The main objective of workmen's compensation legislation is the payment of benefits to injured employees or to the dependents of employees killed in industry regardless of who is at fault in the accident. Obviously, it is necessary that the scope of this legislation be familiar to every employer.
Workmen's compensation laws provide that an employee injured by an industrial accident shall recover a stipulated amount (see Charts I, II, and III) from the employer or his insurer, regardless of negligence, and in return for this shall not have the right to sue his employer at common law for injuries within the act.
II Compulsory and Elective State Laws ⭑ STATE COMPENSATION LAWS are either compulsory or elective. A compulsory law requires every employer within its scope to accept its provisions and to provide for the benefits specified. (See Chart XIII) Under an elective system, the employer may either accept or reject the act, but in case of rejection, he loses the three common law defenses-Assumption of Risk, Negligence of Fellow Employees, and Contributory Negligence.
No compensation law covers all employment. In some states, employees in an exempted class may be brought in voluntarily by the employer. Chart IV explains the procedure under the various laws in "Notice to Employee," "Claim Filing," "How Claims are Settled," "Award Effect," "Review by Agencies," "Modification," "Court Appeals" and "Attorney Fees." ✩ III Scope of Legislation EVERY STATE, except Mississippi, has a workmen's compensation law. In addition there are Federal Workmen's Compensation Laws, such as the Workmen's Compensation Law of the District of Columbia, the United States Employees' Compensation Act for Federal Employees, and the United ⭑ 4* States Longshoremen and Harbor Workers' Compensation Act for private or public employees in nation-wide maritime employment. The latter law excludes the master and crew of vessels.
It is estimated that approximately 45,000,000 workers are protected by workmen's compensation.
While all the states agree as to the principle of workmen's compensation, no two of them have exactly the same benefits. The laws meet particular conditions. As already stated, employers in some states are required to operate under the compensation act, while in others they may refuse to do so if they prefer to risk an injured worker's suit for damages. The degree of protection received by an employee or employer is affected by the various administrative provisions of the different laws.
In none of the states does the law apply to all employees. The principal examples of non-coverage are agricultural workers and domestic servants, exceptions below a fixed number of employees, public employees and casual workers. (Chart IV (a)) A. Numerical Exceptions-In 30 states employers are exempt if they employ less than a certain number of people. Most acts permit voluntary acceptance. Exceptions under the acts are found in Chart IV (a) under the titles "Exceptions and Special Coverage Provisions" and "Hazardous Employment." Nine states provide that their Compensation Laws apply only to hazardous employment.* Those states are Kansas, Louisiana, Maryland, Montana, New Mexico, Oklahoma, Oregon, Washington and Wyoming. In Illinois and New York, the acts are compulsory as to hazardous industries and elective as to other employment.
B. Occupational Disease-Consistent with the basic purposes of workmen's compensation, there has been a tendency during recent years to bring occupational disease under workmen's compensation laws and to liberalize provisions referring to occupational disease in those states with statutes on the subject. To the extent that occupational diseases are specifically included within a workmen's compensation law, an employer automatically has complete insurance protection under the standard Workmen's Compensation and Employers' Liability policy with respect to such diseases.
(See Charts V and VI) In most states where occupational disease is not within the workmen's compensation law, the employer may be liable at common law for the payment of damages. Insurance coverages against this liability may be made The statutes in each case define what hazardous employment means.
 5 available by simply adding a rider to the standard policy and paying the increased premium.
There are now 39 states which, by one method or another, compensate for all or specified occupational diseases. A summary of statutory provisions on occupational diseases will be found in Chart V.
The casualty companies believe the important problem in the occupational disease field is not so much how compensation benefits are to be provided as how the hazards leading to such diseases are to be brought under control. Much progress has been made by combining medical and engineering techniques. Such an approach necessitates a determination of the significance of the hazard from the standpoint of health and the designing of controls to keep exposures below the safety levels established by medical research.
Employers by cooperating with safety engineers of insurance carriers can eliminate many of the hazards which cause occupational diseases.
The development of many new materials and processes has required continued vigilance on the part of both engineer and doctor. The coordination of their services has been one of the outstanding contributions to the solution.
of the occupational disease problem made by casualty insurance companies.
C. Minors Under Workmen's Compensation Law-Many workmen's compensation acts provide coverages for minors. In some states, however, as illustrated in Chart XIV, double compensation or additional penalties are provided and minors in a number of states, as illustrated by Chart XIV, enjoy special benefit provisions by law. Therefore, employers should exercise extreme caution in employing persons who are legally classified as minors.
D. Workers Injured Outside the State-It frequently happens that a worker's occupation takes him from one state into another while working for the same employer. If an injury occurs outside the home state, a question arises as to which state law determines the compensation payable, because about two-thirds of the compensation laws are extra-territorial in effect, either by specific provision or court decision. The answer, depending upon the provisions of the particular laws involved, requires consideration of many circumstances such as the place of employment, the nature of the employment, the place where the contract of employment was made, the residence of the employee, and the place of business of the employer.
E. Public Employees-Thirty-three states require public employees of the states and their subdivisions to qualify under the Compensation Act.
Chart IV (a) summarizes the various provisions of law relating to public employment.
 6 IV Insurance Requirement for Workmen's Compensation A. Security for Payment of Workmen's Compensation Benefits—Nearly all state laws require the employer either to obtain insurance or to give proof of his financial ability to carry his own risk. Chart XIII sets forth the penalties for failure to insure and states whether insurance is required and whether or not self-insurance is permitted.
Seven states have established monopolistic state funds. In those states, employers are required to insure in the state fund and in some instances may qualify as self-insurers. Certain states have competitive state funds; in those states, the employer may purchase his workmen's compensation insurance either from the state fund or from a private insurance company.
While the Chamber of Commerce of the United States has long been on record in favor of compensating workmen injured in the course of their employment without regard to the negligence of the employer, it and other business organizations advocate that employers be permitted to buy workmen's compensation insurance in private insurance companies if they so desire; or employers who can qualify should be allowed to be self-insurers.
The National Chamber's policies state: "To compel employers to insure in a monopolistic State Workmen's Compensation Insurance Fund interferes with the freedom which all private enterprise should possess." Business men's organizations should thoroughly analyze workmen's compensation proposals in their legislatures so they may use their best efforts to protect the true purpose of workmen's compensation and oppose all attempts to abuse it at the expense of either the employees or employers.
B. Self-Insurers-Some business men and many of the larger corporations feel they can assume their own insurance on workmen's compensation to their financial advantage. If an employer does take out a compensation policy, his insurance company steps into his shoes in making compensation.
payments.
Self-insurance in theory operates at its best when an employer has so large a spread of risks that he is able to enjoy the benefit of the law of large numbers. It is, however, necessary that the self-insurer establish his own protective services similar to that furnished him by insurance companies, such as safety engineering, claim adjustment, etc. He also must retain at- 7 torneys and doctors to deal with the numerous and difficult problems incident to proper claim, medical and legal services.
Sums Set Aside for Self-Insurance Funds Are Not Deductible as Expenses in Income Tax Returns The United States Circuit Court of Appeals, Tenth Circuit (Spring Canyon Coal Co. vs. Commissioner of Internal Revenue) has ruled that amounts paid by an employer into a fund set up as a reserve for selfinsurance which is used for payment of compensation and other benefits under the Workmen's Compensation Act of the state-are not deductible from gross income as a business expense for income tax purposes under Section 234 of the Revenue Acts of 1918 and 1921.
The Court said in part: "The whole object of self-insurance is to avoid the expense of insurance premiums. If the petitioner had elected to insure its risks in the State fund or a private company, it would have expended the premium and shifted the risk; instead, it retained the risk and kept the premium. Having elected not to expend the premium it cannot charge a corresponding sum as an 'expense.' The 'expense' incurred and deductible were the sums paid its injured workmen, and not the amounts set aside for their protection; the petitioner is not entitled to deduct as an expense a sum of money which it might have expended for insurance premiums, but did not." The transfer of money to a self-insurance fund, denied as an income tax credit in the Spring Canyon case, is not the most important feature of self-insurance, from a tax standpoint. The greater damage comes from the fact that reserves for incurred but unpaid losses are not allowed as income tax deductions; only the payments are allowed. If an accident occurs involving a lifetime pension valued at $20,000, the employer has suffered a real loss of $20,000 at the time of the accident, but he will get income tax credit in the year of the accident only for the amount of the pension which is paid in that year. Future payments will be deducted in future years. This situation works all right while there is a level relationship between income and accidents. However, take an extreme example of a contractor who does one large job for which his normal workmen's compensation losses are $500,000, and suppose the contract is completed within a year. The income is earned but only a small amount of the losses will be paid during the year.
Their future cost can easily be estimated, but the ability to estimate future cost doesn't warrant an income tax deduction. At tax rates 40% to 90% Recognizable, however, if an award has been made.
8* and with an irregular income, the self-insured employer has a considerable income tax disadvantage. Insurance in many cases is purchased purely because it can dispose of the item of expense and future cost in the year when the income can carry it.
C. Monopolistic Funds-It is important to note that five of the seven monopolistic state funds were created between 1913 and 1915 when the principle of workmen's compensation was still new.
Ohio is the largest State having such a fund. Private service agencies have been developed in Ohio to provide services to employers at their own expense to afford services not received from the state government.
D. Workmen's Compensation Audits—Among the services made available by private insurance companies is that of trained auditors of payrolls.
It is the duty of such auditors to examine payroll records of employers for the purpose of determining the amount of premium to be paid. The auditors ascertain that employers pay only the proper premiums for the risks involved. The correct allocation of payrolls leads to accurate rate-making data. The auditors also reduce materially the time and expense of employers' bookkeepers who otherwise would have to perform these functions.
In addition, auditors aid employers in simplifying maintenance of payroll records for audit purposes. They also offer information on technical questions concerning state requirements, rules and regulations pertaining to the application of rates to payrolls.
It is not the function of insurance company auditors to investigate the business of employers, but to the contrary, to cooperate with them in determining accurate premiums. Payroll data is highly confidential information and is so regarded by insurance companies.
V ⋆ Rate Making National Rate Administrative Bureau-Premium rates for workmen's compensation insurance are compiled on a scientific basis. A bureau has been set up which collects experience on the number of accidents occurring in all of the various industries and businesses. This bureau is nationally recognized by all carriers of insurance and administrators of State Funds.
Early in the history of workmen's compensation it became evident that rate making for compensation insurance could not be handled by each State separately. A conference called in 1915 resulted in an organization for this purpose which became known as the National Council on Compensation Insurance. The States that have independent bureaus cooperate with the 9* National Council on Compensation Insurance in making their rates. The manual rates of this organization are generally used as a basis for compensation rates charged by both stock and mutual companies.
The Constitution of the National Council on Compensation Insurance provides: "The objects of the National Council shall be 1 The making of rates for workmen's compensation insurance ⭑ 2 The collection and tabulation of statistics bearing thereon 3* The development of rating plans and systems that will not only measure the hazard of each risk but that will produce the greatest possible accident prevention effect, and 4 The administration of such rates and rating systems in the interests of the insured, the carrier, and the public." Member companies of the National Council report to that organization experience incurred under workmen's compensation policies in accordance with approved statistical procedure. That experience serves as the basis for workmen's compensation rates determined in accordance with a standard countrywide rate-making procedure approved by the National Association of Insurance Commissioners.
The National Council publishes a Basic Manual-which is standard with all insurance companies-setting forth the National Council rules, procedure and rates applicable to workmen's compensation insurance. In those states where the statutes provide for rate regulation by a state supervising authority, revised compensation rates and supporting data are filed annually with such authority and, in many cases, public hearings are held before rates are revised. In those states, the supervising authority must approve the rates the carriers charge. In a few states, the statutes do not provide for rate regulation by the state authority.
Compensation rates as set forth in the Manual are rates per $100 of payroll. Usually, the premium collected when the policy is written is only an estimate. After expiration of the policy, a payroll audit is required, the actual premium is computed on the basis of the audited payroll and corresponding adjustments are made.
Rate Increases Due to Increased Benefits-It should be noted that during 1947, forty-two state legislatures in their regular sessions enacted changes in their workmen's compensation laws.
Twenty-two states increased the benefits. Obviously, increased premiums will have to be charged to meet the cost of the increased benefits, which ranged from five to more than twenty-five per cent.
*10* VI Claim Administration TWO GENERAL METHODS are in effect in administering workmen's compensation laws.
★ By the courts By a commission or board created by law Chart IV shows the statutory provisions relating to administration.
A. Records and Reports Required of Employers-Of great interest to every employer are the requirements imposed upon him by law for reporting injuries and the fine, if any, imposed for failure to report. In many jurisdictions, except for the preliminary report, the insured's insurance company takes this burden off the shoulders of the employer.
Chart XI summarizes the provisions of the various states for employers' reports of accidents.
B. Courts or Commissions Having Jurisdiction of Workmen's Compensation Disputes—Many employers covered under workmen's compensation.
laws are unfamiliar with the procedure relating to claims, the process and procedure, the basis for review, whether they are entitled to a jury trial, and whether questions of law and fact may be reviewed. (See Charts IV and XI) Chart XII summarizes the provisions for appeal.
C. Suits for Damages-Employers accepting any workmen's compensation.
act are generally exempt from damage suits; those rejecting an act are relieved of paying compensation but are subject to suit with the three usual defenses abrogated. However, where an employee rejects the act and sues an employer who has accepted it-the employer usually retains those three defenses. The conditions of rejection of the act are often so severe as to make the privilege practically inoperative.
Therefore, except in rare instances, the employer has accepted the act and is relieved of lawsuits, the adjustment of his claims either being left to his insurance company or to the findings of the state regulatory body administering the workmen's compensation law. Claim provisions are to be found in Chart IV. This chart sets out the administration, notice to employer, claim filing, how claims are settled, award effect, modifications, court appeals, attorneys' fees. In about two-thirds of the states, the employee may sue for damages when the employer has failed to provide a compensation program or to pay the premiums on it.
 Alabama, Alaska, Louisiana, New Hampshire, New Mexico, Tennessee, Wyoming.
 11 ⋆ VII Types of Benefit A. Death Benefits-The methods for determining compensation for death.
vary, and may depend upon the existence of "dependents." In many laws, widows and young children are conclusively presumed to be "dependents" but other persons may be "dependents" if actually supported, in whole or in part, by the deceased. The great variety of provisions makes it practically impossible to set forth these regulations in a chart. Chart I shows the workmen's compensation benefits for fatal injuries accruing to widows and children under the Acts.
B. Medical Benefits-Medical aid including operations, if needed, is required to be furnished injured workmen in addition to compensation payments. A summary of the statutory medical benefits is set forth in Chart VII.
Early legislation restricted medical aid to the monetary cost, period of treatment, or both. The law has been liberalized in many states, and sixteen states require medical aid to be furnished without limit as to time or amount.
In a number of states limiting medical treatment, if the injured requires additional medical treatment, the administrative authority may allow additional payments.
C. Effect of Liberal Amendments-Liberal amendments to the workmen's compensation laws and interpretations which enable increased claim payments to be made, naturally have a direct bearing on increasing the amount of cost to the employer.
There has been a marked tendency to extend the field covered by workmen's compensation laws by amendments to the laws or their interpretation.
Awards are being made for death and disabilities claimed to have resulted from occupational pursuits; for example, cases of nerve shock and fright from witnessing an injury, and physical disability remotely related to the claimant's employment are being construed to be compensable by liberal interpretations of administrative authorities.
Where the way is open to such abuses, claims for compensation are being made as a means of obtaining relief for wage loss from a wide variety of questionable circumstances. It is in the interest of employer and employee to have fair determinations and interpretations of workmen's compensation.
laws.
D. Amount and Period of Benefits-Three factors determine the amount an injured workman receives under the different compensation acts.
1 The rate-usually a percentage of the wages 2 The duration of payment ⭑ 3 The maximum aggregate amount payable 12 All of the workmen's compensation acts prescribe payment in case of death and also in the case of permanent total disability.
 Death benefits, maximum periods, maximum per week, and minimum per week are set forth in Chart I. Total disability and limitations on personal and temporary disability are set forth in Chart II. Maximum for specific injuries and benefits for partial disability are listed in Chart III.
E. Waiting Periods-Except in Oregon, the statutes provide that a specified period of time, known as a waiting period, shall elapse during which time compensation is not payable. This waiting period affects only compensation; medical and hospital care is provided immediately. A majority of laws provide that if disability continues for a certain number of weeks, payment of compensation is retroactive to the date of the injury. Chart VII summarizes the statutory provisions of waiting periods.
VIII Services Rendered by Casualty Insurance Companies A. Accident Prevention-Casualty insurance companies have attained leadership in accident prevention through years of experience gained through working with American industry. Safety engineers are making outstanding contributions to the creation and maintenance of safer working conditions by establishing safe methods as a fundamental requirement for maximum production efficiency.
Insurance company engineers aid management by studying accident causes, designing remedial equipment or measures and presenting their findings in workable form for management to apply. Continuing accident prevention service of insurance engineers provides management with the technical advice needed to maintain an accident prevention program at peak performance.
During times of war, this accident prevention service has been a valuable aid in the conservation of manpower through prevention of industrial injuries. Despite an unprecedented increase in industrial employment, the accident rate in industry has been maintained at low level; accident prevention engineers have met and conquered the problems of green help, untrained supervision, new processes and exposures.
The cost of accident prevention service is included in workmen's compensation insurance premiums; its benefits are found in lower insurance Except Oklahoma.
 13 rates, increased production efficiency, and more complete utilization of available manpower. These results are the profit values of intelligent loss prevention service provided by casualty insurance companies in the workmen's compensation policy. But the greatest beneficiaries are the men and women who have not been crippled or killed in industrial accidents that have been prevented through the application of effective loss prevention engineering methods.
B. Causes of Accidents-Accident prevention work should be aimed at specific objectives.
Analysis of accident experience is the first step in an accident prevention program. Fatal accidents inspire quick demands for action, yet they occur with the lowest frequency. Disability-producing accidents occur more frequently and produce the greatest costs in insurance benefits and wasted manpower. Minor accidents, with their high frequency of occurrence, point the way to the greatest gain if studied for remedial measures since the degree of an injury is often a matter of luck.
A well-rounded accident prevention program must include the engineering of safety into all phases of operations. The safety engineer can be of great assistance in the planning of the following important divisions of operations: 1 Manufacturing methods ★ 2 Physical and personal safeguards 3* Maintenance procedures 4* Plant housekeeping 5* Management interest 6 Supervisory control 7* Labor training The casualty insurance engineer will cooperate in setting up an accident.
prevention program of continuing benefits in reduced manpower losses, increased operating efficiency and lower casualty insurance cost.
IX Future Considerations A. Second Injuries-The term "second injury" as used in relation to workmen's compensation is unfamiliar to many employers. It means an injury, sustained by an employee who has suffered a previous injury, such that the disability resulting from the combined effects of both injuries is greater than if there had been no previous injury. In the absence of statutory exception, the employer in whose employ the second injury is sustained ordinarily is liable for the compensation due for the total resulting disability. Thus, be14* cause of the potential increased cost of compensation benefits, an employer might be influenced to refuse employment to handicapped persons.
A majority of compensation laws now have provisions limiting the liability of the employer in certain cases to payment for the disability resulting from the second injury considered by itself.
A number of these states have created so-called "second injury funds" with the result that although the employer or his insurance company pays only for the last injury considered alone, the employee receives from the second injury fund the additional compensation due for the combined result of both injuries.
The enactment of additional "second injury laws" is regarded by many as at least a practical solution of the problem of re-employment of handicapped persons.
B. Rehabilitation of the Industrially Disabled-Fourteen states have made specific provision either by way of establishing special funds or providing special maintenance allowances for retraining industrially disabled employees. In addition to such specific provisions all states have now accepted the provisions of the Federal Vocational Rehabilitation Act which provides a federal fund to assist in retraining all people who are disabled by industrial accidents.
The Rehabilitation Act not only provides for vocational rehabilitation but provides surgical service and limited hospital service when needed to carry out the rehabilitation of a handicapped person. In the case of civil service workers, maritime employees, and civilian defense workers the Act provides for complete reimbursement to the state for moneys spent under the terms of the law. For industrial workers and others, it provides for fifty per cent reimbursement.
Many employers who maintain medical departments as well as insurance carriers are carrying out programs for the physical restoration of the industrially injured. Many large industries have developed very comprehensive programs for the safe employment of physically handicapped persons.
Numerous smaller industries are now finding that their programs for the appropriate placement of disabled persons can be modified to their practical use. Stimulus is being given to these developments because of the advent of the return of disabled veterans in increasing numbers. All employees, and employers as well, will benefit, however, from the development of these employment programs.
X✶ Recent Changes In State Laws: DURING THE LAST THREEE YEARS (1945-1947), twenty-two states have enacted occupational disease laws. Because conditions in states vary it is not 15 possible to make these laws exactly uniform. The disease schedules vary from covering one to forty-six specific occupational diseases.
In the following charts we have not tried to explain why there is a difference in State Laws and differences in percentage of wages, maximum limitations, death benefits, permanent total disability, permanent partial disability, etc. The state legislatures must enact the type of law which will meet their individual requirements in each state.
In 1947, forty-two state legislatures were in session and legislation affecting workmen's compensation was enacted by most of these states.

excluded employment Compensation laws are classified as compulsory or elective. A compulsory law requires every employer to accept the act and pay the compensation specified An elective act is one in which the employer has the option of either accepting or rejecting the act, but in case he rejects it he loses the customary common la defenses (assumed risk of the employee. negligence of fellow servants and contributory negligence). In most States workers in excepted or may be brought under coverage of the act through voluntary action of the employer. "See "Exceptions." Compensation lavere classified as compulsory or elective. A compulsory law requires every employer to accept the act and pay the compensation specified.
An elective act is one in which the employer has the option of either accepting or rejecting the act, but in case he rejects it, he loses the customary common law defenses (assumed risk of the employee, negligence of fellow servants and contributory negligence). In most States workers in excepted or excluded employmenta pipe brought under coverage of the act through voluntary action of the employer. In other States, such action of the employer must be concurred in by the enployees.
The exceptions for public employments are given under "Employments Covered Public." State Supreme rules all public employees subject to the act Applying to private employments only.
The law states that it is elective as to private employments of a nonhazardous nature. However, as there are no provisions in the law abrogating the employer's defenses in case he does not accept the act, it is considered, in this table, to be a voluntary one.

'Compensation laws are classified as compulsory or elective. A compulsory law requires every employer to accept the act and pay the compensation specified.
An elective act is one in which the employer has the option of either accepting or rejecting the act, but in case he rejects it he loses the customary common la defenses (assumed risk of the employee, negligence of fellow servants and contributory negligence). In most States workers in excepted or excluded employments may be brought under coverage of the act through voluntary action of the employer. In other States, such action of the employer must be concurred in by the en ployees.
2Applying to private employments only. The exceptions for public employments are given under "Employments Covered Public." The law states that it is elective as to private employments of a nonhazardous nature. However, as there are no provisions in the law abrogating the employer's defenses in case he does not accept the act, it is considered, in this table, to be a voluntary one. State Supreme rules all public employees subject to the act without right of rejection.

For failure to provide security for 30 days after notice by Commissioner, employer liable to fine of not more than $25 per day after first 30 days.
Fine of 10 cents per day per employee (maximum $50, minimum $1 per day); if default continues for 30 days employer may be enjoined from doing business. Employer liable to suit with defenses abrogated.

In addition, employer may be enjoined from doing business. Employer also liable to suit with defenses abrogated.

Example No. It is an ideal location for fast development and sound investment.
Affiant says the nucleus of his organization was made up of six men who worked for him before the war and who were not union men. Others were added to the crew which totaled between nine and eleven men.
Affiant says for the most part he has relied upon his employees when recruiting new help, and has not made union membership an issue when hiring new men. He has been consistant in not discriminating between union and non-union help.
Affiant says that in the summer of 1945 and spring of 1946 he was visited by John J. Church, executive secretary of Building and Construction Trades Council and several business agents of affiliated building trades unions. He says he was informed on the second visit that if he did not sign a closed shop agreement, his project would be picketed. He was specifically threatened by the business agent for the Electrical Workers Union No. 1, A.F. of L. with the boycott of utilities. He claimed the employees of Union Electric Company of Missouri were now affiliated with the A.F. of L. and would honor his picket line.
as electric.
This would affect gas service as well Affiant says that on May 28, 1946 the boycott was started by the Building and Construction' Trades Council, and later joined by the electrical union.
This boycott is still in force under the direction of the Building and Construction Trades Council..

-2Affiant savs that on May 28, 1946 when the boycott was established, he made it clear to each one of his employees that he was free to decide his future course without prejudice. On May 29, 1946 all employees returned for work and reaffirmed their loyalty. One bricklayer with over thirty years experience in his trade remarked in broken English, "You been good to 'unser' we be good to you." Two other employees said, "We just won't do anything to hurt you." Affiant says this statement excludes one man who worked seven weeks on a temporary basis. This man was a personal friend who belonged to an industrial union and had no experience in the construction industry. He asked for help while his union was on strike, and affiant gave him a job. This was consistant with affiant's policy of non-discrimination. This man could not jeopardize his seniority in his union, so he did not return for work on May 29th.
Affiant says he has made a pact with his employees that he will under no circumstance do anything to hurt the employees or any of the subcontractors.
Affiant says his minimum wage is $1.25 per hour and his maximum wage is $2.00 per hour. Neither he nor his employees bid against union contractors. He hires his men on a year round basis and assumes responsibility for giving steady full employment to the limit of his resourses. Pursuant to this objective in January 1946, into joint venture, neither of them taking a salary, and both of them investing unstintingly of their time and money. The profits were to be shared equally without regard to investment. The twofold purpose was to establish Gordon W. Caldwell in business, and to make the employees less dependent on one man for their employgordon W. Caldwell is a Marine Corps pilot with three and a half years of service, served in the Philippines and China and he is now in active reserve. Affiant gives his age as 34 years, the affiant took his brother Gordon W. Caldwell ment.
his brother as 26 years.
Affiant says he has recently been informed by one of his purchasers that the employees of Union Electric Company of Missouri will not install utilities in his subdivision until instructed by their union, even though a picket line may fail to appear at the subdivision.

-3Affiant says that one of his suppliers of building material has been threatened with secondary boycott if he permits affiant to haul any material from his yard.
Affiant says that his total investment including borrowed capital is over $100,000.00. This does not include any salary for affiant since June 30, 1945 or any salary for Gordon W. Caldwell since January 1946. He is borrowing at the rate of $5,000.00 per month to keep his organization.
Affiant says that since January 1946 he has sold only one home. On October 30, 1946 he received $7,950.00 in cash and has not closed another deal since that date. This purchaser signed application for utility service on October 30, 1946. By November 6, 1946 the electric was still not run to the meter box; so affiant extended a temporary line from the home of Gordon W.
Caldwell at his own expense. The gas service line to this house was installed in March 1946 before the boycott was instituted.
The meter was installed Saturday, November 9, 1946 and the purchaser moved into his home November 12, 1946. The purchaser has made frequent attempts to obtain his electric service but has been unsuccessful. He has appealed, without results, to the local Veterans Administration Office and to the Public Service Commission at Jefferson City, Missouri. He has nearly three years service in the infantry of the United States Army and participated in the Battle of the Bulge.
Affiant says he has eight other homes practically ready for Occupancy, and five additional homes under roof. Without boycott all of these units could have been completed and occupied five months aso, even allowing for material shortages.
