Me hitch Notary Public Jublic EXAMPLE NO. 2 SUBJECT: BOYCOTT PREVENTS DELIVERY OF PHARMACEUTICAL PRODUCTS COMPANY: E. R. SQUIBB SONS, LOS ANGELES, CALIF.
UNION: TEAMSTERS (AFL) AFFIDAVIT OF C.M. VAN KIRK, ADMINISTRATIVE VICE-PRESIDENT, E. R. SQUIBB SONS, NEW YORK, N. Y.

--X In the Matter of E. R. SQUIBB SONS : STATE OF NEW YORK ss.: COUNTY OF NEW YORK) -X C.M. VAN KIRK being duly sworn, deposes and says: That I am Administrative Vice President of E. R. Squibb Sons, a corporation organized and existing under the laws of the State of New York, having its principal place of business in the City of New York, State of New York.
That E. R. Squibb Sons is engaged in the business of manufacturing and selling medicinal and pharmaceutical products. That in the course of its business E. R. Squibb Sons sells and arranges for delivery, from its warehouse in Los Angeles, of products essential to the public health and welfare such as penicillin, ether, vaccines, amniotin, serums, biologicals, arsenicals, home necessities and professional specialties. That said products are distributed to hospitals, clinics, sanitoria, drug stores and doctors which require reliability in deliveries and continuity of service.
That this affidavit is made upon information and belief.
The sources of my information and grounds of my belief are the complaint dated September 27, 1946, made by E. R. Squibb Sons in the Superior Court of the State of California, County of Los Angeles, for an Injunction, Preliminary Injunction,& Temporary Restraining Order in an action against General Warehousemen's Local Union No. 598, et al, and the affidavits of L.L. HERBERT Branch Manager, and RAY ROBBINS, Warehouse Foreman, of E. R. Squibb Sons, 1855 Industrial Street, Los Angeles, California, submitted in support of said complaint.
As That E. R. Squibb Sons is a tenant of E.S. STANLEY, doing business as Star Truck and Warehouse Company, in premises 1855 Industrial Street, Los Angeles, California.
tenant, E. R. Squibb Sons is permitted the co-use of Landlord's sidings, freight elevators and receiving and shipping docks.
That commencing August 16, 1946 General Warehousemen's Local No. 598, chartered by the International Brotherhood of Teamsters, Chauffeurs, Warehousemen and Helpers of America, affiliated with the American Federation of Labor (hereinafter called AFL) through its business agent, Mr. James A. Wallace, attempted to get Squibb branch employees to sign an authorization for representation under the National Labor Relations Act. On August 21, 1946, Mr. Wallace explained to Mr. Herbert that a majority of Squibb's warehouse employees had signed as members of AFL,, and Mr. Wallace requested Mr. Herbert to draw up a contract with the AFL.
After many conferences with Mr. Wallace and Mr. Gene Blackwell, organizer for the Western Werehouse and Produce Council of the American Federation of Labor, the A,F.L.
advised Mr. Herbert that it would petition the NLRB for an election.
The Election was held on September 13, 1946, sometime after 10 a.m., in the Squibb Warehouse. There were nine eligible voters. While the election was being held, Mr. Herbert saw Mr. Wallace in the presence of Mr. Blackwell, who said that he thought the election would go five to four either one way or the other, and that they hoped they would win or else. When asked what "or else" meant, Mr. Blackwell said that if they lost the election, Mr. Mcgee (Secretary Treasurer or AFL) would not like it and it was liable to be rough so he hoped the union would win. Mr. Blackwell and Mr. Wallace came to Mr. Herbert's office when the election returns were delivered.
The result of the election was that out of nine voting employees seven voted against joining the A. F. of L. union and two voted for joining the union.
Mr. Herbert observed that both Mr. Blackwell and Mr. Wallace were quite disturbed over the outcome of the election and their attitude, which had been friendly during the period of negotiation, changed completely. On leaving Mr. Herbert's office, they said "You will hear from us later.
About 11:00 A.M. Friday morning, September 13th, Mr. Herbert was advised that Mr. Wallace was on the Star Truck and Warehouse dock with an A. F. of L. button on his shirt demanding of all carriers that they refuse to accept shipments for E. R. Squibb Sons and that no carrier be permitted to deliver shipments to E. R. Squibb Sons; that the A. F. of L. union was on strike against E. R. Squibb Sons.
Mr. Wallace and his associates remained on the dock during the balance of the working day and no carrier would accept shipments from Squibb nor deliver to Squibb.
On Saturday morning, September 14th, at 10:30 A.M., Mr. Herbert received a telephone call from Mr. Jorgenson of the J C Transfer Company advising him that Wallace of the A. F. of L. threatened to get the license number of his truck and inform different companies not to receive Squibb shipments.
Mr. Jorgenson also told Mr. Herbert that on 9:30 A.M. Friday - 3morning, September 13th, at the very hour when Mr. Herbert was talking in his office to Mr. Blackwell, Mr. Wallace was on the Star Truck and Warehouse dock and told his driver not to move any Squibb shipments until after the results of the N.L.R.B.
election which was to take place Friday morning at ten o'clock.
Around 12:00 o'clock Friday morning, September 13th, Mr. Herbert asked Mr. Morgan Stanley to find out just what was happening and why shipments were not being received by our carriers. Mr. Stanley advised Mr. Herbert that as far as he could learn from his workers the A. F. of L. was on strike against Squibb. Mr. Stanley also advised Mr. Herbert later in the day that at 1:30 P.M., Mr. Wallace of the A. F.
of L. dropped into his office and told him that the A. F. of L.
was officially on strike against Squibb and that the strike was effective since 10:30 Friday morning.
Mr. Morgan Stanley also told Mr. Herbert that at about 2:30 P.M. he telephoned Mr. Mcgee to get a verification of Mr. Wallace's statement and that Mr. Mcgee of the A. F. of L.
told him the A. F. of L. was officially on strike against Squibb. However, Morgan Stanley said that the Star Truck and Warehouse would continue to receive our goods and give us a free flow of merchandise.
On Monday, September 16th, Mr. Leo Anderson, our attorney, and Mr. Herbert met with Mr. Mcgee of the A. F. of L. and asked Mr. Mcgee just why our shipments were being stopped. He said that our shipments were being stopped because our people had not voted in favor of the A. F. of L.
union. They then asked Mr. Mcgee what Squibb could do to put T JOCK Λ an end to this situation so that Squibb's merchandise could Mcgee said to have our people sign with the again move.
A, F. of L. union and then everything would be all right; and if they would not sign to fire them and he would see that we would have desirable employees. Mr. Anderson told Mr. Mcgee that such was not Squibb policy; that Squibb believed our employees were entitled to the right to decide for themselves and that it was not legal for Squibb to ask its employees to join or not to join any union. Mr. Mcgee said he did not believe in elections of the N.L.R.B., or all these laws. What he preferred was to use the club, as they used to do in the old days; that we had a choice of either signing our employees over to the A. F. of L. or being shut down.
Mr. Herbert told Mr. Mcgee that E. R. Squibb Sons had always enjoyed good labor relations and that our policy was one above question and that we had always preferred to deal aquarely and fairly; why did not Mr. Mcgee call off the strike and start all over again?; that maybe within a few months they could try again to organize our employees in the right way and that at that time we would be glad to bargin with the A. F. of L. provided that the majority of our employees voted in favor of the A. F. of L. union. Mr. Mcgee said "No." During the entire period from about 11:00 A.M. September 13th until the morning of September 19th, no carrier was permitted by the Teamster Union defendants to deliver or reiceive any of our goods.
- ·5.· " A detailed statement of the activities of the A. F. of L, are set forth below: 1.
On Wednesday afternoon, September 18th, a United Parcel Service truck drove up to our dock and attempted to pick up the orders going out to hospitals and other users. After considerable argument between the A. F. of L. representatives and the driver of the United Parcel Service truck, and a phone call to the United Parcel Service office, the truck withdrew without the merchandise it had come over for. After the truck withdrew, Mr. Herbert telephoned Mr. Haver of the United Parcel Service and asked him why the truck had left without the customary pick up. Mr. Haver told him that he would be glad to pick up and deliver our merchandise to Hospitals and other users because of the nature of our products, but because of the result of Wednesday's pick up attempt he asked for police protection. At this time Mr. Herbert arranged with Mr. Haver to have two large United Parcel Service trucks, two drivers and two helpers arrange to make a pick-up at 10:00 A.M.
Herbert notified the Police Department that we had been unable to operate after the union representatives had refused to allow any Squibb shipments to be made or received, that we had been told that the union was on strike against Squibb, this strike was called as the result of an N.L.R.B. election which they lost, and the police assured us that there would be police protection and the trucks would not be interfered with while they were being loaded. Later in the day - 6 - j Mr. Herbert was informed by Mr. Edgar Stanley, owner of the Star Truck and Warehouse, that if a carrier attempted to - make a Squibb pick up, the union representatives would close the doors of the dock; that they had arranged for a signal the signal would be the yelling of the word "Timber" by Mr. Wallace or one of his associates, and when the signal "Timber" was given, all of the doors of the Star Truck and Warehouse docks would be closed.
2. About 9 o'clock Thursday morning, September 19th, the police arrived. At or about 10:00 A.M. two United Parcel Service trucks drove up Industrial Street attempting to back in to the dock. They were stopped by Wallace and the men associated with the A. F. of L. union, and after much argument were able to back the truck up to the dock. When the truck backed into the dock, the A. F. of L. union representatives closed all of the dock doors. The argument continued between the United Parcel Service driver and the union representatives for about thirty or thirty five minutes.
Then one of the drivers went into Mr. Herbert's office and called his office. He explained the situation to them, and gave them the number of strikers which he estimated at two hundred to two hundred and fifty and said that if he was ordered to load he would so so. The company advised him to withdraw the truck without loading for fear of violence to their employees and damage to their equipment.
-7- !

3. After the United Parcel Service truck withdrew,Mr. Herbert telephoned the G. I. Trucking Company, a non-union trucking company, to arrange for a pick up as many Hospitals, institution s and other users were demanding our merchandise.
This carrier's truck arrived approximately fifteen to twenty minutes after the United Parcel Service truck had left the scene.
This truck was able to back into our dock with the assistance of the police. After the truck was in position to be loaded, Sgt. Stevens and two police officers came into my office and asked if we were going to open the doors and attempt shipment.
Mr. Edgar Stanley was in Mr. Herbert's office at this time and he said he did not have anyone to open the doors because all of his boys had been called off the job, but that we might open the doors and make shipment. The flats, loaded with merchandise for many Hospitals and other users, were taken down stairs and loaded on the G. I. Trucking Company truck.
During the period of loading, there was much swearing, threats, and disturbances created by the strikers, who showed no respect whatsoever for the police officers or the public in general. After the truck was loaded the driver and the driver's helper pulled away from the dock leaving the building.
4.
Then When the second G.I. truck pulled into position, Mr. Herbert saw one of the strikers strike a blow at the driver's helper through the window of the cab door.
several strikers ran towards the truck where the driver and the driver's helper were seated. The police officers then rushed in to restore peace and order. After a short time the - 8 - · - officers had the principal violent agitators under physical restraint and order was restored.
5.
6. On Saturday afternoon, September 21st, at approximately 1 P.M. Mr. Herbert received a telephone call from Mr. Adams and Mr. Wallace of the A. F. of L. union telling him of three Hospital emergency calls they had received resulting from the publicity in the newspapers. One of the calls was from the Santa Fe Hospital whose stocks of Penicillin were exhausted.
Mr. Peterman, the pharmacist of the Hospital, said he did not have enough Penicillin to last the day. Mr. Adams and Mr. Wallace asked Mr. Herbert if we could arrange to handle this shipment and stated that they would arrange to have our incoming shipments of Penicillin and other essential drugs handled in the usual way.
Mr. Wallace and Mr. Adams did not keep their word.
They refused, up to Friday, September 27th, (the date on which E. R. Squibb Sons brought action for a Temporary Restraining Order) to allow carriers to deliver Penicillin products, although these shipments were properly identified.
7.
On Saturday, September 21st, Leo Anderson and Mr. Herbert received many phone calls from Mr. Edgar Stanley requesting that they attend a meeting to be held at 2:30 o'clock with the head of the Motor Transportation Company, the heads of the Warehouse Association, and with three representatives of the A.F. of L Union. They thought that they could work out a means of - - 9 - operating, as the Star Truck and Warehouse Building was shut down. They met with these gentlemen at 2:30 Saturday afternoon, September 21st. Mr. Mcgee said that if we would call a meeting of our people Monday morning, September 23rd at 8:30 A.M., that he felt that they would sign them up, that he could convince our employees it would be better for them to join the union and have them sign up with the A.F. of L. Then the strike would be callthe pickets withdrawn and we could operate as in the ed off, past.
Mr. Leo Anderson told Mr. Mcgee that such a meeting could be arranged but asked him what would happen if the majority of the employees refused to sign up with the A. F. of L. Mr. Mcgee replied that if the majority of the employees refused to sign up, the strike would be continued until they shut us down. Mr. Leo Anderson said that the only way we would call such a meeting with our employees and with the union would be provided the union representatives would sign an agreement that in the event they were unsuccessful in signing the majority of our employees they would withdraw the pickets and restore our carrier service and start over again from scratch. This Mr. Mcgee and his associates refused.
8.
On Saturday afternoon, September 21st, about 6 P.M Mr. Herbert received a telephone call from Mr. Edgar Stanley, in which Mr. Stanley told him that he had made arrangements with the union whereby Star Truck and Warehouse could be opened provided Squibb agreed to the following: (a) Squibb could ship and receive via the Mateo Street dock.
(b) All employees of the warehouse would enter and leave the building through the Mateo Street dock and back stairs.
- 10 - (c) All pick ups by Hospitals and other users would be made through the Mateo Street dock.
After consulting with both Mr. Anderson and Mr. Sibley, Our General Attorney, Mr. Herbert told Mr. E. S. Stanley that for the next few days, but without prejudice to our rights to use the main dock and other facilities as usual, the G. I. Trucking Company would use the Mateo Street dock, provided this would permit the building to be opened, and that all incoming goods and Outgoing goods handled by the G. I. Trucking Company would go Over the Mateo Street dock if this would permit the building to be opened; but that all of our employees, regardless of whether they work in the warehouse or in the office, would use the Industrial Street entrance; which is the only public entrance and the entrance used by all employees of other tenants in the building; that all pick ups made by Hospitals and other users would be made in the usual way and not through any special dock; that all Parcel Post shipments and mail would go out in the usual way and that we would continue to receive our merchandise on the Santa Fe Star Truck and Warehouse spur as cars were spotted for us.
On Thursday morning, September 26th, Mr. Stanley had a guard at the front entrance (the Industrial Street and only public entrance) of the Star Truck and Warehouse building and refused entrance to all men employed by E. R. Squibb Sons in the warehouse, with the exception of the foreman.
This refusal was made only to Squibb warehouse employees at the only public entrance to the building.
The 11 - Warehouse employees of other tenants were permitted to use the front entrance as usual.
9. That on or about Friday, September 20, 1946, at or about 8:30 A.M., Mr. Herbert was informed by J. E. Bell, Sales Manager of the Los Angeles branch of F. R. Squibb Sons, that E. S.Stanley had informed said J. E. Bell that a couple of Teamster Union men had told E. S.
S. Stanley that they were after Mr. Herbert, and that the union men would get Mr. Herbert, as well as clean out the police force.
I am informed and verily believe that of the nine (9) arehouse employees of E. R. SQUIBB SONS, eight ignored the activities of the pickets and fully performed their duties every working day.
By reason of the activities described above, E. R. SQUIBB SONS issued the above mentioned complaint, verified on September 27, 1946, to the effect that General Warehousemen's Local Union No. 598, Truck Drivers Local Union No. 208, and Freight Handlers and Helpers Union No. 357, were all associated with one another and with the American Federation of Labor, and were acting in concert with the Los Angeles Central Labor Council Joint Council of Teamsters No. 42, in attempting to prevent Squibb from receiving and shipping Squibb products and to prevent common carriers and trucking companies from performing services for Squibb, by physical intimidation, coercion, mass picketing, threats and obscene language.
- 12.
- : That said complaint alleged that Railway Express Agency, Inc., United Parcel Service, System Freight Service, Jorgenson City Delivery, Simmons Transportation Lines, Ernst Trucking Service, Western Truck Lines, Ltd., Asbury Transportation Co., Arrowhead Freight Lines, Ltd., H. N. Transfer Co. and E. S. Stanley, doing as STAR TRUCK WAREHOUSE COMPANY were told in substance business that if they received, shipped or handled in any way goods or products belonging to Squibb they would and effect whatsoever become involved in labor difficulties themselves and that picket 1ines would be put around their places of business and strikes of their own employees would be called. Said complaint further alleged that the employees of said common carriers were told that if they handled any merchandise coming from or going to E. R.Squibb Sons Los Angeles premises, serious labor difficulties would ensue, the plants of said employees would be picketed and that the union membership of said employees would be suspended or terminated.
Based upon said complaint Judge A. W. Ashburn of the Superior Court of the State of California in and for the County of Los Angeles, issued on September 27, 1946, a Temporary Restraining Order, and Order to Show Cause why a preliminary injunction should not be entered. Subsequently, and on October 29, 1946 Judge Alfred E. Paonessa of said Court, issued a permanent Injunction. From said Injunction, it appears that the court, among other things, enjoined the defendants named in said complaint from engaging in any combination, agreement or concerted activity to cause the employees of any common carrier or warehouse to refuse to handle goods of E. R. Squibb Sons or to cease - · 13 - performing any services in connection with receiving or handling goods of E. R. Squibb Sons. Said Injunction also restrained threat of boycott or picketing of the affected common carriers or warehouses; the intimidation of Squibb or its employees or customers; and the picketing of Squibb's place of business in Los Angeles.
UNION: BROTHERHOOD OF PAPERMAKERS (AFL) AFFIDAVIT OF EDWARD DOCHERTY, TREASURER, HOLYOKE WIRE CLOTH COMPANY, HOLYOKE, MASS.
LETTERS FROM FORMER CUSTOMERS NOW UNABLE TO BUY HOLYOKE WIRE CLOTH COMPANY PRODUCT BECAUSE IT DOES NOT BEAR UNION LABEL STATEMENT. RE UNION LABEL IT AMICK I Gf WALIDVALL (E ELAYED DOORBELL FORT WIRD OCH Gie AMDIANA TOR HOLYOKE 10LYOR? Wire Cloth COMPANY GEORGE DOCHERTY, President FINE FOURDRINIER WIRES AND CYLINDER COVERS EDWARD DOCHERTY, Treasurer Holyoke, Massachusetts February 18th, 1947.
To Whom It May Concern; This is to certify that we are unable to sell our Fourdrinier Wires to Holyoke Paper Mills, due to the fact that they do not bear the union Label.
We have been in business since 1919, and have operated as an open shop since 1935.
In April 1946,Eagle Lodge No.1 International Brotherhood of Papermakers of Holyoke,notified the Holyoke Paper Manufacturers that they would no longer handle our wires, and the Manufacturers in turn notified us that they could not buy from us owing to the stand of the Papermakers, this resulted in our losing business which we had been doing for nearly twenty eight years.
This same group tried to have this boycott adopted at the National meeting of the International Brotherhood of Papermakers, but were unsuccessful, Had they succeeded we would have lost our entire business,however unless Secondary Boycotts are outlawed they will continue to try until they do succeed.
ED/D.
Yours very truly, Holyoke Wire Cloth Co.
Edward Mockuty 2008.
Sworn subscubed before thes adhday fidbruary aitlyn halder Notary Public Treas.

To Whom It May Concern;- As a supplement to my affidavit of February 18th, I wish to state, that our employees realizing the danger to themselves of a Boycott, proceeded to organize 100%, applied for and received a Charter from the A.F.ofl. as a Federal union.
This Charter was almost immediately suspended because the American Wire Weavers Protective Asscn. claimed jurisdiction over our weavers.
In concerns of a similar nature to ours the Wire Weavers belong to this body, and the other employees either belong to some other union or are not organized, in fact some shops have more non-union than union men, but they put an American Wire Weavers Protective Asscn, label on their wires which states that the wire is 100% union made, this statement in itself is false.
Prior to the suspension of the Charter our employees were 100% organized, but we have been informed by unimpeachable authority, that it made no difference how well organized our employees were, unless they joined the American Wire Weavers Protective Asscn, the papermakers would not recognize them as organized.
In other words it is a deliberate attempt by an outside party to force our employees to join an organization which they want nothing to do with, they feel that it is their right as American Citizens to make their own choice, as indeed it is according to law,and they also feel that this Boycott is a deliberate attempt to deny them their rights as free americans.
I might add to this that 40% of these employees are veterans of World War 11, working for their full pay exactly like our other employees, and not using the G.I. bill to supplement their pay.
ED/D.
Yours very truly, Holyoke Wire Cloth Co.
Edward Klocherty NEUMEYER DIMOND, INC.
We have made a courtesy call" on the Wisconsin. Tissue Mills every year, and when our Mr R.C.Warwick called in May 1940, Miss B. Peterson told him that they still had the Holyoke Wire in stock, and that they were not able to use it, and certainly not place further orders with you until such time as the Holyoke Wire Cloth Company became a Union mill.
We have found this " Secondary boycotting" very apparent in the State of Wisconsin, which has resulted in our being unable to sell any quantity or wires in this area, which as you know contains a very large number of Paper Mills, all of whom are very good friends of ours.
Such orders as we have been able to obtain have been or a complimentary nature, and in cases of emergency when the mills have run short, and represent nothing like the share of the business which we should obtain in this State.
Nevertheless, the Management or these mills are reluctant to place orders with a non-Union wire miil for fear of creating or giving any possible cause for labour troubles Amongst their Paper Mill personnel.
The fact that your personnel earn more money, and have Group Insurance, Hospital Insurance free of cost, plus a bonus every year, which beneiits they would not all receive in a Union mill, makes no difference, nor does the fact that your personnel elect to remain non-Union.

Mr Edward Docherty, The Holyoke Wire Cloth Company., Holyoke, Mass: February 19th 1947 We know that your relations with your personnel have been excellent since the Company was established many years ago, and you have not had any strikes or similar troubles in your business life.
With such an excellent record, and because your personnal prefer as workers in a free country not to join a Union, it does seem un-just and un-American, that the Company should be penalised by loss or business due not to the Management, but Union labour forcing Management to discriminate against Management because you allow your workers complete freedom of action, and co not force them to join a Union if they do not wish it.
With kindest personal regards, Yours very truly, NEUMEYER DIMOND INC., Mcrenger President.

Gentlemen, Attention of Miss B.Peterson.
We have just received your letter or April 28th asking about our Union status, we are non-union anu have been so for nine years, although we never think about it, as the question just never arises.
This is the first time to our knowledge that there has been any question of putting our wires on a machine, and we are shipping wires all over the United States.
As to not snapping the wire which was scheduled for May shipment, we wish to auvise that this wire went 1orward the first thing yesterday morning with several Ower wires which were marked for May shipment without a speciiic dave on them.
You say that the wire is of no use to you under the circumstances, and it is certainly of no use to us.
We made them up on the order in good faith with never a thought about Unionism because we have had no occasion to consider this angle, we have no other customer using a wire or a size near enough to yours to make them re-saleable.
It seems as though if you explain the circumstances to your men they would put these two wires to use, as you can tell them that you will purchase no more, as a matter of fact we will not solicit you, knowing this condition exists.
We are not ashamed of our non-union status, our men are well paid, in many instances making more than Union weavers in this vicinity, we carry group insurance for them also hospital insurance at no cost to them, and we give them each a one hunureu dolar bond at Xmas.
We know that you are not interested in this phase of the maɩter or we could enumerate other advantages which these men enjoy.

ALL PRICES AND QUOTATIONS ARE SUBJECT TO CHANGE OR REVISION WITHOUT NOTICE We have been asked to describe our experience in connection with the use by our Company of Fourdrinier Wires from the Holyoke Wire Cloth Company.
The situation is quite simple from the fact that we are prevented from using any wires or products made by this concern because such products do not carry union labels. Our employees refuse to open the box containing a wire by that company and if it comes to the matter of actually putting any such wire on the Fourdriniers of our paper machines, our employees would walk out rather than comply.
Clearly, this is a case involving an outright boycott whereby we are restrained from conducting business of any kind with the Holyoke Wire Cloth Company, whose product is satisfactory for our use and who are entitled to our patronage insofar as we may see fit to use their merchandise.
We consider it to be an unfortunate if not illegal condition which is worse from the fact that it must certainly jeopardize the ability of that company to properly operate in the city of Holyoke and it is obviously to the advantage of the community to maintain as much industry or manufacturing as possible.
We have long felt that this company was entitled to our consideration and have been dissatisfied that any compulsion should be exerted upon us as described above.

TO WHOM IT MAY CONCERN: February 18, 1947 We are unable to use Fourdrinier Wires on our Paper Machines, from the Holyoke Wire Cloth Company, because these wires do not carry Union labels.
In April 1946 we were notified by the local Fapermakers' Union that their members could not put on wires made by the Holyoke Wire Cloth Company.
This is an example of the secondary boycott wherein we are restrained from purchasing Fourdrinier wires from a particular source whose product might be very well qualified for our use.
A logical further step would be for the Union to designate the customers to whom our manufactured products might be sold.

mpshire Lond Your Great Grandfather used Carew Quality Papers in the days of the Quill BOYCOTTS AND UNION LABELS At a time when employees were attempting and needed encouragement to unionize and bargain collectively, the union label was developed for the purpose of indicating to employees and friends of labor that a certain product was union-made.
The chief purnose was to indicate that the labelled product had been made under fair working conditions as opposed to unfair or "sweat shop" conditions. It was directed to the purchaser of the particular product and was intended to persuade him to buy union made products.
Under such conditions, and used for such a purpose, many states adopted legislation bringing the union label under the protection of trade-mark laws and other laws preventing unfair competition through misbranding or mislabeling of products.
It was and is illegal for a manufacturer falsely to advertise the source of his product, its components size, weight, etc. It was properly considered that falsely using a union label was just as illegal. But, the fact that manufacturers or wholesalers or retailers were (and are) protected in the use of trade-marks or trade names against unauthorized use of such trade-marks or trade names never was thought to justify a misuse by the manufacturer, wholesaler and retailer of such a trade-mark to restrain trade.
Is there any doubt of the status cf an agreement between a manufacturer and his wholesalers and retailers agreeing not to handle any electrigal materials or products, or paper, or paint, except that which bears a certain label?
That very thing has been held an illegal restraint of trade.
Is it any the less so when the restraint is founded on a union label?
Today the union label is not used to indicate a material fact to a prospective purchaser.
It is used, generally by the American Federation of Labor unions, as the means of enforcing a boycott of non-union or other union-made products.
The International Brotherhood of Electrical Workers refuses to allow its installation workers to handle materials not manufactured by other IBEW members. The Carpenters union refuses to work on lumber not cut by AFL workers. The Teamsters, particularly, police their own and all other AFL so-called "disputes" to obstruct deliveries into or out of establishments declared to be "unfair", whether or not the NLRB has found an "unfair" labor practice to exist. Usually, it has not done so.
The label is no longer used as a trade mark to evidence to the prospective purchaser the fact that the product is made under fair labor conditions. It is used as an economic club, to prevent a willing purchaser from exercising his choice.
Its design and purpose to create a monopoly is just as plain as in the case of a manufacturer above mentioned.
It is a monopoly exercised by labor.
Its use by a union for such a purpose is a violation of the constitutional rights of employees, not members of that particular union, to work. It is a violation of the NLRA guarantees to workers of a free choice of their bargaining agent.

 It is, however, not an illegal restraint of trade under The the Sherman Act because of the interpretation of the Norrislaguardia Act, in the Allen-Bradley and Hutcheson cases, Supreme Court placed the responsibility for correction on the Congress.

and their union UNITED STEEL WORKERS OF AMERICA, CIO, LOCAL 2394 UNIONS: UNITED ASSOCIATION OF PLUMBERS AND STEAMFITTERS and BUILDING TRADES UNIONS, AFL STATEMENT BY W. B. HOLTON, JR., PRESIDENT, THE WALWORTH COMPANY H A Summary of Statement by President W. B. Holton, Jr., of Walworth Company: We are being forced out of the fabricating business through the medium of the AF of L boycotting which started in 1942.
We have lost better than 50% of our contracting business in New England.
We have been shut out entirely by the major constructing engineers, turnkey operators, and large consumer--not through any wish of theirs--but entirely due to the club which the AF of L holds over their head in their control of the erection field.
Some companies have discontinued sending us their inquiries.
The only thing that has not been affected is Export, but this is only 10% of our normal business anyway.
As it stands today, a preponderate majority of all our regular fabrication has been taken away from us through the AF of L boycott. And the situation will get worse if something is not done about 15.
More than 80% of valve and fitting producers have affiliations with the CIC or unions other than the Af of L.

PREFACE 1. DEFINITION OF A "PIPE FABRICATOR" One who processes pipe, welding fittings, and forged steel flanges into what is termed "fabricated assemblies".
These assemblies may consist of pipe bends, welded headers, or welded assemblies, with fittings and flanges made up in one complete unit.
In no case does a pipe fabricator actually manufacture a basic product.
He starts with pipe or tubing which is a finished product in itself and the same is true of welding fittings and forged steel flanges.
2. BASIC FINISHED PRODUCTS WHICH GO INTO FABRICATION AND THEIR LABOR AFFILIATIONS (A) PIPE TUBING: To the best of our knowledge, 100% of all the pipe and tubing manufactured in this country is produced in plants having affiliation with the CIO.
(B) WELDING FITTINGS: There are five principal producers of this commodity.
Two manufacturers are affiliated with the CIO.
One manufacturer is affiliated with the Afofl.
Two manufacturers have no affiliation with either the CIO or Afofl.

fittings produced originate in plants which have no affiliation with the Afofl.
(c) FORGED STEEL FLANGES: There are a number of manufacturers producing this line and we are not acquainted in detail as to their labor affiliations.
But we do know the principal producers which represent 75% of the production and to the best of our knowledge none of these have any affiliation with Afofl.
ERECTING PIPE FABRICATORS: There are a certain number of "pipe fabricators" who not only process pipe, fittings, and flanges but also take contracts for the erection of complete piping systems, viz:- Fabricated Assemblies Fittings Valves Fittings and Valves are a very important factor in any piping system and here again we have more than 80% of the Valve and Fitting producers having affiliation with the CIO or unions other than Afofl.
CONCLUSIONS: (a) Any pipe fabrication shop in the country, independent of its labor affiliations is handling finished products, 95% of which is produced by plants which have no affiliation with the Afofl.
(b) The same is true of "erecting fabricators" particularly when you take into consideration the valve manufacturers.

" " THE WALWORTH SITUATION The Walworth Company (Boston) has been fabricating pipe for over 50 years.
We have built up a very creditable reputation, particularly in:- (a) Contracting field in New England which accounted for approximately 20 per cent of pre-war business; (b) Among some of the larger designing engineers, oil turnkey contractors, and larger consumers, yielding 70 per cent of pre-war business; (c) Export, representing 10 per cent of pre-war volume.
The agitation relative to Afofl boycotting of prefabricated material produced by shops not having Afofl unions really got under way in 1942.
We felt no effect of this during the War years of 42-43-44 as we had turned over practically our entire production to the War effort, producing fabricated assemblies for ships being constructed by Bethlehem Shipbuilding.
This ship work was finished early in 1945 and then we took steps to reestablish ourselves on a peace-time We immediately ran into difficulties.
basis.
We found our contracting customers in New England friendly enoughtbut rather reluctant to give us any business of consequence.

This matter came to a head in Boston in October, 1945, when we furnished a number of fabricated assemblies for the Boston arena through the Walworth-English-Flett Company of Boston.
These assemblies were delivered to the job-site and we were then notified that our fabricated assemblies were not acceptable to the Union Steamfitters and that they could not be installed. The reason given was that the material was not produced by an Afofl shop.
Mr. Walworth, of the Walworth-English-Flett Company, following several days of negotiations with the Union organizer (Mr. Archie Gillis of the Afofl) finally managed to have the piping accepted. But only after the contractor agreed to torch-cut each assembly on the ground and then have it rewelded by an Afofl welder on the job-site.
The matter was checked personally with Mr. Gill18, of the Afofl, who stated that he had been instructed by his headquarters not to erect any pre-fabricated piping made up by anyone other than Afofl union members. Mr. Gillis further stated that these orders were issued by his general organizer, Mr. John J. Mccarthy of the United Association of Plumbers and Steamfitters, Machinists Building, 9th and Mt. We worked with the successful contractor initially, gave him considerable help on design and estimating, and thought we were in line for the order.
However, the final outcome of this was that the Beacon Piping Company of Boston, who had the contract, finally informed us that they could not give us the business--even though our price and delivery was satisfactory--for the simple reason that we did not employ Afofl welders and benders and that our material would not be acceptable because the Afofl union refused to erect it.
Our Boston plant, which handles all our pipe fabricating, was organized by Local 2394, United Steel Workers of America (CIO) in 1938 and was certified by the National Labor Relations Board as the bargaining agent for our employees in this plant. We have never been approached by the Afofl with a request to recognize it as the bargaining agent at this plant.
With our plant located in Boston, one of our most important outlets has been the contracting trade in the New England area.
Normally we enjoyed a preference due to location, delivery, and quality of material produced.
However, since the Fall of 1945, we have lost better than fifty per cent of all this business because of the activity of the Afofl boycott.
What is more alarming than this, however, is the si tuation which has faced us over the last 12 months with the larger consumer and engineering contractor accounts.

Badger, Standard Oil, Lummus, etc.
Through the medium of these accounts we have been able in the past to build up very substantial backlogs on fabrication through large fabricated contracts. These orders were the backbone of our business and permitted us to operate our shop on a profitable basis.
We have not had an order now for 12 months from any one of the four contractors mentioned above.
For awhile we were permitted to quote on Stone Webster's requirements but never got an order and, in following through, were advised that we just didn't "fit into the picture." We could get no definite statement out of any of the Stone Webster personnel other than the fact that they could not jeopardize their over all contracts by handling CIO fabrication. Stone Webster have now discontinued sending us their inquiries.
As for Badger, they too will make no open statement Off the record, we were told but we can't get any business.
it is too risky for them to take a chance on labor troubles.
Frankly, I do not know what the situation is with Standard 011 at the moment as we haven't had any inquiries from them for quite some months.
Last Fall we had an attractive specification of fabricated material for a papermill in New Jersey. The engineers were Alvin H. Johnson, Inc., N. Y. C.
Our bid was approximately $15,000 and we were advised that we were in line for the order. However, just T before the order was placed, we were asked what our labor affiliations were and when the owners found out that we had a CIO shop we were given no further consideration.
On the Macon Kraft contract, which we arranged through Rowland Tompkins last August, the estimate of fabricated pipe, vales and fittings involved was around $500,000-- half of which was supposed to be fabrication. Due to a final decision on the part of the engineers to make all the process piping welded, this dropped the actual fabrication from $250,000 down to less than $50,000.
We did a lot of checking on this proposition to find out where we would stand on CIO fabrication and finally concluded that it would be to the best interests of all concerned if we sublet it to an Afofl shop. Midwest, at Passaic, Jersey, are handling this work for us.
New The only reason we did this was to avoid any possible complication for the owners in the matter of possible labor trouble. The whole job involves an expenditure of over 10 million dollars and it wasn't logical to jeopardize the complete construction for possibility of trouble on $50,000 worth of fabrication.
Over the past nine months we have noticed an increasing tendency on the part of consumers and engineers to request information as to labor affiliations when sending out fabricated inquiries. When the answer to this question 18 CIO then we are usually advised that we are wasting your time quoting.

SEATTLE CENTRAL LABOR COUNCIL AND CLAUDE O'REILLY, its President, and C. W. DOYLE, its Secretary; SEATTLE BUILDING TRADES) COUNCIL and HARRY CARR, its President, and FRED SMITH, its Secretary; SEATTLE METAL TRADE COUNCIL and W.C. LINDELL, its President, and JOSEPH JARVIS, its Vice President; and JOHN DOE NUMBERS ONE TO ) THREE AND JANE DOE NUMBERS ONE TO' THREE, Respondents.
This is a jurisdictional dispute. At no time during the course of the controversy did the relationship of employer and employee exist between the plant and the pickets, For a number of years, H. F. Swenson, doing business as Sweden Freezer Manufacturing Company, operated a small plant in Seattle for the manufacture of ice cream freezers for commercial use. He had never employed union labor.
However, for several years, every six months or so, he had been contacted concerning the unionizing of the plant by representatives of various craft unions connected with the American Federation of Labor. January 11, 1946, the Building and Construction Trades Council of San Francisco wrote a letter to the Seattle Building and Construction Trades Council, stating that the Sweden Manufacturing Company had been placed on its unfair list, and asking that an investigation be made. January 23rd, representatives of three craft unions called on Mr. Swenson and showed him the letter. A discussion followed lasting a couple of hours, at which time it was agreed that the men would come back later.
When the representatives had left, Mr. Swenson found a Mr. Stewart waiting for him. He represented the International Association of Machinists. (This is a national -1organization consisting of six hundred thousand members. It has two thousand Locals, has been in existence since 1888, and has a membership of between twenty-three and twenty-five thousand in the Seattle area. It was disassociated by the American Federation of Labor in January, 1945.) Mr. Stewart informed him that his (Swenson's) employees had asked the Machinists to represent them, and he had called a meeting at its hall. He presented Swenson with a notice of the meeting and asked him to put one in the envelope of each employee. Swenson refused to do this, taking the stand he should not take part in any controversy between any of the various unions.
Be that as it may, on February 1st, the International Association of Machinists filed a petition with the National Labor Relations Board for certification as the exclusive bargaining agency of all the employees of the Sweden Manufacturing Company. On the same day, the National Labor Relations Board informed the employer of the petition.
Meanwhile, February 5th, Mr. Mccaffrey of the Steamfitters Local Union No. 473, Mr. Day of Local B-46, International Brotherhood of Electrical Workers, and Mr. Jarvis of the Sheet Metal Workers Union, Local 99 (all affiliated with the American Federation of Labor), called on Mr. Swenson. He told them he was busy. They returned in a couple of days or so. At that time, they stated that they wished to go into the plant to talk to the men, but Swenson would not permit them to do so. He explained that the National Labor Relations Board had instructed him not to take part in anything which might influence the men.
February 5th, Mr. Swenson, in response to the notification received from the National Labor Relations Board, called on Mr. Mcclaskey, its field examiner in Seattle, and gave him the required information concerning the number of employees, Kinds of work performed, and so forth.
The following day, Mcclaskey contacted the representatives of the Electrical Workers, the Steamfitters, and Sheet Metal Workers, and met with them February 7th. He informed them of the filing of the petition by the International Association of Machinists, advised them concerning the National Labor Relations Board's rules as to intervention, which provide that any labor organization desiring to intervene must substantiate its claim with membership evidence amounting to at least ten per cent of the number of employees in the bargaining unit which it claimed was the appropriate one for the purposes of collective bargaining. (In this respect, if the protesting unions had been able to show that they had one member who was an employee of the plant, they would have been allowed to intervene.) The rules of the Board also provide that a showing must be made within five days of the receipt of the notification. The unions were given seven days. No showing was made.
February 18th, an agreement for a cross-check was entered into between the Sweden Manufacturing Company and -2the International Association of Machinists. It provided for a cross-check by the regional director of the National Labor Relations Board of all appropriate union and employer records, including union membership cards and applications, authorization cards to be furnished by the union, and a payroll list to be supplied by the employer. It was agreed that, should a majority of the employees designate the union, copies of the agreement and of the attached notice would be posted in conspicuous places for a period of five days.
agreement then provided: The "At the conclusion of the 5-day posting period, if the Regional Director is of the opinion that no valid cause to the contrary has been shown, he will issue a Report on Cross-Check finding and determining that the Union has been designated and selected as the exclusive bargaining representative of all employees in the Unit." The attached notice provided: "The Agreement for Cross-Check appearing below has been executed in settlement of a question concerning representation arising under the National Labor Relations Act.
The cross-check has been made pursuant to such agreement and it appears there from that the Union has been designated by a majority of the employees within the Unit.
"PLEASE TAKE NOTICE that unless cause to the contrary is shown to the undersigned Regional Director on or before five (5) days from the date hereof, he will issue a Report on Cross-Check finding that the Union has been designated and selected as the exclusive representative of the employees in the Unit, which will constitute complete and final disposition of the question concerning representation.
The report on the cross-check was issued February 28th. The following finding was made: "Number of employees in the Agreed Unit "Number designating the Union named below The report continued: 29 1 "The undersigned, therefore, pursuant to Section 4 of the Agreement for Cross-Check, hereby finds and determines that INTERNATIONAL Association of Machinists is the exclusive representative of all the employees in the Unit defined in Section 1 of the Agreement for Cross-Check for the purposes of collective bargaining in respect to rates of pay, wages, hours of employment, and other conditions of employment." Meanwhile, February 11,1946, the Electrical Workers Union, Local No. B-46 (one of the American Federation of Labor unions involved in this dispute), filed with the Secretary of Labor, the National War Labor Board, and the National Labor Relations Board, a notice of intention to strike under the War Labor Disputes Act. The notice stated the intention of the union members to cease their employment 3 with the Sweden Manufacturing Company due to a labor dispute.
It further stated: "A concise statement of the issue is: The Union's desire and said firm refuses to recognize the Union as the sole bargaining representative for employees engaged in electrical construction, maintenance and repair in connection with building construction repair and maintenance, and with respect to construction, installation, repair and maintenance of electrical equipment,, machines, motors, generators, switchboards, sub-stations and apparatus, etc.
"The Union is authorized by a large majority of said employees as their sole collective bargaining agency.
It should be remembered that at this time no member of the Electrical Workers Union was employed at the plant.
February 14th, the Seattle Building Trades Council informed Mr. Swenson by letter that charges had been preferred against his company, that a hearing would be held February 19th, and inviting him to be present and show cause why action should not be taken against him. Similar letters were written on February 18th by the Seattle Metal Trades Council and, March 1st, by the Central Labor Council. To one or two of the letters Mr. Swenson replied and asked for further time, which was granted. He later requested additional information as to the charges. He never advised them concerning the coss-check which was then in progress. He attended only one of the hearings.
March 25th, at a meeting of the Seattle Metal Trade Council's executive board, it was moved, seconded, and carried to put a picket line around the Sweden Manufacturing Company. The next day, March 26th, pickets appeared at the plant. There was no mass picketing. They were limited to two during the day and one at night. There was no violence.
The workers were not molested and were permitted to go back and forth through the picket line. However, the effects of the picketing were felt immediately. On the first day, a Railway Express Agency's truck drove up to the loading platform and made a delivery. On its way out, the pickets stopped the truck and talked to the driver. It never returned.
Later that day, another truck came to the plant, was stopped by the pickets, who talked to the driver, and the truck drove away without making a delivery. The company was unable to get deliveries in or out of the plant. It was without some materials and very low in others. It was just about out of fuel. Members of the Teamsters' union refused to go through the line. The company owned a pickup truck, but could not operate it for the reason that the driver, who was a member of the Teamsters' union, could not cross the line.
Mr. Swenson testified that, -4IF 15, had not the restraining order been issued, he would have had to close immediately. Later, April 23rd, a temporary injunction was issued, and the case was set for trial on its merits. At the conclusion of the trial on its merits, the restraining order and temporary injunction were dissolved, and this appeal followed. Subsequent to the appeal, the appellant applied to this court to have continued the temporary restraining order pending appeal. August 12th, this court sustained the trial court in refusing to fix a supersede as bond pending appeal. Swenson v. Seattle Central Labor Council, 125 Wash.
The trial court found that a labor dispute existed.
Stat. (Sup.), 7612-13), defines a labor dispute, and provides: "(a) A case shall be held to involve or to grow out of a labor dispute when the case involves persons who are engaged in the same industry, trade, craft, or occupation; or have direct or indirect interests therein; or who are employees of the same employer; or who are members of the same or an affiliated organization of employers or employees; whether such dispute is (1) between one (1) between one or more employers or associations of employers and one or more employees or associations of employees; (2) between one or more employers or associations of employers and one or more employers ciations of employers; or (3) between one or more employees or associations of employees and one or more employees or associations of employees; or when the case involves any conflicting or competing interests in a labor dispute' (as defined in this section) of 'persons participating or interested' therein (as defined in this section).
or asso- "(b) A person or association shall be held to be a person participating or interested in a labor dispute if relief is sought against him or it, and if he or it is engaged in the same industry, trade, craft, or occupation in which such dispute occurs, or has a direct or indirect interest therein, or is a member, officer, or a gent of any association composed in whole or in part of employers or employees engaged in such industry, trade, craft, or occupation.
·M (c) The term 'labor dispute' includes any controversy concerning terms or conditions of employment, or concerning the association or representation of persons in negotiating, fixing, maintaining, changing, or seeking to arrange terms or conditions of employment, regardless of whether or not the disputants stand in the proximate relation of employer and employee. " Mr. Mccaffery, business agent for the Steamfitters Local, in relating the conversation had at the plant, on the -5second visit, between Mr. Swenson and Mr. Day, Mr. Jarvis, and himself, testified as follows: "Q.

A. I would say that it was said, but as to who said it, I couldn't remember...

THE WITNESS: Well, if I can answer that, it was stated, but as to who stated it, I couldn't say. I can't remember that. Q. (By Mr. Ambler) It was made by one of the members of your committee? A. Yes, but who made it I don't know.
There were several men there. I couldn't remember that far back as to who said it." At the time the picketing started on March 26th, the International Association of Machinists was, by order of the National Labor Relations Board, the exclusive bargaining agency for all of the employees at the plant, even though no cont ract had been drawn up at that time. This brings us to a determination of the question as to whether or not a jurisdictional dispute between rival unions is a "labor dispute" as defined by 7612-13.
(2d) 270, held that, where the National Labor Relations Board has determined the exclusive bargaining agency, as provided in the Wagner Act, the re cannot thereafter be a labor dispute between the employer and the minority union. The only distinction which we have been able to find between that case and the one at bar, is that there, after one union had been certified by the National Labor Relations Board, the employer entered into a working agreement with it as the sole collective bargaining agency for all employees, while here, no agreement had been entered into. That seems to us to be a distinction without a difference. Here, the International Association of Machinists had been certified as an exclusive representative of all the employees "for the - -6AGVA YOU Jadi..

purposes of collective bargaining in respect to rates of pay, wages, hours of employment, and other conditions of employment." It would have been unlawful for the employer to have negotiated with the American Federation of Labor Unions while that order was in effect. As between the employer and the picketing unions, there was no labor dispute.
There could not have been a dispute "between one or more employees or associations of employees and one or more employees or associations of employees, " as provided in the act, because here, no members of the picketing unions were employees of the Sweden Manufacturing Company. A jurisdictional dispute between rival unions can become a "labor dispute provided the union members involved are employees of the same company, and provided further that no exclusive bargaining agency has been set up or provided for. Here, there was no labor dispute.
It is urged that State ex rel. Lumber and Sawmill Workers v. Superior Court for Pierce County, 24 Wn. (2a) 314, 164 P. (2d) 662, is decisive of this case, because there after the Congress for Industrial Organization unions had been constituted the sole bargaining agencies by the National Labor Relations Board, the American Federation of Labor picketed the plants.
But because Plaster employed nonunion labor in the construction of a building a mile and a half away, members of the unions picketed the respondent's cafe. Walking back and forth in front of the restaurant, a picket carried a placard which read: "'This Place Is Unfair to Carpenters and Joiners Union of America, Local No. 213, and Painters Local 130, Affiliated with American Federation of Labor. 'n Later, the wording was changed as follows; "The Owner of This Cafe Has Awarded a Contract to Erect a Building to W. A. Plaster Who is Unfair to the Carpenters Union 213 and Painter Union 130, Affiliated With the American Federation of Labor. "" The Texas court found that Ritter's Cafe was picketed " for the avowed purpose of forcing and compelling plaintiff Ritter) to require the said contractor, Plaster, to use and employ only members of the defendant unions on the building under construction in the 2800 block on Broadway.'" Contemporaneously with this picketing, the Restaurant Workers Union, Local No. 808, called Ritter's employees out on strike and withdrew the union card from his establishment. Union truck drivers refused to cross the picket line to deliver food and other supplies to the restaurant. The effect of all this was "to prevent members of all trades-unions from patronizing plaintiff's cafe and to erect a barrier around plaintiff's cafe, across which no member of defendant-unions or an affiliate will go.
The Texas court enjoined this procedure, but the decree forbade neither picketing elsewhere (including the building under construction by Plaster) nor communication of the facts of the dispute by any means other than the picketing of Ritter's restaurant.
On p. 724, the court stated; "The economic contest between employer and employee has never concerned merely the immediate disputants. The clash of such conflicting interests inevitably implicates the wellbeing of the community. Society has therefore been compelled to throw its weight into the contest. The law has undertaken to balance the effort of the employer to carry on his business free from the interference of others against the effort of labor to further its economic self-interest. And every intervention of government in this struggle has in some respect abridged the freedom of action of one or the other or both.
"The task of mediating between these competing interests has, until recently, been left largely to judicial lawmaking and not to legislation. 'Courts were required, in the absence of legislation, to determine what the public welfare demanded; -- whether it would not be best subserved by leaving the contestants free to resort to any means not involving a breach of the peace or injury to tangible property; whether it was consistent A " -8- ; -309 S...' Ibid., at 707. 'The boundary at which the conflicting interests balance cannot be determined by any general formula in advance, but points in the line, or helping to establish it, are fixed by decisions that this or that concrete case falls on the nearer or father side.' Hudson Water Co. v. Mccarter, 209 U.S. 349, • 255.
"In the circumstances of the case before us, Texas has declared that its general welfare would not be served if, in a controversy between a contractor and building workers' unions, the unions were permitted to bring to bear the full weight of familiar weapons of industrial combat against a restaurant business, which, as a business, has no nexus with the building dispute but which happens to be owned by a person who contracts with • -9My :: 07.
4.9% ing the builder. The precise question is, therefore, whether the Fourteenth Amendment prohibits Texas from drawing this line in confining the area of unrestricted industrial warfare..
"It is true that by peaceful picketing workingmen communicate their grievances. As a means of communicating the facts of a labor dispute, peaceful picketing may be a phase of the constitutional right of free utterance. But recognition of peaceful picketing as an exercise of free speech does not imply that the states must be without power to confine the sphere of communication to that directly related to the dispute. Restriction of picketing to the area of the industry within which a labor dispute arises leaves open to the disputants other traditional modes of communication. To deny to the states the power to draw this line is to write into the Constitution the notion that every instance of peaceful picketing--anywhere and under any circumstances--is necessarily a phase of the controversy which provoked the picketing. Such a view of the Due Process Clause would compel the states to allow the disputants in a particular industrial episode to conscript neutrals having no relation to either the dispute or the industry in which it arose.
"In forbidding such conscription of neutrals, in the circumstances of the case before us, Texas represents the prevailing, and probably the unanimous, policy of the states. We hold that the Constitution does not forbid Texas to draw the line which has been drawn here. To hold otherwise would be transmute vital constitutional liberties into doctrinaire dogma. We must be mindful that 'the rights of employers and employees to conduct their economic affairs and to complete with others for a share in the products of industry are subject to modification or qualification in the interests of the society in which they exist. This is but an instance of the power of the State to set the limits of permissible contests open to industrial combatants.' Thornhill v.
Alabama, 310 U.S. 88, 103-04." The United States Supreme Court has, by these cases, established this rule: Peaceful picketing is an exercise of the right of free speech. Organized labor has the right to communicate its view either by word of mouth or by the use of placards.
This is nothing more nor less than a method of persuasion. But when picketing ceases to be used for the purpose of persuasion-- Just the minute it steps over the line from persuasion to coercion--it loses the protection of the constitutional guaranty of free speech, and a person or persons injured by its acts may apply to a court of equity for relief.
The facts in this case convince us that the picketing complained a did not constitute an exercise of free speech as contemplated by our founding fathers. Neither was it a means of Communicating the facts of a labor dispute as interpreted by the United States Supreme Court. The National Labor Relations Board had, by order, designated the International Association of Machinists as the exclusive bargaining agency of all the employees at the plant.
The purpose of the picketing was to forde the employer to deal with the picketing unions in defiance of this order, under -101 042 the threat that, should he refuse to do so, his plant would be closed and his equipment prevented from being installed any place in the United States. This was coercion. Appellant was forced to close his plant. This resulted in irreparable loss to him, and his employees were thrown out of employment. He had no adequate remedy at law. The restraining order theretofore entered should have been continued.
The decree is reversed.
We concur: Millard, J.
Robinson, J.
Schwellenbach, J.
Concurring in the result: (opinion) Steinert, J.
Simpson, J.
Dissenting (opinion): Abel, J.
Jeffers, J.

Editorial The window-smashing at the Dinner Party on W. Magnolia this morning was an inexcusable piece of hoodlumism.
Burbank has had far too much of this sort of thing. Fifteen months ago it was on the Warner Bros. picket line. In recent weeks because of labor difficulties the Dinner Party cafe has been subjected to vandalism of the worst kind.
Patrons have been insulted, acid thrown on their cars.
Stink bombs have exploded within the restaurant. And now rocks have bashed in five plate glass windows.
This is hoodlumism at its worst. Responsible labor leaders should call a halt. If they had the slightest control of the rank and file of their organization, such criminal acts would never take place. In many instances leaders have openly egged their followers on to violence. Such leadership is not inspiring.
The window smashing at the Dinner Party should be thoroughly investigated by the police. It could hardly have been the act of one individual. The whole business smacks of teamwork of a most reprehensible character. And it is to be presumed that there are some people who will cry, "Laborbaiter," to all who condemn such malicious mischief and urge that the perpetrators be pried out from beneath their rocks and dealt with sternly.

THE DINNER PARTY 3001 WEST MAGNOLIA BLVD BURBANK, CALIFORNIA February 19, 1947.
Dear Mr. Le Sauvage: As a small business man operating a cafe on a partnership basis, I am presenting a case history of a union's activity in its endeavor to pressure me into forcing my employes into the Culinary Workers' Union against their will. Our experience is one of thousands that clearly indicate the necessity for changing the present labor laws so that we local business men are relieved of the continued attacks of power drunk, irresponsible labor leaders.
Prior to our opening in November, 1945, the local business agent of the Culinary Workers' Union approached me relative to staffing our cafe with all union help. We carefully selected our force upon their ability and personality. Upon opening, the business agent requested us to sign a closed shop contract for our thirty-five employes. I stated that I would sign if the employes wished to join, but not one expressed such a desire, nor have they to this day. The business agent upon being told of the workers decision stated, "---it is mandatory that you sign, the employes have no say in the matter." In June, 1946 we were handed an ultimatum by the business agent, who said "if you don't sign up you will have to suffer the consequences. In October a picket line was established, and two weeks later a stench bomb was tossed into our cafe. About four weeks later another stench bomb was thrown in, and about two weeks after this last bomb, acid was thrown on the cars of two of our patrons. Next the seat cushions in my partner's car were slashed as were the tires on the cars of several of our employes. At one time roofing nails were found strewn along the curb in all directions adjacent to our establishment, This continued vandalism reached a climax in the early morning of January 14, 1947, when rocks were thrown through each of our five plate glass windows.
This damage may result in our insurance being cancelled.
Mind you, no dispute exists between the employes and the employer.
Not only have we been the victim of these repeated acts of vandalism, but for fifteen weeks it has been necessary for me to sneak away and pick up all of our supplies and provisions due to secondary boycott. Because of the boycott it has been necessary on several occasions to change our source of supply as the union discovered it.

I am confident that you will agree with me, that something must be done to prohibit a contimation of such action by disgruntled labor leaders who have been unable to sell their services to employes. If the closed shop could be outlawed, and workers given the privilege of selecting representation of their own choosing, I think it would tend to diminish acts of violence.
I further submit that unions should be restrained from using economic pressure on employers, and coercion, intimidation, and violence on employes where no dispute exists between the two. Finally, secondary boycott is a vicious weapon for which the employer has no defense and should be ruled unlawful.
Hoping that these facts can help you in your cause and mine, I remain Yours very truly, Armen fecureg The Duner Party 3001 W. union label. The Contractors' Association took up the matter with the Pacific Coast Office of the A.F. of L., who confirmed the ruling of Local B-569.
I understand from this contractor that a letter has been sent by the A.F. of L. to all the contractors advising them that we are unfair. This contractor also stated that he thought we would probably be picketed sometime in the near future.
Several months ago the Service Shop ordered a set of coils for rewinding the stator and rotor of a 100 hp slip-ring motor for one of the local newspapers.
Shortly before we were ready to start actual rewinding, the Union informed the newspaper that the Service Shop could not do the job because it was unfair. The newspaper asked what Shop the Union recommended and the Union recommended one of our competitors. Our customer preferred to await the time when our Service Shop could do the job.
Our Company recently sold some $700,000 worth of equipment to Fischback and Moore, electrical contractors, for installation at the U.S. Naval Station.
A complaint has developed in connection with some 150 submersible circuit breakers. On Thursday, January 23, 1946 I was down on the job looking the situation over and making arrangements to rectify the complaint and was just about ready to leave when the customer's electrical superintendent informed us, Mr. J.S. Spurck of the Los Angeles Office, the customer's man in charge of the job and myself, that all the men would be pulled off the job if any of our men did any work down there.
This statement resulted in a lot of discussion, during which we found out, however, that the customer's electrical superintendent is the secretary of Local B-569 and was therefore speaking with authority when he made the statement that the men would be pulled off the job.

EDMONDS, SECRETARY MANAGER, UTAH POULTRY PRODUCERS COOPERATIVE ASSOCIATION EXHIBIT FROM BOOKLET PUBLISHED BY UTAH CITIZENS FOR SOUND LEGISLATION, 433 ATLAS BUILDING, SALT LAKE CITY, UTAH AFFIDAVIT OF CLYDE C. EDMONDS STATE OF UTAH COUNTY OF SALT LAKE SS CLYDE C. EDMONDS, being first duly sworn, deposes and says: That he is and was at all times herein referred to, the secretary and manager of the Utah Poultry Producers Cooperative Association, of Salt Lake City, Utah; that he has read the article entitled "Utah Foultry farmers 'Take-It-On-Chin' Via Secondary Boycott" in a booklet entitled "The Squeezer and The Squeezed"; that the facts related there are substantially correct except in these particulars: The period referred to in the second paragraph of the articl (when there was a contract with the American Federation of Labor Union) was 1938 rather than 1939; While an election was ordered by the National Labor lelation Board, none was held because the American Federation of Labor Union conceded the election to the Independent Union.
Further deponent sayeth not.
Clyde C. Edmonds gth Subscribed and sworn to before me this day of February, 1947.
My Commission Expires: July 5, 1947 Frant Macherlans Notary Public residing in Salt Lake City, Utah Utah Poultry Farmers "Take-It-Onthe SQUEEZER and the SQUEEZED The-Chin" Via Secondary Boycott In Utah we have a group of 5000 farmers who cooperatively sell eggs, poultry and turkey's through the Utah Poultry Producers Co-op Association.
Early in 1939, the American Federation of Labor reported to the Utah Poultry Producers Association that it represented a majority of a certain class of the employees of the Association's plant. Acting in good faith, the management agreed to recognize the union as the bargaining agent for its employees. It further agreed to deduct union dues from the employees' wages in accordance with Utah law and with the employees' request.
Employees Dissatisfied This management-union agreement gave rise to consid erable dissatisfaction among the employees. This situation continued for nine months with the employees' dissatisfac tion continually growing. The employees finally took things into their own hands and formed an independent union.
The employees then cancelled the authorization under which the Utah Poultry Association management was deducting AFL dues. The American Federation of Labor ordered the management to continue the deductions, but in accordance with instructions from the employees, deductions were stopped. Then AFL leaders instituted a Secondary Boycott.
Here's How It Worked This boycott was conducted as follows: Employees of chain stores in the Salt Lake City area, where Utah Poultry Producers Co-op Association eggs, poultry and turkeys were marketed, had been previously organized by AFL. The stores were told that if they pur chased the Utah Poultry Association's products, their AFL employees would refuse to handle them.
Unwilling to "get caught in the middle" of this dispute and to avoid strikes and picketing at their places of busi ness, the stores heeded the AFL mandate and the Utah Poultry Producers Co-op Association lost its most profitable outlet for "Milk White Eggs" and other products, It Costs Money To Ship By selling their products in this area, close to home, the Utah Poultry Association saved freight and handling costs.
Therefore the farmers who produced the eggs and poul try received more money for their products. In addition, over a period of years through advertising and careful grading the Utah Poultry branded products had earned ready public acceptance in this market.
But the Secondary Boycott was effective and the Poultry Producers were forced to find a new market for their products outside the state and pay additional handling and shipping costs which reduced the amount of money the farmers received for their eggs, chickens and turkeys.
Government Conducts Poll *fter the Secondary Boycott was instituted the National Lacor Relations Board conducted an election to decide who the employees at the plant desired to represent -15.
them: the AFL or the independent union. Under this gov ernment conducted election the independent union received an overwhelming majority, Notwithstanding this fact, the AFL leaders continued their Secondary Boycott.
Continues Year After Year And it continued through 1940, 1941, and until September 1942, when it was finally lifted. While the Secondary Boycott was 100% effective, it failed to achieve the aim of AFL leaders — forcing the Utah Poultry em: ployees into joining their union.
During the years that the Secondary Boycott was im: posed the farmers who produced the eggs, chickens and turkeys lost many thousands of dollars for they received less for their products.
This is truly an example of a Secondary Boycott being of absolutely no benefit to anyone, yet at the same time financially injuring many.
 Under present Utah statutes, this same situation COULD be repeated!
The Secondary Boycotts mentioned are typical examples. Our files are filled with many, many, many, many more.
- - Utah Citizens for Sound Legislation - 16.

Upon information and belief: Local B-569 has occasionally solicited individual employees to join that union, but has thus far met with no success.
