The San Diego Shop has, nevertheless, been classified non-union, or "unfair", with the result that members of the local employed by contractors and customers are apparently refusing to install apparatus repaired or othery worked on by our Service Shop.
wise 11. This boycott on the part of the union is resulting in a serious loss of business to us. No exact figures or estimates are available at the present time to indicate the volume of business affected because the Shop was newly organized and had not fully realised its anticipated volume at the time the boycott was first imposed. It is known, however, that numerous customers are not sending business to our Shop which would otherwise be placed with us.
12. To date we have not been able to find any effective means of stopping or counteracting this boycott and it looks to us as though our hope of obtaining relief against this interference with our normal business lies in remedial legislation against activities of this kind by the new Congress.
Sworn to before me this day of February, 1947 Notary Public Rymaga Taylor rial) (Notorial) Seal 契 ​ The SQUEEZER and the SQUEEZED Secondary Boycotts Are Creeping Into Utah Although Utah people may not generally realize it, Secondary Boycotts have also crept into this state. A few labor leaders have "put the finger" on specific companies and groups in the effort to swell the membership of their particular organizations.
For example: - In October 1941, the Teamsters' Union wanted to unionize three wholesale grocery companies in Salt Lake City the Utah Wholesale Grocery Company, Symns Utah Grocer Company and John Scowcroft Sons Co.
"Either Take It, Or..." The Teamsters' representative told the wholesale grocers they would "either take the Closed Shop or they (the union) would give it to them the hard way!" The companies refused. And so...
The Teamsters' Union men jammed the wholesale grocers' driveways with trucks and parked a locked car in one of the entrances so customers could not get to the loading docks to obtain their merchandise.
The Teamsters' Union men jumped on the running boards of cars and tried to persuade the wholesale com panies' customers not to come into the wholesale grocers' places of business.
Retailer Is Threatened The Teamsters' Union men threatened a retailer that he'd better not accept merchandise from the wholesale grocers if he didn't want rocks thrown through his window.
Then the Teamsters' Union imposed a vicious Secondary Boycott.
Their men in cars followed delivery trucks and threatened to place picket lines in front of retail grocery stores if they accepted merchandise from the wholesale grocers.
Issue Orders To Men Union leaders ordered member employees of all interstate motor carriers not to handle any of the wholesale grocers' merchandise, either local or interstate.
They blocked the railroad spur tracks leading to the wholesale grocers' warehouses, thus preventing shipments from coming in.
One small retail merchant, the Sheffield Cash Grocery, needed merchandise to stay in business and accepted groceries which had been labeled "hot" by the union. Because Sheffield dared purchase from one of the wholesale grocers, the Union placed a picket line in front of Sheffield's two stores then located at 2204 South Seventh East and 1649 South Eleventh East.12 DATE: 1/3/45 SUBJECT: JURISDICTIONAL STRIKES MONOPOLISTIC PRACTICES UNION - SECONDARY BOYCOTT COMPANY: MOTOR WHEEL CORP., LANSING, MICHIGAN UNION: 182 UAW - AFL BLDG. CONST. TRADE COUNCIL, AFL UAW - AFL.
Co. has had excellent relations with its own uni on The Company used its own maintenance men to set up new machinery.
The Lansing Bldg. Const. Trades Council insisted that outside mechanics be brought in to install new machines.
The plant union workers sided with the company.
On January 3, 1945, company was notified by Council that it had "prohibited any of its members to do further building for your Corp. direct also refusing to furnish any building mechanics to any contractors of the AFL to do further work for your Corp." No building supply house was allowed to make delivery of anything to the Motor Wheel Corp. This included even office supplies, pencils, paper, small tools, pipe fittings, cement, in fact, anything and everything.

SOURCE: From testimony Clarence C. Carlton 3/4/46 before Senate Labor Committee Example No. 13 DATE: October 1945 SUBJECT: SECONDARY BOYCOTTS COMPANY: LAKESHORE ELECTRIC MANUFACTURING CORP.
UNION: THE INTERNATIONAL BROTHERHOOD OF ELECTRICAL WORKERS -- AFL CIO As the result of an election held in October 1945, the CIO union was certified as the bargaining agent for the Lakeshore employees.
The IBEW had contested the election and lost.
Almost all of Lakeshore's customers operate under contracts with the IBEW.
Following the election, nearly all of these customers withdrew their business, with the result that the electrical equipment business of Lakeshore dwindled to practically nothing.
The Company, faced with loss of business, refused to bargain collectively with CIO union on the grounds that the CIO organizer had represented to employees that membership in the union would not affect the business of the company or interfere with employees' jobs.
The Company was thereupon ordered to cease and desist from refusing to bargain collectively with the CIO Union and was affirmatively directed to bargain with the union and to post in its plant a notice to that effect.
Therefore, the manufacturer cannot save his business from effect of the secondary boycott without viola ting the NLRA.
He cannot encourage employees to join the IBEW without violating the Act, and if he bargains with the CIO, his business is destroyed by the IBEW boycott.
SOURCE: The Case of the Lakeshore Electric Manufacturing Co.

ARMY, GOVERNMENT TNT PLANT, WELD ON SPRING, MISSOURI UNION: SOUTHWESTERN TELEPHONE WORKERS UNION (Unaffiliated) I.B.E.W. NO. 1 (AFL) The AFL IBEW No. 1 is claiming the right to do work which an unaffiliated union, the Southwestern Telephone Workers Union, also claims as its rightful field.
Work involved in the dispute embraces the laying of cables and wires up to the point in the telephone system where the actual installation of telephone begins, according to James Morell, business agent of the electrical workers union, who said the system is 70 per cent completed.
Negotiations are now in progress between representatives of the Southwestern Bell Telephone Company, of Morrell's union and of the S.T.W.U., as well as of the Army, in an effort to effect a settlement. According to Morell, Local No. 1 has had an agreement with the Southwestern Bell Telephone Company for 25 years under which the AFL union has been granted the right to do the work it now claims at the TNT plant.
This agreement, it was learned, covers the St. Louis netropolitan area, but the question in dispute is whether the agreement also covers work under a government contract.
Members of the S.T.W.U. are not installing any telephones at the TNT plant, or doing any other type of work at the plant until the dispute is settled.

W., LOCAL NO.3 A jurisdictional dispute between two unions has deprived 678 Bronx families of telephone service and cost the New York Telephone Company thousands of dollars in lost revenue.
The dispute between the U.T. O. and local of the I.B.
E. W., concerns the right to install telephone wires in the Parkchester housing development in the East Bronx. Local No. 3 claimed exclusive jurisdiction until all 58 buildings were finished. The U.T.O. claimed the right to work in any individual building as soon as it was finished.
The U.T.O. refused to install the instruments if the wiring was done by Local No. 3, and Local No. 3 threatened a general aonstruction strike on the unfinished sections of the project if the telephone workers did the wiring.
The Metropolitan Life Insurance Company, owner of the project, bowed to Local No. 3's strike threat, and 678 families who ordered telephones were unable to get them.
The situation was created in March 1940, when the:.
first buildings were opened, and nothing has happened since then to improve it.
Now all the apartments are finished, and Parkchester hopes that within a month or two Local No. 3 will have finished its work on the project. When Local No. 3 goes the telephone workers will move in.
In the intervening twenty months two ineffective steps were taken to remedy the situation. The Public Service Commission held an open hearing in January and concluded that the matter should be taken to the courts. A tenant and the telephone company brought suit in Supreme Court, various motions were made and denied, and the insurance company appealed to the Appellate Division. Νο decision has been rendered.
Local No. 3 is an affiliate of the AFL and the UTO is an independent union. Local No. 3 was recently involved in a similar jurisdictional dispute with a union of employees of the Consolidated Edison System, which ended in a National Mediation Board ruling against Local No. 3 after a brief general strike of electrical workers on construction projects, throughout the city, including work at the New York Navy Yard, in Brooklyn.
SOURCE: NEW YORK HERALD TRIBUNE, NOVEMBER 8, 1941.

INTERSTATE FOOD SHIPMENT - COMPANY: MINNEAPOLIS FRUIT DISTRIBUTORS YAKIMA VALLEY (WASH.) APPLE PACKERS UNION: GENERAL DRIVERS' UNION LOCAL NO. 544 (AFL) A boycott was imposed in Minneapolis upon several carloads of apples from the Yakima Valley of Washington which were consigned to Minneapolis distributors. When the cars arrived, General Drivers' Union, Local 544, AFL, immediately instructed its members to refuse to unload the apples because the goods had been boycotted under orders from the international union. This action was taken despite the fact that the employers consummated an agreement with the union only a few months ago. Here again is an example of the employer being placed in the middle of a dispute to which he is not a party.
The boycott was imposed, it is reported, because several co-operative shipping and growers associations on the Pacific Coast have been declared "unfair. " The teamsters have resorted to their device of the "hot cargo" to bring pressure on the shippers and growers to force their employes to become union members.

FOLYAMA Example No. 22 DATE: JANUARY 1947 SUBJECT: MONOPOLISTIC PRACTICES IN CLOSED SHOP DEMANDS SECONDARY BOYCOTT COMPANY: PHILADELPHIA WHOLESALE PRODUCE COMPANIES UNION: AFL TEAMSTERS The teamsters union is bargaining representative for about 98% of the market employees. About 200 clerical workers have stubbornly resisted unionization.
In January, the white collar workers quit their jobs briefly in a show of solidarity against teamster pressure directed against individual employers to force adoption of closed-shop contracts.
In retaliation, the teamsters declared an embargo against movement of produce and fruit from freight terminals to the market. This resulted in a 90% stoppage of deliveries.

יד Example No. 23 SUBJECT: BOYCOTT - FAMILY GROCERY STORE COMPANY: HODROFF FAMILY, MINNEAPOLIS, MINNESOTA UNION: NOT IDENTIFIED Store owned and operated by the Hodroff family.
Aside from the mother, the father, and the son and daughter, only one other person was employed, a young man still a minor. The union demand was that everyone except Mr. Hodroff, Sr., join the union. The son is a veteran.
The family members, owners of the store, refused to join.
The store was kept open until 9 o'clock evenings for the benefit of community patrons. Other stores in vicinity closed at six. Threats were telephoned to family members at night indicating they would be sorry if they did not comply with union's demands.
After the second such threat, plate glass windows in the store fronts were smashed causing several hundred dollars worth of damage.
The store was picketed and suppliers were prevented from making deliveries. After about three weeks, the owners capitulated and signed up.
SOURCE: MINNESOTA RESTAURANT ASSOCIATION, INC.

"",,、!} Example No. 24 DATE: October 12, 1942 SUBJECT: SECONDARY BOYCOTT COMPANY: GANTNER MATTERN COMPANY UNION: ILGWU - formerly CIO now AFL - San Francisco, Calif.
One of the most flagrant misuses of "Hot Cargo" and the secondary boycott involves Gantner Mattern Co. of San Francisco, manufacturers of knit goods. Following a strike by members of the International Ladies' Garment Workers' Union, the Teamsters' Union determined that the complaint was 111founded, and passed through the picket lines. However, when the ILGWU, formerly aligned with CIO, reaffiliated with AFL, the Teamsters promptly labelled as "hot" Gantner Mattern's incoming supplies and outgoing products. The company has long engaged in manufacturing vital war goods for the Army and Navy.
Moreover, it was vindicated by the National Labor Relations Board of charges brought by the Garment Workers. Yet not until eight months after Pearl Harbor would the Teamsters serve this important manufacturing concern. By wilful flaunting of the NLRB through continued use of the secondary boycott, labor definitely impeded the war effort at this plant.
SOURCE: CALIFORNIA COMMITTEE AGAINST "HOT CARGO" 门 ​ Example No. 25 DATE: 7/10/46 SUBJECT: JURISDICTIONAL DISPUTE COMPANY: VARIOUS SHIPPING COMPANIES UNION: AFL MARITIME COUNCIL OF GREATER NEW YORK The AFL council strung 1,000 pickets along the Staten Island Piers and successfully prevented work on 9 vessels as a retaliatory gesture for the refusal of the CIO Longshoremen on the west coast to load vessels that were manned by members of the AFL Seafarers Union.

: Example No. 28 DATE: May 1, 1946 SUBJECT: BOYCOTT SECONDARY - COMPANY: PACIFIC PRESS, INC.
UNION: CENTRAL LABOR COUNCIL, AFL For a number of months there had been a jurisdictional There had been The company row between various labor unions in this plant.
several National Labor Relations Board elections.
was negotiating with the unions of the Printing Trades Council, AFL and the Lithographers, CIO. The unions demanded a closed shop contract. The company refused to compel one group of unionized workers to join another labor union or employees who did not belong to any union, to join as a price of their jobs. Whereupon, the Mailers' Union, demanded the discharge of three non-union employees. When the management refused, a strike was called. About one-third of the 474 employees quit work.
Secondary boycott practices have been extensive, involving refusal to move freight cars, boycotting of the Downtown Shopping News, Life and Time, innocent third parties, because printed in this plant, the picketing of a downtown store because it advertised in the Shopping News, the loss of much advertising to the paper because advertisers were threatened with boycotts and picket lines, and, in one case, with complete closing down of its business. There has been considerable violence and sabotage. Workers have been beaten up.
Stink bombs have been thrown into workers' homes, as well as into the plant. A pitched battle between police and pickets resulted when the unions attempted, by mass picketing, to block employees from going to their jobs. The courts issued an injunction against these unlawful practices. William Bassett, secretary of the Los Angeles Central Labor Council has been convicted of contempt of court and given an alternate jail sentence or fine.
SOURCE: Merchants Manufacturers Association, Los Angeles Press Release of July 30, 1946 J T Example No. 29 DATE: May 1, 1946 SUBJECT: BOYCOTT SECONDARY - COMPANY: LOS ANGELES DOWNTOWN SHOPPING NEWS UNION: CENTRAL LABOR COUNCIL, AFL This organization had no dispute of any character with its own employees, but because it is printed by Pacific Press where a labor dispute did exist, the DOWNTOWN SHOPPING NEWS was boycotted by the unions affiliated with the Central Labor Council, AFL. A number of its advertisers were called upon by union representatives and threatened with picket lines and boycotts if they continued to advertise in the SHOPPING NEWS. One of the downtown department stores was picketed because it advertised in this paper and deliveries of merchandise to and from the store were interrupted. A large chain store was threatened with a picket line and the walk out of its entire force of employees if it continued its advertising in the DOWNTOWN SHOPPING NEWS. When the company secured a court order prohibiting the labor unions from indulging in these unlawful practices, the picket line was immediately withdrawn from the department store. The boycott against the paper continues.

ROBINSON COMPANY UNION: CENTRAL LABOR COUNCIL, AFL The management of this company was waited upon by representatives of various unions concerned with the Pacific Press strike and secondary boycott. The store was urged to remove its advertising from the Downtown Shopping News because that publication is printed by Pacific Press. Upon the store's refusal, pickets were placed at customers' and delivery entrances.

Example No. 31 SUBJECT: VIOLATION OF CONTRACT - DATE: 6/3/44 JURISDICTIONAL STRIKE SECONDARY COMPANY: STROH BREWERY CO., DETROIT, MICHIGAN UNION: BREWERY WORKERS UNION BOYCOTT: Contract provided for compulsory arbitration of all disputes and absolutely prohibited all strikes.
In Spring 1944 Brewery Workers Union became involved in a controversy with the Teamsters Union over the delivery of soft drinks from certain plants. The dispute in no way involved the Stroh Brewery Co. However, Brewery workers refused to deliver Stroh beer to any Stroh customer who accepted soft drinks delivered by Teamsters Union.
After warnings and attempted negotiations with union, Stroh suspended certain drivers until they would agree to deliver to Stroh customers.
Whereupon the union called a strike and deliveries were completely stopped May 15 to June 3, 1944.
This strike was in violation of No Strike pledge and idled 431 employees.

SAUNBY, Acting President of the League for Avocado Workers Your editorial entitled "The Shortest Way to Unionization" was pretty good as far as it went, but to be complete it should have dealt with the jurisdictional disputes and life and death fights now going on between union and union.
These battles are a vital part of the picture as a whole, and you do not even have to go as far as Gardena to find them. They are going on right here in the city.
A local affiliate of the Teamsters' Union has demanded of our union that we give up our own organization (whose existence they ignore) and 'join their union which is so big and powerful it can do so much more for us; yet it is somehow so vulnerable the mere existence of our little independent union constitutes a very grave and serious menace to the life and welfare of their superior organi zation.
Calavo Growers have a suit in court and a temporary restraining order in force.
Yet May 14 as we served papers in our suit, which is filed but not yet heard, a man who claimed to be business agent of the Teamsters' local laughed in our faces and said legal service of papers and court restraining orders meant nothing to him, and one of the pickets indorsed the statement.
Meanwhile the packing plant is shut down, and valuable food fruit now ripen ing in the groves cannot be packed and marketed. Fruit now packed and under refrigeration is deteriorating. People die of starvation while the pickets tramp their beat.
This may look like "small potatoes" and not count for much. But it is part of a nation-wide pi ture. This stuff is surely and not so slowly putting the entire country into an unnecessarily bad industrial and economic condition, from which, at best, recovery will at least be long and painful if ever complete.
Our rotten, antiquated, lopsided labor laws need overhauling and reconditioning.
That is a slow job, but now is the time to start it. Turn the heat of public indig.
nation on our apparently fear-bound and paralyzed legislators in Washington and elsewhere. Then say it with votes Nov. 5 and do not be deceived by another h and avalanche of health and welfare proposals. Most of them are just propaganda to distract attention from the real issue which is that freedom in America is paralyzed in the grip of a tyrannical dictatorship in the name of labor.

One such concern is The Baker Taylor Co., one of the leading wholesalers of books in the country, and the other is the so-called Bock Publishers Shipping Service, which is a joint stock and shipping room operated by several of the leading book publishing companies, including Harper Bros., Dodd, Mead Co., and Rinehart Co.
In December of last year, the union called a strike against The Baker Taylor Co. Within a very short time, the employes of Book Publishers Shipping Service refused to ship books consigned to The Baker Taylor Co. Strong representations were made to the union, which ignored the matter for several days. It became academic when the strike of The Baker Taylor Co. employes was settled a few days later.

Example No. 51 SUBJECT: JURISDICTIONAL DISPUTE - JOB "RIGHTS" COMPANY: NEW JERSEY BELL TELEPHONE COMPANY, PLAINFIELD, NEW JERSEY UNION: INTERNATIONAL HODCARRIERS, BUILDING AND COMMON LABORERS UNION, LOCAL 221, AFL UNITED BROTHERHOOD OF CARPENTERS AND JOINERS OF AMERICA, LOCAL 155, AFL A jurisdictional dispute between carpenters and laborers has halted construction of the New Jersey Bell Telephone Company's $750,000 building at Fifth Street and Park Avenue.
Agents for the locals involved do not call the dispute a "strike." Martin Garɛfallo, business agent for the International Hodcarriers union, declared his men are working when they have work to do. Work stoppages attributed to the same cause are also said to have tied up construction operations in Dunellen and Clark Township.

Avlat EXAMPLES NOS. 53 SUBJECT: SECONDARY BOYCOTTS AGAINST LOS ANGELES RESTAURANTS COMPANIES: UNIONS: PATMAR CORPORATION ROBERTS BROTHERS RICHLOR'S LAWRY'S TOM O'SHANTER CULINARY WORKERS (AFL) TEAMSTERS (AFL) LETTERS SHOWING EFFECTS OF BOYCOTTS ENFORCED BY PICKETS WHO ARE NOT EMPLOYEES OF RESTAURANTS BEING PICKETED PATMAR CORPORATION 945 NORTH SEPULVEDA BOULEVARD EL SEGUNDO, CALIFORNIA TELEPHONE EL SEGUNDO 36 February 19, 1947 Mr. Geo. R. Le Sauvage Natnl. Restaurant Assn.
New York, N. Y.
Dear sir: We are engaged in the business of operating a Drivein Restaurant, Cocktail Lounge, Motel and Liquor Store; employing about sixty persons.
About two weeks ago a Culinary Union organizer came in to our kitchen and informed our Manager and our Chef that all persons working here must join the Union. He was told by our Chef and by our Manager that it was not their desire to belong to any union and that they both had been engaged in this business for many years and saw no reason to join. He was also informed by them that there were a number of persons working here for the very reason that they would not be compelled to join a union; in spite of the fact that the location of this establishment is rather remote and transportation and housing facilities are very poor.
This, the organizer told them, made no difference; they had decided to "take" this place and unless all employees joined the union, a picket line would be placed here and no deliveries would be made to us by any of our purveyors. It did not matter to them whether or not the employees wanted to join once they decided to "take" a place.
In fact the employees have nothing to say about it.
The purpose of this letter is to point out another case of a Union causing labor trouble where no trouble exists and none of the employees belong to the union. Our wages and working conditions are perfectly acceptable to our employees under the present conditions. Should this establishment be forced to unionize, we will lose many of our most valued key employees because they refuse to join an organization against their will.
We urge you to do all in your power to remedy this most undemocratic and grossly unfair situation.
Very truly yours, Patma potion By Pres.

The Peas The Waldives The Hollywood ICO and WESTERN VERMONT WE SHIRE SUNSET CAHUENGA OUVE BURBANK Example No. 55 Richlor's 156 N. LA CIENEGA BOULEVARD BEVERLY HILLS, CALIFORNIA February 21, 1947 Mr. George R. Le Sauvage, National Restaurant Association, 58 W. 23rd Street, New York City.
Dear Mr. Le Sauvage: Pickets appeared in front of our place last June, simultaneously with their arrival in front of our good neighbors, Lawry's.
The situation is similar: We were simply told that we must sign, a union contract which provided that all our employees ja the union. Refusal on our part meant picketing.
Picketing meant we would be unable to obtain supplies, because truck drivers will not deliver to us when a picket line is formed.
We objected th this blackmailing form of coercion, and told our employees the situation. They rallied to our support, said they had no desire to be forced into the union, and when the pickets appeared our personnel remained at work 100%. This has been the situation ever since. We have been able to obtain supplies, but at a considerable cost over what we should be paying. Our business has not suffered because the public recognizes the racketeering methods being used and in fact we have probably gained in customers. But the fight against this form of coercion is expensive and it is highly uneconomic, without even considering its un-American abuse of union power.
If our employees want unionism, we would be only too happy to sign up with the union bosses but we can not conscientiously do this unless it is the expressed desire of the employees. And our employees are happy because they are well treated, paid better than union members, and believe they are in the right.
You will be performing a valuable service if you can make our representatives in Congress see the abuses being practiced by the unions here, and provide remedial legislation.
Very truly yours, RICHLOR'S, Inc.
Kawrencex Frank, Pres.

Example No. 56 Lowry's THE PRIME RIB 150 NO. LA CIENEGA BOULEVARD BEVERLY HILLS, CALIFORNIA Feb. 21 Dear Admiral: Glad to see you are on the job in connection with the assembling of material to present the case of the restaurants in this fight to obtain legislation that will put an end to the bad misuse of union power.
The folder enclosed, "Why the Pickets?" succinctly states our case -- a situation which is so wholly un-American and which violates everyone's innate understanding of right.
It is high time our men in Congress do something to alleviate this condition and forever make it impossible to impose unionism on people who have denied its value to them.
The unions have made no attempt at any time to unionize our employees -- 58 in number. They apparently realized the futility of this, and acted on the belief (We are told) that we would soon "cave in" if they placed pickets in front of our restaurant. They believed that this tactic would force us as employers to sign a union contract which provided that our employees join the union. Our scale has always been higher than that paid by the employers who operate under closed shop agreements, and our employees have never expressed a grievance.
Appearance of the pickets caused no change in our personnel; everyone remained on the job. It caused no noticeable change in business, because the public realizes we are the victims of a vicious racket. But it has cost us money to protect the interests of our workers, at the rate of more than $2,000.00 a month. You see, union truck drivers with materials destined for us, will not go through the picket line -- hence we must use various subterfuges and our own trucks to get our supplies, a costly procedure and one which would be entirely obviated if our legislators provided legislation which would make impossible the picketing of any establishment where no labor dispute exists, and by labor dispute should be maant the expressed wish of a majority of the employees.
Thanks for anything you can do to reduce this great waste.
Watter Van de Kar WALTER VAN DE KAMP RALPH FRANK Tam O'Shanter Inn Renowned for Hamburger, served in a variety of ways.
2980 LOS FELIZ BLVD. LOS ANGELES, CALIF.
Dear Admiral: It was June 15, 1946, when the union pickets first appeared at our restaurant and they have been here ever since. First they had made a weak attempt to interest some of our employees in joining the union, but there apparently was no encouragement from this quarter, and we were told that as employers we would have to sign a union contract which would force every employee into the union.
We objected to this form of dictation, taking the position that they had a perfect right to try to organize our employees but we felt we had no right to force them into the union by signing a closed shop contract.
We were given a deadline for compliance with the union requests, and when that passed, the union pickets appeared.
The pickets are not former employees. In fact we lost only one men a union bartender, who very reluctantly left nd who explained he would lose his union card which he has held for so many years if he remained on the job at our establishment.
We have never had any labor trouble. In fact, our labor turnover is almost nil. We pay above the union scale, and working conditions are excellent in every respect.
Forcing our emplo yees to join an organization against their will is contrary to what we consider right, and we shall continue to fight this legalized form of blackmail until we are given relief by our State or national governments in the form of remedial legislation. In the meantime, this effort to obtain materials (union truck drivers won't pass through the picket line with supplies destined for us) is expensive for us, and picketing must be costing the unions quite a sum.
As far as we know, the pickets are hired by the hour, and they have no concern over the issue or any sympathy with the basic idea; they are simply doing a job for which they are being paid.
Is this America?
Sincerely, C Tam Shanter Inns, Lta.

I. BOYCOTTS CONSTRUCTION INDUSTRY Source: Senate Committee Reprint No. 12 79th Congress 2nd Session A. The Hutcheson Case The Hutches on case (312 U. S. 219) is a landmark in the Antitrust Division's campaign before the war against restraints of trade in the construction industry, because it was the first definite set-back regarding restraints in the labor field by the decision of the Supreme Court in favor of the defendants.
To oversimplify the issue, the case arose out of a jurisdictional dispute in the Anheuser-Busch plant in St. Louis between the United Brotherhood of Carpenters and Joiners of America and the International Association of Machinists.
The indictment alleges that there had been a series of agreements covering a period of several years between the machinists and carpenters in the Anheuser-Busch plant under which the machinists should do "the erecting, assembling, installing, and repairing of all metal machinery or parts thereof." The carpenters' agreement provided that-- The work to be done by the members of the union under this contract shall be as, when, and where determined and designated by the employer.
In spite of this joint agreement, the complaint alleges that the defendants-- made demands upon Anheuser-Busch, Inc., to employ millwrights who were members of the United Brotherhood of Carpenters and Joiners of America instead of machinists, to perform various parts and all of the work of erecting, assembling, installing, and setting machinery in the St. Louis brewery and manufacturing plants of Anheuser-Busch, Inc.
The controversy developed into a strike not only of the millwrights but a sympathetic strike of other members of A. F. of L. employed by Anheuser-Busch and against other construction companies building plant facilities for the brewing company. The defendants, it is alleged brought about a boycott of beer brewed by the company and distributed printed material, including notices in the trade publications of the carpenters union, denouncing the brewing company as unfair to organized labor and calling upon union members and friends of labor to refrain from purchasing the company's beer.

IX. BOYCOTTS CONSTRUCTION INDUSTRY The Hutches on case went to the Supreme Court and was decided February 3, 1941, against the Government and in favor of the carpenters union. The Government argued that There is no form of labor warfare so opposed to the public interest and to the interest of organized labor itself as the jurisdictional strike which stops the commerce of an employer who is trying to be fair to organized labor, An employer who finds himself the victim of such a strike is powerless.
According to the indictment, the Bedford-Bloomington district in Lawrence and Monroe Counties of Indiana is an important producing center for limestone used in the building industry. This district supplies 82 percent of the limestone sold in the United States and 90 percent of the limestone used in building construction in the Chicago area.
There are a number of firms engaged in quarrying the stone, some of which is sold in the form of rough blocks called quarry stone and some is finished into fabricated stone which is measured, milled, and cut to specified sizes, shapes, and designs at or near the quarries.
In the process of fabrication 15 to 20 percent of the rough quarry stone is chipped off and must be disposed of.
In metropolitan areas, therefore, there are two freight hauls for the waste stone: Inward to the fabricating yards, and outward to get rid of the waste if fabrication is done near the construction site.
The indcitment alleged that the Chicago cut-stone contractors and the unions of stone cutters and carvers had entered into a working agreement to the effect that the contractors and the unions would not install any fabricated stone finished in the Bedford-Bloomington district and the members of the Building Trades Council had refused to work in or on buildings upon which limestone fabricated in that district was used. This boycott by the unions applied not only to the stone cutters but to other building-construction labor including the iron workers and carpenters' unions.
The difference between letting contracts for stone work which is cut in the Chicago area and contracts for the same work fabricated near the quarries in Indiana runs into large figures. In the case of one contract alone referred to in the testimony, the difference amounts to $60,000. The low bid by an Indiana company on a particular contract was $212,000, which was low by about $60,000, as against Chicago contractors, or a difference of nearly 30 percent.
The cut-stone case was the subject of two trials in the northern district of Illinois, The first one was before I. BOYCOTTS CONSTRUCTION INDUSTRY - Judge Woodward and the second one before Judge Igoe. In the first trial a verdict of guilty was rendered against some of the defendants, and others were found not guilty. Those found guilty were the Building Trades Council, the stone cutters union, one of the contracting firms, and six individual´ defendants. Those not guilty were one of the contracting firms and two of the defendant contractors. The case was dismissed on motion by the Government against some of the other defendants. As to those defendants found guilty the court granted a new trial.
The second trial resulted in a directed verdict by Judge Igoe, and the case against the remaining contractor defendants and the labor unions was dismissed.

1.17 BOYCOTTS CONSTRUCTION INDUSTRY C. Ready-Mixed Cement On June 24, 1940, an indictment was returned against a number of labor organizations and individuals in the Chicago area on the grounds of restricting the use of ready-mixed cement and cement mixers on construction jobs in and near the city (United States v. Michael Carrozzo, et al.). The indictment said that the latest development in the production of concrete is the use of truck mixers; that trucks may either agitate the cement previously muxed on the way to the job or may mix the aggregates with water in transit or at the site. It was alleged that mixing trucks are generally used in all the metropolitan areas of the United States except Chicago.
Practically all cement workers in the Chicago area are members of the International Union of Operating Engineers or International Hod Carriers, etc. It is alleged that the defendants prevent the employment of members of the union from using truck mixers, or if mixers are used the same amount of labor must be employed as would be used under the old methods of mixing on the site. In other instances the unions strike if truck mixers are brought on the job.
The indictment also alleged that the sale of truck mixers was forbidden in the Chicago area in competition with other mixing devices, and that the defendants warned manufacturers of the mixers that they would not be permitted to sell, the mixers in the area. Prospective purchasers of trucks were also warned that they would not be permitted to use them.
Demurrers to the indictment were filed by the defendants on various grounds including that of immunity against prosecution under the Sherman Act granted by sections 6 and 20 of the Clayton Act and under the Norris-Laguardia Act. The se demurrers were sustained by District Judge Sullivan of the Northern District of Illinois, Eastern Division. Judge Sullivan said in part: The indictment alleges, and the Government contends, that the use of truck mixers would result in a savings of labor costs through reduction of the number of men employed. Defendants insist that this is exactly what they are objecting to, and the purpose of the strikes, or threatened strikes, was to compel cement contractors (contractors?) in the Chicago area to accede to the union's demands that the services of the se engineers be retained on jobs where truck mixers are used; that these are activities within the legitimate objects of a labor union, 3. Noons BOYCOTTS - CONSTRUCTION INDUSTRY After further discussion Judge Sullivan continues: There appears to be nothing unreasonable in these demands, and only lawful means seem to have been used to accomplish them. I am of the opinion that defendants, under the circumstances, are immune from prosecution under the Sherman Act, by reason of the provisions of the Norrislaguardia Act, which enlarges the scope of section 20 of the Clayton Act.
With respect to the alleged restraint of trade in the sale of truck mixers in the Chicago market, Judge Sullivan said in part: Here there is no restraint charged which would be calculated to restrict production of truck mixers or in any way control the market. There is no restraint of commercial competition such as would substantially affect market prices; no agreements to fix prices, divide marketing territories, apportion customers, restrict production, or any like practices which would tend to raise prices, or take from buyers the advantages accruing from free competition in the market.
If, as alleged in the indictment, prospective purchasers were warned against buying truck mixers on the grounds that the unions would not permit their use in the Chicago area, it is not apparent that the buyers have the advantage of free competition in the market.
A number of demurrers were filed by the defendants and on February 1, 1941, District Judge Sullivan held that the laborunion activities charged in the indictment are legitimate and lawful, and that the defendants are immune from prosecution under the Sherman Act by reason of the Norris-Laguardia Act.
On April 7, 1941, the Supreme Court affirmed the judgment of the district court sustaining the demurrers on the authority of United States v. William L. Hutcheson et al. (See United States v. International Hod Carriers, etc., 313 U.S.539.) BOYCOTTS - CONSTRUCTION INDUSTRY D. Prefabricated Houses In September 1940 the Antitrust Division obtained an indictment in the eastern district of Illinois against a group of producers of millwork and local unions of carpenters, hod carriers, etc., in the East St. Louis district, which had combined to shut out millwork and other supplies from other areas and to prevent the erection of prefabricated houses in the city and adjacent areas ( United States v.
B. Goedde Company et al.). The indictment was outlined in three parts in harmony with a later court decision which distinguished the three subject matters; namely, background information, boycotts against nomunion products, and boycotts accompanied by violence against the erection of prefabricated houses.
A number of motions to quash the indictment and please in abatement were filed by various groups of the defendants.
Judge Lindley of the eastern district of Illinois on September 6, 1941, quashed the indictment partly on the grounds of the "inflammatory" background statement and that a number of the allegations were not amenable to the antitrust laws because of immunities granted under the Clayton Act and the Norrislaguardia Act by recent cisions of the Supreme Court, notably in the Hutcheson case. More specifically, Judge Lindley said that the background material calling attention to conditions "which should never be countenanced in a free society" was prejudicial to the defendants.
 Thus is presented pictorially to the jury a revolting condition, abhorrent to the innate sense of right and fairness in every decent human being's breast. Inasmuch as any interested party may read the indictment to the jury and it may properly be submitted to that body for consideration at the conclusion of the trial, it is apparent that this statement of conditions which "should never be countenanced in a free society," and which naturally tends to inspire in all law-abiding people resentment and righteous outrage will tend to destroy that ideal of American jurisprudence, a fair, impartial trial as guaranteed by the Constitution.
With respect to the second group of allegations in the indictment, Judge Lindley said: Under ***, (par. 49), it is alleged that the unions warned builders that they would not work with unlabeled materials; warned purchasers BOYCOTTS CONSTRUCTION INDUSTRY - that such material would have to be removed and returned to the manufacturer and forced the makers of such material to remove the same from the job and return it to the plants. It would seem obvious that these acts are likewise granted immunity by the Clayton Act. Under 3 (par. 50) we have alleged intimidation of builders by strikes and threats to strike, thereby forcing them to buy union-labeled material, although similar products could be purchased at lower prices in other States. This too is exempt within the language of the Clayton Act. The resulting effect upon interstate commerce is purely incidental (U.S. v. Hutcheson, 312 U.S.
219 at 241). Each of the acts thus far mentioned apparently is, by the Clayton Act, recognized as a legal economic weapon of labor unions.
With respect to the third part of the indictment, namely, refusal to erect prefabricated houses manufactured outside of Illinois, Judge Lindley said in part: Under 3 (d) (par. 50), it is charged that the union refused to install material manufactured in States other than Illinois and refused to erect prefabricated houses manufactured outside of Illinois and under 4 (par. 51), that they have violently assaulted and beaten laborers not affiliated with them, threatened violence and bodily harm to members of such persons' families and threatened to destroy property not labeled with union labels and that they have forcibly prevented members from other labor organizations from working upon the material. Each of the se latter acts is granted no immunity by either the Clayton Act or the Norris-Laguardia Act. Neither of those legislative enactments contemplated that it be legitimate for unions to combine with others for the purpose of preventing the use of materials moving in interstate commerce; to employ as a means, violent assault and battery in the achievement of keeping out such material or to destroy property which they may deem undesirable. Such acts have never been stamped by Congress with a certificate of approval.
Judge Lindley finally concluded that -- ✦✦✦ in view of the fact that my adjudication is subject to review not only by the United States Circuit Court of Appeals but, perhaps, also by the Supreme Court of the United States, it seems to me only fit and proper that, in an effort to ت BOYCOTTS - CONSTRUCTION INDUSTRY avoid unnecessary labor and expense to the parties involved, I act so as to secure a final adjudication as to the sufficiency of the indictment, if possible, before trial.
It is with this thought and with the hope that there may be an early review of my decision that I conclude that the motions to quash should be sustained.
This case was not appealed since the demurrers were sustained on both substantive and technical grounds. Prior to the amendment of the Criminal Appeals Act on May 9, 1942 (U.S.C.A. 682) the Government had no appeal where an indictment had been quashed on technical grounds and the same was true where the indictment was quashed on independent groundsone technical, the other substantive.

BOYCOTTS - CONSTRUCTION INDUSTRY E. A. F. L. vs. C. I. O. Unions Early in 1940 the Antitrust Division brought two cases involving strikes of A. F. of L. unions against CIO unions which had been certified after election by the National Labor Relations Board. These cases involved the operations of a plywood mill in the State of Washington and the New Orleans Trucking Association of New Orleans, La. In each case the Supreme Court on direct appeal affirmed the judgment of the district courts sustaining demurrers filed by the defendants on authority of the Hutcheson case.
(1) The Plywood Case.
In On February 1, 1940, an indictment was returned against the United Brotherhood of Carpenters and Joiners of America; two A. F. of L. carpenters unions in Chicago; and five individuals including William L. Hutcheson, alleging restraints of trade against the Harbor Plywood Corp. which operates a factory in Hoquiam, Wash. This firm produced Douglas fir plywood to the value of more than $3,000,000 annually.
July 1938 Plywood Local 2861 of Hoquiam, Wash., an affiliate of A. F. of L., petitioned the National Labor Relations Board to hold ane election to determine the bargaining agent for the Company workers. In August of the same year the Plywood and Veneer Workers Union Local 2521 of Hoquiam, Wash., an affiliate of CIO asked for an election for the same purpose.
On September 10, 1938, the election was held and the CIO Local 2521 won the election. After the election, it is alleged that the Brotherhood of Carpenters, Chicago District Council of Carpenters, and Carpenters Local 58 of Chicago threatened and called strikes of employees of purchasers and users of the products manufactured by the Harbor Plywood Corp. in whose plant the CIO union had won the election. It is alleged that a white list was prepared from which the Harbor Plywood Corp.
was omitted and also a blacklist on which the company's name appeared.
The defendants ordered the members of the United Brotherhood of Carpenters and Joiners not to work on any products manufactured by the Harbor Plywood Corp. In October 1938 a strike was called by local 58 of Chicago against the Universal Form Clamp Co. of that city which was a purchaser of plywood products of the Harbor Corp. of Washington. The Chicago District Council of Carpenters submitted a white list to the Universal Form Clamp Co. in which the Harbor Plywood Corp. did not appear. It is alleged that this was submitted for the purpose of compelling the Universal Co, to cease their purchases of products manufactured by the Harbor Corp.

1.
TO* : BOYCOTTS CONSTRUCTION INDUSTRY - The defendant unions filed demurrers on five points, the most important of which for present purposes was point 3, on the grounds that the activities are lawful and legitimate objects of labor organizations and fall within sections 6 and 20 of the Clayton Act, the Norris-Laguardia Act, and the National Labor Relations Act.
On February 7, 1941, District Judge Woodward of the northern district of Illinois, eastern division, entered an order without opinion sustaining the demurrers to the indictment on the authority of United States v. William L. Hutcheson, et al., which had been decided by the Supreme Court a few days before on February 3, 1941.
On April 7, 1941, the Supreme Court affirmed the judgment of the district court in sustaining the demurrers on authority of the Hutcheson case.
(2) New Orleans Truckers.
On January 15, 1940, an indictment was returned against the Building and Construction Trades Council of New Orleans and about 20 A. F. of L. local construction unions and their business agents in New Orleans on the grounds that they refused to receive construction and other materials transported by the United Transport Workers Local 806, CIO.
The indictment alleged that in March 1939 the National Labor Relations Board ordered an election, as a result of which the United Transport Workers Local 806 was certified by the Board as the bargaining agent for employees of various trucking and drayage firms in New Orleans. As a result of the election some of the trucking and drayage firms through the medium of the New Orleans Trucking Association, Inc., entered into a contract with Local 806 as the collective-bargaining agent of the employees of these trucking firms.
It is alleged that after the petition for election was filed, but before the election, the International Brotherhood of Teamsters, etc., Local 207, A. F. of L., applied and was admitted to membership into the Building and Construction Trades Council of New Orleans, and that thereafter the local union and the council entered upon a campaign to defeat and force out of existence the United Transport Workers affiliated with CIO.
It was alleged that all of the defendant labor unions which were affiliates of A. F. of L. would not receive material from trucking and drayage firms employing members of United Transport, Local 806. As a consequence of this refusal, a large number of interstate shipments of materials and building fixtures were held up and it became necessary for J I T.C BOYCOTTS CONSTRUCTION INDUSTRY - the members of Local 806 to sublease the rights under their contracts with rail and water carriers, to other truckers acceptable to the A. F. of L. unions.
As examples of interstate shipments which were held up, the indictment cites pump parts and a motoe shipped from Ohio to New Orleans; wall ties from Chicago to a New Orleans contractor for use in a school building; and shipments of steel juncrion boxes shipped from St. Louis to New Orleans.
As a part of the conspiracy it was alleged that the New Orleans Building and Construction Trades Council adopted a resolution forbidding all union men to handle material delivered by nonunion drivers "meaning and intending to mean by the term 'nonunion drivers' members of the United Transport Workers Local Industrial Union, No. 806." The defendants filed demurrers on the grounds that the Sherman Act was inapplicable to the case by reason of section 20 of the Clayton Act and the Norris -Laguardia Act.
On February 26, 1941, the district court for the eastern district of Louisiana without opinion sustained the demurrers in the light of the decision of the Supreme Court in the Hutcheson case, decided on February 3, 1941. (See United States v. Building Construction Trades et al., 313 U.S. 539.) Summary The Government sought to distinguish the Plywood case and the New Orleans Trucking case from the Hutcheson case on the grounds that the CIO union had been selected as the sole bargaining agent at an election under the National Labor Relations Act. In the brief to the Supreme Court the Department said in part as follows: The National Labor Relations Act by its terms (sec. 8 (5)) forces the employer to recognize the union which is certified by the National Labor Relations Board. It does not by its terms force the defeated union to recognize the choice. The only protection which the employer has against retaliatory action by the defeated union is in the Sherman Act. If this protection is denied, it will mean that only the large concerns with great resources can survive the attempt of one labor uni on to use its organized power to set aside an order of the National Labor Relations Board. The fundamental absurdity of construing the Sherman Act in such a way as to put the small employer in a position in which he will be ruined whichever way he turns 1.
- BOYCOTTS CONSTRUCTION INDUSTRY is not one which can be achieved by any appropriate process of statutory construction. The Hutcheson case lays down the sound principle that the Sherman and the Clayton Acts must be construed by reading subsequent statutes dealing with relations between employees and employers as a "harmonizing text." That principle puts the labor activities set out in this case clearly within the prohibitions of the Sherman Act.
A little later the brief points out that the employer against whose products the strike is called has no choice in the selection of the union which shall be the sole bargaining agent for his plant, Under the National Labor Relations Act, if the employer assists a labor union in organizing his plant, thus preventing his employees from making a free choice of their bargaining agency, a contract between the employer and the assisted union is invalid.1 In such case the employer can be required to cease giving effect to the contract and, if the rival union which he had sought to exclude be composed of a majority of his employees, he can be compelled to bargain with it.
It would be an absured interpretation of congressional policy to assert that it was the intent of Congress that labor unions should be given freedom from the prohibitions of Federal law when they are engaged in trying to force an employer to enter into an illegal contrac or to engage in illegal conduct for which he may be penalized if he yields.
On April 7, 1941, the Supreme Court affirmed the judgment of the district court sustaining the demurrers in the New Orleans Trucking case on the authority of the Hutcheson case.
(See United States v. International Hod Carriers, etc., 313 U.S. 539.) BOYCOTTS CONSTRUCTION INDUSTRY - F.
Plumbing and Heating During 1939 and 1940 the Antitrust Division obtained a number of indictments in the plumbing and heating industry, one of which was national in scope and others of which were local. Among the local indictments were the Associated Plumbing and Heating Merchants of King County, Wash. (Seattle area); Heating, Piping, and Air-conditioning Contractors Association of Southern California (Los Angeles area); and the Plumbing and Heating Industries Administrative Association, Inc., of the District of Columbia.
The most far-reaching indictment in the plumbing field was styled United States v. The Central Supply Association, et al., returned March 29,1940. This indictment covered the entire distribution field of plumbing supplies, namely, the manufacturers, the jobbers, the master plumbers, and the journeymen plumbers who were members of the A. F. of L. Speaking in more detail, 12 corporations were indicted, who were manufacturers of plumbing supplies; 15 Jobbing companies; including 4 overlapping manufacturers; 8 master plumber associations; and 10 associations of journeyman plumbers, including the national organizations of the master and journeymen plumbers.
In addition, many individuals in the various groups were named in the indictment and also a large number of coconspirators among the various distributing groups in the plumbing industry.
The base of the inverted pyramid, so to speak, in the plumbing industry is represented by the big three manufacturersamerican Radiator Standard Sanitery Corp., Crane Co., and Kohler Co. According to the indictment, these three companies produce about 70 percent of the cast-iron enamelware and vitreous chinaware and more than 50 percent of the brass goods used for plumbing in the United States. About 80 percent of all plumbing supplies, except pipe, is produced and distributed by the 12 defendant manufacturers, according to the indictment.
There are two main methods of distributing plumbing supplies: (1) The old-line or restricted method from manufacturer to jobber, to master plumbers, to consumer.
(2) The so-called direct-to-you method in which plumbing supplies are sold by menufacturers directly to the consumer.
The gist of the indictment runs to the effect that the old-line method should be more properly called the "restricted 1.
?
..

CING IV. BOYCOTTS BOYCOTTS - CONSTRUCTION INDUSTRY system of distribution" in which boycotts and restrictive practices against other systems of distribution are applied down the line at the various stages of distribution.
It was alleged that under the closed and restricted system, manufacturers would sell only to jobbers who confined their sales to master plumbers, jobbers would sell only to the master plumbers, the master plumbers would refuse to install plumbing supplies which they did not sell, and the unions would not work for master plumbers who would install plumbing supplies the master plumbers did not sell, The manufacturers are alleged to have refused to sell plumbing supplies to mail-order houses, cooperatives, and other direct-to-you outlets, and the master plumbers are alleged to have refused to contract for the installation of plumbing supplies handled by these outlets.
With respect to the journeymen plumbers, namely, United Association of Journeymen Plumbers and Steam Fitters for the United States and Canada, the indictment alleges in part: (h) The defendant United Association has- (1) Urged and advised its affiliated local unions, to reach understandings and make agreements with county and city associations of master plumbers affiliated with the defendant NAMP to the effect that their members would work only for members of, or master plumbers approved by, or master plumbers who abided by the rules and regulations of, said associations of master plumbers.
 (2) Urged and advised its affiliated local unions, to have their members refuse to install plumbing supplies which were not sold and distributed through the "restricted system of distribution," even though the person or persons wishing to have the said plumbing supplies installed were willing and able to bargain collectively, and to abide by all union rules and regulations as to wages, hours, and working conditions.
A number of other restricted practices were alleged with respect to each stage of distribution of plumbing supplies..
• As to the effects of the combination, it was alleged that the "restricted system of distribution" made the cost of purchasing and installing plumbing supplies higher to the consumer by a large percentage. In the purchase price alone of 2°T.
LOVEM MATI BOYCOTTS CONSTRUCTION INDUSTRY - the supplies, not including the cost of installation, it was alleged that the price under the direct-to-you system was from 15 to 40 percent less than the price at which supplies are sold to the consumer under the "restricted" system. It was also alleged that the "restricted" system enabled the plumbing unions to "impose unreasonable, arbitrary, and artificial regulations and requirements as to the installation of said plumbing supplies.

Naval Ordnance Plant Centerline, Michigan Timken Detroit Axle Company - Clark and Fort Streets Detroit, Michigan Boycott at Chevrolet Gear and Axle Plant On March 8, 1937, Davidson's Industrial Caterers was subjected without prior notice to a sit-down strike of its employees located at 271 Rademacher, servicing the Ternstedt Manufacturing Company. After the strike was called, Davidson's was approached by representatives of Local 234, A.F.L., who stated that the strike had been called for the purpose of organizing the employees, and made wage demands which were regarded as excessive by Davidson's.
With operations still suspended, approximately one month later, while contract negotiations continued in good faith at the Ternstedt Plant, union stewards in the Chevrolet Gear and Axle Plant stood at the lunch wagons of Davidson's and demanded that the employees of this plant refuse to patronize Davidson's on the Admiral G. R. Lesauvage February 26, 1947 -2excuse that it was not fair to organized labor. The stewards upon inquiry by Davidson's stated that they were acting as the result of a request made on their plant Local 235, UAW-CIO, by Local No. 234, AFL. A virtually complete boycott resulted. Davidson's was forced to suspend operations. The combined strike and boycott lasted until April 20, 1947, when, finding no apparent signs of moderation of the union demands, and feeling that it could resist no longer, Davidson's assented to the union demands.
(Milk) Boycott at Chevrolet Plant On or about April 10, 1939, Davidson's was approached by Local 235, UAW-CIO, at Chevrolet Gear and Axle Plant, whose representatives objected to the use of milk and cream purchased by Davidson's from Chaslen Farms Dairy, Inc., Northvillo, Michigan, on the grounds that this dairy was not union organized. Union committeemen were stationed at the entrance to the lunch wagon area in which Davidson's operated and demanded that the workers refrain from purchasing merchandise of any kind from Davidson's because of the use and services of nonunion milk and cream. This boycott resulted in closing down Davidson's lunch wagon operations and was maintained until about July 5, 1939, when the creamery became organized.
(Contract) Boycott at Pontiac Motor Division On or about November 1, 1939, while engaged in negotiating a contract with Davidson's, Local 653, UAW-CIO - the same Local in which the plant employees were organized - declared a boycott of Davidson's by the same means mentioned above, that is, through the stationing at Davidson's lunch wagons of local union stewards, who demanded that plant employees cease to patronize Davidson's, on the excuse that this concern was unfair to organized labor. This although the wage demands were still under negotiation. Photostat of a circular distributed to plant employees by Local 653, is attached hereto marked "Exhibit A". The boycott resulted in suspending Davidson's operations, the suspension continuing until December 11, 1939, when, having resisted as long as it reasonably could, the strike and boycott were settled by Davidson's assenting to the union demands.
(Bread) Boycott at Chevrolet Plant On or about March 1, 1940, representatives of Local 235, UAW-CIO, of Chevrolet Gear and Axle Plant, advised Davidson's, who was then using bread baked by Continental Baking Company, 2915 Grand River, organized by an AFL union, that Davidson's must patronize a bakery organized by the CIO, the only concern so organized at that time being Gordon Baking Company, Detroit. Upon the refusal of Davidson's to change its bread supply, a boycott was declared on all sandwiches through the same procedure described above. This boycott resulted in a serious decrease in receipts of Davidson's, and continued until about April 15, 1940. The boycott was settled, when resistance could no longer be maintained, by Davidson's assenting to the union demands.
(Milk) Boycott at Chevrolet Plant On or about September 6, 1941, Davidson's was approached by representatives of Local 235, Chevrolet Plant, in reference to a general increase in the cost of milk to Davidson's of 1/2 cent per pint bottle, approximating an increase of 1 cent at the retail level, which increase Davidson's had made effective on the plant The union representatives demanded that Davidson's rescind the increase, menu.

(Contract) Boycott at Pontiac, Naval Ordnance, and Chevrolet Gear and Axle Plants On or about September 22, 1942, while engaged in negotiating a new contract with Local 653, UAW-CIO, at the Pontiac Plant, another boycott was declared by the union in the same manner already described, as well as by the distribution of circulars, a photostat of which is attached hereto, marked "Exhibit B". The boycott had the same result, the suspension of Davidson's operations.
With the boycott against Davidson's still in effect at the Pontiac Plant, other locals located in plants serviced by Davidson's, including Local 154, UAW-CIO, at the U. S. Naval Ordnance Plant; and Local 235 at the Chevrolet Gear and Axle Plant, gave indication that they had been approached by Local 653 of the Pontiac Plant, to declare a similar boycott. On October 8, 1942, hand bills were passed out at the Chevrolet Plant by union stewards or representatives, a photostatic copy of which is attached hereto, marked "Exhibit C". Further, at a meeting held that night at the Chevrolet Plant for the purpose of discussing the contract negotiations, and attended by representatives of the three unions mentioned, statements addressed to Davidson's were made by the representatives of the unions located at the Chevrolet Plant and the Naval Ordnance Plant, to the effect that "You know what you will get if you don't sign the contract". As the result, realizing that resistance was futile, Davidson's assented to the demands.
Boycott at Pontiac Motors Division of General Motors and U. S. Naval Ordnance Plant On March 31, 1944, the Regional War Labor Board, Detroit, ordered a wage increase for Davidson's employes which resulted from demands at the Pontiac Motor Division of General Motors, Pontiac, by Local 653, UAW-CIO, representing both the Pontiac Motor employees and Davidson's employees. In the meantime, Davidson's had applied to the Office of Price Administration, Detroit, for menu price increases sufficient to offset the increased wage cost called for by the wage contract as ordered. An appeal to the National War Labor Board was necessitated, and while this appeal was still pending, Local 653 demanded that the wage increase called for in the contract be placed in effect. The boycott was declared and placed in effect on April 17, 1944, through the same procedure described above, as well as the distribution of a circular, a photostatic copy of which is appended hereto, marked "Exhibit D".
The boycott resulted in suspending Davidson's operations from April 17, 1944 to May 9, 1944.
Also on April 17, 1944, Local 154, UAW-CIO, representing plant employees at the Naval Ordnance Plant, declared a similar boycott, through the same procedure described above. This boycott resulted in the stoppage of Davidson's wagon service representing 70% of Davidson's dollar volume, and in greatly reduced business at the plant.

Lesauvage February 26, 1947 -4On or about May 6, 1944, with the boycott still in effect, Lavidson's met with Local 154 representatives, with the management of the plant represented, at which meeting these representatives repeated previously made demands that Davidson's increase the size of the food portions that were being served and at the same time reduce prices, threatening to use the influence of the union should Davidson's refuse. Davidson's were made aware through these representations that the Local 154 representatives, or their superiors, were actuated by ignorance or misrepresentations as to the cost of food and food service operations. When it became apparent that representations made by Davidson's as to actual costs were without result in modifying the union demands, Davidson's, at the request of the management of the plant, withdrew from the catering contract, thus losing the business which it had been its devoted effort in the spirit of fairness and good will to maintain.
Boycott at Timken-Detroit Axle Plant and Chevrolet Gear and Axle Plant As the result of new contract negotiations with Local 1064, UAW-CIO, and Local 234, AFL, representing Davidson's employees, Davidson's made application to the Office of Price Administration, Detroit, for menu price increases with which to pay the required wage increases. On July 15, 1946, Davidson's placed in effect price increases as approved by this agency at the Timken-Detroit Axle Plant. A boycott was thereupon declared through the same procedure described above, on Davidson's operations, by Local 155, UAW-CIO, representing the plant employees, on the asserted ground that the price increases were unnecessary. The boycott was continued until about August 30, 1946, when it was partially rescinded. While not now serious, its effects are still felt. Of 18 lunch wagons formerly in operation at this plant, but 17 are now in operation, testifying to the reduction in volume of sales.
Following the boycott at the Timken Plant, a similar boycott was declared by Local 235, UAW-CIO, at the Chevrolet Gear and Axle Plant, when the approved Office of Price Administration prices were placed in effect in this plant, on or about August 22, 1946, through the stationing of union stewards in the Davidson's lunch area, as described above. This boycott has never been rescinded and while not now serious in its effects, is still felt in reduced volume at this plant.
At this point it should be said that the several boycotts described herewith all had serious effects in reduced volume of business lasting as long as 90 days in virtually every instance.
Davidson's resisted, as well as it was able, in good faith, the heavy wage and other demands, successively made by the several unions, as stated above, in the belief that these demands, if met, would result in menu prices beyond the ability of the plant workers to pay. Facts as to Davidson's cost situation were offered in proof of this contention, during the various negotiations. Nevertheless the wage demands were insisted upon perforce.
As the result, Davidson's operations at the Naval Arsenal had the outcome stated, while those at the Pontiac and Chevrolet Gear and Axle Plants, are at present and have been for the past three years, economically unsound. Menu prices cannot be further increased in our judgment without pricing ourselves out of the market, while no further economies appear.

Admiral G. R. Lesauvage February 26, 1947 -5Because of this situation in which we find ourselves, due to apparently irresponsible union action, we believe that the disproportionate powers given to labor unions, including use of the boycott, are destructive of business and a menace to everyone concerned. We believe that these arbitrary powers, including use of the boycott, should be discontinued.
Samenche GENEVIEVE Kaminski Notary Public, Wayne County, Mic.
