Some of them have but there are those long-haired boys in the Department (I don't say they are all long-haired and I do not say that those in control are long-haired) who have influenced those who are in control. A lot of things I do are influenced by those who work for me. They do not work for me, I guess. I probably work for them.
They tell me what to do and I try to do it. That is the way our organization goes. They have a lot of influence on me, just as your associates and your employees have a lot of influence on you.
And the policies of Mr. Thompson and Mr.
Raushenbush and the other boys were to the effect that one man within the government was worth more in promoting socialism than 300 out of the government. He can bore from within.
He can spread it around and get people thinking along these screwy lines and so unknowingly and unwittingly you find yourself going down a path that you have no right to go down and should not go down. I say there is a considerable amount of that influence within the government and I think within the Department, and I am not accusing any one body, either.
- 35 - I had a talk with Mr. Chapman on three different occasions. He told me he believed in public power and he was for public power, but he did not think that public power should replace private power. He felt there was a place for both, and that they could be coordinated. We are not coordinated, but we are working at it and I have hopes that we will get there yet.
FIRST OFFER REJECTED The first offer was rejected. Then came a submission of plans back and forth. Each side was afraid of the other. I think that is absolutely true.
We were afraid to take our hair down and I do not know whether they were afraid to take theirs down or would not take it down, but, if you read the cockeyed stuff they submitted to us and asked us to sign, you would realize that their gall was refreshing.
All they asked of us was to turn our steam plants over to them and our system and, if there was anything left after they were done with it we could use that. One of their propositions reads almost exactly that way. Of course, that was a refreshing and new kind of an insult to our intelligence.
They did not think we would take that, of course, and we bickered back and forth.
ELECTION PLAYS PART Then came an election and the private power boys did not want to go ahead because there was this election coming up. The public boys were a little afraid that the election was not going to go the way it actually went. (Laughter) I said this to them, "You don't have the right to gamble with your stockholders' business. You are here as businessmen to conduct a business and, if you can make a fair and equitable contract now, now is the time to make it.
" Did you ever try to get fifteen people to agree to any one thing that has half a hundred pages in it -- fourteen of them on your side and one on the other? They wanted to dot every "i" and cross every "t" and change the "the" to "a". I learned something about our fellows too, we are just an average American group. That is all we are.
As I say, the election went as some of our boys did not hope it would go, and I was thinking that maybe we ought to be prepared if it went that way. Then the power boys began dotting the "i's" and crossing the "t's". Did you ever see a dog have a cat in a corner? That is exactly what I mean. He is afraid to go in after it and the cat knows darn well that he will go so far and not come in. Then the cat sees a clearing and gets out.
MAKES OWN OFFER Finally, I said, "You boys are too much for me.
We are going to make an offer of our own. I won't live long enough and I will wear out too quickly to go ahead and satisfy as many masters as I have here to satisfy.' I took the Texas Power and Light contract, which was a coordinating contract, and which the Speaker of the House felt was a good thing, and he said it was a good thing. In that contract it provides fundamentally the same thing that we have in ours, to let the government get the benefit of our system.
We said, "You do not need to duplicate it. Here it is. You can get coordinated service from it.
You can take any customer from us that you are authorized to serve under the law. We are not going to quarrel with you on that.
We told them that we would take the power and buy it at their rate. That has been set by FPC.
They call it their rate. It is a temporary rate for three years. We said that for the rural electric cooperatives and government institutions, we would buy the power from them and sell it back to them at the same price we bought if for.
FIRMS UP GOVERNMENT POWER We pointed out: "You do not have firm power except for six hours, so you will get twice as much by selling it to us, because you will sell it twice as fast for six hours, twice as many kilowatts for six hours. So we will pay you twice the money you can get if you sell it unfirmed and uncoordinated.
"We will sell you the power to firm it up for 4 mills for your customers or customers served for your account from our lines. We furnish the lines and facilities. And we will do it for nothing.
We will take the line losses. We will suffer the operating expenses. We will pay the taxes on the lines, and the power we bought and all the rest of it. We want to coordinate it.
"If you take a customer from us that we have served for two years prior to the date that the government started serving it, other than rural electric cooperatives and establishments operated by and for the benefit of the Federal Government, then you will pay us our rate for that class of service as of the date the contract becomes effective." LIKE TEXAS CONTRACT That is the same proposition as in the Texas contract, except in the Texas contract they say, "We will let you use our system for half the power from Denison Dam." - 36 - We say, "You can bring into Oklahoma all the whole system. We let them use it. We let them power you need to supply all of the customers you are authorized to serve in Oklahoma, bring it in from anywhere. We will take it and use our system to distribute it, and we will distribute it for you.
In Texas they said, "We will take half from that dam." They got a lot of protection. They kept everything out. In Texas, which is a sort of a nation unto itself, they would not let them bring any power in or take power out. Their operation is intrastate. Under that deal they do not get under the control of the FPC. In that way they say, "If you take a customer other than the rural electric cooperatives or establishments operated for or by the federal government, then you have to build your own lines. We will deliver it to a certain substation, but you will build your own lines to take it from there. If you take him, you will reimburse us in an amount equal to the difference of the cost of the power and energy at the lowest then effective rate of the company and the lowest then effective rate of the government." In effect, the two are exactly the same. The arithmetic is the same. The language is different.
They had that in Texas and we thought it established a precedent. The Department of Interior said that in Texas there was only one municipality and in Oklahoma we had 25 municipalities that owned their own systems, so, if they paid us that difference, they would be subsidizing us.
WHO IS SUBSIDIZED?
Remember the kind of power this is. We take in this peaking power. We intermingle it with the power from our resources and we deliver power at any place on our system that they want to take it out as firm, continuous power.
In the Northwest and in Montana they have "wheeling contracts". It was brought out in the hearing the other day that they pay them a mill per KWH or something like that, and that is a fair price to wheel. We make firm power out of peaking power. As the Administrator of the Southwestern Power Administration said, he does not have the kind of power he needs. "All I have is bananas and I want to take out fruit salad." It is a pretty good way to put it. Well, he puts in bananas and we give him fruit salad.
So I figured out this so-called subsidy and what it amounted to actually in Oklahoma and it would cost $280,000 a year for the municipal power.
They pay us more than they get for it. So they say they subsidize us.
One of the senators asked me, "Well, what about this subsidy?" I said, "I have heard about this subsidy business, this subsidizing of us, until I could just jump and scream. We subsidize them. We give them a take any customer they are authorized to take.
And when you consider this $280,000 and consider that we change peaking power into firm power and deliver it to them any place over our own lines, it would figure out that it would cost today about a mill, and it will continually get less." OFFER TURNED DOWN Well, they turned that deal down. They turned it down with this language: They took our language, where it was said that if they took any customer that we had served other than cooperatives and the federal government during the preceding two years they would pay us for the power at our rate effective for that class of service and added to it provided that our rate was no higher than the SPA rate.
-- Oh boy, that is the same deal again! "You give us your system. You buy the power from us and sell it back to us and we will take and run you out of business right now." So we said, "Somebody must have made a mistake. Please, mister, you did not mean that." Then this contract was for 20 years and could be cancelled by either party after five years on three years' notice, and the rates were subject to review each three years after the first three years and could be cancelled on three years' notice if the parties could not agree upon the rates. That meant that the contract could be out at the end of the first six years.
They proposed to add on to that, that it could be cancelled at any time by the Secretary of the Interior on two years' notice if he did not believe it was advantageous and to the benefit of the people.
But we had to spend over a million dollars in facilities to interconnect these government agencies. Mind you, they would get 3 million dollars in round figures -- $2,920,000 -- the first year if they took over what they could. And at the end of two years after they had taken our business, they proposed to cancel the contract.
We had everything in there and they would make out of what they got for the power more than we charged them for it by the sum of $1,667,000 a year.
Of course, I must be wrong, because they did not accept it. We offered them a good proposition. I think we will still get together, but I tell you, if we do not get together and if the electric light industry is socialized, that is the one key thing through which you can socialize every industry in this United States. (Applause) THE REA SCHEME But, in 1949, another scheme was born. The - 37 - Rural Electrification Administration has been granted tremendous funds for lines to rural electric cooperatives to bring electricity to those in rural areas not receiving central station service.
We have always believed in the wisdom of that act.
The debates and the reports of the Committees on this Rural Electrification Administration act provided that no funds should be loaned for the construction of competing lines or for the taking over of business already served by private companies, and that these loans should not be made for the purpose of serving in communities of over 1500 inhabitants. But the Rural Electrification Administration has the funds.
In 1949, loans were granted by the Rural Electrification Administration to certain super co-ops who have not been serving and who never will directly serve any rural farm customers. The Rural Electrification Administration loaned to these super co-ops large sums of money in each instance for the construction of steam plants and duplicating delivery lines and facilities. Then in turn these super co-ops entered into contracts with the Department of the Interior, by which the Department agrees to purchase all of the electric output from the steam plants, leaving no part of such plant output in the control of the cooperatives. In addition, these super co-ops deliver to the Department all of their lines and delivery facilities.
The Department agrees to pay to the super co-ops annually for such lines an amount such as would amortize their cost over a period of 40 years and to pay all maintenance and operation expense. At the end of 40 years, the Department by the payment of the mere sum of $10 would own all of these lines. The contract provides that the lines are already delivered to the Department and that the super co-ops have no control of them. In turn the Department sells back to the super co-ops sufficient power and energy, up to the maximum capacity of the steam plants, for the demands of the customers of the rural electric cooperatives which will be served by the super co-ops. The super coops pay to the Department a rate higher than that which the Department pays to the super co-ops for their steam plant electric power and energy.
CONGRESS GIVEN RUN-AROUND The present demands of the rural electric cooperatives which are proposed to be served by these super co-ops do not approximate the capacity of the steam plants which are to be constructed.
We find that under these contracts the Department has run around the Congress and has acquired steam-plant capacity and transmission and delivery facilities which the Congress has not approved. Not a single farmer will be served by these gigantic loans who would not otherwise be served. Not an inch of rural electric transmission line will be built from these funds to a farmer who is not now being served. If the Administrator of the Southwestern Power Administration is correct in his testimony, this means that the private companies cannot exist side by side with this public power agency. It means that there will be no pooling with electric companies. Nothing can save the private companies except a pooling agreement such as that desired by the Congress, desired by the Administrator of the Southwestern Power Administration, and desired by the companies.
PUBLIC POWER MONOPLY The debates in the Congress and the reports of the Committees on Section 5 of the Flood Control Act of December 1944 stated that there was no intent to create a giant public power monoply. It was always thought that the intent was to bring about pooling of these two great resources -- government hydroelectric power and private companies' steam plant electric power and energy.
But in a United Press release of March 2, 1950, it was said that the Department of the Interior had stated in a public address that if present plans are carried out the federal government will be in a position in 10 years to operate a power system stretching from New Orleans to Seattle; and that an Interior Department power expert said that it would be comparatively simple to tie in the Tennessee Valley Authority system and make the federal system stretch from coast to coast; and that this giant electric grid would be federally owned and most of it would be controlled by the Secretary of the Interior; "that it would be a far bigger electrical empire than ever dreamed of by some of the utility moguls of the 20's." PUBLIC SHOULD BE INFORMED Do the American people know this? Did Congress intend this? Is there any virtue in a huge public power monopoly with no control whatever by the people? Is this position in sympathy with the theory of the pooling of public power and private companies? Or is this a carrying out of the original intent of the coalition of Socialists and left-wingers in 1923? Should the people first be given an opportunity to vote upon this kind of a question?
Such a program circumventing the Congress and without the approval of the people is not democracy. It has in it all of the essence of bureaucratic administration and totalitarianism. We would not fear this question if it could get to the people.
PRIVATE ENTERPRISE CAN DO IT Mass production and mechanization is what made this country. The Assistant Secretary says that he does not think private enterprise can double capacity every ten years, every six years. I disagree with that! We have done it right along. And we will do it. Just take the fear of placing their money in jeopardy away from the investors and, bingo! How did the American industry produce for the war more than all the rest of the world together when the rest of the world was mobilized for war and we had to mobilize? We did it in a year and a half and we were outproducing them all - 38 - METHODS AND OBJECTIVES IN POWER POOLING EXPERIENCES IN THE PACIFIC NORTHWEST by KINSEY M. ROBINSON KINSEY M. ROBINSON, president, The Washington Water Power Company, Spokane, is a member of the Chamber's Natural Resources Committee. He started as a teamster with the Idaho Power Company in 1911 and rose to be president and general manager in 1934. In 1938 he went with the Washington Water Power Company in his present capacity. Mr. Robinson speaks with intimate knowledge of the working of one of the most successfully operated power pools in the Nation, a project that covers nine great watersheds in over 800,000 square miles, with 190 power plants that produce four and one quarter million kilowatts of electric power.
THE NORTHWEST POWER POOL comprises 11 major watersheds and covers an area of approximately 800,000 square miles. In this area operate 11 major electric systems, and 190 power plants.
It is said to be one of the largest power pools in the world.
Consequently, I shall not attempt to speak for other members of this Northwest integrated system, but only from personal experience. On one thing all of us agree: distances are great out West and we have some mighty big rivers. Our principal river, the Columbia, extends its drainage over seven states and into British Columbia.
It dominated the landscape even before the age of glaciers. The river carries a wallop that is scarcely equalled in America, with 1,020,000 second feet recorded at Bonneville Dam during the time of its highest flow. That is a lot of water.
It is easy to see why the Northwest possesses 40 per cent of all potential hydro electric energy in the nation.
Today, I wish to add only a thought or two regarding cooperative planning and the use of electric power. Within the limits of allotted time, I should like to tell you something about the feasibility of power pooling on a big scale; I should like to state that cooperation between federal, municipal, and private power systems is not only possible, but something to be desired, and also good business.
And finally, with due tribute to many people over the Nation, men of vision who conceived and first developed the idea of pooling power, I wish to join other expressions that may be made during this conference that we need an acceptable federal power policy. Without this policy none of us can work in security or peace.
NORTHWEST POOL NOT NEW Certainly power pooling as an idea is not new to us. Many of the private companies in the Northwest have had interconnected systems for more than 25 years. For example, the Pacific Power and Light and the Washington Water Power companies were interconnected in 1915, with additional connections established in 1924 and in 1926. Out West the Milwaukee Railroad was electrified as early as 1918, a direct result being a 110,000-volt transmission line between the company I represent and Puget Sound Power and Light. These are examples of voluntary cooperation. East of the Cascades, the Washington Water Power and the Montana company were interconnected in 1923; two municipal systems, Seattle and Tacoma, had preceded this by two years.
Utah and Idaho power companies integrated their systems in 1928. Coercion was never necessary.
Everything was accomplished in harmony. In 1941, a 154,000-volt line was built to tie Montana with the Utah Power and Light. This line completed a six-company pool of the Electric Bond and Share Company systems. Unquestionably it was a progressive step, and gave power advantages to the five states of Oregon, Washington, Montana, Idaho and Utah. It should be pointed out, also, that by this time all manner of operating difficulties had presented themselves. Techniques were established. Data pertaining to resources and area demands had been analyzed and compiled.
WAR SERVICE Therefore, when March, 1941 rolled around, and Grand Coulee produced power for the first time, there was much talk of war in the air. Also there was much need of complete pooling of all power resources in the area. Urged by Interior Secretary Krug, the Grand Coulee and the Bonneville systems, already operating as a single unit, joined the pool. That was 1942. The Northwest system had become one of the world's greatest pools.
There is little doubt that the Northwest pool contributed much not only to the area, but to the war effort. The four and one-half million total horsepower of electrical operating capacity made possible at least 100,000 kilowatts of additional load on the combined systems. This was enough power - 39 - -- to build one 10,000-ton Liberty Ship every day of the year or it produced 275,000 pounds of aluminum every 24 hours. At least twenty-five million dollars was saved for better use. The pool enabled regions with little war load to transfer surplus power into the 13,000-mile web of interconnecting lines and make it available where needed. It saved thousands upon thousands of barrels of critical fuel oil, and thousands of tons of coal. Interconnection also proved a good neighbor arrangement. The northwest family was learning to cooperate as neighbors should.
They were combatting drought, storms, floods, and unusual load problems in a democratic land in a democratic way.
POST-WAR PROBLEMS At one time it was speculated that when the war emergency ended, the pool would logically fall apart. Termination of the war, however, did not end coordination. The possibilities of a diverse system extending 1,100 miles down the map, challenged the minds of our engineers. Our engineers were attempting to calculate total precipitation and runoff in the watersheds. They were challenged by maximum power demands, none of which occurred at the same time on all systems.
They puzzled over time zones, weather forecasts, and changes in population. They discovered that the peak demand of the combined systems after interconnection was considerably less than the sum of all the individual system peaks. Engineers learned in time of emergency that even the impossible sometimes happens; that a vast system which by all rules of the game should fall apart when the speed of generators falls below the danger point, keeps right on running.
Technical difficulties in large-scale pool operation are not to be scoffed at. On a typical October night 50,000 kilowatts may be passed from Idaho to Utah; 20,000 will go from Utah to Montana; 30,000 kilowatts from Washington to Montana; the Bonneville-Grand Coulee system may transfer 60,000 kilowatts to the Washington-Pacific system; 20,000 kilowatts from the Puget Sound into the Washington system; 20,000 from Bonneville to Tacoma system; 40,000 to Portland General, and so on until the figures weary the mind. Twenty-three storage reservoirs are involved, not to mention personality factors even more complicated. The usual restraints imposed upon executives or found in contractual agreements cannot exist with operators of a power pool. Once basic principles are agreed upon, operators must be free to act instantly if speed is required. Mutual confidence and understanding are absolutely necessary.
ALL FOR THE COMMON GOOD All in all, we have learned many important things.
Included in these items is the fact that if men with different viewpoints will sit down and calmly discuss a problem, some area of agreement can usually be found. Also, it is to be concluded that men with strong wills and individual thinking, can and do subordinate personal desires for the common good. That is a lesson worth remembering!
POWER SHORTAGE At this point I should mention our power shortage. This shortage unquestionably helped to consolidate area-wide participation in the power pool. By 1948 the shortage was critical. As elsewhere throughout the nation, the war had left the area with a deficiency of generating equipment.
Our household use of electricity, averaging about 5,000 kilowatt hours annually, was above the national average by nearly two and one-half times.
We had experienced an influx of population amounting to 40 per cent. New industries were pounding at the door. Other industries, already established, such as aluminum, required exceptionally large blocks of power. The manufacturers of generating equipment were booked 100 per cent for years ahead. To make matters still worse, some of us were completely blocked in future hydro development. I look back upon two power sites owned by the company I represent, both of which were pre-empted by the government and flooded over by the Coulee reservoir. In fact, federal agencies had plans for most of the favorable power sites in the area, and assumed responsibility for the region's power supply.
To cite specific figures, I might mention that the winter of 1948 and 1949 promised an area power deficiency of 150,000 kilowatts. The shortage actually reached 300,000 kilowatts. A situation of this kind would naturally emphasize advantages of pool operation. To strengthen pool administration, we organized what is called the Northwest Utilities Conference Committee. This committee, organized on the administration level, deals only in matters of policy, and brings together for periodic discussion, representatives of federal, municipal, and private systems. The conference committee was organized in January, 1947. We gave immediate attention to such matters as new generating capacity on the Columbia River and its tributaries, appropriations from Congress, and transmission facilities that would be required in years ahead.
POOL BENEFITS Just as the power pool contributed to the war effort, so now in time of peace during periods of unavoidable emergency, the pool benefits the entire Northwest. The 300,000 kilowatt deficiency referred to was met with only minor inconvenience to the public. Not only this, but voluntary cooperation in the pool made available for area use some 600,000 additional kilowatts of electric energy. If the cost of hydro development by the federal government amounts to approximately $500 per kilowatt, and recent estimates have given this figure, then the pooled savings have - 40 - a cash value of $300,000,000, not to mention the port in this fight against socialism. Once this cost of expensive transmission lines and substations. Can anyone discover an easier way to save money?
For myself, I can see no possibility of permitting the Northwest Power Pool to fall apart. Even though generating capacity will be raised over the next few years to approximately 7,600,000 kilowatts, we cannot overlook the fact that the age of electricity has settled firmly upon us.
New uses will be constantly added. Hazards of drought, flood, and cold can never be eliminated.
It is quite possible that the demand for power will be a perpetual challenge to our supply. And, consequently, the need for cooperation in regard to all power resources can never be completely laid aside.
FEDERAL DICTATORSHIP NOT NEEDED The complexity of the situation is one reason why federal bureaus have wanted to step in and take a hand. It is one of their main arguments that central control is essential to area harmony.
Still, many of my acquaintances will agree that the sword of Damocles hanging over the head of private utilities can be postponed or eliminated by honest cooperation of all agencies in the area.
If we would prove to Congress and to voters that federal power dictatorship is not needed, it seems to me that voluntary cooperation is worth while.
Personally, I can see no reason why a few difficulties should make us inactive. It is far better to try with small success than not to try at all.
Progress in the electric industry has always faced difficulties. Perhaps it always will. It seems out of the question that huge multiplepurpose dams constructed by the government can be eliminated from the landscape. Nor should we expect municipalities, some of which have generated electric power dating back almost to Edison at Pearl Street, to relinquish interest in the electric business. These factors are here to stay. We must make the most of them.
INFORM THE PUBLIC Some people think it futile to expect cooperation from the leaders of government agencies who are determined to wipe out private enterprise in the power field. They tell us that the lion does not make peace with the lamb it intends to devour.
Yet, most of us agree that we fully intend to resist increased political domination. Not only do we expect to give continued good service, remain financially sound, and give more and more recognition to community obligations, but we intend to point out the benefits of our private enterprise system. In addition to sound operation, it is a serious obligation we face, this matter of adequately informing the public regarding our situation. The electric industry faces an equally serious responsibility to persuade other business firms, whether allied or independent, to lend suphas been accomplished, after the public is made to understand that security and prosperity flow from non-federal enterprise, rather than from planned economy, then and only then can private utilities expect a fair deal.
PROPER FEDERAL FUNCTIONS Some of my experiences in Northwest power operation have been vexing and painful. As a result of these experiences, I wholeheartedly agree that the federal government should limit its power development to special projects, and encourage private capital rather than dominate the field.
Generating plants, whether constructed under the guise of river control, irrigation, or something else, should be economically feasible. Certainly the distribution of power by government agencies should not extend beyond backbone transmission lines.
Nor can I see any reason why several million customers now served by private utilities, all good taxpayers, should be denied a fair and equal share of power generated at government plants.
It would be just as reasonable to make preference clauses regarding the use of highways, and give cars that may be owned by public agencies, the right-of-way at intersections, or prior claim to the highways. Our present laws favoring public groups should cause us to puzzle as to what constitutes citizenship in America. These laws, in a nation whose very foundation rests upon equality, should be reexamined and corrected.
"CHEAP" FEDERAL POWER The cost of power from a well-run public power project will be practically the same as the product from an equally-well-run company in private hands, except for the tax discrimination. These days the public simply does not get something for nothing. Yet by methods of accounting, government projects are made to appear less costly.
Profits invariably appear more favorable. Subsidies, interest, and tax exemptions -- all of which must be paid for by the taxpayer give federal power a most unreliable balance sheet, and a prestige that is misleading to say the least. To survive, private enterprise must have fair treatment.
-- The record of past achievement in the electric industry needs no apology. Should our customers examine the record of production, our wise use of resources, or this important matter of comfortable living, private enterprise wins by many lengths. The past is our reasonable guarantee of the future. America, and the private enterprise system will continue to be dynamic. We do not need planned economy.
COOPERATION ON BOTH SIDES However, there is one thing vitally needed, and - 41 - in closing, I would repeat it over and over again.
What we do need in this country is a clarification of federal attitude toward private enterprise, and, at this time, especially toward the electric industry. Personally, I believe that harmonious cooperation can be attained. I believe that the leaders in private enterprise desire to be both tolerant and fair. We are willing to meet government more than half way. All we ask is that certain minority groups in government cease this destructive campaign to wipe out private enterprise; that they think more of the general welfare and less of political control. If this is done, the road ahead can be made relatively simple and beneficial to all. An unwillingness on the part of either group can lead only to ultimate destruction of our present economy. I, for one, shall always be ready to work for any cooperation between government and private industry that promises harmony, improved standards of living, and preservation of that individual security which up to the present has made this nation great.
- 42 - NEEDED · A FEDERAL POWER POLICY by ERNEST L. WILKINSON ERNEST L. WILKINSON, a native of Utah, is a graduate of Brigham Young University and of George Washington University and Harvard Law Schools. After teaching law at the University of California and New Jersey Law School, he practiced law in New York City with the firm of Hughes, Schurman and Dwight of which Chief Justice Hughes was the senior partner. Since 1935 he has practiced law in Washington. His extensive practice includes suits against the government in the Court of Claims, acting as general counsel of the National Council of Farmer Cooperatives, and legal work for the National Grange.
(NOTE: Senator Arthur V. Watkins, of Utah, was on the program to discuss this subject, but at the last minute found that he had to be in Utah on the day of the meeting. He asked his friend, Mr. Wilkinson, who is also familiar with the water problems of the West, to substitute for him.) THE CHAIRMAN has alluded to the fact that I am a graduate of Harvard University Law School.
Whether in Washington that is a term of honor or dishonor depends entirely on the company one is in. The most recent story in Washington is that the way to get ahead in government is to go to Harvard, then come to Washington and turn left.
Somehow I didn't seem to be susceptible to this contagion for when I came to Washington I decided to turn right and go into private practice.
I state this at the outset because I want to make it plain that I am not a government employee, either on Senator Watkins' staff or otherwise.
Like most of you present, I am devoting most of my time and toil and effort to making enough to support the Government. Some cynic has recently remarked in Washington that the only persons not now working for the government are those employed by the government. I come to you as one who is in private industry, believes in private industry, and hopes that the disastrous experience of socialistic and communistic nations will soon point the way to a continuance, and, where needed, a restoration of private industry in this country.
THE WATKINS BILL The source material for my talk today consists of an address given in the Senate of the United States by Senator Watkins of Utah upon the introduction of S. 3376, a bill to provide for the organization of interstate water and power users' association for the purpose of entering into contracts for the repayment of the cost of water and power projects on interstate streams and for the ownership, operation and maintenance of such projects. I have also drawn from an article entitled, "New Developments in Valley Authorities," written for the Rocky Mountain Law Review by our distinguished Chairman, Governor Carr. What I say, however, I say on my own responsibility. That statement ought to give the Senator plenty of rope to slide out on if what I say is wrong.
"" The subject assigned is that of a national power policy. What I have to say would better be called, "A New Plan for the Operation and Ultimate Ownership of Water and Power Projects Where There Are Multiple Uses of Water. This includes hydro-electric power along with the other uses of water. Admittedly the other uses are of more importance in the western part of our country where the Anglo-Saxon institution of irrigation is a paramount part of the economy.
The principles underlying the plan of Senator Watkins of which I speak, however, are principles which may be adapted and applied elsewhere.
THE PROBLEM INVOLVED I think it would be well at the outset for me to very briefly state the problem involved. In the arid West, water is the very lifeblood of the nation's economy. Its most important function is for consumptive uses such as are involved in irrigation, municipal, mining, industrial and allied miscellaneous uses. The basic goal of all river development, therefore, at least in the West, is not merely the development and distribution of cheap hydro-electric power, but the widest expansion of the economy of which hydroelectric power may be only a part. As stated by Senator Watkins on the floor of the Senate at the time he introduced his bill, "An abundant supply of low-cost electric energy can be a great aid to growth, but without an adequate supply of water for consumptive use, it is valueless. There can be no progress without life-giving water." The basic policy of all reclamation and flood control programs therefore must be "to put to the fullest possible beneficial use every drop of water available for consumptive use. Irrigation, municipal, mining, industrial and miscellaneous demands must be satisfied if there is to be any further expansion in the West. Recognition - 43of this elementary truth has given rise to planning future projects by westerners in such a way that the consumptive use of water is given the first consideration. Everything else is secondary." "" As a part of that problem it should be noted that "the easy-to-build western reclamation flood control projects have already been constructed or are well on their way. From here on the reclamation and flood control program calls for the construction for the most part of giant multiple-purpose projects on the interstate streams of the arid West." The construction, operation, ownership and maintenance of these giant multiple-purpose projects necessarily encompasses the construction of two different types of projects. The development on any one interstate stream taken as a whole in and of itself should be self-liquidating; otherwise it should not be constructed. Many of the large subsidiary projects on any one stream will also be self-liquidating. But there will be numerous small irrigation and industrial water projects planned on the main streams and on the tributaries of some of the large interstate rivers which will not be self-liquidating. Their construction will therefore require the financial aid available from the large power revenues that will be obtained from other units of the particular basin development. It is the development of these small and somewhat localized projects which will encourage the development of new homes, new farms, new industries in short, which will bring new colonization and new development of the West. These new developments will in turn help create a market for power and thus assist in the success of the entire enterprise.
ALTERNATIVES -- Theoretically at least four alternative methods have been suggested for the construction of these giant multiple-purpose projects on large interstate streams.
PRIVATE ENTERPRISE The first is the suggestion of the rugged individualist that they should all be built, maintained, and operated for profit by private enterprise.
The size of large interstate streams however, generally makes it unrealistic for private capital to develop all the uses and power on these large interstate streams. Also the long period which it requires to pay off the original investment makes it particularly un-alluring to private enterprise as an investment field. As realists we must recognize this is all the more so in these days of high and often-times confiscatory taxes. I suppose therefore that in the present situation we can hardly look to private industry to construct large multiple-purpose projects on the large interstate streams in the West.
VALLEY AUTHORITIES The second alternative is the creation of regional governmental authorities with power to control river flows and river development along the general lines of the Tennessee Valley Authority. With the encouragement of the Chief Executive, the proponents of such legislation are looking hopefully toward the new Congress in 1951 with a determination to bring at least one such measure out of committee for discussion and vote on the floors of both Houses. It is generally believed that the Columbia Valley Authority Bill will be presented first and that the Missouri Valley Authority Bill will then be considered, providing, of course, the Columbia Valley Bill passes.
These regional authorities proceed on the theory of government construction, government operation, and government ownership.
These regional authorities, whether for good or evil, carry us further along the road of government ownership and regimentation. A comprehensive development of the Columbia River alone along the line of a regional authority would possibly make the United States the greatest utility owner and operator in the world with the exception of Russia. Developments on other river basins such as the Columbia are now being planned which would make immense additions to the power units under the control of the United States.
There are at least nine large power projects on the upper Colorado River alone. To these should be added the vast program of dams and power plants on the Missouri Valley river system. The construction, development and operation of these projects by the United States will of course go a long way toward nationalization of the power industry in the western part of the United States and eventually in the whole country. They will create, whether for good or bad, giant federal utility bureaucracies.
I need say nothing further about this alternative here because it has already been discussed.
AGENCY DEVELOPMENT A third alternative is contained in a provision of the omnibus Rivers and Harbors Bill which would develop the Columbia Valley with a Basin Fund controlled and expended by the Department of the Interior. This also means complete governmental control, operation and ownership with all of its attendant departures from our traditional concept of private enterprise.
- A NEW APPROACH THE WATKINS BILL The fourth alternative, which in these days of dependent governmental thinking is a new ap- - 44 - proach, is contained in the bill introduced by of irrigation projects were constructed in the Senator Watkins on April 10th of this year. Before giving an analysis of that proposed legislation, let me parenthetically state that Senator Watkins, who is a lawyer by profession (he wasn't trained at Harvard) has devoted most of his private legal practice to representing irrigation and power projects in the state of Utah and probably has had as much practical experience in the multiple use of water as anyone in the Congress.
The Watkins bill, instead of providing for government control and operation and permanent ownership, provides for the development of western river basins through the construction of multiple-purpose reclamation projects operated and controlled by the people living in the river basin rather than by the federal government or its agencies or bureaus. Further, the states, as entities, would not supplant the government agency or the proposed Authority. Instead the states and their citizens would be represented by interstate water and power users associations which would, in turn, contract with the Bureau of Reclamation for the construction of the projects.
Role of Reclamation Bureau The planning and construction of projects would remain in the Reclamation Bureau, but these would constitute the sole functions of that government agency. The operation of the projects, when completed, their maintenance, the sale of power and water rights, and the control of their finances, would be in the hands of the proposed new interstate agencies, the stockholders of which would be the local users of the water. This would recognize original reclamation law theory and provide "grass roots management." Senator Watkins' plan would make the interstate project self-liquidating through the sale of water and hydro-electric power. The government in the first instance would supply the cost of construction but the operation in each instance from the start would be by the interstate water and power users' associations and, upon payment back to the government of the cost of operation, equitable ownership would pass to the water users' association.
Local Ownership The Utah Senator demands a return to the basic policy established in the first Reclamation Act of 1902, which required landowners and water users to repay the costs of construction of projects on an amortized basis. When that was done, the operation, maintenance and the beneficial ownership came to those who made repayment of the costs. He points out that during the hurryup days of the depression, projects had to be started without contracts for the repayment of costs, in order to furnish employment. Under certain policies adopted at that time, a number West with no provision for the repayment of the costs or for the transfer of the operation and maintenance and equitable ownership to the water users when the income from rentals would equal the cost of construction. The Central Valley program in California is an example of this kind of a situation. In these projects there was employed a so-called contract for rental of water to the landowners and other water users.
There is no evidence of any intent ever to turn these projects over to the people in the areas who by their patronage will eventually pay for the cost of construction and distribution.
Many projects have already been constructed under the original Reclamation Act. Under the repayment contracts, these projects, soon after they were constructed and tested, were turned over to the water users to operate and maintain.
Some of them have power units, although at this stage of reclamation history the power units were incidental rather than the main part of the projects.
Many of these projects including incidental power plants are now in successful operation.
The only connection the Bureau has with these projects is to check occasionally to see that the management is protecting the physical features of the dams, reservoirs, power plants and other facilities. The government interest is that of a creditor who is entitled to see that security is protected. When the reimbursable costs are paid, the government has no further interest except to hold the legal title under the Reclamation law. The equitable title is in the water users.
Water User Associations In the first reclamation projects the government contracted directly with the individual land owners and water users. As the program grew, this proved to be very cumbersome. To remedy this situation irrigation districts and water user associations were organized to represent groups of land owners and water users in their dealings with the United States. Repayment contracts were signed by either the District or the Association in behalf of the individual water users.
The use of these entities has been extensive in the West. Their organization and their methods of operation are well known to western water users.
Precedents in other fields of legislation immediately come to mind. The federal land banks were originally chartered and created with government capital for the purpose of supplying needed credit to agriculture. The government originally put $9,000,000. in these banks, which was later increased to $125,000,000. The stock of these banks is owned by federal loan associations.
Their stock in turn is owned by farmer-stockholders who are the borrowers. This capital has now been completely repaid and they are now - 45 - operated by the stockholders with certain supervision by the government somewhat like the Federal Reserve System.
-- When the Grand Coulee Dam was constructed in Washington it was understood and contemplated, and national agricultural leaders were given the assurance by certain political leaders, that the underlying legislation would provide for the ultimate transfer of this dam to private ownership.
The legislation however never contained such a provision.
Reimbursable Costs With respect to the reimbursable costs, the measure does however provide that they shall be allocated among the various activities of the project "in such manner as may be necessary to encourage the widest possible use of water for irrigation purposes and to avoid the imposition upon water users of a share of the joint costs which is excessive in relation to the benefits therefrom.
"" The bill further recognizes the water rights now in existence by reason of existing river compacts to which the states as well as the federal government are parties thereto and also the existing water rights in the states.
Hydro Power With respect to power policy, the Watkins bill would empower such organizations to hold all power receipts in trust for the over-all basin development; for repayment to the federal government of principal and interest, and to provide lower power rates to the consuming public. Insofar as the sale and distribution of power produced by these projects is concerned, the new legislation would in no way change the priority benefits established in existing reclamation law.
In disposing of power produced, the bill provides that initial use shall be for the benefit of the basin development itself. Municipalities and other public corporations or agencies, cooperatives and other non-profit organizations financed in whole or in part by loans made pursuant to the Rural Electrification Act of 1936 as amended would have second preference on the power supply, the great bulk of which then would be sold to public and private power systems.
The bill also restricts basin power sales to the basin area, except that any surplus basin power can be made available to outside areas provided such outside areas are faced with a power shortage and such interchange will not adversely affect such area's power development program.
Revenues derived from such interstate basin corporations would be used to repay the cost of the projects, to provide maintenance and replacement charges and would be so allocated as to repay the costs of basin projects which in and of themselves are not financially feasible, but which the corporation deems desirable in the overall basin development.
Power rates would be fixed by the corporation in consultation with the Secretary of Interior and the Federal Power Commission and would be of sufficient amount to insure repayment of all costs, operating and maintenance charges.
Modus Operandi The method by which Senator Watkins proposes in his bill to accomplish local operation, control and ultimate ownership is so ably set forth by Governor Carr in an article which he has written for the Rocky Mountain Law Review that I can do no better than to quote in part from his summary of the provisions of the Watkins bill, One part of the Bill finds that the development of multiple-purpose reclamation and flood control projects are necessary on interstate streams in order to retain the maximum beneficial use of the waters; that the majority of such projects would include irrigation of lands and other consumptive uses as well as the generation and sale of electric energy to two or more states; and that such development is now impeded by the absence of agencies of the states which may contract with the United States for the construction and repayment of the costs and to provide for the future operation and maintenance of such interstate projects.
Interstate Associations It then authorizes the creation of "interstate corporations" with authority to contract with the federal government for the construction of the project, for the repayment of costs; when reimbursable costs have been repaid, equitable title to the project, the rights to permanent and exclusive use of the water diverted, to the⚫electric energy generated, and to the exclusive use of the proceeds from the sale thereof, will be vested in perpetuity in the interstate power and water users association. "The interstate association would be controlled by a Board of Directors consisting of not less than five nor more than twenty-one members to be elected by the shareholders. The United States will have a Director in the association as long as that body is indebted to the United States.
Members shall be residents of the states in the area to be served and each state shall have a right to elect at least one member of each Board.
"An interesting paragraph provides that the capital stock of the association shall be in such number of shares as may be specified in the Articles of Incorporation and shall be issued to conservancy districts, water users associations, - 46power companies or other organizations or enadvantages tities authorized by state law to subscribe for the stock. The stock shall be issued to the respective groups in proportion to the water rights they may hold in the interstate streams involved as determined by the laws of the states having jurisdiction thereof or by compacts between two or more states.
Power Revenue "Sections 12 and 13 of the proposed Act deal with the disposition of electric power, and recognize that the interstate associations shall take from power revenues and from assessments against actual water users, the funds needed for the payment of the reimbursable costs to the government. So-called public power features existing in the Reclamation laws are then restated with a provision which protects the rights of socalled priority holders of rights to purchase power from Federal Reclamation projects.
"Each River Basin is protected against competition in the sale of power by any other river agency within its borders. The association may not use power revenues for the payment of dividends to its stockholders. Three alternatives for the use and application of these moneys are provided in the Act, but the final disposition of funds after the United States has been repaid, should follow the line of the Upper River Compact on the Colorado, which provides for future agreements between the interested states.
State Compacts "The Bill encourages compacts between the states and calls for cooperation from the associations for the purpose of carrying out their provisions. It is also provided that the stated policies and the provisions of the act shall be applicable to intrastate projects.' "" Governor Carr concludes his summary of the Watkins bill with this statement: "Without being committed at this early date, it would appear that the aims of all interested parties might well be satisfied through the polishing and trimming of this proposed bill." Nature Magazine in a recent issue had this to say: "We are opposed to super-governments and dictatorial tendencies, whether regional or larger. At the same time we are convinced that the waste of natural resources, the bickerings of political jealousies dictated by the economic and biologic illogic of State boundaries are costly in money and in those resources. Some formula must be found - and soon to treat natural resource management on a broader and coordinated basis. It is in the interests of not only those in the region involved but of the whole people - who pay the bills to do so." - - · The Watkins bill, I submit is worthy of serious consideration as the correct answer. To those who believe in free enterprise and private industry, its advantages are manifest. In a field where government help has often been sought, it provides for the development of multiple-purpose projects on interstate streams by the use of government funds, but it provides for the repayment of these funds and the ultimate control and operation by the individual citizens who, through their industry and toil, will be patrons and users of the different projects in the different interstate streams. It is an encouragement to private ownership, to economy of operation, to local "grass roots" management instead of drifting with the tide and calling for a larger bureaucracy and more government domination. It calls for return of individual enterprise and reliance upon local initiative. Let no one believe that the western farmer, rancher, or metropolitan water user places so-called cheap power and water rates above freedom of enterprise and ultimate ownership and partnership in the projects we are building. The Watkins bill is more nearly consistent with their desires and with the genius of the Constitution as envisioned by our constitutional fathers who, if they were permitted to be acquainted with what is going on in this world, would be completely aghast at the way we are surrendering our liberties for the siren cry of government paternalism. George Washington, the father of our country, expressed the fear that our constitutional fathers had of being dominated by Government when he said, "Government is not reason, it is not eloquence . it is a force! Like fire, it is a dangerous servant and a fearful master.
"" -- The Watkins bill provides a way for the construction of multiple-use projects on our interstate streams but protects against the permanent encroachment of bureaucracy. It protects against the dangers of which the father of our country spoke and yet permits the use of government machinery and credit for the development of our natural resources. By its method, however, government is continued as the servant and not the master of our people.
- 47 - CHAMBER POLICY The roundtable symposium on CAN FEDERAL AND PRIVATE POWER LIVE TOGETHER, was presented as an educational feature at the 38th Annual Meeting of the Chamber of Commerce of the United States, to promote greater public interest and understanding on this most timely subject. As such, the speakers were free to express their views regardless of Chamber policy.
The Chamber's policy declarations on this and other natural resources subjects are published. Anyone interested may receive them by writing to the Natural Resources Department, Chamber of Commerce of the United States, Washington 6, D. C.

HARVARD UNIVERSITY BUREAU FOR RESEARCH IN MUNICIPAL GOVERNMENT "CENTRALIZED MUNICIPAL PURCHASING " Compiled by the CIVIC DEVELOPMENT DEPARTMENT This report is compiled in response to inquiries received from Member Organizations and is designed merely to present facts without advocacy COMMERCE OF THE C UNITED STATES OF AMERICA CHYBER OF COMMERCE OF THE FULLED 2LVES DA MOTOMIHCAW CLAIC DEAFFOLMEZI DELYKLHEAL enistupni od nanogers of bollaroc il Hoger ald ob i has anolitesing0 indigo bis Voorka wodtby use of les bougi CENTRALIZED KNICIPAL PURCHASING A Report Compiled by the CHAMBER OF COMMERCE OF TIL UILELE EZATER Civia Development Departs ent Jahn Ihlder, Manager Movement for Centralized Purchasing Many arguments have been advanced for centralised purchasi ng and the principle has been applied in connection with both state and city governments. The arguments favoring centralisation have been ably enumer ated by Benry Bruere* but practical difficulties in the way of introucing centralised purchasing methods have retarded the adoption of the plan.
In his book published in 1920 Professor Willie B. Muure writes: "the advantages of a complete central-purchasing policy govering all the needs of all departments, have not yet been demonstrated by the experience of any American city. Early Purchasing Experience The need for more efficient methods in goverment purchasing has long been realised. The first attempts at centralization of this work seems to have been «ivocsted in connection with purchasing done by various departments of state governments. This movement resulted, in several states, in the appointment of boards of control, sometimes called boards of affairs. In some instances boards of this kind have attempted to undertake the making of purchases for various state institutions and to keep appropriate accounts thereof. The boards in Minnesota, West Virginia and Oklahoma are examples of first attempts of this kind.
Centralised micipal Purskasing In connection with a recent study of government purchasing Arthur Thomas made a survey of the extent of centralised municipal purchasing and he reports that "of 113 cities from which statements have been received, or See The New City Goverment (New York, 1912) page 237 and "Cooperative Centralized Purchasing in the City of New York". Payor's Central Pur chasing Committee, New York, 1915, page 14.
See "Principles and Methods of Municipal Administration" By William B.
Munro (New York, Hacmillan, 1920) pages 24-25.
 See "Cyclopedia of American Goverment. A. G. Mclaughlin and A. B.
Hart, Riitors investigated 64 have centralized their purchasing in greater or less degree." Mr. Thomas enumerates eleven cities that have adopted centralised purchasing snd in eme instances the dates when the plan was authorized, as follows: New York City, 1915, Chicago, 1898, Minneapolis, 1911, Cleveland, 1913, Philadelphia, 1903, Los Angeles, 1915, Baltimore, Des Moines, Cincinnati, Milwaukee and St. Louis.
Centralized Purchasing in New York City Since 1907 the New York State Legislature had before it in one form or another a suggestion for establishing a Central Purchasing Department for New York City. The New York Charter Commission in 1909 prepared an Administrative Cođe, which was not passed by the legislature, which proposed a buresa ď supplies whose functions included the standardization of all city supplies and a comprehensive plan of central purchasing. In 1910 a seall commission on the standardization of supplies was appointed under the direction of The Board of Estimate and Apportionment. Three years later this commission was merged in a bureau of standardisation of supplies, which bureau, in turn, browse the sup plies division of the Board of Entimate's bureau of standards. Comptroller Prendergast in 1913 submitted a plan for a central purchasing department previding for a board of purchase to be composed of the mayor, comptroller and president of the Board of Aldermen, and two executives - a "general purchasing agent" and a "general storekeeper” – but this plan was never acted upon.
In November of 1914 Mayor Mitchel appointed a central purchasing committee to attempt the actual work of central purchasing for the departmenta imediately under his direction.** Some very constructive work was done by this Committee*** and in 1917 a step was taken toward centralizing, under the Committee, open market as well as contract purchasing.
Legislation for Central Purchasing There have been numerous references in governors' messages of recent years to the need for centralized state purchasing sperales. In 1919 the governors of five states - Arkansas, Colorado, Michigan, Montana, and Wyoming - recommended the creation of central state purchasing agencies. Legislation of this character has been collected by the Legislative Reference Section of the New York State Library, at Albany, and issued in the form of a typewritten "Digest of Laws on State Purchasing Departments" in December, 1921. There have deen one or more instances of legislation aiming at the creation of centralised purchasing departments for counties. Wisconsin has passed an "Act creating a central purchasing board in cities of the first class (Chapter 297, Public Laws 1917); New York has passed an "act to spend the general city law relating to the creation of purchasing departments and agencies" (Chapter 250, Laws 1921) and other states without doubt have followed this lead.
See "Principles of Goverment Purchasing" by Arthur G. Thomas. Published for the Institute of Government Research by Appleton, New York, 1919, Page 23, In 1915 the State legislature passed a measure providing for the creation of a centralised purchasing department for New York City but objections to the form provided resulted in its being vetoed by the Mayor.
See its report entitled "Cooperative Centralised Purchasing in the City of New York" (New York: Mayor's Central Purchasing Committee, 1915) from which Cities that have Centralized Purchasing In so far as the Civic Development Department has been able to undertain from available sources without undertaking a special investigation, the following cities have made some attempt to centralise the purchase of municipal supplies: Atlanta, Georgia Baltimore, Maryland Birmingham, Alabama Boston, Massachusetts Bridgeport, Connecticut Calgary, Canada Cedar Rapids, Iows Chicago, Illinois Cinaienati, Ohio Cleveland, Ohlo Columbus, Ohio Dayton, Ohio Denver, Colorado Des Moines, Insa Fort Worth, Texas Houston, Texas Indianapolis, Indians Kansas City, Missouri Los Angeles, California Memphis, Tennessee Milwaukee, Wisconsin Minneapolis, Minneso ta New York, New York Pasadena, California Philadelphia, Pennsylvania Pittsburgh, Pennsylvania Portland, Oregon Sacramento, California St. Louis, Missouri St. Paul, Minnesota San Diego, California San Francisco, California Waltham, Massachusetts Washington, D. (Copy) CHAMBER OF COMMERCE OF THE UNITED STATES Washington February 15, 1938.
Hon. Clarence F. Lea, Chairman, Committee on Interstate and Foreign Commerce, House of Representatives, Washington, D. C.
My dear Mr. Chairman: The membership of the Chamber of Commerce of the United States is strongly of the opinion that the train length bill now before your Committee is a most undesirable measure. Its passage would, in our judgment, be against the interests of safety of both railroad employees and the public and place a heavy burden upon the railroads and the shipping and traveling public. Reasons which lead to these conclusions are shown in our report, "Train Length", which I wish to submit for the record.
So many of the National Chamber's member organizations have expressed the desire to appear at this hearing that I understand it has been impracticable for your Committee to grant full hearing to all of them. As it is the member organizations which determine the National Chamber's policies, it seems appropriate at this hearing for us to request that whatever time you might be able to give us be accorded to them.
Besides those organizations whose witnesses are to appear before you, the Chamber has in the last few weeks heard from a large number reiterating their grave concern about this bill. I submit herewith a list of the organizations so expressing themselves and, without burdening the record with the large volume of resolutions and letters received, I should like to submit for your consideration the accompanying excerpts which will indicate some of the reasons why our members are so apprehensive about the bill.
I would add that, if there are any points in our report regarding which members of your Committer have questions, we should be glad to have a representative of the Chamber in attendance at the hearing respond to such inquiries.

Hearing on Train Length Bill S. 69 before House Committee on Interstate and Foreign Commerce, Feb. 16, 1938.
Mr. Crosser: The next witness is Colonel Barber.
STATEMENT OF MR. ALVIN B. BARBER, MANAGER OF THE TRANSPORTATION AND COMMUNICATION DEPARTMENT, CHAMBER OF COMMERCE OF THE UNITED STATES WASHINGTON, D. C.
Mr. Barber: Mr. Chairman and gentlemen of the Committee: Chairman Lea about three weeks ago told me that the Committee was receiving so many requests from chambers of commerce, boards of trade, associations of commerce, and such organizations, that he suggested the Chamber of Commerce of the United States might do something to get these representatives together and see if they could not condense their testimony a bit to save the time of the Committee, and especially, if possible, to avoid long recitals of the history of the case and other things with which the members of the Committee are thoroughly familiar.
The Chamber of Commerce of the United States in the last two months has had an enormous flood of correspondence from its members throughout the country regarding this bill. They could hardly believe the bill was being taken seriously, because it seemed so contrary to their understanding of economics of transportation. Now, however, they are beginning to feel that it may be a serious matter and, therefore, we have had a tidal wave of letters and telegrams, resolutions and protests of all sorts about it.
I wish to say, Mr. Chairman, that so far as I know there has not been, and I have not encountered any suggestions of, stimulation of that flood by the railroads.
Carrying out Chairman Lea's request, the clerk furnished us a list of the representatives of chambers of commerce who had applied for time before your committee. We wrote them as to the Chairman's attitude about this hearing, that he did not want a whole lot of duplication. In response we heard from a considerable number saying that they would be content with filing briefs. We heard from others saying that they wanted to be heard, and finally, this group simmered down to between fifteen and twenty whom we got together yesterday.
Now, we thought in order to have this perfectly straight and not have it thought to be a frame-up on the part of the Chamber of Commerce of the United States, we would suggest that these gentlemen organize themselves, which they have done. They elected Mr. Hochstedler of the Chicago Association of Commerce as their chairman and I understand that he will be the first witness for that group.
I would like, Mr. Chairman, with your permission, to read a brief letter from the president of the Chamber of Commerce of the United States, Mr.

-3 Davis, and to submit certain papers which are mentioned in the letter.
Mr. Crosser: Colonel Barber, before you proceed with that.
Mr. Barber: Yes, sir.
Mr. Crosser: You stated that there were fifteen or twenty of them and that they had selected a spokesman.
Mr. Barber: Yes, sir.
Mr. Crosser: Do you mean that all of those fifteen or twenty are to appear hore before the Committee?
Mr. Barber: Those fifteen or twenty, Mr. Chairman, are the ones who, you might say, have been by natural processes selected from perhaps one or two hundred as Mr. Davis' letter and attachments will show you. We have in the last few weeks, heard from several hundred and I have a large volume of resolutions which I would like, at the proper time, to file with the Committee, I am not asking you to print them in the record, but I would just like to file them so that you will know that they are on hand.
Mr. Crosser: How long do you expect this Committee to sit, with fifteen or twenty, or more, on one group?
Mr. Barber: Mr. Chairman, Mr. Hochstedler draws my attention to the fact that on this list of witnesses the time that each is expected to occupy is given and a total of 82 minutes is asked for according to the list.
Mr. Crosser: Mr. Barber: That is, if no questions are asked?
Well, for some of these gentlemen we have allowed some time for questioning, in making up this list and the estimate of time they will consume. At any rate, Mr. Chairman, the spirit of those gentlemen is in harmony with the desire of Chairman Lea and, I am sure, of your Committee. Our job yesterday was to coordinate their statements and get them boiled down. Yet they could not keep from boiling over a bit. They have some extraordinary testimony, because they know the subject. They are on the spot. They are the men who know about the transportation problem - who know the industries affected by it - who know how these industries are going to be damaged if this bill goes through.
Mr. Crosser: Very well.
Mr. Martin: Mr. Chairman.
Mr. Crosser: Mr. Martin would like to ask a question.
Mr. Martin: I think I will quit asking questions, but I would like to make one observation at this time. It looks to me that this bill is going to establish a record. Now, I think the worst thing that a committee of Congress could do would be to shut off witnesses and have people go away and say that a committee of Congress would not give them a hearing. I was even in favor of considering the Ludlow amendment. So, you see that I go the limit. I am in favor of considering an amendment that I was not in favor of.

: I had the clerk check up on the holding company bill. I think the holding company bill was by far the most important piece of legislation this Committee has considered in years. Yet, that only occupied thirteen days.
Now, we are on the nineteenth day of these hearings, and apparently we are not more than half through.
Mr. Bulwinkle: There is one difference.
With the holding company bill we did hold a great many afternoon sessions. If you take the hours, I imagine we were in session more hours on the holding company bill.
Mr. Martin: I do not know just how the clerk checked the time. He gave me a memorandum saying that it was thirteen days, and we have given this nineteen days, and we are about half way through.
It strikes me that this is more or less of a practical and technical question and that we can get most of our information from the railroad companies and the operators.
Mr. Crosser: You may proceed, Colonel Barber.
Mr. Withrow: Mr. Chairman.
Mr. Crosser: Mr. Withrow.
Mr. Withrow: I just had this one question to ask the Colonel. You said that in your experience throughout with this matter you have not run into anything which indicates that the railroads have in any way stimulated any of this opposition, or generated any opposition to the Train Length Bill.
Mr. Barber: That is true, sir.
Mr. Withrow: You of course have seen the advertisements that have been run by the railroads, the double ads, and a number of other double ads in magazines that are read by all of the people.
Mr. Barber: Yes, sir.
Mr. Withrow: Do you not feel as though probably those ads might have stimulated some of that opposition you have run into?
Mr. Barber: Yes, sir; but that is open and above board. I was referring to the kind of pressure that is talked of a good deal and commonly resented.
And, I may say, Mr. Chairman, that I have asked a good many people whether they have been under any pressure of that kind and the answer has uniformly been in the negative.
Mr. Withrow: You have not used any pressure in your headquarters?
Mr. Barber: No, sir. We have followed our usual procedure, which is that whenever a matter is up before the public, a matter of importance to business, we let our members know about it and they are just as free to come here and testify against legislation which the Chamber favors as they are to testify for it. We do not try to throttle our members in any way.

· 5Mr. Withrow: Colonel, have you give any consideration to investigating as to the amount of the cost of these advertisements, this propaganda; because it is that? It is legal and above board as you say, but it is propaganda effort toward the end that the railroad companies might well conserve not only their energies, but likewise save some of this money and divert it into other channels that might be reflected in more efficient services or might be reflected in reduced freight rates.
ying Mr. Barber: Mr. Chairman, if I were to express the attitude of business about the necessity for that kind of advertising, I think the unanimous judgment would be that this bill ought never to have come up, that the question before you ought never to have been raised and that the railroads ought not to be compelled to spend money for that advertising. This is true because this bill is so contrary to every transportation man's knowledge of the economics of transportation.
I am afraid I am getting a little bit enthusiastic, Mr. Chairman, but I would just like, if I may, to refer to Mr. Martin's comment that this is, so to speak, a private fight between the railroads and their employees. These gentlemen who are down here representing the business interests of the country do not look at it that way at all. We think this is most of all an issue which is of concern to business people and to the consumers of the country. I am very enthusiastic about this, Mr. Chairman, because it happens Mr. Pettengill: You are very "unenthusiastic"?
Mr. Barber: Yes, sir; because it so happens that I was thrown into transportation work before the World War as an officer of the Corps of Engineers and then, after the War, Mr. Paderewski got me to come up to Poland and help his people get their railroads and other transportation services on their feet. I was up there about three years and got in touch with all of the railroads of Central Europe. During the war in France I had been in touch with the French and British railroads, so feel I know some thing of this subject from a comparative viewpoint. I have been with the Chamber of Commerce of the United States now for fifteen years, and our job is to study and find out what is the best thing for the public, for the business public, the consumers and everyone else.
When I was in Poland, one of the things I was telling their railroad people all the time was "Increase the size of your equipment. It is the only way to get economical transportation." I even preached that idea to the Germans, who have followed it. I could go back to my records which I have at home, and show you a memorandum Imade on the subject in 1920. When I last saw the head of the equipment branch of the German railroads, about eight years ago, he told me that they had done those very things where they could increased the size of their power and their loads.
That is the way to economy. It is the way to get transportation to move things cheaply. I believe, from my studies of transportation in Europe as affecting the economic welfare of the countries, that one of the reasons that the United States is so far ahead is that we have this great efficiency in transportation and distribution as well as production. The automatic machines and other elements of mass production are important. But also, a very important, I should say essential, factor is our cheap mass transportation. As a transportation man- I have not worked for a railroad since I was a boy of seventeen or -6 eighteen years -- but as a transportation man I will say that any thing that cuts down the train loads is anathema from the viewpoint of the true interest of everyone the public, the railways, their employees and the consumers.
cannot be otherwise.

Mr. Martin: Mr. Chairman.
