In Mr. Barber: No, sir. We submitted the question involved in the Pettengill Bill to a referendum vote and the matter was taken with extreme seriousness on all sides. There was great controversy about it. Our by-laws require a twothirds vote on referendum to carry any proposal. In other words, if the vote is less than two-thirds but more than one-third our position remains neutral.
the referendum on the Long-and-Short-Haul Clause the vote fell short of the necessary two-thirds by seven votes out of, I think, about 1,600. It was a very close ballot and left the Chamber of Commerce of the United States uncommitted with reference to the Pettengill Bill.
Mr. Pettengill: The vote failed by a fraction of one per cent.
Mr. Barber: Yes, sir.
Mr. Pettengill: Of achieving the two-thirds necessary to bind the Chamber of Commerce.
Mr. Barber: Yes, sir.
Mr. Bulwinkle: I was against it, so, go ahead.
Mr. Barbers Mr. Chairman, I was referring to the safety aspect of this bill and to the fact that for some fourteen years I have been Director of the National Conference on Street and Highway Safety, a body which brought together all the organizations studying traffic safety. We took the grade crossing situation and all other matters of safety very seriously. When this bill first I -7 came up in this Congress the Chamber saw the safety aspects had to studied very carefully. The first thing we did in our office was to get the records of all the previous hearings on this subject and we laboriously read every thing that had been said in the hearings on train length bills right down through the years.
Mr. Mapes! Have you attended all of these hearings during the last six months we have had?
Mr. Barber: No, sir.
Mr. Mapes: Have you got an all year's job?
Mr. Barber: I do not quite understand.
Mr. Mapes: If you want a job for the year, I suggest that you might attend these hearings.
Then Mr. Barber: We started studying this train length bill to see what is there in the safety aspect of it. We studied through those old hearings and tried to find something that looked real as a safety argument for the bill.
we began to examine the Interstate Commerce Commission's accident statistics and studied them thoroughly. We finally came to the conclusion there was no safety argument for the bill there; that the evidence was all on the other side, from the safety aspect.
Mr. Bulwinkle: Then, having arrived at that conclusion, you have not taken up with the railroad companies and asked them not to put on extra excur sion trains on Sundays, where they run fifteen or twenty or more extra trains on a Sunday, on account of safety?
Mr. Barber: I think the answer to that question, Mr. Chairman, is that when you put on a large number of excursion trains on a particular line, of course you do the things necessary, because the obvious need is right there, but you cannot do the same things throughout the whole railroad system. There are more than 200,000 of these grade crossings. Nobody knows where a grade crossing accident will occur. It is like the lightning striking.
Now, Mr. Chairman, this letter is very brief and I would like to read it, if I may. It is addressed to Chairman Lea and signed by Mr. George H. Davis.
"CHAMBER OF COMMERCE OF THE UNITED STATES "Washington "February 15, 1938.
Hon. Clarence F. Lea, Chairman, "Committee on Interstate and Foreign Commerce, "House of Representatives, "Washington, D. C.
"My dear Mr. Chairman: "The membership of the Chamber of Commerce of the United States is strongly of the opinion that the train length bill before your Committee is a most undesirable measure. Its passage would, in our judgment, be against the interests of safety of both railroad employees and the public and place a heavy burden upon the railroads and the shipping and traveling public. Reasons which lead to these conclusions are shown in our report, "Train Length", which I wish to submit for the record.
"So many of the National Chamber's member organizations have expressed the desire to appear at this hearing that I understand it has been impracticable for your Committee to grant full hearing to all of them. As it is the member organizations which determine the National Chamber's policies, it seems appropriate at this hearing for us to request that whatever time you might be able to give us be accorded to them.
"Besides those organizations whose witnesses are to appear before you, the Chamber has in the last few weeks heard from a large number reiterating their grave concern about this bill. I submit herewith a list of the organizations so expressing themselves and, without burdening the record with the large volume of resolutions and letters received, I should like to submit for your consideration the accompanying excerpts which will indicate some of the reasons why our members are so apprehensive about the bill.
"I would add that, if there are any points in our report regarding which members of your Committee have questions, we should be glad to have a representative of the Chamber in attendance at the hearing respond to such inquiries.
"Very truly yours, "(signed) GEORGE H. DAVIS President." I would like to add, Mr. Chairman, that these resolutions and letters of protest are still coming in from member organizations. To bring the list up to date, a number more ought now to be added, and if it is in order, we will continue to send them up to the Committee, although I imagine you will not have time to read them.
I fear I did not carry out very well Mr. Davis' idea of leaving as much time as possible for the member organizations, but there were some points that I was diverted to by the questions from the members of the Committee.

, W. Va.
Francisco York, Train Length A BILL was passed by the United States Senate during the last session of Congress which would arbitrarily limit to 70 cars the length of freight trains operated in interstate commerce. This bill, S. 69, was transmitted to the House of Representatives and is now before the House Committee on Interstate and Foreign Commerce for consideration and report.
Many glaring errors as to essential facts will be found in the arguments which were advanced in connection with the passage of this bill by the Senate. These errors tend to obscure the real purpose of the bill, which is obviously to create superfluous jobs at the expense of the railroads, the shippers and the general public. They also tend to convey the impression, not supported by the facts, that the bill is a safety measure.
This report will point out some of those errors.
It will show that, instead of promoting safety, the bill if enacted into law would greatly increase accident hazards to both railroad employees and the public; and that, instead of involving little added expense to the railroads and being in the interests of efficiency, it would entail great expense and would impair the efficiency of the huge main-line movement of fuels, raw materials, manufactured goods, and farm and orchard products which underlies the economy of production and distribution, and the continuance of low prices to consumers.
Public Hearings No public hearings were held on this subject during the last session of Congress. Had there been, the testimony would undoubtedly have revealed the unsoundness of the arguments advanced by the proponents of the bill.
The Chairman of the House Committee on Interstate and Foreign Commerce has announced that hearings on the bill will be held early in the regular session of Congress beginning in January.

The official records of the Interstate Commerce Commission, however, show that serious accidents of this character, such as result in fatalities, are relatively few, and there is no indication that they are peculiar to long trains.
The Commission's reports reveal two groups of accidents involving fatalities to train-service personnel which are either directly attributed to surge and shock or which appear likely to involve that cause of accident. These two groups are shown in Columns 1 and 2 of Table II. Each group shows a consistent reduction in the past fifteen years from an average of 10 to 12 in the first three years to an average of three in the last three years of the period.
It is worthy of special notice that, according to the Commission's records, all three of the fatalities to road freight conductors or brakemen reported in 1936 as directly due to surge and shock, shown in Column 1 of Table II, occurred on trains of less than 20 cars.
In fact, there is no indication whatever that the accidents listed in either Column 1 or Column 2 were due to length of train. On the contrary, the remarkable reductions achieved in the periods while train lengths were increasing indicate inconsistent with great improvement in safety in so far as concerns such accidents.
Accidents to Train Personnel For comparison with the foregoing relatively small numbers of fatalities in surge and shock accidents, there is given in Column 3 of Table II the total number of fatalities to train personnel in all types of train and train-service accidents. These, it will be seen, are many times more numerous than those involving surge and shock. Many of these accidents are affected by the number of trains run and would be increased by enactment of the trainlength bill, because of the added number of trains that would be required on the rails. Some of the hazards would be increased in direct proportion to the added number of trains, and others even more.
For example, on a single-track railroad, a reduction of 50 per cent in the length of trains would double the number of trains run and would quadruple the number of meetings of trains.
There has been a pronounced decrease in the total fatalities to train personnel, as shown in Column 3 of the table, but they are still many times the aggregate of the possible surge and shock accidents shown in Columns 1 and 2. Even if all of the latter could be eliminated by train-length limitation (and there is no indication that this would be the case), any such saving would be overshadowed by the increase that would follow in the other types of accidents.
Accidents to Passengers The situation is similar with respect to casualties to passengers. There is no indication whatever that such casualties would be reduced by limitation of freight-train lengths, whereas, on the other hand, it is obvious that added hazards to passengers would be created by an additional number of freight trains on the rails.

The increased hazards to passengers as well as the increased hazards to train personnel that would result from multiplication of freight trains must be taken into account as a reason against the adoption of the train-length bill.
Grade-Crossing Accidents A still more serious reason against train-length limitation is found in the situation as to rail-highway grade-crossing accidents shown in Column 4 of Table II. Fatalities in such accidents in the past three years have averaged more than five times as numerous as the total to all train personnel, as shown in Column 3 of the same table, and still averaged nearly three hundred times as many as the aggregate of the possible surge and shock accidents shown in Columns 1 and 2.
Any substantial increase in number of trains moving over crossings would proportionately increase the hazard of collisions with highway vehicles, and would certainly result in additional crossing fatalities far beyond the total number due to surge and shock, whether in long or short trains.
Some have the impression that a remedy for the grade-crossing situation is at hand through the large-scale program of the federal government for grade-crossing elimination. The facts, however, are as follows: Since the first large-scale federal participation in this work in 1933, only 2,804 grade crossings had been eliminated or authorized for elimination under the federal program up to October 31, 1937; in addition, approximately 7,000 were eliminated during 1933-1936 by state and local authorities and the railroads, largely through abandonment of railroad branch lines or relocation of -9kable reduction in fatalities: accidents from an average: years 1922-1924 to an averag annum for the years 1934 alities still exceeds the to cidents to conductors ed hazards to passenge hazards to train persone multiplication of fre count as a reason agains ngth bill.
against train-length ation as to rail-highshown in Column 4 accidents in the past than five times a -ain personnel, a e table, and stil mes as many a and shock acc - of trains movately increase vehicles, and ing fatalities e and shock, dy for the rough the ment for ever, are -ral par4 grade zed for • Octoo were local bann of highways; and, on the other hand, 3,165 new crossings were opened. Consequently, on January 1, 1937, there remained on Class I railroads 232,902 grade crossings compared with 233,701 on January 1, 1926, and 237,183 on January 1, 1933.
While attention is being concentrated upon the most dangerous crossings, many still remain and will remain for years to come, even with the most active prosecution of the grade-crossing program, including both crossing elimination and protection of crossings by the most modern devices which have been widely installed during the past 15 years.
Meanwhile, traffic on the highways at grade crossings is constantly increasing, and the grade-crossing hazard will undoubtedly far overshadow the hazards of all other types of railroad accidents for a long time to come.
Total Effect of Train-Length Limit on Accidents The foregoing analysis of Interstate Commerce Commission statistics shows the fallacy of the claims made for the bill in the interest of safety.
The unsupported claim that the bill might eliminate a very few casualties is vastly outweighed by the certainty that it would greatly increase the total of casualties to trainmen, to passengers and to travellers upon the highways.
In further support of this conclusion is an impressive judicial finding on the subject in the decision of a three-judge federal court for the District of Nevada declaring unconstitutional a law limiting train lengths in that state. The decision turned on the question of safety, and the court said: "A careful review of all the evidence warrants the conclusion that from the standpoint of safety to the public; to travelers on railroads; and to railroad employees, the Nevada Train-Limit Law bears no reasonable relation to safety but if enforced would impair and lessen the safety of plaintiffs' present method of freight train operation in Nevada." and further: "The frequency of train and train-service accidents is directly related to the number of train units operated and when more train units are run than are necessary to handle a given amount of traffic the hazard of accidents in handling such traffic is correspondingly increased." Accident Reduction Methods Notwithstanding the notable improvement which has taken place, the railroad accident situation should not be regarded with complacency. On the contrary, all accident reduction measures should and must be continued.
In this connection it must be borne in mind that during the period of increasing train lengths there was consistent improvement in locomotive and car design, track, signals and details of equipment, including adoption of safety devices of many kinds.
It is true that, despite large recent expenditures, the railroads are still behind in their programs for replacements, which were forced almost to a standstill when railroad traffic and earnings reached the low point of the depression. However, any arbitrary increases in expense such as those involved in the train-length bill would further delay replacement and betterment programs-in other words, they would tend to defeat the purpose of continuous improvement of track and equipment standards.
ECONOMIC ASPECTS THE safety aspects, as shown above, are alone sufficient to warrant strong public disapproval of the train-length bill. The economic aspects, however, are also very serious and require careful consideration, as adoption of the bill would inevitably result in higher transportation costs and inferior service, to the detriment of all concerned-the general public, the shippers, the railroads and their employees.
The consequences in increased costs of railroad transportation would be far more serious than is generally realized. Estimates that have been quoted in the press cover only the expense of running the additional trains. To this should be added -9- .
train units are run than at given amount of traffic the handling such traffic is corre ethods able improvement which road accident situation complacency. On the ction measures should De borne in mind that g train lengths there - locomotive and car tails of equipment, ices of many kinds.

The added costs could be met, if at all, only by higher freight rates.
Railway management has accomplished much since the World War in decreasing unit transportation costs by increasing the productivity of plant and personnel, a large part of the eight-and-a-half billion dollars of new capital expenditures on the railroads in the past fifteen years having been for this purpose-thus making possible the widespread reductions of freight rates which have taken place during a period of rising labor and material costs.
The train-length bill would not only arbitrarily bar the way to further progress in the same directions but would do away with much of what has already been accomplished, imposing a serious burden upon the public.
The basis for the foregoing statements dealing with the economic questions raised by the proposal for arbitrary limitation of train lengths will now be briefly analyzed.
Importance of Long Trains The latest available figures, for the year 1934, show that 44.7 per cent of the total railway freight traffic, measured by car-miles, was moved in trains of more than 70 cars. Furthermore, if the undoubtedly heavier loading of cars on the longer trains were taken into account, it would appear that more than half of the total rail freight movement, expressed in ton-miles, is performed in trains exceeding 70 cars in length.
While most branch-line trains are less than 70 cars in length and would not be affected by the bill, a large proportion of the through trains on the main trunk lines would be affected. There are relatively few short through trains on many such lines, and, if the long trains were required to be cut down to 70 cars, the excess cars would generally have to be handled by making up additional trains with obviously great increase in costs.
The most profitable part of railroad operations, which enables the railroads to maintain service on their whole systems, lies largely in the long hauls of heavy freight trains on their main lines. A large percentage of the branch lines are not in themselves profitable, but their maintenance is economically justified as feeders to the main lines, and they are of inestimable value to the communities they serve.
To limit the length of trains on the main lines, therefore, is to strike at the heart of the opportunity of the railroads to continue the large-scale low-cost transportation service in which they far surpass any other railroads in the world and upon which has been built up the great mass production and distribution of goods in the United States, to the benefit of both producers and consumers.
Cost of Train-Length Limitation Considering, first, the out-of-pocket cost, the 70car limit would necessitate a large number of additional trains. Each additional train would require an additional crew. The net result would be a large increase in the number of employees in train service. The total increase in wages, together with the additional cost of locomotive fuel, lubricants and water, cost of additional locomotive repairs and other costs of like nature, is estimated at much more than a hundred million dollars per year.
Secondly, the increased number of trains would necessitate large immediate capital expenditures.
Additional locomotives would be required even if present powerful locomotives designed for long trains were kept in wasteful service to haul the shorter trains. More trains would require more passing sidings, additional main tracks and new or extended signal systems. Many classification yards.
en if long the more w or ards ber of long trains would have to be redesigned, with more tracks, to handle the larger number of shorter trains. In some cases remodeling of existing yards would not be feasible and new yards would have to be built in other locations. These additions and changes of plant and equipment would necessitate very large capital expenditures, the annual charges.
on which would have to be met from earnings.
But to these two obvious items must be added a third type of expense. This would result from operation of the shorter trains with locomotives designed to handle much heavier trains; maintenance of long sidings and heavier structures built to accommodate longer trains and heavier equipment; and impairment of operating efficiency and economy which has been developed through use of longer trains. To limit trains to 70 cars would be to return in many respects to equipment standards and operating methods of 1922 or earlier.
Taking all three of these types of expense into consideration, while no exact estimate is possible, it is apparent that the cost to the railroads and the public of a 70-car length limit would be several hundred million dollars per year.
Effect on Freight Rates The large increase in railway operating expenses resulting from recent rises in wages and higher costs of materials have already brought about an increase in freight rates and an application for another blanket increase. The great direct and indirect additions to railway costs which train-length limitation would impose would further aggravate the situation.
There is, of course, a limit beyond which rates cannot be raised without diverting traffic to other carriers or suppressing it altogether, and this fact emphasizes the unwisdom of the train-length bill from the viewpoint of the public, the shippers, the railroads and their employees.

I th dorm as shivaol to hobi elvad M Jade einlog ende 0101 oreris tud Gefmaro odt to azeda tamorfos Responsibility of Management As has been shown, the use of longer trains is desirable from both safety and economic viewpoints.
Obviously, however, the most effective length of train varies according to the volume and character of traffic, and the physical characteristics of different railroads and different parts of the same railroad.
Such a restriction as is proposed in the trainlength bill would apply to level and mountain territories alike, and on types of traffic which need not be handled so expeditiously, as well as those requiring speedy movement. It would be a universal hindrance to low-cost, efficient movement of long trains of loaded cars containing classes of traffic not requiring exceedingly fast schedules, and similarly it would bar economical movement of long trains of empty cars, which are practicable in almost every type of territory.
Determination of train lengths best suited for particular conditions is thus a technical operating problem, and is a proper function of railroad management.

VIRGINIA PENINSULA ASSOCIATION OF COMMERCE, NEWPORT NEWS, VA.
***Enactment of the bill would impose upon railroads excessive expense of operation and introduce into the railroads problems, increased hazards of accident, and danger not justified in any way by the alleged advantages that might accrue from such a law.*** We oppose the enactment of this legislation as contrary to logic, reason, necessity, and the best interest of the citizens.
CHAMBER OF COMMERCE, AMSTERDAM, N. Y.
***The addition of 60,000,000 unnecessary train-miles could mean only one thing, namely, about 400 more casualties per year at grade crossings. The usual ratio of fatalities to non-fatal injuries is about one out of four, so we could expect at least 100 additional fatalities. Compare this with the three fatalities in slack-surge accidents in 1935.
ASSOCIATION OF COMMERCE, NEW ORLEANS, LA.
The records of the railroad companies and the Interstate Commerce Commission thoroughly establish the fact that longer trains are productive of fewer accidents than shorter trains and more of them.
CHAMBER OF COMMERCE, RICHMOND, VA.
***We regard it as being extremely dangerous to the safety of the American railroads, and a measure which would, if passed burden financially the carriers, the shippers and the public in general.
CHAMBER OF COMMERCE, TERRE HAUTE, IND.
***Statistics for 1936 show that there was an average of 225,167 railroad employees on Class I railroads in train and engine service who could possibly benefit through enactment of such a law, while safety of more than 700,000 other railroad employees would be adversely affected thereby in view of additional train units operating over the railroads and creating additional hazards to their employment. The additional train units would furthermore prove hazardous to the general public, particularly, at grade crossings.

-16..
SSOCIATION OF COMMERCE, SAINT PAUL, MINN.
It is a well known fact that the railroads in this Northwest territory ave been constructed to handle long trains, especially those in excess of 70 ars, and that the operation of these long trains has been an important factor in eeping the costs of operation down to a minimum. If this bill is passed it will ean a substantial increase in the cost of railroad operation and inevitably esult in increased freight rates and charges, which the public in our judgment s not in a position to pay.
HAMBER OF COMMERCE, LAWRENCE, MASS.
Business interests of Lawrence are unalterably opposed to the train .ength bill. The resulting increased cost of operation added to burdens of aleady over-regulated railroads might well be disastrous.
ASSOCIATION OF COMMERCE, NORFOLK, VA.
***It would appreciably delay, and preclude until remedied, the general stabilization so greatly needed by this nation and its business activities.
Proper and safe train lengths undoubtedly can be determined best by railroad management in keeping with the exigencies of territories and conditions. The disastrous effect and destructive character of this unwise legislation would be universal; it would disrupt transportation to the great detriment of production and distribution. The railroads are the lifeblood of a transportation service absolutely essential to the economic conduct of the nation's business, and such an imposition as this unsound legislation would react to the great detriment of all major fundamentals involved.
CHAMBER OF COMMERCE, JAMESTOWN, N. Y.
The safety argument of this bill is not upheld by experience or facts.
Instead of increasing safety the net effect would be to decrease it, not only for railroad employees but also the public.*** In recent years millions of dollars have been expended in installing heavier rails and bridges, elimination of curves and grades and more powerful engines to increase operating efficiency by hauling longer trains. Is all of this to be wasted by the passage of a bill without merit from any angle? Neither the railroads, industry nor the public are in any position to assume the added burden.
CHAMBER OF COMMERCE, ROANOKE, VA.
***If the proposed legislation becomes law, it will add tremendously to the cost of transportation, and furthermore it will tremendously increase traffic accidents at grade crossings. Such restriction of the operation of trains as the legislation proposes is an abrupt halt to progress and, in fact, will turn the nation backward with respect to transportation.

***Proponents of this bill hold it justifiable solely as a safety measure, while an analysis of the reports furnished by the Interstate Commerce Commission conclusively prove the fallacy of such claims.
CHAMBER OF COMMERCE, PANAMA CITY, FLA.
***Because the railroads of the nation have been and are now distressed financially, the passage of the bill would contribute only additional strife and turmoil in the field of transportation.
CHAMBER OF COMMERCE, CHARLESTON, W. VA.
The safety of the general public obviously could not be increased by adding substantially to the number of freight trains in operation which would be the necessary result of such an act. Such train limitation would add millions of dollars to the operating costs of railroads that could be made up only by increased freight rates which, in turn, would have to be absorbed by the consumers in increased commodity costs or by the shipper, or by both.
CHAMBER OF COMMERCE, GALVESTON, TEXAS ***With the increase in the number of automobiles and the increase in the number of freight trains that would have to be cperated under the proposed bill, no one can dispute the fact that the hazard to the public would be greatly increased.
Therefore, we can not lead ourselves to believe that "safety," in any measure, justifies the bill.
CHAMBER OF COMMERCE, EVANSVILLE, IND.
***General public safety, efficient prompt handling of loaded cars, · and the very existence of the railroads would be impaired by this measure.
CHAMBER OF COMMERCE, DANVILLE, VA.
From our investigation of this bill, we believe that its passage would operate against the public interest as it will not tend to benefit the shippers, will add an additional burden to the present difficulties of the railroads and in the long run will prove of very doubtful benefit to labor.
CHAMBER OF COMMERCE, VINCENNES, IND.
We are of the opinion that the railroads of our country have already been placed in a position that will require an increase in traffic rates in order for them to survive.
It is therefore unjust to place an additional burden on them under these circumstances.

***Instead of bettering conditions from a safety standpoint, our tudy of the situation discloses the fact that it would actually increase he operating hazards of the carriers.*** HAMBER OF COMMERCE, CORPUS CHRISTI, TEXAS.
We believe that this bill should be defeated, for the reason that it ill increase the cost of railroad transportation, and that it is unsound in >rinciple. We believe that it is an unwarranted interference with the manage lent of the nation's rail system. It is uneconomical and wholly unjustifiable.
HAMBER OF COMMERCE, DENVER, COLO.
***It appears that this legislation is distinctly of the "make work" type, and in no manner can be considered as bringing about a greater degree of safety to trainmen that now exists.*** The passage of this legislation would nean prohibitive added costs to railroad operations.
BOARD OF COMMERCE, PARKERSBURG, W. VA.
Our study convinces us that it would not have the effect claimed for it of preventing accidents, but that it would work an unwarranted hardship upon the railroads of the nation, which are, at this time, bearing a burden which is seriously affecting their financial positions.
CHAMBER OF COMMERCE, AKRON, OHIO.
Compared to all other countries, this country has been outstanding in developing long distance mass transportation at low rates. Large units, heavy power plants have produced this result. This bill reverses this trend.
CHAMBER OF COMMERCE, YAKIMA, WASH.
Regardless of other methods of transportation that have been developed in the last ten or fifteen years, the railroads remain a most integral part of our transportation system and the fundamental basis for determining transportation charges. In view of this fact the agricultural producers of this area cannot withstand the financial burden which would be placed upon their shoulders as shippers should the bill be passed by Congress and the railroads compelled to limit their freight trains to seventy cars in length.
CHAMBER OF COMMERCE, NEW HAVEN, CONN.
It is primarily a make-work bill, which, although it might temporarily increase employment due to the necessity of the railroads running additional trains, would, by reducing railroad earning power, ultimately prove detrimental to the whole body of railroad employees.

- 19.- NATIONAL FOREIGN TRADE COUNCIL, INC., NEW YORK, N. Y.
We strongly feel that the passage of a law limiting to seventy cars the length of freight trains operated in interstate commerce would so tend to increase railroad rates as to throw out of balance many of the competitive prices of American products which enable our producers and manufacturers to secure business in foreign markets.
CHAMBER OF COMMERCE, COFFEYVILLE, KANS.
***We are opposed, on general principles, to so much interference to the details of railroad operation. We believe that the railroads should be al lowed to plan their operation on the basis of efficiency, because efficiency in railroad service is the best way to serve the public as a whole.

- Statement of William H. Day, Manager, Transportation Bureau, Boston Chamber of Commerce Mr. Chairman and gentlemen: My name is William H. Day. My experience in the field of transportation covers a period of approximately 35 years. Since 1924 I have been employed as manager of the Transportation Bureau of the Boston Chamber of Commerce.
In an effort to conserve the time of you gentlemen and avoid duplication of testimony. I have been authorized to speak for the New England Shippers Advisory Board, the New England Traffic League, the New England Paper and Pulp Traffic Association, the Associated Industries of Massachusetts, the Manufacturers Association of Connecticut, Inc., the New Hampshire Manufacturers Association, the Boston Chamber of Commerce, the New Bedford, Mass., Board of Commerce, and the Fall River, Mass., Chamber of Commerce, which organizations reprosent in a substantial way the business life of New England, including many thousands of shippers and receivers of freight.
We appear in opposition to S. 69 and any similar lcgislation.
New England is the home of more than 16,000 industries, situated as they are, geographically, in the extreme northeastern section of the country, far away from the sources of their raw materials and many of the foremost consuming markets. Good transportation service at a price that will permit the products to properly move is so essential to their success that they consider it neces sary to actively interest themselves in any proposal offering indications of so undermining the financial structure of the rail carriers serving them as to prohibit a continuation of services at rates now enjoyed.
Mr. Chairman and gentlemen, those I speak for are fearful that the passage of the bill here at issue will have that effect.
Twenty years ago some of our New England railroads thought they were doing a pretty good job when they moved a west-bound carload of freight from an interior New England point to the various Hudson River gateways at the western boundary of New England, a distance of some 200 miles, in 2 days, and I am inclined to believe that they did, considering what they then had to do with.
Now, the rail carriers are subject to criticism when they fail to move such traffic into the middle wost, an average haul of a thousand miles, in approximately the same time.
During the past 10 or 15 years our New England railroads have so speeded up long-haul traffic that today it is not uncommon to find potatoes produced in Aroostook County, Maine, reaching the Boston market, a distance of some 350 miles, quicker than the order of notification or bills of lading necessary to establish ownership in Boston via United States mails.
Now, what has made this service possible? My answer is the greatly improved motive power of today which is able to haul bigger loads, longer distances, more - 21 - quickly, safely, and economically, than the motive power of yesterday could haul Lighter trains shorter distances, and we must not forget that as a prerequisite to this forward step our railroads were obliged to spend hundreds of millions of dollars improving their facilities and equipment.
However, this combination of events has resulted in the best of freight service we ever enjoyed. It has enabled our industries to expand their fields of endeavor and better meet the hand-to-mouth buying methods of today, to the mutual advantage of established business and the railroads.
Naturally we view with a lot of apprehension any possibility of so undermining existing practices as to adversely affect business.
In the early stages of this hearing I heard some of those representing labor express the opinion that the longer trains of today cause unnecessary delay in the handling of freight traffic at terminals and that this practice had reacted to the disadvantage of shippers and receivers of freight. I wish to state, in no uncertain terms, that this statement is absolutely contrary to our experience over a period of years. As a matter of fact terminal delays are now so few and far between that it is becoming increasingly difficult to find them, insofar as long-haul, carload traffic is concerned.
In this connection, it is interesting to observe that considering the railroads of the country as a whole, in the year 1926 some $3,380,936 or 8.8 percent of all freight claims paid was charged to damage traceable to delays while in 1936 this element of damage was reduced to $796,274 or 3.8 percent of all freight claims paid.
As illustrative of the New England situation, in 1926, 6 percent of all of the damage claims paid by the Boston Maine Railroad were traceable to delays, while in 1936 this ratio was reduced to 1.02 percent.
In our opinion this remarkable improvement, both in a sectional and national way is traceable in a substantial degree to the increased efficiency made possible by the train service of today.
Further, I heard representatives of labor express the opinion that the slack produced by the longer trains of today has so contributed to rough handling as to increase losses and damage claims. Again, Mr. Chairman and gentlemen, I want to say that this is contrary to the actual experience of the many thousands of shippers and receivers of freight that I represent; contrary to the facts developed by the railroads through the use of impact recorders, and contrary to the actual claim records of the railroads.
Mr. Boren: Mr. Mr. Bulwinkle: Mr. Boren.
Mr. Boren: Mr. Day, what do you refer to as impact recorders?
Mr. Day: Impact recorders, Mr. Congressman, are a clock device which the railroads or shipper can install in a car in such a way that no one will have any idea it is there. When the car commences to move, the clocking device commences to work and a reel of paper commences to unfold recording, if you please, from the time the car starts until it reaches its destination, through a series of - lines whether or not any rough handling has occurred, and if it has occurred the time will show when it occurred, and that time when checked with the train sheets permits the railroads to determine just where, if you please, the rough handling recorded occurred.
Mr. Boren: You mean then that the machine or the device would record the effect of slack action?
Mr. Day: If as a result of slack action there was rough handling as has been claimed and that rough handling was to an extent as to actually cause damage, the recorder would show it absolutely. There is no question about it.
Mr. Boren: In order that I may understand that clearly, do you refer to rough handling of the car itself or rough handling of some particular box or package of goods in the car?
Mr. Day: I refer to rough handling of the car itself from the time the car starts in motion with one of these devices installed. They commence to work with a series of lines as the paper unfolds. If it jumps into what is known in railroad circles as columns 1 and 2, it is realized that the jar was sufficiently slight as to cause no damage to an extent that would hurt the freight. If perchange the line jumps into column 3, it is recognized that the jolt was of an extent that would reasonably cause damage. Then the railroads immediately see what time that particular jolt occurred and if the time recorded on the sheet is 3:30 p.m., then they take the train sheets and check them to see where, if you please, the train was at 3:30 p.m., and there is the place where the jolt occurred and they can definitely determine whether it was at a station, in a yard, or in roal haul service.
Now, in New England there has been a great deal of missionary work done by the railroads with those devices, particularly on the lines of the Boston and Maine and the Main Central Railroads. Since the matter of long trains became an active issue, particular attention for a period of time was paid to that character of service, and these devices were installed in cars so as to definitely tell them just what the experience was. I believe that for several years the Boston Maine used those devices for an average of between 300,000 and 400,000 miles of road service each year in an effort to satisfy themselves.
just what the actual circumstances were.
Mr. Boren: You indicated here that the railroads have a device that might clearly show the difference between the shock effect of slack action as botwcen long and short trains, if a sufficient number of cars were in the train.
Mr. Day: Well, that is not exactly what I am undertaking to say, Mr.
Congressman. I am undertaking to tell you that the impact recorder, as I understand it, when installed in any car, whether it be on a short train or a long train, will definitely show the railroads upon completion of the haul whether or not there was anywhere in the transit rough handling, and if there was rough handling they can definitely check where that rough handling occurred.
Now, that is all I am undertaking to say.
Mr. Boren: But would you not agree that if that machine or device works as you say it does work that you could put such a device in the rear car of a 70-car train across Arizona and make a graph from the shock effect there, and then put such a device in a long train, one of these through trains we have heard : - -ND 30 much about here, and record on another graph a comparative illustration of exactly what the effect is as between a 70-car and 150-car train?
Mr. Day: Mr. Congressman, I have on occasions conferred with railroad nanagement on the matter of rough handling in an effort to settle questions that shippers were interested in. These recorders have been installed, and I have been furnished and have in my office at the present time, for instance, an actual check showing every thing which happened from the time a car left its original point until it reached its destination. Now, that can be installed in any sort of a freight car on any sort of a train; and my understanding is that it will record the sort of story that I have unfolded to you and in fool-proof fashion.
I further understand that in some sections of the country today shippers who have been experiencing difficulties in the movement of some particular kind of traffic where there has been some damage occurring are anxious to find out the whys and where fores surrounding them, and they, like the railroads, now frequently take these devices and install them in cars where they are experiencing difficulty, even unbeknown to the railroads, and then when the car reaches its destination they take out the long roll of paper and commence to unfold it.
If there appears on there any indication of rough handling they check the time and then go to the railroads and see where that car was at that particular time, and in that way they get their information.
Mr. Crosser (presiding): Are there any further questions?
Mr. Martin: Just one question, Mr. Chairman.
Mr. Crosser: Mr. Martin.
Mr. Martin: That shock-recording device works on the same mechanical principle as speed recorders and many other devices, such as those that register vibration, and so forth.
Mr. Day: That is true.
Mr. Chairman and gentlemen, this bill here before you is being urged as a safety measure. Naturally, the various organizations that I appear for favor safeguarding both the public and the railroad employees to the fullest possible extent; but in view of the perfectly obvious fact that the more trains there are to handle a given amount of business, the more opportunities there will be for accidents, bath to the public and to the railroad employees as a whole, plus the fact that the records of the Interstate Commerce Commission made public by its Bureau of Statistics show very clearly that during the period since the World War, when the average length of trains increased substantially, and the use of so-called longer trains became more and more prevalent, there was a striking improvement in the safety records of the railroads with respect to trains and train-service employees, as well as with respect to employees and the public generally, there is every indication that the real purpose of this bill is to create additional and unnecessary jobs at an expense that will unduly burden our railroads and, perhaps, result in the deterioration of serv ice now enjoyed and which the public can ill afford to lose.
Mr. Bulwinkle: Mr. Chairman.
Mr. Crosser: Mr. Bulwinkle.

- Mr. Bulwinkle: How many extra jobs will it create?
Mr. Day: I would not care to attempt to answer that question.
Mr. Bulwinkle: You made the statement.
Mr. Day: If you willmr. Bulwinkle (interposing): You made the statement it would create extra jobs.
Mr. Day: That is right; but you asked me how many it will create.
Mr. Bulwinkle: create?
Now, how many extra, according to your statement will it Mr. Day: I would only undertake, Mr. Congressman, to answer that in this way: If today, as illustrative, between Boston and the Hudson River gateways at the western boundary of New England, a given railroad is operating six trains, we will say each handling an average of 140 cars--was fou Mr. Bulwinkle (interposing): Well, do they now?
Mr. Day: I would not say that they would average that number; but taking that particular run, long haul west-bound and east-bound freight, coming from your trunk-line railroads to your New England terminal, Boston, the habit is to run very, very heavy trains. I would say that when business is normal such trains would seldom operate with less than 100 cars.
Mr. Bulwinkle: Do they ever reach as many as 140 cars?
Mr. Day: Yes, sir. I have seen them have more.
Mr. Bulwinkle' How many more?
Mr. Day: Oh, I have seen them operating 145, 150, and 152.
Now, if as the result of the passing of this bill the limit of such trains is restricted to 70 cars, then obviously there must be two trains at least to do the work that one is now doing and that means additional engine crews, additional employees at terminals, and possibly to other extents.
That is a question that the railroads can far better answer as to the exact change that will be brought about; but it seems to me elementary that if you have to have two trains to do the work of one you are going to have twice as many employees.
Mr. Bulwinkle: You are speaking now for all New England?
Mr. Day: That is true.
Mr. Bulwinkle: In your statement here this morning you are speaking for all New England?
Mr. Day: New England shippers.

- 25 · Mr. Bulwinkle: And you, of course, know the facts so far as the New England shippers and the railroads are concerned.
Now, how many extra trains would it require in New England?
Mr. Day: I would not dare answer that question.
Mr. Bulwinkle : How many extra employees would be required in New England; not on just one road going into Boston, but on all roads?
Mr. Day: I would not dare answer that question. I would not attempt to, Mr. Congressman, I would say probably there are in the neighborhood of 20 percent of the trains operated in New England, freight trains, that would be adversely affected by any such limitation as is proposed.
Mr. Bulwinkle: In your opinion it would be then increased about one-fifth.
Mr. Day: That is right.
Mr. Bulwinkle: In the number of trains.
Mr. Day: That is right.
Mr. Bulwinkle: You may proceed.
Mr. Day: It is unnecessary for me to remind you gentlemen of the serious financial conditions of our railroads. The only thing they have to sell is transportation and any increase of their operating costs must be reflected in the prices they charge for services rendered. However, as a practical matter, this is much easier said than done, because they are confronted on all sides by most active competition and they have learned by bitter experience that high freight rates not only stimulate the diversion of traffic but also localize business to the detriment of established industry as well as themselves.
Mr. Bulwinkle: Is your organization protosting against the increase in freight rates?
Mr. Day: No sir.
Mr. Bulwinkle: Are you approving of it?
Mr. Day: We have taken this position: We have felt, and still feel, very strongly, that in many instances railroad freight rates have already reached the ceiling of reaso ableness, and consequently there has been considerable doubt in our minds as to whether or not the increase which the railroads are now seeking would accomplish the desired purpose. However, we realized that they were confronted with a real urgency. It seemed to us that they had to obtain additional money and obtain it quickly. It seemed to us that the only way open to them at the moment was through an increase of freight rates and consquently we set aside our own ideas and in the proceeding to which you have referred appeared before the Interstate Commerce Commission declaring ourselves along the lines I have to you.
Mr. Martin; Mr. Chairman.
Mr. Crosser: Mr. Martin.

- - 26.
Mr. Martin: Do you apprehend that the proposed increase would have any appreciable effect on the volume of traffic, that is, in the way of decreasing traffic?
Mr. Day: Yes, sir. I think, Mr. Congressman, that every time railroad freight rates are increased, the natural result is a shrinking of that traffic.
That is accountable to various reasons. First, the railroads are today confronted with most active competition on all sides, and unless other agencies see their way clear to go up with the railroads in equal amount and at the same time, there is always the possibility that they will get some of the business which the railroads formerly enjoyed.
Further, taking New England as illustrative, as freight rates have mounted during recent years the field that our manufacturers can profitably serve has been slowly but surely contracted. In other words, high freight rates tend to localize industries, and as they are localized, distribution is made largely by trucks, thereby depriving, if you please, the railroads entirely of traffic which they had formerly enjoyed.
Mr. Martin: I have observed these automobile caravans going through the western country hooked together, two by two, for a mile or a mile and a half along the road, going from Michigan factories to the Pacific coast, and it looked to me like it ought to be possible to avoid freight rates which would make such an expensive operation as that. That must be a pretty expensive way of getting an automobile to the Pacific coast, to send a man along, pay his wages and all of the attendant expenses.
Is that type of competition, in your opinion, the result of freight rates, or is there some other element in it?
Mr. Day: As to the particular type you mention, now, delivering cars Mr. Martin: The caravan method. Of course there is another type of competi tion also where they load four on a truck and haul them across the country.
Mr. Day: Normally, Mr. Congressman. I believe that price is what regulates the flow of commerce, via one agency or another. It may well be that as to the automobile situation which you have alluded to and which I am unfamiliar with, there might be other circumstances of importance; but I will cite this illustration for you, that in New England today in some sections of New England there are manufacturers, loading daily trucks with merchandise freight which moves to points as far off as Chicago or the Mississippi River crossings without breaking bulk at all.
Now, the reason why that service is used is because it is more economical from the standpoint of charges and the service is fully as good as that procurable via the railroads.
Mr. Martin: I will say here, I am not going to pursue this inquiry and take your time or the time of the committee any further, but I have never been able to understand how it could be more economical and how, so to speak, they can undermine the railways, because I cannot see that the service is in any way comparable from the standpoint of time, efficiency, economy, or from any other standpoint. I have never been able to see how they can truck these cars, or take these caravans across the country cheaper than the great railway system that can haul a mile of cars and cargo. But, I do not want to pursue that any further.

Statement of John A. Wright, Traffic Manager Flint Chamber of Commerce.
My name is John A. Wright, traffic manager, Flint (Mich.) Chamber of Comi am authorized by the Chamber of Commerce of Flint, Mich., to present to the honorable body of the House of Representatives' Committee on Interstate and Foreign Commerce this statement in opposition to Senate bill 69, which limits the length of freight trains to 70 cars.
Mr. Sadowski: Mr. Wright, what is your experience as traffic manager?
Mr. Wright: About 6 years with railroads and industries, legal training; 16 years traffic manager of the chamber of commerce.
Mr. Sadowski: Of Flint?
Mr. Wright: Yes, sir. While the membership of the Flint Chamber of Commerce is comprised of large and small industrial and commercial concerns of the city, we fully recognize our responsibility to provide, so far as possible, safeguards for the prosperity, safety, and well-being of every citizen of Flint.
Flint is an inland city, a highly developed industrial center with a population of approximately 160,000, and is the second largest automobile-manufacturing center of the United States.
The favorable position that Flint occupies in the industrial world has been achieved without the water-shipping advantages available at other cities. Flint's growth and the prosperity of its people may be credited almost entirely to the development of the automobile industry. In turn the development of the automobile industry of Flint has been almost entirely dependent upon adequate and efficient railroad transportation. No other outlet is offered, such as a natural waterway.
Mass production has been met with the railroad's ability to supply mass transportation. The production schedules of Flint's manufacturing have been developed and held almost entirely through the facilities afforded by mass railroad transportation. Obviously, any legislative act which would hamper efficient and economical railroad management would react to the detriment of production in Flint, would curtail industrial employment, and would impose upon our consumers unwarranted additional transportation costs with no material benefits obtained and with little regard given to public interest.
Our opposition is registered, first, on the grounds of safety. Flint has 71 grade crossings, traversed by freight trains which operate at average lengths of 82 cars per train. These train-length records were taken from average operating days of September and October last. They have been carefully checked against other trunk-line operations in Michigan. Flint had nine fatalities in the year 1937 as a result of railroad-auto collisions.
Flint industries and their employees are decidedly safety-minded. The records of the National Safety Council show that the lost-time accidents of the industrial workers of Flint have decreased 75 percent in the last 8 years.

: - - 29 - The lost-time accident record of these same employees is 62 percent below the average of all industrial employees in the United States. Men trained in safety in the industrial plants of Flint are deserving of an outside atmosphere of safety comparable to that which they maintain on their own jobs. However, to Impose a 70-car train limitation would be to increase by 34 per cent the possiility of trains hitting automobiles in Flint, and consequently increase fatality hazards at our grade crossings by one-third.
Secondly, our opposition is registered on the grounds of operating inefficiency which would result from a 70-car train limitation. Delays would be inreased at transfer and terminal points with cars held for later trains. Time in transit would be increased as a result. Empty cars, requiring special equipment for automobile and auto-engine loading, which are ordinarily handled in long trains, would intensify terminal blockades if reduced to a 70-car train maximum, with resultant costly delays, lost sales, and mounting inventories, all of which would seriously threaten employment through the dislocation of schedules of mass production and mass distributionof Flint's products.
Thirdly, our opposition is registered on the grounds of economic waste. We believe judicial notice may be taken of the present precarious condition of railroad credit and the railroads' petition for a blanket freight-rate increase. A safe conclusion may be drawn that to avert further collapse of the railroads financial structure the added costs incident to shortened trains must be borne by the consuming public. Further increased costs to the consumer would stagnate ncreased purchasing power and thwart business and industrial recovery.
I have searched carefully for some logical reason that would justify a 0-car train as being the safest, the most efficient, or the most economical length. I have not found the answer.
I am thoroughly aware of the very care ful and the very thorough study you gentlemen have given this pending legislation. The prosperity of Flint, which is dependent upon the safety and employment of its 45,000 auto workers, who in turn are dependent upon the pricing of our product within the consuming public's ability to buy, will, we believe, be militated against if Senate bill 69 becomes law. We respectfully urge your careful consideration of these consequences to Flint.
I desire to present this extract from the minutes of the board of directors of the Flint Chamber of Commerce held Monday, January 24, 1938: As a result of the information before it and as a result of the discussion which followed, the board unanimously approved the following resolution: "Whereas the Mccarran train-length bill, S. 69, limiting the length of freight trains to 70 cars, has passed the United States Senate without opportunity being given for hearings and is now before the House of Representatives Committee on Interstate and Foreign Commerce; and "Whereas the industrial and commercial interests of Flint are greatly dependent upon adequate railroad transportation facilities; and "Whereas the enactment of Senate bill No. 69 would seriously hamper officient railroad operation and impose millions of dollars additional transportation costs, all of which would be to the detriment of the inter- : - - - 30 - ests of Flint: Therefore, be it "Resolved, That the Flint Chamber of Commerce, by official action of its board of directors assembled January 24, 1938, record itself as being strongly opposed to Senate bill No. 69; and be it further "Resolved, That notice of this official actionof the board of directors of the Flint Chamber of Commerce be transmitted to all members of the Committee on Interstate and Foreign Commerce of the House of Representatives of the United States and to the Honorable Andrew J. Transue, United States Congressman from the Sixth Congressional District of Michigan." By similar action Mr. Wright, traffic manager, was directed to go to Washington for the purpose of making an appearance in behalf of the Flint Chamber of Commerce.
This is to certify that the above is a correct statement of the action taken by the board of directors of the Flint Chamber of Commerce.
WM. A. MILLS, Manager.
I have also been given letters from the Bay City Chamber of Commerce, Bay City, Mich., and the Saginaw Board of Commerce, Saginaw, Mich., asking me to submit their resolutions.
I make no further comment upon them, because they are resolutions made by the board of directors, and I have no definite knowledge of the actual operations or conditions in those two cities.
Mr. Crosser: If there is no objection, they will be made a part of the record.
Mr. Martin: I understood you to say that Flint is the second largest automobile center in the country.
Mr. Wright: Yes, sir.

It could not possibly be as safe a method of transportation; they could not possibly deliver a car in the condition that the railroads could deliver it. And yet that is their method of taking the business away from the railroad companies and doing it themselves. So they are not very much concerned about the railroads, are they? They would take all of the business away from the railroad companies if they could, if they could make a few dollars out of it.

I am only speaking of the reaction that it would have upon the workers, which is a part of the industry.

(The letters and resolutions referred to are as follows:) Bay City Chamber of Commerce, Bay City, Mich., February 4, 1938.
Mr. John A. Wright, Traffic Manager, Chamber of Commerce, Flint, Mich.
Dear Mr. Wright: The attached committee report was adopted by my board of directors today. HAENSEL, Secretary.
Whereas Senate bill No. 69, prohibiting freight or mixed trains of more than 70 cars, will add millions of dollars to the railroads' operating costs and decrease their efficiency without a single benefit to themselves or to shippers; and Whereas the bill, instead of promoting safety, would greatly increase accident hazards to both railroad employees and the public; and Whereas it would impair efficiency in the movement of fuels, raw materials, manufactured goods, and farm products, thereby increasing prices to consumers: Be it G - 36.
Resolved, That the board of directors of the Saginaw Board of Commerce hereby expresses its opposition to the bill and respectfully requests our Representative and the members of the House Committee on Interstate and Foreign Commerce to oppose it.
Carried unanimously.
Saginaw Board of Commerce, Saginaw, Mich., February 2, 1938.
Hon. Clarence F. Lea, Chairman, House Committee on Interstate and Foreign Commerce, Washington, D. C.
Dear Mr. Chairman: This is to certify that the board of directors of the Saginaw Board of Commerce at a meeting held September 21, 1937, unanimously adopted the attached resolution, and on motion designated Mr. John A. Wright, traffic manager of the Flint Chamber of Commerce, to act as its official representative at congressional hearings to register the board's opposition to Senate bill No. 69, prohibiting freight or mixed trains of more than 70 cars.
Very truly yours, CHARLES W. HAENSEL Secretary.

Statement of M. D. Griffith, Executive Vice President, New York Board of Trade Mr. Chairman and gentlemen of the committee, my name is M. D. Griffith.
I am executive vice president of the New York Board of Trade.
This appearance is made under authority of a resolution adopted by the members of the New York Board of Trade mecting on May 12, 1937, at the Hotel Astor, New York City, which meeting was attended by 325 members and guests.
The resolution directs the president of the New York Board of Trade to designate members of the board to attend public hearings and to take such steps as may be appropriate to impress upon the Members of the Congress the board's opposition to this measure.
I quote from the statement preceding the resolution: Motive power has been developed, railroad grades have been estab lished, and other engineering features developed, to permit of the hauling of longer trains. The financial status of the railroad companies of the country demand the utmost economy. This is clearly an instance where legislation should not prevail over engineering skill and economics.
The New York Board of Trade deplores the fact that there are so many of our citizens out of work. It would give its encouragement to any sound plan that would provide employment for our people. We do not believe that S. No. 69 is based on sound economic principles. The railroads cannot create wealth.
The railroads cannot spend more than they take in and remain solvent. The railroads cannot make up deficits out of taxation, The railroads do not have the powers of government. They are controlled entirely by economic laws and the rules of simple arithmetic.
Voluminous testimony has been taken recently by the Interstate Commerce Commission showing the imperative necessity of increased freight rates and passenger fares. The President of the United States was reported in the press to have asked the Interstate Commerce Commission to give the railroads prompt relief. It, therefore, seems a defiance of rational processes to consider at this time legislation which will materially increase the operating cost of the railroads without providing one cent of additional revenue.
The New York Board of Trade would give encouragement to measures which would promote public safety. Our study does not indicate that decreasing the number of cars in a single train, and increasing the number of trains passing over the roadbed would result in the lessening of the accident hazard.
With very deep respect, the New York Board of Trade asks that Senate No. 69 be defeated.

- Statement of C. E Hochstedler, Traffic Director, Chicago Association of Commerce Mr. Chairman and gentlemen of the committee, my name is C. E. Hochstedler.
I have been associated with transportation in one way or another for the ast 40 years. From 1898 until 1924 I was employed by the railroads in various apacities, ranging from telegraph operator to general freight and passenger gent. Since 1924 I have been in the service of the Chicago Association of ommerce, with the exception of a period of 14 months when I served as a member of the auxiliary committee of the Central Freight Association, a railroad organiation, and a period of 3 years during which I served as traffic assistant to the Federal Coordinator of Transportation, during the life of the Emergency ailroad Transportation Act. At present I occupy the position of traffic direcor of the Chicago Association of Commerco, which I have held since October 1, .928, with the exception of the 3-year period referred to.
I am appearing at this time in behalf of the Chicago Association of Commerce and I am also authorized and directed to appear for the Illinois Territory Industrial Traffic League, of which I happen to be the first vice president.
The Illinois Territory Industrial Traffic League is an organization of shippers and commercial organizations located within what is commonly known as the Illinois Freight Association territory, including the State of Illinois, the southern portion of Wisconsin, and the cities located on the west bank of the Mississippi River adjoining the State of Illinois.
In keeping with the request of the chairman that the presentation of commercial interests be divided with a view of avoiding duplication and conserving the time of both the committee and the witnesses, my statement will be confined to the effect of the pending measure, S. 69, upon railroad service, Mr. Chairman and gentlemen of the committee, I want to supplement briefly what Colonel Barber has said about the interest of the shipping public in this measure.
Based upon my contacts, I am confident that if everyone in the State of Illinois or Illinois territory who would have liked to have been heard on this bill had been given an opportunity to appear, if they had not been discouraged in their desire to appear. Illinois alone could have kept you in session for a week.
If there is any one thing for which the railroads are especially deserving of the utmost commendation, it is the material improvement which they have been able to effect in the matter of service since the time their properties were returned to them following the world war, and I have no hesitancy in expressing the personal viow that only through this improvement of service have the railroads been able to compete to a reasonable degree with other transportation agencies which have been developing during this period.
For many years the Chicago Association of Commerce employed an experienced transportation man with the title of "service agent," whose sole duty it was to watch the receipt and loading of freight, the handling of cars through terminal yards and the roadhaul movement, to the end of determining the cause of delays : - This and, in cooperation with the railroads, undertake to improve the service.
position was abolished on October 1, 1925, because by that time the service of the railroads had improved to such an extent and the attitude of the railroad management was such as to convince us that the expense was no longer justified.
A few years later we decided to make a check of the actual movement of all of the cars loaded with less than carload lot merchandise freight in Chicago destined to all break bulk points, and it is a great pleasure for me to be able to advise this committee that from January 1, 1929, to December 31, 1937, a total of 9 years, we checked the movement of 4,794,528 such cars from Chicago and that this chock developed that 4,610,969 cars, or 96.2 percent, arrived at destination and were placod for delivery on scheduled time. Had it not been for floods, snowdrifts, and other conditions entirely beyond the con trol of the carriers, there is no question in my mind that this record would have been in excess of 98 percent, for during many months its record was in the neighborhood of 99 percent.
In addition to the high standard of maintenance of advertised schedules, I should also mention that, except for the shorter distances, the schedules themselves have been shortened by from 1 to 2 days and in some cases 3 days during this same period. While what I have said about the shortening of the advertised schedules and the high standard of "on time" performance is confined to the so-called merchandise cars from Chicago, my constant contact with both carload and less-than-carload shippers throughout the country convinces me that there is no question but that this improvement in the merchandise service from Chicago is fully representative of the service accorded to both carload and less-thancarload freight throughout the country.
As preliminary to the discussion of the effect of the pending measure on railroad service, it is only proper that we should review the changos which have been made during the past several years, resulting in the improved service to which I have reforred.
First, it is a matter of common knowledge that the railroads have expended several billions of dollars in the improvement of their roadbed and track, the strengthening of bridges, the construction of more powerful and speedy locomotives, the installation of safety signals and devices, and so forth, which in turn has enabled the handling of longer trains, facilitated and expedited the movement over the road, and avoided the congestion in terminal areas and car shortages which were so common prior to this period of rehabilitation and progress.
In this connection it is interesting to observe from the authentic statistics of the Interstate Commerce Commission just what happened during the 5-year period from 1924 to 1929. I have used the year 1924 because it is the first year that the statistics of the Commission make segregations necessary to allow some of these comparisons, and I have used the year 1929 because, as is well known, that was the year of the peak railroad traffic.
According to statement No. 8 of Statistics of Railways in the United States, as compiled by the Interstate Commerce Commission for the year 1935, which is the last volume available, on December 31, 1924, the class I railroads of the country, exclusive of switching and terminal companies, had in service 39,119 steam freight locomotives, 1,546 steam locomotives suitable for either freight or passenger service, and 160 electric freight locomotives, a total of - · 40 40,825 locomotives suitable for the handling of freight traffic. On December 31, 1929, the number of locomotives suitable for the handling of freight traffic was 35,591, a decrease of 5,234, or 12.8 percent.
According to statement No. 9 of the same Commission publication, on December 31, 1924, the same class I railroads had in service 2,348,722 freighttrain cars, while on December 31, 1929, the same railroads had in service 2,277,464 such cars, a reduction of 71,258 cars.
With these reductions of 5,234 freight locomotives and 71,258 freighttrain cars, the class I railroads of the country, as per statements 25 and 31.
of the same publication in 1929 handled 63, 053,683,000 more ton-miles of freight and 4,649,924,772 more freight-car-miles than they handled during the year 1924.
And according to statement 30 of this authentic source, they handled this additional traffic, representing an increase of 14.8 percent ton-miles and 10.9 percent car-miles, with an increase of but 9,747,971 additional freighttrain-miles, an increase of but 1.68 percent.
But that is only a part of the story. This additional volume of traffic, handled with fewer locomotives and fewer cars, was, at the same time, handled with no appreciable congestion or car shortage, which I attribute to the operation of longer trains at greater speed, and the more expeditious handling through yard facilities, made possible by the reduced number of trains and the resulting greater utilization of equipment.
By December 31, 1935, the number of locomotives suitable for freighttrain service was 29,460, a decrease of 11,365 or 27.8 percent, below the number in service on December 31, 1924. The number of freight-train cars in service was 1,835,654, a reduction of 513,068, or 21.8 percent, under December 31, 1924.
To many of us who are still sufficiently optomistic to hope for the return of a volume of traffic which will equal or exceed the 1929 peak, there is reason for alarm as to the ability of the railroads to handle such volume with the reduced number of locomotives and cars, even under the most favorable conditions.
While I have felt justified in commending the railroads upon the im provements in rail service during the past several years, in all justice to all concerned and, particularly, the proponents of this measure, I feel that it is only proper that I should advise you that we are in as close touch with other transportation agencies as we are with the railroads and that it is our viow that the railroads will have to make further improvements if they expect to compete effectively with other forms of transportation, particularly highway transportation. As illustrative, the service accorded from Chicago, Ill., to Houston, Tex., via the highway carriers now affords to Chicago shippers third morning delivery, while the service by rail is fourth morning delivery. The service accorded by highway carriers from Chicago, to the north Pacific coast terminals of Seattle, Tacoma, and Portland is sixth morning delivery while the best service available by the rail carriers is seventh morning delivery. A recent check of 117 shipments from Chicago to Los Angeles by truck shows 109 were delivered during the afternoon of the fifth day, while the best rail service now available is seventh morning delivery.
I am convinced that the present cost of service for these longer distances from Chicago to Houston and from Chicago to the Pacific coast by rail is much less than the cost of the service by the highway carriers, but this condition may be materially changed in the event that the prosent measure is approved 41by Congess and these railroads are prohibited from the practice of economical operation by the limitation of train length to a maximum of 70 cars.
It is obvious, therefore, that if the rail carriers expect to continue in competition with highway carriers for the movement of long haul merchandise traffic, they must further improve their service. In my opinion, over a longrange period, the enactment of Senate bill 69 will serve to decrease rather than increase the number of employees in the train service of the railroads because of the diversion of traffic to more expeditious and what may prove to be more economical forms of transportation, resulting largely, if not solely, from the restictions which this and other restrictive bills would impose upon the rail carriers.
Coming now to the effect upon the service to the public of the pending measure which would limit the length of freight trains to 70 cars, the conclusion is inescapable that such a restriction would result in chaos even with the present volume of traffic, to say nothing of the increased volume which the railroads, shippers and, I imagine, the railroad employees, hope will materialize.
The limitation of trains to 70 cars on many railroads would result in a shortage of locomotive power, thus prohibiting the prompt movement of the traffic offered. Obviously, the utilizationof the large powerful locomotives in the handling of the smaller trains would prove uneconomical and result in wasteful operation. With the increased number of trains, delays are bound to occur in road operations, on both single and multiple track lines, and it will not be possible for the railroads to maintain their present schedules between terminals.
With the greater number of arrivals and departures, it will not be possible to perform yard operations with the same expedition as under present conditions, thus adding to the delays and further impairing the service to the shippers.
Cars loaded by shippers, or received from connecting lines, at points between railroad terminals will suffer serious delay by reason of trains leaving the terminals with their full quota of 70 cars, destined to or beyond the next terminal.
And last but not least, these delays in my opinion, and based upon my close contact with transportation over a long period of years, will result in the most severe car shortage that this country has ever known, particularly if there should be any appreciable increase in the volume of traffic offered.
Obviously, entirely aside from the increased expense of operation and the increased opportunities for train-and-grade-crossing accidents which logically will result from the operation of an increased number of trains under the pending measure, the shipping interests cannot do otherwise than view with the utmost concern the possibility of deterioriation of service, which the enactment of this measure would force upon us.
The deterioration of service in turn would serve to divert from the railroads to other transportation agencies an increased volume of traffic, not because the other agencies, under normal conditions, can handle it more efficiently and economically, but solely because of the restrictions imposed by this pending measure and which in plain terms simply means the suppression of efficiency, economy, and progress.

· - In the final analysis, the proponents of this measure would suffer just as much, proportionately, as any of the other interests and I am confident that, instead of improving their condition and assuring additional work, it would have directly the opposite result.
For the benefit of the railroad employees, as well as the railroads, shippers, and other interested parties, we urge that this proposal be defeated.
Thank you.
