- - · 45 · Statement of C. E. Elerick, Traffic Commissioner, Grand Rapids Associa tion of Commerce My name is C. E. Elerick. I am traffic commissioner for the Grand Rapids Association of Commerce, with an office at 5 Lyon Street, Northwest, Grand Rapids, Mich. I have held that position since 1934. My prior experience in transportation and traffic work embraces many years with the railroads as telegrapher, station agent, switchman, brakeman, and freight conductor, as well as 10 years in the general freight department of the Grand Rapids Indiana Railway Co., now a part of the Pennsylvania Railroad Co.
The provisions of this bill have been considered by our transporta tion committee and board of directors, and I have been authorized and instructed to appear here in opposition to the bill.
At the meeting yesterday in the chamber's offices, it was agreed that we would try to cut down our testimony as much as possible, and with that idea in mind, I am cutting out about half what I had intended to say.
To begin with, I would appreciate the privilege of furnishing Congressman Mapes with the number of cars that are handled in and out of Grand Rapids in Pere Marquette train No. 52. I believe that I can get that informa tion and that it will answer the thought that Mr. Mapos had in mind when those questions were asked of Mr. Chamberlain.
Mr. Mapes: Have you the information which you refer to with you, Mr. Elorick?
Mr. Elorick: I can get it from the railroads, and, if necessary, I will have it in affidavit form.
Mr. Mapes: It does not need to be in affidavit form.
say will be true.
Whatever you Mr. Elcrick: I will go to their records and take it off my self.
Mr. Mapes: I wondered if you had it with you?
Mr. Elerick: I do not have it with me now, but I can get it myself from their records.
I have in my possession a railroad freight tariff dated January 20, 1868, in which appears the following provision: Twenty-thousand pounds is the maximum amount to be loaded in any car, All excess over this weight will be charged double first-class rates, and collected in every instance.
Then you had a 20,000-pound maximum loaded car. Those were the days of small cars and small engines, link-and-pin couplers, and hand brakes. Tracks were light-weight stool spiked to almost any kind of ties, and laid on almost any kind of a grade without much attention paid to hills and curves.

- - 46 - Now, compare present-day conditions. Railroading then was really a angerous operation and occupation.
The matter of improvements in railroad facilities and equipment has een stressed, and I will not go into that; but train schedules in a great many nstances are fixed as to the arriving time and leaving time to meet the manuacturing and marketing needs of industry.
The same is true of truck schedules, particularly those performing I contract service. Materials and supplies must arrive in time, regardless of the number of cars in the train in which they are moved. Manufacturing chedules must be maintained, and if the railroads cannot meet the established eods, the truckers can, and they will.
As in other branches of industry, railroad costs have increased greatly In recent years, and no small part of this increase is represented in the higher vages paid railroad employees.
The railroads have one thing to sell, and that is transportation: and If the cost is artifically increased, as it is here proposed, it will have to be net in one or two ways: They will have to increase the freight rates or they vill have to find some way of reducing expenses.
Railroad freight service in the period of increasing train length has improved to a point where delays, embargoes, and car shortages exist in memory only. In my opinion, however, the limiting of trains would be a long step backward to a return to very serious conditions for which the carriers were severely Criticized in the carly 20's.
The country does not want a return to conditions under which it will be impossible for the carriers to render a reasonably satisfactory service, and certainly the country is adverse to an increase in freight rates.
Going back to the matter of train lengths, as related to the train employees, it has been my experience that the brakeman does not place long trains in the extra hazardous bracket, for the reason that the brakeman with the right of choice by reason of seniority invariably insists upon having the rear-end job.
I have worked on trains in excess of 70 cars, and my experience has been that whenever there is an emergency application of the brakes, the men in the caboose know that shock is coming. The sound of the brakes, the motion of the train, and every thing else tell them that there is going to be a shock.
In my experience I have never boen knocked down in a caboose but once, and that is when the engine went in the ditch.
I think also that your shock accidents are happening more often on the trains running along without the application of the brakes, that is, the swinging of the surge back and forth, more often than it does when you get an application of the brakes.
Mr. Crossor: Mr. Mapes wanted to aska question.
Mr. Mapes: When did you leave the railroad service?
Mr. Elorick: In 1906, in the train service; in 1919 as to office work.

Mr. Mapes: What were you, a brakeman?
Mr. Elerick: I worked as a brakeman, as a switchman,, and as a freight conductor.

Mr. Mapes: As a brakeman, were you on a freight train or a passenger Mr. Elerick: As a brakeman, I was on freight trains. I never had any passenger experience except on one or two trips as a flagman.
Mr. Mapes: Do you still carry a card with the brotherhoods?
Mr. Elerick: No. I dropped the brotherhoods when I quit the train service on the Santa Fe.

Mr. Mapes: Do you think there ought to be any limit to the length Mr. Elerick: I do not think so.
Mr. Mapes: Do you think that opinion is held by the fellows who have had practical experience on the railroad to a great extent?
Mr. Elerick: No, but for a different reason than you think.
Now, you take the train with a hundred cars. The conductor has to go out and get the numbers of those cars. It is more trouble to go out and put those 100 cars down on your train book and check your bills. That is the reason the conductor is concerned.
Why I disliked long trains when I was in the train service more particularly than any thing else was because the engineer tried to pull your arms out in getting on the rear end on account of the speed he was able to obtain before the caboose reached you. The engineer usually stopped his engine at the station, so he would not have to walk so very far for his orders, and about half of the time, after he started the train, you would have to climb on in the middle some place and walk back in order to get on. That was my objection to long trains.
Mr. Mapes: You do think that the fellows who operate the trains most of them think that they ought to be limited?
Mr. Elerick: Yes, for more reasons tha one; but I do not think it is because of the feeling that there is any particular danger in the surge and shock of the train.

- Statement of Karl S. Dixon, Assistant Secretary, Ohio Chamber of Commerce Mr. Chairman and members of the committee, I am Karl S. Dixon, assistant ecretary of the Ohio Chamber of Commerce, with headquarters in Columbus, Ohio.
am authorized to speak officially for the Ohio Chamber of Commerce on S. 69, nd will further identify myself with other organizations in behalf of which I hall speak. The first part of my statement will represent a presentation in ehalf of the Ohio Chamber of Commerce. The Ohio Chamber of Commerce is the argest State-wide business organization in Ohio, having in its membership all ypes and classes of business, including agriculture.
The conclusions of the Ohio Chamber of Commerce on this bill were arrived it through study by the Federal affairs committee of the chamber, which made a recommendation thereon to the board of directors. The board of directors ratified the conclusions and all members of the chamber were advised of our conclusions, and not a single dissenting opinion was received from any member.
The Federal affairs committee is composed of 35 men, coming from all parts of the State and interested in various lines of business. Only one of these men represents a railroad, and the item in the committee agenda calling for consideration of this matter did not originate with the railroad man. The board of directors is so composed that no single interest can control its policies.
We are interested in and opposed to the train-limit bill for several specific reasons, as follows: (1) Safety; (2) increased cost to the railroads; (3) service to be rendered by the railroads; (4) the effect upon other industries.
-- Safety: I shall not deal at length with the question of safety because of the fact that this matter has been fully reviewed by other witnesses who have already appeared before your committee. I do wish to point out, however, the fact that operations under this bill if enacted into law -- will add 60,000,000 train miles to the present total and, based on care fully compiled statistics, will mean that at least 90 people will lose their lives each year who would otherwise be permitted to live. Hundreds of others will be injured who would otherwise have sound bodies.
This total of casualties will be increased as years go on because of the fact that there are more grade crossings today than there were 10 years ago, and there will be more 10 years hence than there are now. Building of new highways increases the number of grade crossings more rapidly than they are eliminated by the ambitious program of overhead and subway construction at great cost to the railroads.
Cost: We are also apprehensive lest the direct cost of $100,000,000 per year to the railroads, and the indirect cost for exceeding that figure, will eventually bring about Government ownership of the railroads, to which businessmen and business organizations are strongly opposed.
Businessmen are keenly interested in the financial problem concerning the railroads. The rail companies are applying for a 15 percent increase in freight rates and an increase in passenger fares. Enactment of this law would seriously add to their difficulties. There is a limit to which railroads can pass on charges to the public, because they have the keenest competition in water and highway transportation and increasing competition by air.
Service by railroads: The one thing railroads are most interested in is the rendering of service. That is what they sell. If they thought they could render better service and get more business they would operate shorter trains.
Service to business has been vastly bettered with the advent of the long trains.
If a train already composed of 70 cars should come along, if this new bill is enacted, it would not pick up cars waiting for transit. Men who say that better service would be rendered to business through this proposed law do not speak for business. Business itself is not favorable to this bill. I have with me resolutions recently adopted by both the Ohio Farm Bureau Federation and the Ohio State Grange protesting against the enactment of this bill. They know that this law means poorer service and greater cost. No one has deluded those organizations as to the result of this law.
Effect upon other industries: Ohio industries are being adversely affected because of limited buying by the railroads and the threat of enactment of the bill before your committee. I refer to such industries as the Lima Locomotive Co., the Ohio Steel Foundry Co., the Timken Roller Bearing Co., the Ralston Steel Car Co., the Buckeye Steel Castings Co., the Youngstown Sheet Tube Co., the Republic Steel Corporation, and a number of others. In fact, coal, steel, oil, and lumber companies benefit materially from railroad purchases. An official of the Lima Locomotive Co. recently told me that orders for new locomotives had been adversely affected because the railroads did not know whether or not to order the Mallet type engine designed to haul long trains and efficient in that service only, or to order light locomotives which would be efficient in hauling shorter trains. Hundreds of men have been made idle because of the lack of these orders at Lima alone. In fact, the increased cost of this law would be such that the ability of the railroads to make needed purchases would be further curtailed. It should be borne in mind that the greatest amount of unemployment is in the heavy industries.
It was Situation in Ohio Legislature: In concluding my statement for the Ohio Chamber of Commerce, I wish to point out that there have been introduced in the last several sessions of the Ohio Legislature bills designed to limit freight trains to 70 cars.
In the regular session of the present Ohio Legislature, H. B. No. 97, to limit freight trains in Ohio to 70 cars, was introduced by Messrs. Becker and Cory. This bill was introduced on January 25, 1937.
referred to the house committee on labor, which was regarded as exceedingly friendly to all genuine labor legislation. Several important bills sponsored by organized labor were reported out of that committee, but I wish to stress that, after seven hearings on the Becker-Cory bill, the committee failed to recommend it for passage.
I submit herewith the names of the men on the labor committee of the Ohio House of Representatives. The names are as follows: W. H. Whetro (Lawrence), chairman: Walter Becker (Hamilton): F. J. Carroll (Cuyahoga); J. F.
yers (Franklin); J. E. Curtin (Lucas); John Paskell, Sr. (Perry); S. M. Frakes (Noble); L. H. Myers (Allen); A F. Bernhardt (Lucas); R. A. Knight (Summit); F. E. Simpson (Hamilton); J. J. Mcgowan (Mahoning); Mrs. Alma Smith (Cuyahoga); J. R. Duffy (Cuyahoga); F. G. Guthrie (Portage).
It is our hope that the Interstate and Foreign Commerce Committee of this Congress will make a decision similar to the labor committee of the House of Representatives of the State of Ohio, and will not report out the Mccarran train-limit bill.

· 50 - Position of Associated State Chambers of Commerce: The Associated Statc Chambers of Commerce held a meeting in Chicago on January 29, 1938. While State chambers of commerce are primarily interested in State legislation, they do find their interests vitally affected at times by national legislation, and the Chicago meeting considered nothing but national legislation. That meeting was attended by presidents, secretaries, and other high officials of the State Chambers of Commerce of New York, Pennsylvania, Ohio, Indiana, Illinois, Florida, Arkansas, Wisconsin, North Dakota, Idaho, California, and Oklahoma. After full discussion of the train-limit legislation a resolution was adopted urging opposition to the Mccarran bill. I was duly authorized to state to your committee that the Associated State Chambers of Commerce urge that this bill be not enacted into law.
Other chambers of commerce: I am also authorized and directed to read a statement in behalf of the Youngstown Chamber of Commerce pertaining to S. 69.
It is as follows: Recommendations of the Youngstown Chamber of Commerce Pertaining to S. 69, Trainlength Bill.
The Youngstown Chamber of Commerce is opposed to the provisions of S.
69, commonly termed "the train-length bill." The bill was passed by the United States Senate during the first session of the Seventy-fifth Congress and is now before the House Committee on Interstate and Foreign Commerce.
It is contended by those in favor of the reduction of the number of cars that may be included in freight trains in interstate commerce that the reductionis necessary in the interest of safety. Statistics seem to disprove this contention. Statistics prepared by the Interstate Commerce Commission reveal that since 1922 the average length of freight trains has increased 15.6 percent. Fatalities to train personnel in all types of trains and train-service accidents decreased 60 percent; fatalities to train personnel as a result of accidents occurring at highway grade crossings decreased 19 percent. In connection with the latter figure it should be noted that the number of motor vehicles in the United States increased more than 133 percent, which has probably had some effect on this figure.
Fatalities to railroad employees resulting from accidents due to sudden starts and stops have decreased 75 percent, and in accidents due to the failure of car equipment the decreases has been 69 percent. These figures published by the United States Chamber of Commerce indicate that while the average length of freight trains has increased, fatalities have decreased and therefore S. 69 inflicts an unnecessary limitation upon the length of freight trains.
The Bureau of Safety within the Interstate Commerce Commission now has jurisdiction over matters of this kind. The right of initiating changes in the interest of safety should remain with this bureau, which can consider all aspects of a problem without being limited in its decision by a hard-andfast statutory rule.
To reduce the average length of freight trains involves an increase in expense not only to the railroad but eventually to the shipper. The foregoing statistics indicate that this increased expense is not justified under 51 - the guise of increasing the safety of railroad operation. Opponents to the measure point to the findings of the United States District Court of Nevada, in which the court states that "The Nevada train-length law bears no reasonable relation to safety, but if enforced would impair and lessen the safety of the plaintiff's present method of train operation in Nevada." It is estimated that this bill, if enacted by Congress, will increase the operating expense of railroads doing interstate business approximately $125,000,000. This increase would eventually be reflected in an increase of freight rates. It is reported that substantial increases in operating costs have already been experienced by the railroads. These include $233,000,000 in wages and $104,000,000 in taxes as a result of the social security and railroad retirement legislation.
A reduction of the average length of freight trains would also necessitate additional equipment. One railroad estimates that this would mean in one division alone approximately $2,717,400. As previously mentioned the increase in freight rates that would undoubtedly follow the enactment of this bill would be inflicted upon industry generally. This means that unless those who are compelled to pay the higher rates find some way of reducing other costs of operation the freight-rate increase will be reflected in their business in the form of reduced profits or reduced wages.
The financial condition of many railroads and business enterprises in the United States also offers mute testimony in opposition to the bill.
Business is retarded and not increased as a result of rapidly increasing costs of operation. It would be unwise, therefore, to further complicate the present business condition with legislationof this type that will increase expenses and weaken the financial structure of industry.
The Youngstown Chamber of Commerce is of the opinion that this legislation is not justified as a safety measure and that the undesirable results are so far-reaching in their effects that there is no justification for limiting the length of freight trains to 70 cars.

Lee, managing director of the Day ton Chamber of Commerce, to state to your committee that that organization has officially gone on record as opposed to the train-limit bill. This was done upon the recommendation of the board of directors of the traffic bureau of that organization.
The traffic bureau board is composed of representatives of the industrial and mercantile businesses of the city of Dayton who have charge of the traffic affairs in such industrial and mercantile establishments. DON F. WILLIAMS, Secretary (The letter referred to dated December 13, 1938, is as follows:) Your attention is invited to action by the trustees of the Akron Chamber of Commerce opposing enactment of the bill now before Congress to limit freight trains to 70 cars.
This action was taken on recommendation of our transportation committee.
Ostensibly, the purpose of the bill is safety. Experience of 1936 was that three trainmen were killed on trains shorter than 70 cars and none on longer.
For highway users more and shorter trains create the same hazards as increasing the numbers of crossings at grade.
This bill promises no improvement in service to shippers. It will delay some cars when more than 70 are ready to move.
Compared to all other countries, this country has been outstanding in developing long-distance mass transportation at low rates. Large units, heavy power plants have produced this result. This bill reverses this trend.
Our shippers and railroad men agree that the financial condition of the roads is not such as to justify increased rail expenses unless it be for the purpose of forcing the Government to take them over.
We hope you agree that our reasons for opposing this bill evidence sound thinking in the interest of the general welfare of this country.

Mr. Chairman and gentlemen of the committee, the shippers of Detroit are greatly concerned over the possibility of limiting train lengths to 70 cars.
The transportation committee of the Detroit Board of Commerce, representing a cross-section of Detroit industry, has sent me here to inform your committee of our apprehension.
All railroads in Michigan are single track, with the exception of some parts of the Michigan Central and the Grand Trunk. We therefore will be gravely affected by any proposal to increase the number of trains on our railroads.
Of course, on the double-track lines there would be a proportionate increase.
In the period from May 1936 to April 1937, 33,703 trains, or 19.6 percent of all trains operated in the State, including the Upper Peninsula and branch lines, exceeded the 70-car limit which the legislation in question would fix.
If this maximum had been applicable during the period mentioned, May 1936 to April 1937, 8,648 additional trains at an out-of-pocket expense of $1,224,966 would have been required to haul the 534,916 cars which were handled in trains that exceeded 70 cars in length.
The additional train-miles would have amounted to 783,620, a most wasteful expenditure of railroad funds if there ever was one.
But the alarm of Detroit shippers is not limited to the additional expense which would be saddled upon the railroads. We are equally, or perhaps more, concerned with the threat to Detroit railroad service which lies in this proposed Legislation. Detroit is growing at a rate that will see our present population of 1,770,000 reach 3,000,000 people by 1960. This increase in Detroit inhabicants is the result of a prodigious industrial growth which we have been experencing for the past several years, and which gives every indication of continung in coming years. Records which the Detroit Board of Commerce has painstakingy compiled reveal that $100,000,000 were spent in the Detroit area in 1937 for expansion of some 142 companies.
This industrial expansion with its attendant growth in population raises the transportation problems which we are now attempting to measure and prepare or. The ability of the Detroit railroads to meet this impending demand for additional services must not be constricted through the imposition of any arbirary limitations such as are here proposed.
Our careful study discloses that the fixing of a limit of 70 cars upon rain lengths would seriously disrupt our present service to and from Detroit.
For instance, we know from the records that 39.9 percent of all trains of the ichigan Central handle in excess of 70 cars. We know, further, that when the ranch lines are deleted from this study, more than 50 percent of the main-line trains exceed 70 cars in length. That means that of every two cars which a Detroit shipper forwards, or a Detroit industry purchases, the chances are 1 - - in 2 that his cars will not get in the first available train, but will have to wait until another train of statutory length can be assembled. There can be no other conclusion than that if the lengths of trains are limited to 70 cars, the railroads will hold all trains until that limit is reached. The maximum will most certainly become the minimum. Operating expenses will force such a practice to be adopted.
Other Detroit railroads with their percentage of trains handling in excess of 70 cars are: Detroit Toledo Shore Line, 31.5 percent; Wabash, 24.5 percent; Detroit, Toledo Ironton, 20.8 percent; Pere Marquette, 15.7 percent; Grand Trunk Western, 14.4 percent.
Today there are no cars which are not rush. I say that out of an experience of some 11 years in the traffic department of the railroads before I wont with the board of commerce, in direct contact with the shipping public.
Porduction and supply are so keyed that many industries follow every shipment in-bound and out-bound until it reaches its destination. They know that through being able to determine and calculate the arrival of shipments they can keep their stock of material down, conserving capital and saving interest. Plant tracks are kept busy feeding in-bound material into production lines and taking the products of the assembly lines as they pass final inspection. It is this elimination of waste that has put Ame rica far in front as an industrial nation and enabled the production of commodities within the reach of the man in the street. And not the least of the contributions that have made this possible is the precision of modern railroad service.
There is another reason that Detroit fears the results which will flow from the adoption of this bill. It is this: Automobiles today are loaded in boxcars which are particularly and especially equipped to handle them. These are called rack cars. The rack is a device which enables shippers to load four automobiles in a boxcar without the use of decking. It is largely through the use of these special cars that the railroads are able to retain and regain automobile traffic which otherwise would go to the highway transports and caravans or drivoaways.
At the height of the loading season probably more than 20,000 of the se rack cars are loaded each month in Detroit. I base this conclusion on the fact that one railroad in Detroit loaded 13,883 of these cars in January 1937 and 14,745 in February of the same year. These cars are in groat demand dur ing the loading season. About 99 percent of all automobiles handled by the railroads are in these cars, and for every day a car is delayed in returning to Detroit or other loading point a revenue load is lost to the railroads.
The carriers, therefore, frequently return the cars in solid trainloads from every section of the United States. A hundred and often more of them are bunched into trains and run on manifest schedules. Those which are available after the through trains leave are tacked on the end of symbol trains, which are scheduled with passenger-train precision.
Obviously the bill which you are here considering would cut at the very heart of this equipment arrangement, and I have no doubt that that situation applies equally so far as other equipment is concerned, such as grain cars during the grain-loading season in the West, coal cars, and perhaps refrigerator cars during the height of the citrus-loading season or the Georgia peach crop.

Mr. Dean, I think that you have consumed about the time Mr. Dean: I have just two more sentences.
Mr. Crosser: All right.
Mr. Dean: If the carriers should attempt to mintain their present system of giving these cars the same attention as merchandise loads, great additional expense would accrue to them. If they did not, they would lose the loads to competing forms of transportation.
On behalf of the shippers of Detroit and in the interest of better railroad service, I urge you gentlemen to oppose this bill.

- - Statement of Samuel H. Williams Manager, Transportation and Foreign Trade Bureau, Philadelphia Chamber of Commerce My name is Samuel H. Williams, and I am manager, transportation and foreign-trade bureau, Philadelphia Chamber of Commerce.
I have been instructed to make known to this honorable committee the opposition of the Philadelphia Chamber of Commerce to Senate bill 69 which would limit the length of trains to 70 cars.
The purpose of the Philadelphia Chamber of Commerce is to protect and improve the commerce, business, and manufacturing interests of the port and city of Philadelphia and to advocate and encourage whatever may increase the prosperity or welfare of the citizens of Philadelphia.
Its membership is composed of several hundred industries, firms, and corporations of diversified interests in and contiguous to Philadelphia.
This bill if enacted into law would retard progress in the development of railroad transportation which undeniably is the backbone of our distribution system and which has been developed by the management of the rail carriers to give the best possible services to the general public.
Bosides the detrimental effects upon transportation services, it would increase the cost of transportation which naturally would have to be borne by the zeneral public.

58 - Statement of Donald Macleay, Portland (Oreg.) Chamber of Commerce Mr. Chairman and members of the committee, my name is Donald Macleay.
representing the Portland, Oreg., Chamber of Commerce.
I am It is not my intention to take up the time of the committee with a repetition and reiteration of the many facts and figures and arguments which have been already presented to you during the course of this hearing. I desiro merely to register with you the opposition of the business and agricultural interests of the Portland area to this type of legislation, and their protest against its enactment.
In our opinion, the various facts, figures, and statistics which have been placed in your record establish very clearly that, far from being a safety measure, this bill, if enacted, would provide more hazards to life and limb than now exist.
Our principal concern, however, is with those factors of this matter which will seriously affect the service which the railroads are now able to render to the shippers, and the cost of that service. A mandatory limit on the length of trains will seriously detract from the fast and efficient service with which we are now provided. It is a very serious matter if a shipper of fruit must hold a carload or two of apples; for instance, until there comes through his area a train of few enough cars that there is room for his to be added.
The matter of rate incroases is of vital concern. The Commission has recently authorized one increase and has now pending before it a petition for a further general increase of 15 percent. During the course of the hearings on that petition the railroads have been conclusively shown to be in no position to absorb the tremendous additional cost which this legislation would impose upon them. Of necessity, they must pass on to the shipper the additional burden.
It is not only the railroads which would suffer from this legislation it is every shipper and every consumer of every pound of freight which is moved on rails.
-- The shippers of the Portland area are perhaps peculiarly interested in this matter because of their location upon a water-level route between the interior of the Northwest and the seaboard. Through the Gorge of the Columbia there can bc, and are now, operated with great economy and safety, trains considerably in excess of the limit which would be imposed by this proposed legislation. The benefit of this economical service has contributed much to the development of Commerce in this area. We feel that no good cause has been shown why it should be destroyed by a law which would turn back the wheels of progress; the enactment of which we hold to be unjusitifed from the points of view of service, economy, and safety; and which will impose upon our shippers and consumers an unnecessary burden of additional expense and impaired service.

· 59 · - Statement of P. F. Guerke, Traffic Manager, Chamber of Commerce, Wilmington, Del.
My name is P. F. Guerke. I represent the Chamber of Commerce of Delaware, of Wilmington, Del.
Your chairman has requested that we who represent commercial organizations condense our statements and refrain from repetition of testimony as far as possible. My statement is brief.
Our organization wishes to bo placed on record with this committee as being opposed to Senate bill 69 which proposes to limit the length of railroad trains.
This bill has been carefully considered by our organization and the information and facts on which our conclusions are based are already a part of the record before you in these hoarings.
We are fearful that arbitrarily limiting the length of trains by legislation will greatly increase the cost of railroad operation. That such increases will of necessity be passed on to the shippers in the form of increased transportation charges or impaired service.
That if the increased cost could be absorbed by the rail carriers or passed on to the shippers without loss of traffic to competing forms of transportation we believe that the present excellent service rendered by the railroads will be impaired by a curtailment of fast through dispatch service which has kept pace with improved operating facilities of the railroads to accommodate the so-called long trains.
We believe that the determination of train lengths should be left to railroad management, properly supervised and regulated by the Interstate Commerce Commission with such regulation imposed by the Commission dictated by experience and actual records of accident trends as they relate to the length of trains.
We request that Senate bill No. 69 be not enacted into law.

Re: (The letter referred to is as follows:) San Francisco Chamber of Commerce, Washington, D. C., February 16, 1938.
Hon. Clarence F. Lea, Chairman, House Committee on Interstate and Foreign Commerce, Washington, D. C.
Dear Mr. Chairman: This bill, if passed, would increase the operating expense of the railroads.
by millions of dollars, which would necessarily have to be reflected in higher transportation costs to the shipping public.
Over a period of years, the railroads have increased the efficiency of their motive power units to a point where over 100 cars can be handled by many of them without difficulty. Obviously, it would be uneconomical to operate these units on a 70-car train. The average train length today is about 50 cars, for the entire country, including numerous small trains operated on short-line railroads and branch lines operated by class I railroads.
During the recent hearings at Los Angeles before the Interstate Commerce Commission on the application of the railroads for a 15- per cent increase in freight rates, it was pointed out that the general welfare of California is largely based upon the welfare of agriculture. By far the larger proportion of the perishable crop and a substantial portion of the dairy products produced have to be transported by rail to the eastern consuning markets. Many thousands of carloads are shipped annually. As a matter of fact the four transcontinental : - - 61 - railroads serving the State of California, for each and every day of the year, handle together an average of eight solid trainloads of perishable commodities from point of origin in California to transcontinental destinations. At these hearings the deciduous shippers, and poultry, egg, butter, and cheese producers found it necessary to protect strenuously the proposed increase, because of their condition, which precludes absorbing any more transportation costs. It is not possible for the California producer to pass increased transportation charges to the buyers because of competition from other producing States and the ceiling beyond which prices cannot go without having a consumer strike.
Generally speaking, transportation is the largest single element in the growing, shipping, distributing, and marketing of perishable commodities. From the persuasive evidence offered by competent witnesses as to the detrimental effect of the presently proposed increase in freight rates upon the economic welfare of California agriculture, we believe it self-evident that further increase in freight costs which must result from the added cost of operation incurred from limiting freight trains to 70 cars would be just about the "straw that broke the camel's back." The railroads in this area have in recent years greatly increased the size of their motive power and units, and the topography of the country permits the handling of freight trains greatly in excess of 70 cars. Not only do the carriers in the ordinary routine of their operation handle freight trains in excess of 70 cars to obtain the benefit of advances in the mechanical arts and secure the greatest efficiency and use of their operating plant, but this efficiency from the standpoint of the public interest has been reflected in the lower freight rates paid by the shipping public.
In California there are many miles of single-track railroad. Passage of the 70-car-limit bill would immediately necessitate a large number of additional trains upon the same trackage, with a corresponding decrease in the service presently being rendered to shippers and a corresponding increase in the hazards to the employees of the railroads engaged in train operation and to the public on passenger trains and at grade crossings.
To eliminate this the railroads would have to increase the number of sidings and passing tracks, or double track certain parts of the line when feasible, and make such other expenditures as may be necessary to take care of the traffic and remain in a competitive position with the motor trucks on short-haul traffic.
All of this means one thing--increased operating costs to the railroads and higher freight rates to the public.
We are strongly of the view that the railroads should not be subject to the burden of any legislation on this subject and that it is not required in the interest of safety.
For San Francisco Chamber of Commerce For Oakland Chamber of Commerce By Wm. S. Culbertson.

(The letter referred to is as follows:) Buffalo Chamber of Commerce, Buffalo, N. Y., Mr. Clarence F. Lea, Chairman, Committee on Interstate and Foreign Commerce, House of Representatives, Washington, D.C.
January 8, 1938.
Dear Sir: Train-Length Bill--S.69 In my letter to you of December 31, I requested 5 or 10 minutes to present the views of the Buffalo Chamber of Commerce at the hearing on Senate bill No.
69, which has for its purpose limiting the length of freight trains.
find it will be impossible for me to attend this hearing and would like to submit the position and some of the views of this organization for consideration by your committee.
The city of Buffalo is a transportation center and the railroads serving Buffalo constitute the largest taxpayers and provide by far the greatest number of potential buyers. It has always been the policy of the Buffalo Chamber of Commerce to advocate and support by legislative action which would add to the : - efficient and economical operation of our transportation facilities. It has likevise opposed any proposals which would materially affect the financial stability of our transportation systems or unnecessarily add to the transportation burdens of our industries.
The transportation committee of the Buffalo Chamber of Commerce is constituted of some 15 members, every one of whom has had from 10 to 35 years of experience in various phases of transportation; practically all of them having seen service with the railroads and as traffic managers of private industries.
The transportation committee has given consideration to this proposed legislation and has recommended that the board of directors oppose it.
This recommendation was subsequently approved by the board of directors, and I have been instructed to present our views to your committee.
In considering this matter the transportation committee cannot accept the theory upon which this legislation is proposed--namely, a safety measure. It is their view that the cutting down of the number of cars moved in freight trains to 70 would increase the number of trains 25, 50, or 75 per cent, and would thereby increase the possibilities in like proportions of accidents not only to the rail employees but to the public in general.
The committee is of the opinion that the additional financial burden placed upon the railroads, which would result from the enactment of this legislation, would be unnecessary added expense which the users of the railroads' facilities would be called upon to pay in the nature of transportation charges.
The committee is of the opinion that the increased cost of operation passed along to the industries in the way of increased freight rates would very materially lessen the amount of traffic handled with the consequent further shrinkage of the carriers' revenue, the curtailment of train service, and the consequent decrease in employment.
Insofar as Buffalo is concerned, it is felt that there is no necessity whatever for taking out of the hands of railway management the manner in which the freight trains should be operated. It is their belief that railway managoment is best qualified to pass upon the train lengths under varying conditions and in different territories. If it should develop that the operations within particular territories and localities require some regulation of train lengths, the committee feels that a hard-and-fast law such as provided for in S.69 should not be made to apply in all territories in order to remedy a particular condition.
The Chamber of Commerce is, therefore, opposed to this legislation and submits that your committee disapprove its passage.
Very truly yours, Buffalo Chamber of Commerce, Fred M. Renshaw, Traffic Commissioner.

:.

Mr. Chairman and Gentlemen of the Committee: My name is Charles R. Seal.
I am traffic director of the Baltimore Association of Commerce, at Baltimore.
I am speaking for that organization and also for the Hagerstown, Md., Chamber of Commerce in opposition to the bill.
I have prepared a statement here, which is brief, but it apparently touches upon angles all of which have already been mentioned before the committee, and I therefore hesitate to take up your time by repeating those statements. It deals with the present revenue necessities of the railroads, and their inability to absorb a substantial additional operating expense; mentions their pending plea for increased freight rates; questions the merits of the train-length bill from the safety standpoint on the belief that it would increase hazards attending train operations rather than reduce them, and also emphasizes the effect that would result upon the service rendered by the railroads to shippers.
That is to say, it is our belief that passage of the bill would materially impair the present very efficient service through requiring the operation of a greater number of trains. If the committee pleases, I would be glad to file this statement to be incorporated as a part of my remarks.
The Chairman: We will be very glad to have your statement, Mr. Seal.
Mr. Seal: Thank you.
I appear in oposition to this bill, because its passage would adversely affect the interests of the public generally, including the shipping public and the traveling public, as well as the interests of the railroads and of railroad employees.
The present urgent revenue needs of the railroads are well known to everyone. Their financial condition is such that they need every encouragement toward economy of operation instead of having imposed upon them a substantial additional expense such as would result from the passage of this bill. The added expense from limiting the lengths of freight trains to 70 cars has been estimated at $100,000,000 annually. Such an added expense would threaten the solvency of some of our railroads, and in fact some are already insolvent without it.
Some of the objections to enactment of the bill may be summarized as follows: 1.
The bill is proposed as a safety measure, but it would increase the hazards from train operations, particularly at grade crossings, through requiring an increased number of trains. Crossing hazards and accidents necessarily bear a relationship to the number of trains operated, and an increase in trains would increase the accident hazards of the entire highway traveling public.
It is my belief also that an increase in the number of trains would increase the accident dangers to railroad-operating employees. It is interesting in this connection that the number of accidents injurious or fatal to train employees has, substantially declined during the last dozen years or more when the lengths of trains have increased. This indicates that there is greater safety for everyone from the operation of long trains as contrasted with more short trains.
2. Passage of the bill would destroy investments toward economy that the railroads have made, and prevent further economies. The railroads have been able 70 - - to effect operating economies through investments in heavy motive power and other facilities for handling long trains. But for this progress in economy their operating expenses would be greater than they are today and their financial condition correspondingly worse. This sort of initiative ought to be protected and encouraged. Enactment of this legislation would, however, not only directly increase operating expense, but destroy the value of existing facilities and require the use of additional motive power. It would not only increase the cost of handling loaded cars, but also of nonrevenue returned empties, such as refrigerator and coal cars, which are particularly adapted to mass handling in long trains. One of its most important effects from the standpoing of shippers would be the slowing up and impairment of service necessarily incidental to the increase in the number of trains operated.
3. The railroads now have pending a petition for a further general increase in their freight rates to offset in art the decline in traffic and increases in their expenses which have already taken place. The increased expense that would attend passage of this bill would have to be met either by a further increase in freight rates to be superimposed upon that which the railroads are now seeking, if such a further increase could as a practical matter be made, which may be doubted; or by economies in other ways. The added expense from passage of this bill would result in part from the employment of more train operatives, but any offsetting economies that it might be possible to make would most likely be at the expense, in part at least, of nonoperating employees. But it is to be doubted that economies sufficient to meet this added expense could be made without seriously reducing railway efficiency.
The bill is in our opinion without merit as a safety measure. That it would, on the other hand, have a very harmful effect upon the stability of the widely held investments in the railroads, and upon their ability to provide adequate and efficient transportation service to the shipping public, seems beyond question. It is our hope that the committee will so view the bill and recommend against its passage.

· - 71 - Statement of M. A. Smythe, Chairman, National Affairs Committee, Roanoke Chamber of Commerce.
Mr. Chairman and Gentlemen of the Committee: My name is M. A. Smythe.
I am a past president, for two terms, of the Roanoke Chamber of Commerce and at this time Chairman of the Committee on National Affairs.
I My testimony will be somewhat different from the other testimony that has been given here inasmuch as I an not a professional chamber of commerce man.
am engaged in the school business, operating a private school, and president of the Patrick Henry Hotel, which is one of our largest hotels. The facts and opinions which I am going to present to you, are based on information as gathered by our committee and board at the time this matter came up for action on that board.
Mr. Mapes! What do you mean when you say "our board"?
Mr. Smythe: The chamber of commerce board.
Roanoke, Va., is a city of about 75,000 population. The main lines of the Norfolk Western and Virginian Railways pass directly through the city. The se lines extend from Hampton Roads to the West Virginia coal fields and beyond with many important connections. The general offices and the major shop and repair facilities of the Norfolk Western Railway system are located in Roanoke.
The growth of the city began in 1882 when the Shenandoah Valley Railroad was built to connect with the Norfolk Western Railway at Roanoke, and the employment provided by the two railroads has been largely responsible for the continuous growth of the community.
The Directors of the Chamber of Commerce of Roanoke, which I represent, gave careful consideration on May 10, 1937, to this train-limit bill, and unanimously approved the position of a previous board of the Chamber in opposing this legislation. The Chamber of Commerce of Roanoke feels that the community as a whole would be adversely affected by this proposed legislation, and after careful consideration has delegated me as a representative to state our opposition to the passage of the proposed bill.
We are opposed to this bill for the following reasons: (1) The impressive and continuous improvement in the safety of the overation of our two railroads over a period of years has resulted in great benefits to the well-being of large percentage of our citizens who are railroad employees.
During the past 25 years, there has been a reduction of approximately 87 per cent in the number of casualties to employees of our railroads. During the past 14 years, while the length of trains on the Norfolk Western were increased 51.5 per cent, injuries to firemen and brakenen decreased 72.2 per cent.
It is apparent to us that a multiplication of the number of train units on the lines of the railro ds serving our community must necessarily increase the danger of injury to railroad employees and become an extra hazard to our street and highway traffic. At the present time a number of our principal streets cross the tracks of the Norfolk Western Railway, and there are many hazardous grade crossings on both the Virginian and Norfolk Western Railways in the trade territory of Roanoke.

- 72 - The Norfolk Western Railway handles on an average 3,000 cars in 20 trains daily west from the coal fields. This bill would increase that number to 40, or one train of 70 cars passing a given point every 2 minutes, and this does not take into consideration passenger trains or switching.
Mr. Mapes: Will you repeat that statement?
Mr. Smythe: The Norfolk Western handles on an average 3,000 cars in 20 trains daily west from the coal fields.
Mr. Mapes: With 150 cars per train?
Mr. Smythe: About 150 cars to the train. I do not have the figures, but I think it is approximately the same ratio.
The Virginian would find it is necessary to run 7,738 additional trains yearly to handle its business. This means about 20 additional trains daily or a total of 40 trains every 24 hours. A train would, therefore, pass a given point every 36 minutes on the Virginian and the Virginian is a single-track railroad practically all of the way from the coal fields to the coast.
They also have a limited amount of passenger traffic and some switching crews and so forth.
The Virginian, I understand, has spent around $24,000,000 in straightening its tracks and electrifying part of its system, and building larger engines and cars in order to handle this business on its single-track railway.
We know that this increased number of trains passing these points would materially increase the number of accidents resulting in injuries and deaths.
(2) Roanoke business men are vitally interested in maintaining conditions.
whereby our railroads can successfully compete with other forms of transportation, thereby maintaining regular enmloyment for our citizens who are engaged in all classes of railroad employment. The success of all forms of business in our community depends very largely upon this type of employment, and any legislation that would jeopardize the earnings of our milro ds would mean an irreparable loss to our community.
(3) We re directly concerned that the high standard of railroad freight service we are now receiving shall not be jeopardized by a measure which apparently would curtail the capacity of our railway lines by taking away from then the freedom to operate their lines on an economic basis and make proper use of the millions of dollars that have been spent in strengthening bridges, straightening tracks, electrifying lines, and building engines and freight cars to meet the demands of efficient railway operation and to successfully compete with other methods of transportation.
(4) It is self-evident that this bill, if passed, would materially increase the cost of freight transportation. This increased cost could be met in only one of two ways.
(a) Allowing all railways to increase their freight rates.
(b) Reducing the rate of pay to railway employees.

-M The Either of these could be contrary to sound economic principles and would ullify the progress that our railroads have made during the past 25 years.
Roanoke Chamber of Commerce does not want the purchasing power of our railway employees to be impaired; neither does it want additional costs to be passed on to its customers by a law that places a penalty on progress and the economical operation of its railway systems.
For these reasons we oppose this legislation.

or over 60 years the city of Sheboygan has been nationally known as a furni ture anufacturing center. Other important industries are enameled ware, plumbers' are, dairy products, fruits and vegetables, children's vehicles, knit goods, achinery, beverages, toys, leather and leather goods.

These factories are now operating at a loss or at such a small margin of rofit that any slight increase in overhead from any cause might seriously affect hem by loss of sales territory, or compel them to locate closer to their markets, r in some instances, completely close the plants.
Sheboygan is served by only one steam railroad, the Chicago North Western ailway Co. We are interested in this bill before your committee for two prinipal reasons. Sheboygan is not railroad terminal.
reen Bay on the north and Milwaukee on the south are the terminals for most of the trains running through our city, which is located just about half way be- -ween these two points.
S This railroad, both north and south of Sheboygan, is located on a water rade, and with the power used at present, it is possible for practically every hrough freight train to handle all of the loads ready to move from Sheboygan.
This generally results in trains leaving Sheboygan hauling more than 70 cars, and he service now being rendered to us is far superior to that given us years ago.
If this 70-car law were in effect, it would seriously delay the free and apid movement of our products. In many instances, it might keep carloads of ur products waiting in the local yard or warehouses until a train came along with less than 70 cars. If the carrier were required to run a second train to andle the overflow, it would unnecessarily add to their expense, for in few if iny instances would there be sufficient loads for a second train of 70 cars.
Second, we know that if this bill is passed, it will add to the expenses of the carriers, whose present financial condition is such that they could not absorb even a slight increase. They will simply have to pass it along in the way of in - reased freight rates to the shippers.
If there are any further increases in freight rates, the people whom I am representing, in order to stay in business the aselves, will be forced to find !

- ther means of transportation for their products.
t can use more than one means of transportation.
Sheboygan is so located that In 1929 there was one commarrier truck line operating out of the city. Now there are eight common-carrier ruck lines operating through the city, each with a large fleet of trucks. In addition, there are many contract-carrier truck lines with authority to serve within more or less limited territory many of our manufacturing concerns.
Adequate truck transportation is now maintained at Sheboygan, so that if Sheboygan concerns are compelled to, they can divert substantial additional Connage from the rails to truck lines. However, except for short hauls, even with the railroad's costly packing requirements, they would prefer to ship via railroad.
Sheboygan is located on Lake Michigan and is a lake port. If necessary, water transportation could be used for a good share of the shipments to and from the eastern and Mississippi Valley territories. Even with these other means of transportation available, Sheboygan manufacturers continue to ship the majority of their products by milroad, and will continue to do so unless rates are made prohibitive.
It has been estimated that if this bill is passed it will cost the railroads.
throughout the United States as a whole somewhere between $87,000,000 and $125,000,000 annually, of which Wisconsin's share would be between $2,000,000 and $3,000,000.
The Public Service Commission of Wisconsin advises that 78.2 per cent of the total 7,030 miles of railroad in the State of Wisconsin is already in bankruptcy, and it stands to reason that the extra expenses, if this bill is passed, cannot be absorbed by Wisconsin rail lines.
In conclusion, briefly summarized, we contend that the following will take place if this bill is passed: Service will be impaired; Service will be impaired; freight rates increased; truck transport expanded; industries will relocate plants to improve service and to avoid high freight rates; and the economies of water transportation will become more and more attractive and more available, all to the detriment of the railroads, the city of Sheboygan, and as well the shippers and receivers whom I represent.
Therefore, I want to register their vigorous opposition to this bill.

(The resolutions referred to are as follows:) - 76.
Wisconsin Manufacturers' Association, 701-703 First Central Building, Madison, Wis.
Whereas a law limiting the length of freight trains is unsound; Whereas the safety argument for a train-length law is not upheld by exerience; Whereas the one hundred million dollars a year additional expense estimated is the cost of arbitrary limitation of train length would come out of the pockets of the public; Whereas subjects such as the length of trains should be handled through egotiations between management and employees and not be made the subject of .egislative attention; Therefore, be it RESOLVED, That the Wisconsin Manufacturers' Association are opposed to the 30-called train-length bill now before Congress and that members of this association be urged to communicate with the representatives in Congress requesting them to vote against this proposed federal legislation; and RESOLVED, That a copy of this resolution be sent to each Senator and Member of the House of Representatives from Wisconsin.
Resolution adopted by Wisconsin Manufacturers' Association At Annual Meeting, Milwaukee, December 17, 1937.
Er. J. R. Davy, Traffic Commissioner, Chamber of Commerce, Sheboygan, Wis. I have had about 25 years' experience in transportation matters.
I appear for the Cleveland Chamber of Commerce as its commerce counsel, for the Traffic Club of Cleveland, as one of its members specially delegated by its board of governors, and for the Great Lakes Regional Advisory Board, as its general secretary.
The Cleveland Chamber of Commerce is a non-profit Ohio corporation chartered to foster, protect, and advance the commercial, mercantile, and manufacturing interests of the City of Cleveland. It has about 2,600 members who, taken as a whole, represent every type of business carried on in the city. I have left with the clerk a complete list of the membership of the Cleveland Chamber of Commerce and the Traffic Club. I have not done so in the case of the Great Lakes Regional Advisory Board, because I understand that a complete list of the membership of all of the boards will be filed.
The Chairman: That list will probably be filed, but I presume that it is not intended to extend it in the record.
Mr. Brown: I am not very familiar with the procedure of making records before the committee, and I see no need of including it unless it is customary to include such a thing.
The Chairman: Well, it is not customary with this committee to include printed statements, or printed lists.
Mr. Brown: Well, it would take a good many pages in the record, and I will just file them for reference.
The Chairman: We try to include in the record those things that are instructive as to the facts or arguments.
full.
Mr. Brown: I had no intention of asking that those names be printed in The Chairman: Very well.
Mr. Brown: The Great Lakes Regional Advisory Board is an organization of shippers located in the territory bounded by a line beginning at the Niagara frontier and following the line of the Erie Railroad through northern New York, Pennsylvania, and Ohio to a point where the Erie crosses the Grand Rapids line of the Pennsylvania Railroad, thence north to the Indiana-Michigan State line, and thence westerly to Lake Michigan, including all of the southern peninsula of Michigan.
This board is composed of about. 1,400 members, representing all types of business in its territory which are interested in shipping or receiving property by rail. It was organized in 1923 for the purpose of improving the ∞- operation between railroads and their patrons in matters affecting their joint interests, particularly those connected with the supply and use of freightcarrying equipment and other operating problems.
The Traffic Club of Cleveland is an association of men connected with transportation agencies and industrial concerns. Its membership is limited to men - - who have reached certain designated positions with their respective employers, which insures their having substantial experience in the business of transportation. The club has aproximately 470 members and has been in continuous existence since 1914.
All three of these organizations are opposed to the provisions of Senate Bill No. 69, here under consideration, and in one way or another have communicated their views to this committee or to Representatives of the districts in which their activities are primarily located. All of then have instructed me to appear here and urge you most earnestly to take unfavorable action on the bill.
I shall not attempt any extended statistical statement, for that sort of information has been, or will be, laid before you in considerable detail. Instead I shall briefly state our conclusions, each of which we believe must necessarily follow any careful consideration or the known facts.
Although we understand that this bill is submitted as a safety measure, it would result in being exactly the opposite. Whether or not it is true that an arbitrary shortening of maximan train lengths would reduce certain types of accidents, the concurrent increase in the number of trains would increase the number of accidents of other types which are far more prevalent. It is obvious that the operation of a greater number of trains would increase grade crossing accidents, which are the most prevalent type, and would increase all accidents involving more than one train.
The bill would, in the first instance at least, increase the number of crews necessary to conduct road-haul operations, but even when viewed as a "make-work" measure it is open to the very serious objection that it would benefit a relatively small percentage of railroad employees, namely, those engaged in overating road trains. In other words, any benefit which might come from the adoption of this bill would accrue to a very small number of our citizens, and its costs, of which I shall speak later, would fall u on the entire nation.
The next serious objection which we have to this ensure lies in the fact that it would disrupt and impair freight service, which, by a gradual process since 1923, has been brought to the highest state of efficiency ever experienced in this country. A few examples are sufficient. Let us say that any one of the railroads serving Cleveland has 70 cars of freight for movement. In that case the train could be made up inmediately and all of the freight started on its way to destination. Then let us assume that instead of 70, 80 cars happen to be tendered. Of these, 10 must be left over until another 60 are accumulated, whereas physically the entire 30 could be dispatched at the same time.
Or, again, there are a number of fast through trains between Chicago and the East which move through Cleveland daily. It is now possible to have several cars of rush freight included in such trains, which would be quite impossible were they limited to 70 cars in length. This sitution would be far more imortant to a smaller community than to Cleveland, because while we would be able to build up 70-car trains of our own, a smaller city would not.
If it be urged that a reduction in train length would increase rond speeds, it is sufficient to answer that we are now served by trains moving at the highest rates of sneed ever atvained for freight service, and there is not a great deal to be desired in the way of increased speed. On the other hand, no purpose would be served in reducing the tonnage and increasing the speed of a great many trains now operated in our territory, because the freight handled is such that it does - 80not require quick delivery. There is no reason under any normal circumstances to increase the road speed of trains of coal, iron ore, stone, sand, and so forth, and the same is true of the great trains of emoties which are continually moved from one territory to another in anticipation of traffic needs. This matter of train service could be expanded to great length. It is one of the most serious objections which the organizations I represent have against this bill.
Now, turning to the matter of cost and its effect upon the railroads, their emloyees, and the public generally: The direct and indirect costs which this bill would add to railroad operations are certain to be very large. There is first the direct cost resulting from an increase in the number of trains operated, which is made up of wages, fuel, supplies of all kinds, wear and tear on power units, and so forth. The indirect costs are at least equally important and arise through the artificial obsolescence which would occur to power, tracks, bridges, and so forth, which have been installed for the purpose of economically handling trains of great tonnage, and the necessity of additional capital expenditures for changes and additions to yard facilities caused by the operation of a large number of trains, additional passing tracks due to the same cause, unless the service difficulties which I have before indicated are to be accentuated by a shortage of power, this will would necessitate additional locomotives for the handling of the same amount of tonnage as is now being moved. We have made no attempt at an independent study of a total of all these costs. It is enough for us to know that they would be very large in amount.
In one way or another, all of the costs of any form of transportation must be borne by the people of the United States, and, as the financial situation of the railroads is such that they cannot absorb any substantial increases out of their revenues, they must be first reflected in increased charges, and probably in increased charges on freight traffic. Although the cost of transportation is reflected in prices to ultimate consumers, the charges in the first instance are borne by those who ship or travel, and it is they who are most directly and immediately affected. Most of the people I represent are of that class.
We oppose this bill because we believe that it would be injurious to all of those affected by it. First, the railroads, now in an extremely precarious position, will be forced even nearer to bankruptcy unless and until they can recoup these added costs through increased rates and fares. Second, the shippers will be likewise injured unless and until they can pass the added transportation costs along to the general public in increased prices for their products.
Finally, we believe that even the railroad employees, to whose advantage this bill appears to be, will find it to be most harmful. That last statement perhaps needs some explanation.
The railroads are even now before the Interstate Commerce Commission seeking 2 substantial increase in their charges to cover increases in their operating expenses, and there is a rather general agreement on the part of the shippers, though I believe them to be quite unwilling, that the necessities of the situ ation require increases in railroad rates. However, there is a limit beyond which railroad charges cannot go. I personally believe that many rates are now at or very near their ceiling under present general economic conditions.
impossible to continue to add to railroad rates and fares without diverting traffic, at least temporarily, to competitive means of transportation, and much more important without destroying traffic altogether by localizing industry.
Each diversion or cessation of traffic by reason of high-cost transportation It is - • 81reduces the opportunities of employment in the railroad business, and these reductions fall most directly upon railroad road-service employees.
Transportation by railroad in this country is an industry in which mass production is vitally necessary to both costs and efficiency, but the industry must continue on some minimum basis in order that the country may live.
Thus, if the volume of movement is reduced, its unit costs must rise and the public will be called upon to pay the added costs, which they cannot otherwise avoid, because of sheer necessity.
There is an additional factor in the nature of cost beyond that of direct charges for railroad services. It is impossible to overstate the importance of the position which the railroad industry occupies in the economic and business structure of this country. Impoverishment of the railroads is strongly reflected in general business conditions. It is difficult to imagine any one situation which would tend more strongly to retard general economic and business recovery in this country than an unnecessary blow directed at the efficient operation of the railroads. In short, we are convinced that recovery will be hampered by measures which will replace modern railroad operating methods by wasteful processes which will further impair the purchasing power and the credit of the railroad industry.
This bill is not simply a matter of controversy between railroad trainservice employees, on the one hand, and railroad omers and management, on the other. It involves the welfare of every railroad employees, every patron of the railroads, and every citizen of this country. When so understood we are confident that your action will be unfavorable to it.
Thank you.
The Chairman: We thank you.
