: - Statement of J. J. Underwood, Seattle Chamber of Commerce.
My name is J. J. Underwood, and I represent the Seattle Chamber of Commerce.
The Seattle Chamber of Commerce is composed of more than 3,000 business men of the city of Seattle. After careful and exhaustive study by its Transportation Committee, it is opposed to the enactment of this legislation on the ground that the measure, if passed, would increase freight rates, and that it would tend to increase rather than decrease the number of accidents on railroad transportation systems.
The Board of Directors of the Seattle Chamber of Commerce and members of its Transportation Committee are convinced that the enactment of this bill would increase the cost of railroad operation in a sum estimated to be between 75 and 100 million dollars per annum. Inasmuch as railroads cannot operate without profit, this increase would, as a matter of course, be reflected in increased freight charges. These increased charges would add to the present financial difficulties of the large railroad systems, one of which is already in the hands of the receiver, and all of which have petitioned the Interstate Commerce Commission for a rate increase because they are unable otherwise profitably to operate.
Ultimately, the increased cost would have to be passed on to the consumer.
The basic industries of the State of Washington are fisheries, fresh fruits, wheat, lumber, wood pulp, and other forest products, all of which are large in bulk.
With the exception of lumber and forest products, the commodities produced in the State of Washington are seasonal. The incoming freight, generally speaking, is composed of manufactured products which are not so bulky. As a result of this condition a large number of cars return empty to the west coast.
In some cases this runs as high as 40 per cent of the traffic borne by the railroads that yields no profit. This legislation would affect not only the trains of loaded cars but also the empty ones, thereby still further increasing the cost of railroad operation, and this cost eventually would be passed on to the shippers, and ultimately to the consumers. In the production of fruit and fish the loss night have to be borne by the producer because his products are highly competiive with other foods. When apples are too expensive many people substitute other fruit for them. If fish is too costly they use other foods.
In the western and far western areas there are thousands of miles of single rack lines upon which a limitation of train length would result in operating lifficulties and a necessarily less efficient and more costly service.
Advocates of this legislation have sought to justify it as a safety measure out an analysis of the situation shows that the bill, if enacted, would increase substantially the hazards incident to railroad operation. If the trains are limited in length as provided in this bill, it would become necessary to run 37,000,000 additional train-miles to handle the volume of traffic which the railroads carried in 1930, generally regarded as a normal year, or 60,000,000 additional brain-miles to mndle the smaller volume of traffic carried in 1936. The se figures were obtained by the Transportation Committee of the Seattle Chamber of Commerce from railroad executives of many years experience, who certainly should be the best authority on the operation of railroads.
The necessity for operating more train-miles, obviously, would have the following results: G 84- (1) Accidents, injuries, and fatalities would be increased, particularly in connection with grade crossings and train collisions.
(2) Entirely aside from the increase in operating expenses, enormous capital expenditures would be required to provide additional engines and additional main tracks, side tracks, and yards necessary to handle the extra trains. The se capital expenditures would bring about an increase of many millions of dollars in the annual fixed charges of the railroads.
(3) Economical and efficient use of the existing railway plant and facilities would not be possible, and modern operating methods, which include the running of longer trains, could not be employed. Modern operation has been made possible by the expenditure of billions of dollars during the past 15 years on tracks, structures, and equipment, the usefulness of which would be impaired by the proposed artificial limitation of train length.
(4) Higher operating expenses and new carrying charges on large additional investments would be reflected in higher freight rates.
(5) Railway service to the public would be impaired because of congestion on the road and in terminals and because of the inability of trains already carrying 70 cars to pick up additional loaded cars which are ready to move.
Since 1923, the railroads have spent more than $8,000,000,000 for additions and betterments to their properties, for the purpose of keeping the industry abreast of the times and enabling it to render cheap and efficient service to the public.
Much of this money was used to provide more powerful locomotives, larger and stronger cars, strengthened track and bridge structures, and reduced grades and curves. The result has been to make possible modern methods of railroad operation, including the running of longer trains, which have reduced unit costs of operation, greatly improved safety conditions, increased the speed of trains, and, generally, produced the best and most satisfactory service in railroad history.
To limit the length of trains to 70 cars would destroy or impair the usefulness of a large part of the improvements and betterments of recent years.
operating methods made physically possible at a great cost would become impossible by fiat of statute. It is imperative in the public interest that the railroads should be permitted to take advantage of the most advanced and economical type of operation which science and progress in the art have made possible.
Limiting the length of freight trains would result in impaired service to shippers. The average length of freight trains is now only 46 cars. Under some circumstances, and to meet some requirements, relatively long trains are run and other circumstances to meet other requirements short trains are run.
The operation of longer trains, when cars are ready and waiting to make up such trains without delay, affords the railroads a vitally needed opportunity for economy in operation, together with an opportunity for minimizing congestion and - • 85resulting delays. The arbitrary division of longer trains into a greater number of shorter trains would eliminate these opportunities and would interfere with expeditious service because the unnecessary addition to the number of trains run would cause congestion both in terminals and on the line, which, in turn, would retard the movement of traffic. Even with the present volume of traffic, service would be impaired and in some instances the adverse effect would be very serious. In periods of heavy traffic, there would be a threat of a break-down in service, involving car shortage, embargoes, and other hardships which modern Operating methods have suceeded in avoiding.
For the reasons stated, the Seattle Chamber of Commerce respectfully asks this committee not to report favorably to the House of Representatives this legislation.

My Chairman and Members of the Committee: My name is Neal E. Williams. I ppear as Commerce Counsel, Fargo, N. Dak.
Fargo, N. Dak., is a city of approximately 20,000 people in the heart of the led River Valley, which is often referred to as the bread basket of this nation.
Fargo's trade territory reaches back into Minnesota about 150 miles to the ast of us, and out into Montana a distance from Fargo of approximately 920 miles.
It is an important jobbing city and it is an important railroad center. It is there the main crossover is of the two north transcontinental railroads, the Worthern Pacific and the Great Northern.
As a special assistant to the attorney general of the State of North Dakota, It became my duty to come down here to Washington just a week ago and oppose the railroads' request for a 15 per cent increase in freight rates. We opposed that request because of the condition of the agricultural industry.
The farmers of North Dakota and northwestern Minnesota in shipping their products have to bear the transportation charges to the first primary market, and the railroads serving that territory are compelled to move that grain tonnage to the primary markets at fast rates of speed. When our crop comes in in the fall of the year we have to move it down to the primary markets in order to get the remittances back on the grain. That grain at times is rolling down there in trains exceeding 90 cars.
The Interstate Commerce Commission decides these cases in the coldest neutrality of the law, and when the Interstate Commerce Commission went in and fixed the freight charges on a 90-car train of grain from a representative point in North Dakota to Minneapolis or Duluth, they increased that grain train's earnings on July 1, 1935, from $14,821.85 to $17,219.45. It stepped up the grain rates and increased the cost of marketing our grain.
Now, using that as a base Mr. Bulwinkle: How much would that be a bushel, Mr. Williams?
Mr. Williams: A bushel?
Mr. Bulwinkle: Yes.
Mr. Williams: Well, it is about $200 a carload, and there are about 2,000 bushels to the car. That is it.
Now, we came down and we onnosed that increase.
That is an increase This proposed 15 per cent increase is opposed because it would shoot the earnings up on that 90-car train from $17,219 to $19,820.
over the June 30, 1933, rates of $4,989 a train load.

Now anything that you do to impair the efficiency of the operation of the se northern railroads serving our territory will rebound directly back on agriculture. We know it. And the Interstate Commerce Commission decides these cases, as I have pointed out, in the coldest neutrality of the law, and if you take this 90-car grain train and you clip 20 cars from it, you reduce that grain train that proportion. That train is earning $17,219 today, and if you cut 20 cars off it, you reduce its gross earnings by $3,828.40. That is going to come back right up to us as producers. And that is what the railroads are up against out there. I have been at Fargo for 17 years and our Chamber of Commerce has had more cases before the Interstate Commerce Commission than any other Chamber in the United States in those past 17 years. The Great Northern has a ruling grade on its main line from Minneapolis to Brush River of three-tenths of 1 per cent, which is almost water level, and one west to Williston of four-tenths of 1 per cent, which is practically level. Up in the Red River Valley we raise a lot of potatoes--we produce approximately 12,000 carloads of potatoes a year and a 600-acre farm will produce 200 carloads of potatoes, and it is necessary that the farmer get those potatoes into cars and get them to market before the freeze. This puts a burden on our railroads to assemble all of those trains for all of that tonnage from the branch lines, bring them in on the main line and whip them into trains and get them to the markets before the freeze. That is a burden on transportation that is lifficult for anybody who is not familiar with it to conceive.

- - 90 - Statement of Donald A. Callahan, Idaho State Chamber of Commerce Mr. Chairman and gentlemen, my name is Donald A. Callahan, Wallace, Idaho.
I am representing the Idaho State Chamber of Commerce at the request of Mr. L.
F. Parsons, the secretary, who is unable to be here to protest the passage of this bill.
Our objections to it are briefly as follows: Idaho is a producing State, producing heavy raw materials, principally, and shipping them to the coast and also to the middle western markets. Those shipments are bulk.
One of our biggest problems is to secure empty cars to carry those products when the season demands. One of the very important things is to have empty cars available, because a considerable portion of these goods, fruits, and so forth, tre perishable and nearly all of them, with the exception of perhaps timber and metals demand an early reaching of the markets, and the markets are long distances away.
Idaho, as a producing State, is largely at the mercy of railroad freight cates. That is our most important thing in our economic life and our objections chen are two: First of all because we need those cars moving in large trains when the season demands, to get early to market. Secondly, we need the empty cars brought to us when the season demands and distributed at the various points.
If there is any interruption in that it is a very material thing and constitutes a very heavy loss.
Consequently, we are interested in the freedom of the railroad companies in being allowed to manage their industry and operate long trains if they see fit to furnish the cars to us and to take the long train loads that go back in season.

CITY MANAGER PLAN of COMMERCE OF UNITED STATES AMERIC THE MUNICIPAL GOVERNMENT HARVARD UNIVERSITY CUREAU FOR RESEARCH IN MUNICIPAL GOVERNMENT HARVARD UNIVERSITY JUL 8 1943 OF GRADUATE SCHOOL PUBLIC ADMINISTRATION A REPORT Compiled by the CIVIC DEVELOPMENT DEPARTMENT Chamber of Commerce of the United States Washington DC e THE CITY MANAGER PLAN OF MUNICIPAL GOVERNMENT A summary of opinions of Chambers of Commerce and others, in a limited mumber of cities in which the City Manager plan is operating, on the success or failure of this form of municipal government in these communities; list of pamphlet material on the City Manager plan; directory of City Manager cities; and a list of cities that have abandoned the City Manager plan.
-Guil decchi pement deftchamber of Commerce of the United States ai Washington, D.C.
ت FOREWORD Requests for information on the City Manager Plan of Municipal Government from Chambers of Commerce and business men have shown a marked increase in the last two years.
To find out how business men appraise this plan, Chamber of Commerce secretaries in City Manager cities were requested to give us the judgment of their business men on the success or failure of this form of municipal government in their communities, including the advantages and disadvantages indicated by their local experience. A summary of this opinion is reproduced in this report, which contains comments from 67 cities.
Most of these expressions are favorable to the City Manager Plan. But it is a fact that a number of cities, which have operated under this plan, have discarded it for some other form of municipal government.
For purpose of reference, there is included here a list of pamphlet material on the City Manager plan, a directory of City Manager cities, and a list of cities that have abandoned the City Manager plan.
F. Stuart Fitzpatrick, Manager Civic Development Department.

THE CITY MANAGER PLAN OF MUNICIPAL GOVERNMENT A Report Compiled by the CHAMBER OF COMMERCE OF THE UNITED STATES Civic Development Department F. Stuart Fitzpatrick, Manager INTRODUCTORY Since When, in 1908, the Mayor and Council of the city of Staunton, Virginia, employed a "General Manager" for the city and passed an ordinance delegating to him administrative detail and responsibility, a new form of municipal government was foreshadowed. It remained, however, for Sumter, South Carolina, in 1911, actually to inaugurate the city manager plan.
that time 430 cities, embracing a population of more than 7,000,000 persons, have adopted this form of city government.
Forty-seven of these cities have populations exceeding 50,000 and fourteen have populations of over 100,000. Thirty-five states in the United States, five Canadian Provinces and Ireland now have cities operating under the council-manager plan.
Fifteen states (Idaho, Indiana, Kansas, Kentucky, Louisiana, Massachusetts, Montana, New Jersey, New York, North Carolina, North Dakota, Ohio, Oklahoma, Virginia and Wisconsin) have passed legislation permitting their cities by simple referendum procedure to adopt the city manager plan. During 1929, 12 states considered legislation on this subject. New Hampshire passed two enabling acts, (Laws of 1929, Chapters 69 and 186) authorizing towns and cities to adopt the council-manager plan.
West Virginia granted a council-manager charter to Logan and amended the Wheeling charter. The state legislature of Washington passed a bill enabling second and third class cities to adopt the council-manager plan by local referenda, which failed to become law because of the Governor's veto.
There follows a summary of opinions of chambers of commerce in a number of cities in which the city manager plan is operating, on the success or failure of this form of municipal government in these communities, a list of pamphlet material on the city manager plan, and a directory of city manager cities.

ARIZONA Phoenix (Pop. 65,000)* Phoenix adopted the city manager plan of government in 1914 and it is believed that the citizens are well satisfied with this form of government although changes in the city administration have resulted in several changes in Unquestionably this form of government has been one of the many city managers.
contributing factors to the remarkable growth of the city.
CALIFORNIA Bakersfield (Pop. 26,179) It is believed that the city's advancement in recent years has been due to the more modern method employed in transacting city affairs and that the considerable sums of money that have been saved to the city can be attributed to the efficiency of the city manager plan. The public in general is well pleased with the results obtained and consider themselves fortunate in securing the services of the type of men who have served as their city managers.
Berkeley (Pop. 81,583) Berkeley.
"We consider the city manager form of government highly successful in Taxes in this city have been kept at a minimum despite a rather large increase in population growth. The city manager form of government is, after all, really business-like administration applied to municipal affairs. It eliminates politics and sets up a high standard of efficiency in governmental departments. Our community is entirely 'sold' on this form of government, and I am confident that it will be maintained indefinitely by our citizens." Population figures are for 1930. Starred (*) are estimated and unstarred are preliminary 1930 census.
 Note - Long Beach (Pop. 168,000)* Long Beach has operated under the city manager-council form of government since the adoption of the charter July 1, 1921.
Since that time the city The population has inhas undergone the greatest development in its history.
creased from 55,000 to 160,000 within this period and extensive public improvements have been accomplished. The business of the city has been systematized and departments coordinated with the result that greater economy and efficiency have been obtained.
"The advantages of the present plan are obvious in that there is a greater centralization of responsibility, less politics and more business-like management in the conduct of our government." Popularity of the plan is evidenced by the fact that three attempts on the part of a minority to change the form of government to the Mayor and Council plan have been unsuccessful.
Sacramento (Pop. 93,685) Business interests of the community are a unit in their approval of It is felt that under no circumstances would this method of city government.
they return to the former regime.
obtained.
San Jose (Pop. 54,547) A definite business administration has been Although experiencing difficulties at its inception, the city manager plan is operating successfully with a local business man possessing executive ability and practical experience as manager. It is believed that the city manager plan has been largely responsible for the city's growth and advancement.
The plan has (1) eliminated at least the most objectionable features of politics from the city hall and schools, (2) coordinated several municipal departments, lowering the cost of their maintenance and increasing their efficiency, (3) : eliminated politics from appointments by means of civil service, (4) installed a budget system, (5) given San Jose a zoning ordinance regulating the growth of the city, (6) created a more active interest in community affairs.
The city is operated at a much less per capita cost than any other city in California; its per capita indebtedness is less than that of any other California city and its per capita tax levy is less with one exception, than any other California city. All of this has been accomplished without sacrificing needed public improvements for the sake of economy.
Santa Rosa (Pop. 16,000)* The city manager plan was adopted by Santa Rosa in 1922, displacing the mayor-council form.
This form of government has been successful, the tax rate has been lowered and many notable improvements have been made for the city.
COLORADO Colorado Springs (Pop. 35,550) The city manager plan has been successful with a capable local business Efficiency among city employees has been obtained.
The manager man as manager.
has given a satisfactory administration saving money through good management.
Examples cited: (1) Laying 400,000 square yards of pavement the first three years after the plan had been adopted at a saving of $225,000 under the lowest bid by contractors.
(2) Building a new central fire station without a bond issue.
(3) Purchasing and rehabilitating the electric and gas departpartments which are now being operated by the city.
(4) Building a storage reservoir for the municipally owned water department at a cost of $250,000 which was financed from earnings of the water department without an increase in water rates.
One of the principal savings is the general reduction in light rates for all classes of service, from 5% to 26%.
FLORIDA Clearwater (Pop. 10,000)* Operating under the commission-manager form of government very satisfactorily but attributes the success of the plan to personnel of the manager and city commissioners. Public is thoroughly satisfied.
get.
The city is working efficiently on approximately 50% of its 1926 budthe city's utilities, consisting of gas and water, make a profit of $33,000 per year above all bonds and charges as compared to considerably less than half that sum in 1926 in spite of the fact that there are not as many users of gas and water at the present time as there were in 1926.
Dunedin (Pop. 2,000)* The city manager plan with the supervision of the police, water works and fire department in the hands of the city manager has been satisfactory. The millage has been cut twice in the past few years, from 10 to 8. The valuation of property has been lowered and the budget system has been installed. The budgets for the years 1927, 1928 and 1929 have been substantially reduced each year as follows: $94,700 72,872 66,145 Through the introduction of machinery and labor saving devices, the number of city employees has been reduced 50%. Public improvements have gone forward, the amount of paving and sidewalks having doubled during the past three years.

: Ft. Lauderdale (Pop. 13,187)* The city adopted the commission-manager form of government in June 1925. Five city commissioners are elected and these five commissioners select one of their number for Mayor.
It is believed that the commission-manager form of government is a great improvement over the old council-mayor form, as all executive matters are handled by one individual and all purchases made by a purchasing agent under the direction of the city manager, effecting a great saving and enabling the administration to reduce taxes during the past year approximately onequarter of a million dollars.
Leesburg (Pop. 4,734)* "Leesburg has had the commission form of government with a city manager in charge for the past six years. It is our belief that very few of our citizens would care to go back to the old council form of municipal administration.
"In 1923, when the present city manager took office, the tax millage was 26. It has since that time been reduced to 18 mills. The municipally owned water, light and ice plants have been rehabilitated and converted from liabilities into assets. The rates have been decreased three times during the six year period." Miami (Pop. 156,000)* Miami has had the commission manager form of government since 1921. The city is operating under a charter which is considered excellent as it is clear, clean-cut and concise, leaving no doubt in the minds of the city officials as to their duties and responsibilities.
The manager plan has been unqualifiedly successful in Miami and has met with the approval of the public.
By means of a strict economic program designed to relieve the heavy tax burden, the 1928-1929 total taxes were reduced 42% under the 1926-1927 budget.


Ocala (Pop. 8,500)* Council-city manager form of municipal government has met with public approval. The advantages of this plan result from the concentration of the executive powers in the hands of the city manager under the direction of the city council, thereby insuring that all activities will be properly cared for.
Punta Gorda (Pop. 3,100)* The city is governed by the commission-manager form which provides for three commissioners, one to be elected each year and the oldest of the three in term of office automatically becomes Mayor.
Through the efforts of the city manager the city is making progress.
Taxes have been reduced and through careful and economical management many thousands of dollars will be saved during the coming year.
Winter Haven (Pop. 7,138)* The city manager form has been in operation since 1924 and has been very popular at all times.
GEORGIA Griffin (Pop. 15,672)* Griffin adopted the commission government in December 1918 with a board of three commissioners and a city manager.
Extensive public improvements have been carried out under the city manager regime and it is confidently believed that the public is very much in favor of this type of municipal government.
IOWA Dubuque (Pop. 41,678) "The city manager plan of government has given the type of service from the City Administration that our citizens demand. The administration has operated in an economic way, and it has responded to our needs and requirements in a manner that is satisfactory to a great majority of our citizens.
"The citizens appreciate the benefit to the community through the city manager plan and would not consider going back to the inefficient method of the older days." In 1914, 59.18 cents of the tax dollar went to support the city government. In 1924, four years after the adoption of the city manager plan, this figure had been reduced to 33 cents.
Mason City (Pop. 25,000)* Mason City, with a non-paid council of business men, is very well satisfied with its two years' experience under the city manager plan.
Among the outstanding accomplishments during this period have been: (1) reduction of outstanding warrants, (2) bonds bought up with a saving in interest and taxes kept level, (3) permanent registration of voters, (4) employment of full time assessor, who has increased the city's revenues through the constant enrollment of new properties, (5) the organization of fire and police departments, (6) purchase of new equipment such as mechanical sweeper, street grader, fire truck, etc.
KANSAS Atchison (Pop. 16,470)* Atchison is operating under the commission-manager form of government which was inaugurated eight years ago and has proven pre-eminently satisfactory.
"At the time the government was taken over by the city manager plan our bonded indebtedness was $1,261,600 and our tax rate was 20 mills in the city.
Since that time we have retired $778, 680 of the original bonded indebtedness, leaving our present debt $482,920, and at no time during the eight years under the city manager form of government was the tax rate held at the twenty-mill point. At the present time our tax rate is 14.45 mills. It is planned to reduce our city indebtedness about $84, 600 in 1930 leaving a balance of $358,320 outstanding and it is also planned to reduce our city tax rate to about 12.45 mills, or a net reduction of two mills.
The other city "Two of the present commissioners have served continuously since they were placed in office in the beginning of the city manager form of government and were re-elected at the last election without opposition.
commissioner has been serving in the place of the former commissioner who held that position, but whose untimely death caused the vacancy. Insofar as Atchison is concerned the city manager form of government is a great advancement over the old political system." Newton (Pop. 10,000)* The city manager form of government has been on trial in Newton for only one year. It is believed that it is popular with a majority of the people.
When the city manager plan was inaugurated the salaries of the commissioners were reduced from $900 per year to $50 a year with the result that a higher type of citizen has been attracted to the office.
Salina (Pop. 19,000)* Salina adopted the commission-manager form of government in 1921.
"According to records, the city of Salina has made more progress during the time that this form of government has been in operation than during all previous years combined and at the same time has received more for the dollar spent.
"While there has been some increase in taxes, it is due to the many improvements which the city has made, such as, the building of a very large public auditorium, sewerage disposal plant, pumping station, adding fire equipment and the improvement of parks and playgrounds. The city has also acquired the water plant, which has shown a profit of from $25,000 to $70,000 a year, this money going back into the improvement of this utility. The city at the present time has one of the lowest tax rates in the state, notwithstanding the many improvements and the additions to the various city departments." Wichita (Pop. 110,000)* "This city has enjoyed the commission-manager form of government since 1916, and under that form has thrived and flourished and developed from a city which then had a population of 53,148 until today we have a population of 115,000.
"Since 1916 wages and salaries have enjoyed a normal increase in municipal government, as well as in every business. The normal and natural growth of our city has made it necessary to add police and firemen, and in addition to these, many new and valuable public services are being performed by the city which were not performed under the old regime. In spite of these things our tax levy has been increased by only 2.3 mills." Winfield (Pop. 12,903)* Winfield adopted the commission-manager plan in 1921. The three commissioners elected at large are a Board of Directors and employ the city manager.
This makes the commissioners responsible to the people for the policies and ordinances of the administration. The manager is responsible only to the commission and the other city employees are responsible to the manager.
At the time of the adoption of the plan the tax rate was 10.75 mills.
This rate has been slowly reduced until during 1929, the rate for city purposes was 5.90 mills, the lowest tax rate of any first or second class city in Kansas.
The city is in splendid financial condition with around $400,000 in cash and securities in the treasury. Extensive public improvements have been carried out during the past two years, such as, new city hall, construction of buildings at the fair grounds, without additional tax levy or bond issue. The city owns its public utilities, water, light and gas.

: .

An attempt to overthrow the city manager plan and return to the old form of government was defeated in September 1929 by a small majority.
For use in that campaign a pamphlet was issued to furnish facts regarding the income, expenses and work done for the five years of council-manager government ending December, 1928, as compared with the last five years of the old form of city government, 1919 to 1923. This pamphlet summarizes the principal accomplishments during the first five years of the council-manager form of city government as follows: Taxes equalized thru revaluation.
A sound and economical program for financing the city and retirement of city debt.
An increase of 20% in street maintenance.
An increase of 400% in permanent street work.
City sewer program materially increased.
Greater number of streets accepted and improved.
Increased standard of school maintenance.
Increased yearly contributions to Public Library.
Increased demand for care of city's poor and higher standards of relief maintained.
Police Department -increased pay traffic system installed.
Fire Department completely motorized salaries increased, improved fire alarm system -- more hydrants -- more time off for men.
Park System extended and improved.
Recreation Department -- additional playgrounds purchased in Back Bay and Fore Street districts.
Improved and extended street lighting systems.
MASSACHUSETTS Fall River (Pop. 134,300)* The city manager plan has not been in operation in Fall River long enough to pass upon its merits.
Norwood (Pop. 12,627)* The town manager plan of government was adopted by Norwood in 1915 and T: has been in operation ever since. It has been successful in this city and it is believed to be the best type for a town the size of Norwood.
The plan has been in operation long enough to determine its defects and a committee is now working to bring about certain needed changes indicated by the fifteen years of experience.
The town manager plan has enabled the town to obtain the services of high grade men in the city government, which would not have been possible under the old form.
Although successful and considered the logical method of handling governmental activities, it is pointed out that the success of this plan and of every other form of city government depends upon the type of men whom the citizens select to serve them.
MICHIGAN Grand Haven (Pop. 9,000)* The progressiveness of the city has greatly increased since the introduction of the city manager form. It is the consensus of opinion that the concentration of responsibilities of management in the hands of one man is far superior to the old aldermanic form of government.
Since its introduction a filtration plant has been acquired, a new street lighting system installed, extensive street paving improvements carried out and a better control of the city's finances put into operation. Ward politics have been eliminated.
Grand Rapids (Pop. 185,809)* The commission-manager form was adopted in 1917. During the last ten years the city has grown faster than in any previous decade.
This form of government has attracted high grade men to serve the community on the city commission. It is believed that the administration of the city government has been efficient and has saved considerable sums of money. There is no desire on the part of any substantial interests to revert to the old form of government.
Jackson (Pop. 54,870) The commission-manager form of government in Jackson has served to eliminate political factions to a great extent in the operation of the city's affairs. The city manager form is popular and it is the consensus of opinion that this city would never return to the old aldermanic form.
Kalamazoo (Pop. 54,707) Kalamazoo has had the commission-manager form of government since 1918.
At its installation there was a need of public improvements, insufficient operating funds, and a low tax rate. With the introduction of the new form of government, which departmentalized activities, there was organized a first class budget and purchasing department. The budget is strictly adhered to and public utilities are charged with all proper expenses.
The city went on a pay-as-you-go system in 1918 and since then only one or two small bond issues have been made. The per capita city government indebtedness for all purposes is now about $1.00 per capita (not including schools and not including special assessment indebtedness). The city is in good condition; it has no operating deficit. The public is well pleased with this form of government.
With the introduction of the city manager form of government the tax rate rose from nine to thirteen mills. This rate dropped back to eleven mills where it has remained for three or four years.
Muskegon (Pop. 41,338) Muskegon changed to the commission-manager plan in 1921. Several outstanding benefits have resulted. The city's affairs have been administered in a businesslike manner. Taxes have increased somewhat but services have been correspondingly multiplied. The city is not only on a pay-as-you-go basis but has accumulated a reserve of several hundred thousand dollars.
An outstanding program of public works has been carried through, including major storm sewer system, modern street paving, and such new services added as snow removal, garbage disposal and collection, infant welfare clinic, etc.
Political expediency in the construction of public works has been replaced by a carefully planned program, extravagance in the conduct of city affairs by a sane and economical administration and reasonable continuity of service by outstanding business men has been achieved.
Otsego (Pop. 3,200)* It is believed that the city manager form of government is more efficient and economical and facilitates the handling of governmental matters with less delay and red tape. The public is well pleased, as evidenced by defeat of a petition to return to the old form by vote of 3 to 1.
Pontiac (Pop. 65,544) Pontiac has operated under the city manager form for the last nine years and during the preceding nine years was under the commission form of municipal government. Each of those eighteen has been closed with substantial cash balances in the budget funds and not once in the eighteen years has the city lapsed in its payment of obligations, nor has it refunded a single bond nor found it necessary during that period to borrow in anticipation of taxes or for any other purpose to meet operating expenses.
The city manager plan has eliminated "politics," "territorial ambitions" and the log rolling so often found in the ward system.
Sault Ste. Marie (Pop. 14,175)* The city has been operating under the commission-manager form of government since 1917. It is the consensus of opinion that this plan of government is the nearest approach possible to eliminating politics from city government.

The bonded indebtedness of the city has been reduced every year since the city manager plan was adopted until in 1929 it was only approximately $120,000.
MINNESOTA White Bear Lake (Pop. 2,022)* The city manager plan encountered many difficulties shortly after its introduction in White Bear Lake. At the city election in the spring of 1928 a new administration and city manager were put in charge.
Since that time popularity of this form of government has increased and it is believed that it will function satisfactorily.
MISSOURI Kansas City (Pop. 457,000)* The July 3, 1929, issue of "Kansas City's Public Affairs," weekly bulletin of the Kansas City Public Service Institute, 501 Orear-Leslie Building, Kansas City, Missouri, is devoted to a discussion of "Three Years of Councilmanager Government in Kansas City." This booklet presents a review of the first three years of council-manager government in Kansas City, pointing out both the successes and failures of the city manager plan in that city.
NEW MEXICO Albuquerque (Pop. 35,350) The city manager-commission form of government has been eminently successful in Albuquerque and not once since its inauguration has there been any question of changing back to the aldermanic form.
NEBRASKA Alliance (Pop. 7,500)* Since the adoption of the city manager form of government in Alliance in 1921, the keeping of public records has been systematized. A detailed report : of the city's business is made annually.
The duties and responsibilities of each department have been definitely fixed, a centralized purchasing system installed and through cash discounts, it has been possible to save almost enough to pay the total bill for stationery and printing for all departments.
The street department has been reorganized, modern equipment has been purchased and the street superintendent selected by virtue of his qualifications for the position.
Before adopting the city manager plan the water department was operating at a loss. It has since been put on a self-supporting basis and is earning a small profit for the city.
Before 1921 annual write-off of accounts uncollectible ran into the thousands of dollars.
averaged $100 per year.
Uncollectible accounts for the past four years have not Extensive improvements in the organization of the police department, such as, the installation of the finger print system, simple but complete identification records, provision for instruction and training personnel, etc., have been accomplished under the city manager plan.
During the eight year period immediately preceding the adoption of the city manager plan only $15,000 of the general bonded debt was paid off as compared to $158,000 during the first eight years under the plan.
NEW YORK Auburn (Pop. 36,481) Public is very well satisfied with the city manager form and though there have been no unusual savings under this plan, the centralized management of municipal affairs has resulted in greater efficiency.

NORTH CAROLINA Greensboro (Pop. 53,422) Greensboro has made progress since the adoption of the city manager plan. It is believed that the confidence of the public in this form of government has been responsible for their willingness to vote large amounts of money for physical improvements. Under the city manager plan it has been possible to induce the highest type of men to stand for council.
Extensive public improvements have been carried through in the paving of many miles of streets, grade separations, enlargement of water supply, etc.
High Point (Pop. 36,708) Under the city manager plan, the work of the various departments of city government has been systematized, budget system established and great improvements obtained in the collection of taxes and special assessments.
public is very well pleased with this form of government.
Rocky Mount (Pop. 23,000)* The Majority of people of Rocky Mount prefer the city manager plan of government to any other plan. The finances of the city are in good condition.
OHIO Ashtabula (Pop. 27,500)* The city manager form is considered superior in every way to any other form of municipal government. Its popularity was demonstrated at several past elections where proposals initiated to change the city charter to the old mayorcouncil form were overwhelmingly defeated. Greater efficiency in the administration of the city's affairs has been obtained.
Cleveland (Pop. 1,242,000)* Three attempts have been made to overthrow the city manager form of government in Cleveland. At first election the plan won by about 7,000 votes, the second time by about 2,300 votes, and the third time by a little over 3,000 votes.
Hamilton (Pop. 52,108) Hamilton.
The public is satisfied with the city manager form of government in This form was adopted in January, 1928, and the city's tax rate has dropped from $9.43 in 1927 to $8.63 in 1930.
When the present administration took control in 1928 the Gas Department was encumbered with $164,000 indebtedness and operating at a deficit of $4,000 per month. To date all current bills have been paid and a substantial amount of the indebtedness has been cancelled and gas rates reduced.
At the end of the first few years under the city manager plan the operating costs of the Electric Department had been reduced 25% in spite of the increase of approximately 25% in the number of consumers. Net earnings in this department alone for 1928 were more than $100,000 with its anticipated earnings for 1929 reaching nearly $400,000.
With the installation of a Budgetary Control Accounting System, through which it was possible to reduce the clerical force in the utilities office, a saving of more than $11,000 per year was effected.
A Bureau of Purchases, set up in 1928, saved during the first eleven months of its existence approximately $1,800 in cash discounts.
Springfield (Pop. 200,000)* The city manager form of government has become firmly established in Fort Worth and it is believed that less than 10% of the people would consent to change to any other form of government.
Among the outstanding accomplishments in Ft. Worth under the city manager regime the following are cited: (1) During the first year a delinquent tax collection drive was initiated with the result that $739,198,57 in delinquent taxes was collected. A tax revaluation survey was conducted and the unit system of equalized tax evaluation was put in operation which in the first year repaid the people for the cost of the work.
(2) The methods of accounting have been thoroughly re-organized.
(3) Materials and supplies are now purchased on a competitive basis.
It is estimated that centralized purchasing saves the taxpayers of Forth Worth from 10% to 20% on all purchases made. A saving of more than $10,000 a year has been accomplished through the erection of gasoline tanks, in order that gasoline may be purchased in carload lots.
(4) The cost of paving has been reduced 20% although the thickness of the concrete has been increased from five to six inches. A conservative estimate of the money saved on street paving for the four years is in excess of $161,000.
By standardizing monolithic sewer sections, $116, 250 has been saved.
bids on pipe requirements and a closer supervision have saved $123,680 in the installation of sanitary sewers.
Competitive (5) Garbage collection has been extended to the outlying districts and, in spite of this, the cost is cheaper by approximately $50 per day.
(6) The reduction of electric light rates is saving the city $30,000 per year and domestic consumers $173,000.
(7) Efficiency in the fire and police departments has been brought about through the introduction of the civil service, the establishment of the police school and fire prevention bureau.
(8) Welfare work, formerly maintained through contributions from the public, is now handled as a municipal function, with an appropriation of approximately $100,000 per year, without increasing the tax rate, (9) In the 43 years prior to 1925, 192.85 miles of water mains were laid. Since June 1, 1925, 193.35 miles have been laid. Cast iron pipe worth $57,000 has been reclaimed. There has been an increase of 12,701 in the number of water consumers since 1925.
Sherman (Pop. 21,000)* "Sherman's present charter was adopted in 1915 It was a very radical change from the old aldermanic form of government to the modern Council-Commissionmanager Plan. The City Manager is appointed by the Commission subject to the approval of the Council. The department heads are appointed by the Manager and are subject to him.
are many. Ashburner and passed an ordinance giving him all administrative detail and responsibility and the title of General Manager.

The city manager plan has worked out successfully, resulting in economy The public is thoroughly satisfied.
in city affairs and increased efficiency.
WEST VIRGINIA Bluefield (Pop. 25,000)* There is no question in the minds of the public as to the desirability of the city manager plan. Bluefield has completed eight entirely satisfactory years under the city manager plan.
"An extensive improvement program has been carried through; the city's affairs are run on a business and efficiency basis and in the last election directors were selected for their business ability rather than their political affiliations." Clarksburg (Pop. 28,831) "Under the city manager form, political factions have been greatly reduced in our city government and the affairs of our city are handled by a trained executive in as efficient and systematic a manner as those of any private business institution. Investigation of the charter or ordinance establishing the plan in these cities revealed that the manager had very limited powers, and they were not included in the official directory.

6,000 - The following cities have been reported as having operated under the city manager plan and later abandoned the plan for some other form of municipal government. All of these cases have not been investigated and the list must not be considered authentic. A partial investigation disclosed that in some instances the City Council adopted an ordinance providing for a city manager and this ordinance was later rescinded by a new Council unfavorable to the city manager idea. In some cases, the plan was declared unconstitutional after adoption. Two or three cities lost their city manager charter on being annexed to or consolidated with neighboring communities. There are included also some cities where the city manager's office was combined with that of the mayor and a mayor-manager plan put into effect.
(Directory of City Manager cities and above list revised, 1932.) COMMERCE OF CHAMBER OF THE UNITED STATES OF THE CITY MANAGER PLAN OF MUNICIPAL GOVERNMENT HARVARD UNIVERSITA JUL 8 1943 OF GRADUATE SCHOOL PUBLIC ADMINISTRATION Ser A REPORT Compiled by the CIVIC DEVELOPMENT DEPARTMENT CHAMBER OF COMMERCE OF THE UNITED STATES WASHINGTON, D. C.

THE CITY MANAGER PLAN OF MUNICIPAL GOVERNMENT A summary of opinions of Chambers of Commerce and others, in a limited number of cities in which the City Manager plan is operating, on the success or failure of this form of municipal government in these communities; list of pamphlet material on the City Manager plan; directory of City Manager cities; and a list of cities that have abandoned the City Manager plan.
Gere to left 7 Chamber of Commerce of the United States-in Washington, D.C.

FOREWORD Requests for information on the City Manager Plan of Municipal Government from Chambers of Commerce and business men have shown a marked increase in the last two years.
To find out how business men appraise this plan, Chamber of Commerce secretaries in City Manager cities were requested to give us the judgment of their business men on the success or failure of this form of municipal government in their communities, including the advantages and disadvantages indicated by their local experience. A summary of this opinion is reproduced in this report, which contains comments from 67 cities.
Most of these expressions are favorable to the City Manager Plan. But it is a fact that a number of cities, which have operated under this plan, have discarded it for some other form of municipal government.
For purpose of reference, there is included here a list of pamphlet material on the City Manager plan, a directory of City Manager cities, and a list of cities that have abandoned the City Manager plan.
F. Stuart Fitzpatrick, Manager, Civic Development Department.

THE CITY MANAGER PLAN OF MUNICIPAL GOVERNMENT A Report Compiled by the CHAMBER OF COMMERCE OF THE UNITED STATES Civic Development Department F. Stuart Fitzpatrick, Manager INTRODUCTORY When, in 1908, the Mayor and Council of the city of Staunton, Virginia, employed a "General Manager" for the city and passed an ordinance delegating to him administrative detail and responsibility, a new form of municipal government was foreshadowed. It remained, however, for Sumter, South Carolina, in 1911, actually to inaugurate the city manager plan. Since that time 430 cities, embracing a population of more than 7,000,000 persons, have adopted this form of city government.
Forty-seven of these cities have populations exceeding 50,000 and fourteen have populations of over 100,000. Thirty-five states in the United States, five Canadian Provinces and Ireland now have cities operating under the council-manager plan.
Fifteen states (Idaho, Indiana, Kansas, Kentucky, Louisiana, Massachusetts, Montana, New Jersey, New York, North Carolina, North Dakota, Ohio, Oklahoma, Virginia and Wisconsin) have passed legislation permitting their cities by simple referendum procedure to adopt the city manager plan. During 1929, 12 states considered legislation on this subject. New Hampshire passed two enabling acts, (Laws of 1929, Chapters 69 and 186) authorizing towns and cities to adopt the council-manager plan.
West Virginia granted a council-manager charter to Logan and amended the Wheeling charter. The state legislature of Washington passed a bill enabling second and third class cities to adopt the council-manager plan by local referenda, which failed to become law because of the Governor's veto.
There follows a summary of opinions of chambers of commerce in a number of cities in which the city manager plan is operating, on the success or failure of this form of municipal government in these communities, a list of pamphlet material on the city manager plan, and a directory of city manager cities.

ARIZONA Phoenix (Pop. 65,000)* Phoenix adopted the city manager plan of government in 1914 and it is believed that the citizens are well satisfied with this form of government although changes in the city administration have resulted in several changes in city managers.
Unquestionably this form of government has been one of the many contributing factors to the remarkable growth of the city.
CALIFORNIA Bakersfield (Pop. 26,179) It is believed that the city's advancement in recent years has been due to the more modern method employed in transacting city affairs and that the considerable sums of money that have been saved to the city can be attributed to the efficiency of the city manager plan. The public in general is well pleased with the results obtained and consider themselves fortunate in securing the services of the type of men who have served as their city managers.
Berkeley (Pop. 81,583) "We consider the city manager form of government highly successful in Berkeley. Taxes in this city have been kept at a minimum despite a rather large increase in population growth. The city manager form of government is, after all, really business-like administration applied to municipal affairs. It eliminates politics and sets up a high standard of efficiency in governmental departments.
Our community is entirely 'sold' on this form of government, and I am confident that it will be maintained indefinitely by our citizens." *Note Population figures are for 1930. Starred (*) are estimated and unstarred are preliminary 1930 census.

CALIFORNIA CONT'D - Jong Beach (Pop. 168,000)* Long Beach has operated under the city manager-council form of government since the adoption of the charter July 1, 1921. Since that time the city has undergone the greatest development in its history. The population has increased from 55,000 to 160,000 within this period and extensive public improvements have been accomplished. The business of the city has been systematized and departments coorlinated with the result that greater economy and efficiency have been obtained.
"The advantages of the present plan are obvious in that there is a greater centralization of responsibility, less politics and more business-like management in the conduct of our government." Popularity of the plan is evidenced by the fact that three attempts on the part of a minority to change the form of government to the Mayor and Council plan have been unsuccessful.
Sacramento (Pop. 93,685) Business interests of the community are a unit in their approval of this method of city government. It is felt that under no circumstances would they return to the former regime. A definite business administration has been obtained.
San Jose (Pop. 54,547) Although experiencing difficulties at its inception, the city manager plan is operating successfully with a local business man possessing executive ability and practical experience as manager. It is believed that the city manager plan has been largely responsible for the city's growth and advancement. The plan has (1) eliminated at least the most objectionable features of politics from the city hall and schools, (2) coordinated several municipal departments, lowering the cost of their maintenance and increasing their efficiency, (3) eliminated politics from appointments by means of civil service, (4) installed a budget system, (5) given San Jose a zoning ordinance regulating the growth of the city, (6) created a more active interest in community affairs.
The city is operated at a much less per capita cost than any other city In California; its per capita indebtedness is less than that of any other California city and its per capita tax levy is less with one exception, than any other Califoria city. All of this has been accomplished without sacrificing needed public im- >rovements for the sake of economy.
anta Rosa (Pop. 16,000)* The city manager plan was adopted by Santa Rosa in 1922, displacing the ayor-council form.
This form of government has been successful, the tax rate has been lowered nd many notable improvement's have been made for the city.
COLORADO olorado Springs (Pop. 35,550) The city manager plan has been successful with a capable local business an as manager. Efficiency among city employees has been obtained. The manager has iven a satisfactory administration saving money through good management. Examples ited: (1) Laying 400,000 square yards of pavement the first three years after the plan had been adopted at a saving of $225,000 under the lowest bid by contractors.
(2) Building a new central fire station without a bond issue.
(3) Purchasing and rehabilitating the electric and gas departments which are now being operated by the city.
(4) Building a storage reservoir for the municipally owned water department at a cost of $250,000 which was financed from earnings of the water department without an increase in water rates.

One of the principal savings is the general reduction in light rates for all classes of service, from 5% to 26%.
FLORIDA Clearwater (Pop. 10,000)* Operating under the commission-manager form of government very satisfactorly but attributes the success of the plan to personnel of the manager and city compublic is thoroughly satisfied.
issioners.
The city is working efficiently on approximately 50% of its 1926 budget.
The city's utilities, consisting of gas and water, make a profit of $33,000 per year bove all bonds and charges as compared to considerably less than half that sum in .926 in spite of the fact that there are not as many users of gas and water at the resent time as there were in 1926.
Dunedin (Pop. 2,000)* The city manager plan with the supervision of the police, water works and ire department in the hands of the city manager has been satisfactory. The millage as been cut twice in the past few years, from 10 to 8.
as been lowered and the budget system has been installed.
The valuation of property The budgets for the ears 1927, 1928 and 1929 have been substantially reduced each year as follows: $94,700 72,872 66,145 Through the introduction of machinery and labor saving devices, the number f city employees has been reduced 50%. Public improvements have gone forward, the mount of paving and sidewalks having doubled during the past three years.
t. Lauderdale (Pop. 13,187)* The city adopted the commission-manager form of government in June 1925.
ive city commissioners are elected and these five commissioners select one of their umber for Mayor.

 It is believed that the commission-manager form of government is a great improvement over the old council-mayor form, as all executive matters are handled by one individual and all purchases made by a purchasing agent under the direction of the city manager, effecting a great saving and enabling the administration to reluce taxes during the past year approximately one-quarter of a million dollars.
Leesburg (Pop. 4,734)* "Leesburg has had the commission form of government with a city manager in harge for the past six years. It is our belief that very few of our citizens would are to go back to the old council form of municipal administration.
6.
"In 1923, when the present city manager took office, the tax millage was It has since that time been reduced to 18 mills. The municipally owned water, ight and ice plants have been rehabilitated and converted from liabilities into ssets. The rates have been decreased three times during the six year period." iami (Pop. 156,000)* The Miami has had the commission manager form of government since 1921.
ity is operating under a charter which is considered excellent as it is clear, lean-cut and concise, leaving no doubt in the minds of the city officials as to heir duties and responsibilities.
The manager plan has been unqualifiedly successful in Miami and has met ith the approval of the public.
By means of a strict economic program designed to relieve the heavy tax arden, the 1928-1929 total taxes were reduced 42% under the 1926-1927 budget.
cala (Pop. 8,500)* Council-city manager form of municipal government has met with public pproval. The advantages of this plan result from the concentration of the execuive powers in the hands of the city manager under the direction of the city council, hereby insuring that all activities will be properly cared for.

FLORIDA CONT'D unta Gorda (Pop. 3,100)* The city is governed by the commission-manager form which provides for hree commissioners, one to be elected each year and the oldest of the three in erm of office automatically becomes Mayor.
Through the efforts of the city manager the city is making progress. Taxes ave been reduced and through careful and economical management many thousands of ollars will be saved during the coming year.
inter Haven (Pop. 7,138)* The city manager form has been in operation since 1924 and has been very popular at all times.
GEORGIA riffin (Pop. 15,672)* Griffin adopted the commission government in December 1918 with a board of aree commissioners and a city manager.
Extensive public improvements have been carried out under the city manager gime and it is confidently believed that the public is very much in favor of this pe of municipal government.
I OWA buque (Pop. 41,678) "The city manager plan of government has given the type of service from e City Administration that our citizens demand. The administration has operated an economic way, and it has responded to our needs and requirements in a manner at is satisfactory to a great majority of our citizens.
"The citizens appreciate the benefit to the community through the city nager plan and would not consider going back to the inefficient method of the der days." ital ..
: In 1914, 59.18 cents of the tax dollar went to support the city government.
In 1924, four years after the adoption of the city manager plan, this figure had been reduced to 38 cents.
Mason City (Pop. 25,000)* Mason City, with a non-paid council of business men, is very well satisfied with its two years' experience under the city manager plan.
Among the outstanding accomplishments during this period have been: (1) reduction of outstanding warrants, (2) bonds bought up with a saving in interest and taxes kept level, (3) permanent registration of voters, (4) employment of full time assessor, who has increased the city's revenues through the constant enrollment of new properties, (5) the organization of fire and police departments, (6) purchase of new equipment such as mechanical sweeper, street grader, fire truck, etc.
KANSAS Atchison (Pop. 16,470)* Atchison is operating under the commission-manager form of government which was inaugurated eight years ago and has proven pre-eminently satisfactory.
"At the time the government was taken over by the city manager plan our bonded indebtedness was $1,261,600 and our tax rate was 20 mills in the city. Since that time we have retired $778,680 of the original bonded indebtedness, leaving our present debt $482,920, and at no time during the eight years under the city manager form of government was the tax rate held at the twenty-mill point. At the present time our tax rate is 14.45 mills. It is planned to reduce our city indebtedness about $84,600 in 1930 leaving a balance of $358,320 outstanding and it is also planned to reduce our city tax rate to about 12.45 mills, or a net reduction of two mills.
"Two of the present commissioners have served continuously since they were placed in office in the beginning of the city manager form of government and were re-elected at the last election without opposition. The other city commissioner has een serving in the place of the former commissioner who held that position, but 10se untimely death caused the vacancy. Insofar as Atchison is concerned the city anager form of government is a great advancement over the old political system." ewton (Pop. 10,000)* le year.
The city manager form of government has been on trial in Newton for only It is believed that it is popular with a majority of the people. When le city manager plan was inaugurated the salaries of the commissioners were reduced rom $900 per year to $50 a year with the result that a higher type of citizen has en attracted to the office.
lina (Pop. 19,000)* Salina adopted the commission-manager form of government in 1921.
"According to records, the city of Salina has made more progress during he time that this form of government has been in operation than during all previous ears combined and at the same time has received more for the dollar spent.
"While there has been some increase in taxes, it is due to the many imovements which the city has made, such as, the building of a very large public ditorium, sewerage disposal plant, pumping station, adding fire equipment and the provement of parks and playgrounds. The city has also acquired the water plant, ich has shown a profit of from $25,000 to $70,000 a year, this money going back to the improvement of this utility. The city at the present time has one of the west tax rates in the state, notwithstanding the many improvements and the addions to the various city departments." chita (Pop. 110,000)* "This city has enjoyed the commission-manager form of government since 16, and under that form has thrived and flourished and developed from a city which en had a population of 53,148 until today we have a population of 115,000.
"Since 1916 wages and salaries have enjoyed a normal increase in municipal vernment, as well as in every business. The normal and natural growth of our city has made it necessary to add police and firemen, and in addition to these, many new and valuable public services are being performed by the city which were not performed under the old regime. In spite of these things our tax levy has been increased by only 2.3 mills." Winfield (Pop. 12,903)* This Winfield adopted the commission-manager plan in 1921. The three commissi oners elected at large are a Board of Directors and employ the city manager.
makes the commissioners responsible to the people for the policies and ordinances of the administration. The manager is responsible only to the commission and the other city employees are responsible to the manager.
At the time of the adoption of the plan the tax rate was 10.75 mills..
This rate has been slowly reduced until during 1929, the rate for city purposes was 5.90 mills, the lowest tax rate of any first or second class city in Kansas. The city is in splendid financial condition with around $400,000 in cash and securities in the treasury. Extensive public improvements have been carried out during the past two years, such as, new city hall, construction of buildings at the fair grounds, without additional tax levy or bond issue. The city owns its public utilities, water, light and gas.
MAINE Portland (Pop. 70,452) The city manager form of government was adopted in Portland in 1923. An attempt to overthrow the city manager plan and return to the old form of government was defeated in September 1929 by a small majority. For use in that campaign a pamphlet was issued to furnish facts regarding the income, expenses and work done for the five years of council-manager government ending December, 1928, as compared with the last five years of the old form of city government, 1919 to 1923.
pamphlet summarizes the principal accomplishments during the first five years of the council-manager form of city government as follows: This Taxes equalized thru revaluation.
A sound and economical program for financing the city and retirement of city debt.
An increase of 400% in permanent street work.
An increase of 20% in street maintenance.
City sewer program materially increased.
Greater number of streets accepted and improved.
Increased standard of school maintenance.
Increased yearly contributions to Public Library.
Increased demand for care of city's poor and higher standards of relief maintained.
Police Department - increased pay -- traffic system installed.
Fire Department completely motorized salaries increased, improved fire alarm system -- more hydrants -- more time off for men.
Park System extended and improved.
Recreation Department -- additional playgrounds purchased in Back Bay and Fore Street districts.
Improved and extended street lighting systems.
MASSACHUSETTS Fall River (Pop. 134,300)* The city manager plan has not been in operation in Fall River long enough to pass upon its merits.
Norwood (Pop. 12,627)* The town manager plan of government was adopted by Norwood in 1915 and has been in operation ever since. It has been successful in this city and it is believed to be the best type for a town the size of Norwood.
The plan has been in operation long enough to determine its defects and a committee is now working to bring about certain needed changes indicated by the fifteen years of experience.
The town manager plan has enabled the town to obtain the services of high grade men in the city government, which would not have been possible under the old form.
Although successful and considered the logical method of handling governmental activities, it is pointed out that the success of this plan and of every other form of city government depends upon the type of men whom the citizens select to serve them.

MICHIGAN Grand Haven (Pop. 9,000)* The progressiveness of the city has greatly increased since the introduccion of the city manager form. It is the consensus of opinion that the concentraion of responsibilities of management in the hands of one man is far superior to he old aldermanic form of government. Since its introduction a filtration plant as been acquired, a new street lighting system installed, extensive street paving mprovements carried out and a better control of the cities finances put into operaward politics have been eliminated.
-ion.
rand Rapids (Pop. 185,809)* The commission-manager form was adopted in 1917. During the last ten ears the city has grown faster than in any previous decade.
This form of government has attracted high grade men to serve the community on the city commission. It is believed that the administration of the city governent has been efficient and has saved considerable sums of money. There is no deire on the part of any substantial interests to revert to the old form of governent.
ackson (Pop. 54,870) The commission-manager form of government in Jackson has served to eliminte political factions to a great extent in the operation of the city's affairs.
he city manager form is popular and it is the consensus of opinion that this city ould never return to the old aldermanic form.
alamazoo (Pop. 54,707) Kalamazoo has had the commission-manager form of government since 1918.
t its installation there was a need of public improvements, insufficient operating unds, and a low tax rate. With the introduction of the new form of government, hich departmentalized activities, there was organized a first class budget and urchasing department. The budget. is strictly adhered to and public utilities are harged with all proper expenses.

The city went on a pay-as-you-go system in 1918 and since then only one or two small bond issues have been made. The per capita city government indebtedness for all purposes is now about $1.00 per capita (not including schools and not including special assessment indebtedness). The city is in good condition; it has no operating deficit. The public is well pleased with this form of government.
With the introduction of the city manager form of government the tax rate rose from nine to thirteen mills. This rate dropped back to eleven mills where it has remained for three or four years.
Muskegon (Pop. 41,338) Muskegon changed to the commission-manager plan in 1921. Several outstand ing benefits have resulted. The city's affairs have been administered in a businesslike manner. Taxes have increased somewhat but services have been correspondingly multiplied. The city is not only on a pay-as-you-go basis but has accumulated a reserve of several hundred thousand dollars.
An outstanding program of public works has been carried through, including major storm sewer system, modern street paving, and such new services added as snow removal, garbage disposal and collection, infant welfare clinic, etc.
Political expediency in the construction of public works has been replaced by a carefully planned program, extravagance in the conduct of city affairs by a sane and economical administration and reasonable continuity of service by outstanding business men has been achieved.
Otsego (Pop. 3,200)* It is believed that the city manager form of government is more efficient and economical and facilitates the handling of governmental matters with less delay and red tape. The public is well pleased, as evidenced by defeat of a petition to return to the old form by vate of 3 to 1.

MICHIGAN CONT D Pontiac (Pop. 65,544) Pontiac has operated under the city manager form for the last nine years and during the preceding nine years was under the commission form of municipal government. Each of those eighteen has been closed with substantial cash balances in the budget funds and not once in the eighteen years has the city lapsed in its payment of obligations, nor has it refunded a single bond nor found it necessary during that period to borrow in anticipation of taxes or for any other purpose to meet operating expenses.
The city manager plan has eliminated "politics," "territorial ambitions" and the log rolling so often found in the ward system.
Sault Ste. Marie (Pop. 14,175)* The city has been operating under the commission-manager form of government since 1917. It is the consensus of opinion that this plan of government is the nearest approach possible to eliminating politics from city government.
The bonded indebtedness of the city has been reduced every year since the city manager plan was adopted until in 1929 it was only approximately $120,000.
MINNESOTA White Bear Lake (Pop. 2,022)* The city manager plan encountered many difficulties shortly after its introduction in White Bear Lake. At the city election in the spring of 1928 a new administration and city manager were put in charge. Since that time popularity of this form of government has increased and it is believed that it will function satisfactorily.

MISSOURI Kansas City (Pop. 457,000)* The July 3, 1929, issue of "Kansas City's Public Affairs," weekly bulletin of the Kansas City Public Service Institute, 501 Orear-Leslie Building, Kansas City, Missouri, is devoted to a discussion of "Three Years of Council-Manager Government in Kansas City." This booklet presents a review of the first three years of council-manager government in Kansas City, pointing out both the successes and failures of the city manager plan in that city.
NEW MEXICO Albuquerque (Pop. 35,350) The city manager-commission form of government has been eminently successful in Albuquerque and not once since its inauguration has there been any question of changing back to the aldermanic form.
NEBRASKA Alliance (Pop. 7,500)* Since the adoption of the city manager form of government in Alliance in A detailed report of 1921, the keeping of public records has been systematized.
the city's business is made annually. The duties and responsibilities of each department have been definitely fixed, a centralized purchasing system installed and through cash discounts, it has been possible to save almost enough to pay the total bill for stationery and printing for all departments.
The street department has been reorganized, modern equipment has been purchased and the street superintendent selected by virtue of his qualifications for the position.
Before adopting the city manager plan the water department was operating at a loss. It has since been put on a self-supporting basis and is earning a small profit for the city.

NEBRASKA CONT'D Alliance (Cont'd) Before 1921 annual write-off of accounts uncollectible ran into the thousands of dollars. Uncollectible accounts for the past four years have not averaged $100 per year.
Extensive improvements in the organization of the police department, such as, the installation of the finger print system, simple but complete identification records, provision for instruction and training personnel, etc., have been accomplished under the city manager plan.
During the eight year period immediately preceding the adoption of the city manager plan only $15,000 of the general bonded debt was paid off as compared Co $158,000 during the first eight years under the plan.
NEW YORK uburn (Pop. 36,481) Public is very well satisfied with the city manager form and though there ave been no unusual savings under this plan, the centralized management of municipal affairs has resulted in greater efficiency.
NORTH CAROLINA reensboro (Pop. 53,422) Greensboro has made progress since the adoption of the city manager plan, t is believed that the confidence of the public in this form of government has been responsible for their willingness to vote large amounts of money for physical Under the city manager plan it has been possible to induce the highmprovements.

Extensive public improvements have been carried through in the paving of many miles of streets, grade separations, enlargement of water supply, etc.

NORTH CAROLINA-CONT D High Point (Pop. 36,708) Under the city manager plan, the work of the various departments of city 5overnment has been systematized, budget system established and great improvements obtained in the collection of taxes and special assessments. The public is very well pleased with this form of government.
Rocky Mount (Pop. 23,000)* Majority of people of Rocky Mount prefer the city manager plan of government to any other plan. The finances of the city are in good condition.
OHIO Ashtabula (Pop. 27,500)* The city manager form is considered superior in every way to any other form of municipal government. Its popularity was demonstrated at several past elections where proposals initiated to change the city charter to the old mayorcouncil form were overwhelmingly defeated.
