Cleveland (Pop. 1,242,000)* Greater efficiency in the administration Three attempts have been made to overthrow the city manager form of government in Cleveland. At first election the plan won by about 7,000 votes, the second time by about 2,300 votes, and the third time by a little over 3,000 votes Hamilton (Pop. 52,108) The public is satisfied with the city manager form of government in Hamileon. This form was adopted in January, 1928, and the city's tax rate has dropped from $9.43 in 1927 to $8.63 in 1930.
When the present administration took control in 1928 the Gas Department was encumbered with $164,000 indebtedness and operating at a deficit of $4,000 per month.
To date all current bills have been paid and a substantial amount of the indebtedness has been cancelled and gas rates reduced.

OHIO-CONT'D amilton (Cont'd) At the end of the first few years under the city manager plan the operating osts of the Electric Department had been reduced 25% in spite of the increase of pproximately 25% in the number of consumers. Net earnings in this department alone or 1928 were more than $100,000 with its anticipated earnings for 1929 reaching early $400,000.
With the installation of a Budgetary Control Accounting System, through hich it was possible to reduce the clerical force in the utilities office, a saving f more than $11,000 per year was effected.
A Bureau of Purchases, set up in 1928, saved during the first eleven onths of its existence approximately $1,800 in cash discounts. Worth (Pop. 200,000)* The city manager form of government has become firmly established in Fort Worth and it is believed that less than 10% of the people would consent to change to any other form of government.
Among the outstanding accomplishments in Ft. Worth under the city manager regime the following are cited: (1) During the first year a delinquent tax collection drive was initiated with the result that $739,198.57 in delinquent taxes was collected. A tax revaluation survey was conducted and the unit system of equalized tax evaluation was put in ; operation which in the first year repaid the people for the cost of the work.
(2) The methods of accounting have been thoroughly re-organized.
(3) Materials and supplies are now purchased on a competitive basis.
It is estimated that centralized purchasing saves the taxpayers of Forty Worth from 10% to 20% on all purchases made. A saving of more than $10,000 a year has been accomplished through the erection of gasoline tanks, in order that gasoline may be purchased in carload lots.
(4) The cost of paving has been reduced 20% although the thickness of the concrete has been increased from five to six inches. A conservative estimate of the money saved on street paving for the four years is in excess of $161,000. By standardizing monolithic sewer sections, $116,250 has been saved. Competitive bids on pipe requirements and a closer supervision have saved $123,680 in the installation of sanitary sewers.
(5) Garbage collection has been extended to the outlying districts and, in spite of this, the cost is cheaper by approximately $50 per day.
(6) The reduction of electric light rates is saving the city $30,000 per year and domestic consumers $173,000.
(7) Efficiency in the fire and police departments has been brought about through the introduction of the civil service, the establishment of the police school and fire prevention bureau.
(8) Welfare work, formerly maintained through contributions from the public, is now handled as a municipal function, with an appropriation of approximately $100,000 per year, without increasing the tax rate.
(9) In the 43 years prior to 1925, 192.85 miles of water mains were laid.
Since June 1, 1925, 193.35 miles have been laid. Cast iron pipe worth $57,000 has been reclaimed. There has been an increase of 12,701 in the number of water consumers since 1925.

A LIST OF CITIES THAT HAVE ABANDONED THE CITY MANAGER PLAN The following thirty-five cities have been reported to the International City Managers' Association, Chicago, Illinois, as having at one time operated under the City Manager Plan of Municipal Government and later abandoned the plan for some other form of municipal government. Not all of these cases of supposed abandonment have been investigated. The starred cities adopted the city manager plan by popular vote and abandoned it by the same method.

Bounds, Assistant Civic Development Department Third Issue) INDUSTRIAL RELATIONS LIBRARY APR 10 1948 HARVARD UNIVERSITY Company Leave Policies for Employees in Reserves of the Armed Forces including the National Guard OF COMMERCE O THE UNITED ST STATES OF AMERICA A summary of a survey of reserve leave practices of representative companies holding membership in the Chamber of Commerce of the United States Chamber of Commerce of the United States Washington 6, D. C. November 1947 FOREWORD THE importance of the civilian Reserves of the Armed Forces and the National Guard as an integral element of an adequate program of national security has long been recognized. The attitude of the Chamber of Commerce of the United States on this vital matter was expressed by the membership at the Annual Meeting on April 29, 1947 in the adoption of the following policy declaration: "The Chamber of Commerce of the United States commends the many employers who are granting special leave of absence to their employees who are members of the Reserve of the Armed Services and the National Guard, in order to enable these employees to maintain their Reserve status, and urges the local chambers and the trade associations to use their influence to see that all employers follow this patriotic practice." To determine the extent to which company policies were in accord with this declaration, a survey was conducted among a representative group of companies holding membership in the Chamber. The results of the survey are herewith presented and reflect the current reserve-leave policies in a wide variety of companies, including those in manufacturing, finance, utilities, and distribution.

Companies Which Have Not Adopted Reserve Leave Policies Appendix I-Examples of Company Reserve Leave Policies Appendix II-Material Sent to Member Companies in Making Survey 3 Company Leave Policies for Employees in Reserves of Armed Forces including National Guard EMPLOYERS throughout the nation are giving considerable attention to the determination of leave policies for employees enrolled in the Reserves of the Armed Forces, including the National Guard. This is evident from the returns of a survey conducted recently by the Chamber's National Defense Committee among a wide variety of companies in many fields of activity and not confined to manufacturing alone.
The survey was conducted to determine the extent employers were adopting such leave policies for employees absent from the job for short periods each year to take military reserve training. There were 1,256 employers who responded to the questionnaire which was sent to 6,995 companies. (See Appendix II for copy of the questionnaire, cover letter, and information chart describing the various reserve components.) The following information was requested: 1. Have reserve leave policies been adopted and if not is the adoption of such policies contemplated?
2. Those having reserve leave policies were asked: a. Length of military training leave granted.
b. Effect of training leave on employee's regular vacation.
c. Nature of pay arrangements for employees during reserve training period.
d. Differences in reserve leave policies for salaried and for hourly-rated employees.
3. Estimated number of male employees in the reserves and approximate total number of male employees in the company. (Same information was requested for female employees in the reserves.) 5 Companies Which Have Adopted Reserve Leave Policies IT IS EVIDENT that many companies have given careful consideration to the matter of leave policies for employees in the military reserves. Of the 1,256 companies reporting on the survey, 720 have adopted reserve leave policies.
on Regular Vacation Effect of Reserve Leave No. of Companies Reserve leave granted in addition to regular vacation Reserve leave applied on one week of regular vacation Reserve leave applied on two weeks of regular vacation Reserve leave granted but not in addition to regular vacation.
Other and Unspecified Total companies reporting leave policies No. of Companies Companies with reserve leave policies 720 Companies with no reserve leave policies Total companies reporting.
1,256 Length of Training Leave Granted As would be expected, most of the companies with reserve leave policies grant their employees two weeks or fifteen days each year for military training purposes as this period is the usual reserve training requirement. Of the 720 companies with reserve policies, 420 grant two weeks or fifteen days annually. In addition, 179 companies state that such leave is granted for "Time Required for Training" which would be in most instances two weeks or fifteen days.

Not specified Total companies reporting reserve policies Effect of Reserve Leave on Employees' Regular Vacations The majority of the companies with reserve leave policies grant their employees training leave in addition to their regular vacations.
There are 590 companies which give such leave in addition to vacations, as compared to 74 which grant military leave but not in addition to regular vacations.
In comparing the policies of companies with respect to length of reserve leave granted and the effect of such leave on the employees' regular vacations it is noted that of 420 companies giving two weeks' reserve leave, 374 state that such leave is in addition to regular vacations.
Of the 179 companies which grant reserve leave of "Time Required for Training," 148 allow such leave in addition to regular vacations.
There are 34 companies which give leave in addition to regular vacations in the group of 35 which grant one week reserve leave.
Pay Arrangements During Reserve Leave Period There is considerable variation in the policies adopted by employers with respect to the nature and extent of compensating employees while on leave for reserve training. The practice followed by 270 companies is to pay the employee the difference between his usual earnings from the company and the pay received from the reserves while in training. There are 161 companies which allow full pay to their employees while taking reserve training, and 219 companies indicate that their employees are not paid during the reserve training period.

6⭑ The relationship of pay arrangements to reserve training are applied as between salaried length of reserve leave and effect on regular vacations reveals some interesting factors. As was pointed out above, of the 420 companies which grant two weeks (or fifteen days) of reserve leave, 374 allow such leave in addition to regular vacations. In this group of 420 companies, 206 pay the difference between regular earnings on the job and the pay received from the reserves while in training. There are 112 which grant the two-weeks' leave without pay, and 84 give the employees full pay during the reserve training period.
In the group of 179 companies which allow leave for “Time Required for Training" there are 148, as was pointed out above, which give the leave in addition to regular vacations. In this group of 179 companies, 46 pay the employees the difference between earnings from the company and reserve pay. Thirty companies grant full pay during reserve training, and 85 give the leave but without pay.
The seventeen companies which use "Other Methods of Pay" compensate their employees during training periods in a variety of ways.
Several companies in this group give their employees one-half pay while they are away from the job taking reserve training. One company gives its employees ten half-days with pay while in reserve training. A few grant their employees either one or two weeks with pay during the training period, depending on the length of time the employees have been with the company.
Differences in Reserve Policies for Salaried and Hourly-Rated Employees A majority of the reporting companies, 575 in all, apply the same reserve policies to both salaried and hourly-rated employees. Of the 720 companies reporting reserve policies, only 28 indicate that different policies with respect to Difference in Reserve Leave Policies as Between Salaried and Hourly-Rated Employees employees and hourly-rated employees.
Size of Companies With Reserve Leave Policies The companies which reported having reserve leave policies represent a wide range with respect to size when measured by total number of male employees. The largest number of reporting companies, 184 out of a total of 720 with reserve policies, employ from 100 to 499 male workers. There are 116 companies with reserve policies which have from 500 to 999 male employees.

Types of Companies With Reserve Leave Policies There is represented a wide variety of different types of companies which reported having reserve leave policies. Manufacturing companies constituted the largest group with 381 reporting reserve leave policies out of a total of 720 companies with such policies. There were 127 companies with leave policies in the field of finance which includes banks and insurance companies. In the utilities field, which includes railroads, airlines, light and power companies, and the like, there were 96 companies which reported having reserve leave policies.

 7 Companies Which Have Not Adopted Reserve Leave Policies OF THE 1,256 COMPANIES reporting in the survey, 536 state that no reserve leave policies have been adopted. It is interesting to note, however, that of this number 132 indicate that such policies are either in the process of adoption or will be adopted in the future.
No. of Companies Companies with no reserve leave policies but intend to adopt such 132 Companies with no reserve leave policies and do not intend to adopt such policies 275 Companies with no reserve leave policies and future policy plans unspecified... 129 Total companies reporting no reserve leave policies..
The majority of the 132 companies which have no leave policies but intend to adopt such policies at a later date indicate that the need has not yet been felt inasmuch as their employees have not requested leave for reserve training. A number of other companies in this group also state that reserve policies will be adopted when the reserve training programs of the Armed Forces and the National Guard are more fully crystallized.
The 275 companies which have no intention of adopting formal leave policies reason, in the main, that their companies are too small to be seriously affected and also that each request for reserve leave will be handled on an individual basis to accommodate particular circumstances.
Several state that their usual leave policies are liberal enough to allow their employees to take reserve training without setting up special reserve leave policies.

"To that end, effective today and continuing until further notice, employees of this Company belonging to military and/or military reserve organizations will be granted leave of absence not exceeding two weeks per year for training purposes.
"This leave of absence will have no effect on the employee's regular vacation privileges.
"Furthermore, the Company will make up in cash the difference between the employee's regular straight time earnings and the amount received from his military organization for the period involved." Automobile Manufacturing Company: "Salaried employees with one year or more of continuous service shall receive full base salary for the time spent in such (military reserve) leaves not to exceed two calendar weeks in any one calendar year for all types of service (in addition to regular vacation).
"An hourly rated employee who is working at the time he is called for such service and who has been continuously employed in the plant or operation for a period of one year or more shall be paid at the rate of $5.00 per day, seven days a week, not to exceed a total of fourteen days per year for time so spent in all types of service., of not to exceed two weeks plus necessary travel time in any one year; that such leave be in addition to regular vacation privileges; and that, upon furnishing the Personnel Department with a statement from his commanding officer or other authority of the amount of compensation received from the Government for such period, each such employee shall be paid an amount equal to the difference between what his total compensation for such period from the Company would have been and that received from the Government, if less." 11 APPENDIX II Exhibit 1.
CHAMBER OF COMMERCE OF THE UNITED STATES OF AMERICA WASHINGTON July 23, 1947 To Executives of Member Companies: As a patriotic duty, many young men and women, veterans and non-veterans alike, have entered civilian reserve components of the Armed Forces, including the National Guard. In so doing, they have assumed certain obligations which affect their status as employees of private businesses, notably, participation in organized activities that require short periods of absence from their jobs in civil life.
Employers who are granting special leave of absence to their employees who are members of the military reserves were commended in a declaration by the organization members of the National Chamber at the recent Annual Meeting.
We wish now to determine the extent to which employers have established definite policies applicable to their employees who are in the military reserves. We shall appreciate receiving information from your company.
Enclosed are two copies of a question sheet which we hope you will complete and return one copy to us, retaining the other for your files. How many of your male employees have reserve status? (Est. No.) 7. How many male employees do you have normally? (Approx. No.) If any of your female employees are reserve members, please furnish information under IV and V for them also.

 13 Principal Reserve Components WHAT IT IS: WHO IS ELIGIBLE TO JOIN: TRAINING REQUIREMENTS: LENGTH OF ENLISTMENTS: PAY AND ALLOWANCES: NATIONAL GUARD A state and federal volunteer organized civilian force, available to the Governor in peacetime for local security and disaster relief, and subject to induction into the Army in time of national emergency. Units organized by states and comparable to the Army's various arms and services, both ground and air.
Male citizens between the ages of 17 and 35, with or without prior service in the Armed Forces, who meet the Army physical and other qualifications.
Maximum of 48 weekly two-hour drills each year, usually in armories provided by the states, and taken outside of normal working hours.
Members also required to attend a 15day encampment each year. Encampments will be by units and all who belong to a unit will be expected to attend. However, there are various types of units (such as infantry, air, etc.) and these different units may take their 15-day training at different times. (Not all units ready for such training in 1947, but many will be by 1948.) For men with no prior service, enlistments are for 3 years; reenlistments of former service men may be for one or three years. Commissions of officers remain in effect as long as officers are qualified. An officer may resign his commission during peacetime, but an enlisted man cannot resign prior to expiration of his enlistment. (Technically, an enlisted man can be courtmartialed for failure to attend summer training.) Members are subject to call to active duty in time of national emergency, at which time none can resign.
For each weekly drill, all personnel receive one day's base pay at the rate for the equivalent rank in the Army.
During encampments all personnel receive the same rate of pay and allowances, including longevity, for the equivalent rank in the Army. Uniforms are issued to enlisted men, and purchased privately by the officers, as in the Army. Some states provide extra pay and uniform allowances in addition. Transportation is provided all personnel to and from encampments.
ARMY ORGANIZED RESERVE CORPS A major civilian federal component of the Army, subject to induction into the Army in time of national emergency.
Consists of Officers' Reserve Corps and Enlisted Reserve Corps, organized into units comparable to the Army's various arms and services, both ground and air. (Also, there are being organized specialized affiliated reserve units sponsored by various industries, such as railroads, telephone companies, etc.) Male veterans with honorable discharges from the Armed Forces who meet the Army physical and other requirements, are eligible for the Officers' Reserve and the Enlisted Reserve.
It is contemplated that in the future, citizens with or without prior war service, who meet the Army physical and other qualifications, and are engaged in a civilian activity which is organized into industrial units will be eligible to enroll in affiliated service units.
The program when implemented provides for a maximum of 48 weekly drills of two hours each per year, but at present, because no pay has been provided for such drill, drill is on an individual and voluntary basis.
May be required to take a 15-day annual encampment, and members attend as part of units. At present a considerable variety of additional training available for all personnel on a voluntary basis. Affiliated reserve units sponsored by industries are trained in readiness for induction into the Army in event of national emergency to perform tasks similar to civilian occupations. Extent of drill and field training to vary by type of industrial unit.
Three years for enlisted men, five years for officers. Members may resign for cause during peacetime, but cannot resign in time of national emergency.
Subject to call to active duty in time of national emergency.
No pay for weekly drill at present.
(Legislation to provide such pay now pending.) During 15-day annual encampment all personnel receive same rate of pay and allowances for the equivalent rank in the Army. Transportation provided to and from encampments.
NAVAL RESERVE A federal civilian component of the Navy and subject to induction into the Navy in time of national emergency.
Consists of: Organized Reserve, Volunteer Reserve (the two major components and the only ones discussed below) and also the Fleet Reserve and Merchant Marine Reserve.
(See "Note" below for Marine Corps Reserve.) Male citizens, with or without prior service in the Armed Forces, between the ages of 17 and 50, who meet the physical and other qualifications of the Navy. Former WAVES are also eligible.
Organized Naval Reserve members attend a maximum of 48 weekly drills annually, and must take a two-week cruise (or a two-week tour of shore duty) each year. The two-weeks of annual training may be taken at different times during the year. Those attending such training are not taken as units from any one employer or community but are taken by type of training and skills. Those who fail to attend more than 90% of the weekly drills annually or fail to take the two-weeks of training may be transferred to the Volunteer Reserve.
Volunteer Naval Reservists are not required to take weekly drill or the two-weeks of training each year but are urged to do so if possible.
Four years in both the Volunteer and the Organized Reserve. Members may resign at any time during peacetime, but cannot resign during time of a national emergency and are subject to call to active duty.
Organized Naval Reserve members receive one day's base pay for each weekly drill, at the same rate of pay for the equivalent rank or rate in the Navy. Same rate of pay and allowances as Navy during the two-week annual training. Enlisted men are furnished one complete set of uniforms but must purchase replacements thereafter. Officers given a uniform allowance of not to exceed $100 when first entering the Organized Reserve, and receive $50 every 4 years for uniforms. Transportation provided to and from points of annual training.
Volunteer Naval Reserve members receive no pay. If they elect to attend weekly drill and take the two-weeks of annual training they may receive the same compensation as the Organized Reserve if funds are available.
NOTE: The Marine Corps Reserve is similar to the Naval Reserve with respect to training, pay, etc. It consists of the Organized Reserve, the Volunteer Reserve (the two major components and the only ones discussed here) and the Fleet Reserve. The age requirements are 17 to 32, with the provision that men may be taken above 32 years of age to the extent of previous service. Prior service not required for membership, but applicant must meet the Marine Corps physical and other requirements. Organized Reserve members are required to take weekly drill and attend two weeks of annual training, with full pay for the equivalent rank or rate in the Navy. Members attend annual training as units. Volunteer Reserve members are not required to attend weekly drill or take two-weeks of annual training, but if they elect to do so, they receive credit for promotion, but receive pay only for active duty training. When uniforms are required to be worn, enlisted men (Continued on back of sheet) REFERENCE "A Program for National Security"-Report of the President's SOURCES: FURTHER INFORMATION: Advisory Commission on Universal Training (Compton Commission)-May 29, 1947, pages 417-445.
