INFORMATION FOR EMPLOYERS ★ OCTOBER 1947 THE UNITED COMMERCE OF CHAMBER OF STATES OF ww DEPARTMENT OF MANUFACTURE CHAMBER OF COMMERCE OF THE UNITED STATES WASHINGTON 6, D. C.

FOREWORD WITHIN TWO WEEKS after the passage of the Labor-Management Relations Act a publication of this Department was made available to employers in order that they might become acquainted quickly with its more important provisions.
A need for a companion treatise devoted to information about the provisions of the law which are of particular benefit to employees has become evident. Employees are entitled to know the facts about the law and the important contribution it can make to improved employer-employee relations. The present publication has been prepared to meet this need.
This pamphlet is not intended for redistribution to employees.
Its purpose is to place in the hands of employers information about the law which they may find useful in preparing their own communications to employees and their families. Statements contained herein may be "lifted" without giving credit to the Chamber, or, perhaps more effectively, the statements may be enlarged upon in the light of the conditions in an individual company.
This pamphlet may have a collateral use. Principal executives may wish to supply copies to branch managers, plant superintendents and supervisory employees generally. For that purpose the pamphlet is available in quantities at nominal cost.
Department of Manufacture An employee can be required to join a union after he takes a job only if a majority of the employees affected vote or have voted by secret ballot that they want their labor organization to have a union shop. The union shop can then be established, but only after the employer and the union agree to it.
An important new protection for the employee, when a union shop agreement is in effect, is that failure to pay his union dues and fees is the only ground on which the union can request the employer to discharge him. Moreover, a union can be required to discontinue charging unreasonably high initiation fees. If the employees no longer want a union shop, they can request the National Labor Relations Board to revoke the authorization to the unions to negotiate such an arrangement.
Two other protections to the employee under a union shop arrangement are: (1) a union may not arbitrarily deny membership to the employee; (2) a union may not discriminate against employees or force the employer to discriminate against them. Thus, where a union shop contract is in effect, a union may not deny to members the right to vote in union elections. The practice of permitting an employee temporarily to work in a union shop under a "work-permit," terminable at the union's will, and refusing to admit him to the union is forbidden.
PRESENTING GRIEVANCES The employee's rights to deal with his employer regarding grievances is enlarged. The employee now may himself present a grievance to his employer, who is required to receive it.
Where there is a union contract, a representative of the union must be allowed to be present at the settlement, which, also, must conform to the terms of the contract. With respect to grievances the new law thus gives protection to both the individual employee and the contracting union.
Furthermore, the Act expressly encourages the setting up of sound grievance procedures under bargaining contracts. Should the employee desire, he may use the grievance procedure established by the contract, rather than present his grievance individually. He has the right to have his union represent him in presenting grievances, free of interference.
RIGHT TO FILE CHARGES The employee is given the right to complain to the agency which administers the new law (the National Labor Relations Board), if he is injured by certain activities of employers or unions, known as unfair labor practices.
The grounds on which an employee may claim the Board's protection have been enlarged. If an employee has been discharged because of membership in a union, or because he refused to join a union; if either union or employer intimidates him into joining or not joining a union; if the employer interferes with the internal affairs of his union; if the employer refuses to bargain with his union; if the union calls an illegal strike or an illegal boycott, or charges an unreasonable initiation fee, or discriminates against him in regard to union membership-in any of these situations, the employees affected may file charges with the Board against the offending party.
If illegally discharged, an employee may be granted back pay from either employer or union, depending on which one caused his discharge.
The employer may not discharge him, nor may the union cause him to be discharged, for making a complaint or for giving testimony regarding any of these matters.
RIGHT TO QUIT WORK Every employee has the personal right to quit work at any time, for any reason. The law expressly protects him against involuntary servitude. It states that nothing in it shall "require an individual employee to render labor or service without his consent." The law does not make a "slave" out of him in any way, shape, or manner.
RIGHT TO STRIKE Moreover, the employee's right to strike, that is to stop work in combination with other employees, is protected. The new law seeks to prevent certain types of strikes which are detrimental to voluntary collective bargaining. Principal among these are jurisdictional strikes and strikes to change contract terms without giving notice as required by the law. Strikes which are found to imperil the national health and safety may be delayed for a limited time while special effort is made to settle them with government assistance.

An employer's right to lock out his employees The law protects the employee from liability is sharply limited. He cannot do so to discourage a union organization drive, or to break a union, or to force the union to change its contract without giving 60 days notice before the contract's termination date.
No restrictions have been placed on the right of an employee to strike for increased wages and improved working conditions. As stated by the General Counsel of the American Federation of Labor an employee may participate in "strikes against employers for pure economic objectives, such as improvements in wages and working conditions, engaged in by the unions representing the employees of such employer." Of course, while a contract is in effect, the union and its members must honor its terms, as must the employer.
It is not illegal under the new law to strike to protest an employer's unfair labor practice or a breach of contract, to secure recognition for a union lawfully entitled to it, or to force an employer to bargain with a union which has bargaining rights. However, the Act does provide alternative remedies to make such action unnecessary.
PICKETING Furthermore, an employee may engage in peaceful picketing to support such strikes. Besides, he may lawfully refuse to cross a picket line around any other plant than his own, if that picket line is in aid of a strike authorized by a union in that plant which the employer is required to recognize.
THE CHECK-OFF The new law protects every employee against having union dues automatically deducted from his pay unless he consents in writing.
OTHER FINANCIAL PROTECTION The union which has bargaining rights for an employee may agree with the employer that a welfare fund be maintained for health, retirement, and similar purposes. The Act protects the employee by requiring that the funds be used only for these purposes, and that both employer and employee shall have a voice in their administration.
for mistakes his union may make. A union may become liable for damages after court action because it has authorized some illegal act, such as destruction of the employer's property. The individual member cannot be required to help pay these damages, except where he has been guilty of active participation.
The employee, moreover, may hold his employer liable in court if the employer damages him by violating the contract under which the employee works. If, for example, the employer refuses to arbitrate a dispute as required by the contract, and thereby injures the employee, the latter may sue. The employee may similarly sue a union if he is damaged as a result of an illegal strike or boycott. If a union calls a jurisdictional strike which closes down his plant and lays him off his job, he can sue the union for the pay he lost.
RIGHT TO HAVE INFORMATION ABOUT THE UNION'S FINANCES An employee is entitled to have a copy of his union's annual financial report, otherwise the union may not use the services of the National Labor Relations Board. Failure to fulfill this requirement does not, however, exclude the individual employee from appealing to the Board in case of any unfair labor practice that injures him.
RIGHT TO VOTE A union which demands that the employer recognize it as sole bargaining agent for the employees may, in order to prove it represents the majority of the employees, and to protect its position, request that an election be held in the plant to decide that question. The individual employee, or group of employees, may likewise file such a petition with the National Labor Relations Board.
Elections will be held whenever the union has met the requirements for using the Board.
The employee, if eligible, is entitled to vote in any such election. If the union wins the election, he has the right to be represented by that union and to require the employer to bargain with it over wages, hours, and other conditions of employment. The employee also has the right to ask the Board for an election to revoke the union's authority to act for him, provided he has the support of 30% of his co-workers. These elections are conducted by National Labor Relations Board agents by secret ballot, and the result is determined by the wishes of a majority of those voting.
Each employee thus has the same right to vote for or against the election of a union to represent him in dealings with his employer that a citizen has to vote for or against the election of one who is a candidate for public office.
COMMUNISTS Employees are protected from having Communist officials in their union. Before unions can use the Board, each official of the local and national or international union must have filed affidavits swearing they are not Communists.
Filing false statements is a criminal offense.
FREE SPEECH In protecting the right of employees to organize and select the union of their own choice, the Act forbids an employer to influence their action by threats of loss of rights or promises of benefits.
For example, an employer may not threaten to close down or move his plant if an organizing campaign is not stopped. Nor may he promise a wage increase on the eve of an election to induce employees to vote against the union.
CONCLUSION THIS SUMMARY is not an attempt to set forth in detail all the provisions of the Act. Rather it is designed to highlight those provisions which affect the employee, both as an individual and as a union member.
It will readily be seen that individual employees not only lose no rights they already had, but also are granted new ones they did not heretofore possess. It will also be seen that the legitimate activities of their unions are in no way curtailed.
It is hoped that employers will find the above information helpful as ideas which they can pass on to their own employees in whatever manner is most appropriate in each particular case.
October 1947 Sens FE?
Employer-Employee Relations Activities of Trade Associations COMMERCE OF OF THE UNITED STAT EE TRADE ASSOCIATION DEPARTMENT Chamber of Commerce of the United States WASHINGTON, D. C.
It is intended particularly to make available to trade associations information relative to typical employer-employee relations activities which have proved to be practicable and of value to members.
It is further hoped that this report will be useful not only to trade association executives and members, but also to all persons interested in the constructive services performed by trade associations.
The scope and character of the material included has been determined largely by the interests and needs of associations as evidenced by requests for information received by the Chamber.
This report was prepared by Milton A. Smith, Assistant Manager of the Trade Association Department.
Henry P. Fowler, Manager TRADE ASSOCIATION DEPARTMENT PURPOSES AND VALUE OF TRADE ASSOCIATION EMPLOYER-EMPLOYEE RELATIONS ACTIVITIES Few fields offer better opportunities for trade association services than that of employer-employee relations. In many lines of business or industry this activity comprises one of the most valuable of association services. The ever-growing complexities of industrial relations have given rise to a need for a wide range of information, advice, and special services which trade associations are generally in an advantageous position to provide for their members.
In a study made before the War,1/61 per cent of reporting associa · tions indicated some activity in this field. Recent inquiries indicate a marked and continuing growth both in the number of associations having such programs, and in the scope of activities undertaken by particular associations.
One of the major reasons for the increased association activity in this field has been the realization that management has often arrived at the bargaining table not as well-equipped as labor. On this point a manufacturing executive said: "Union leadership has been smart, and it has trained its negotiators to be smart. When a union negotiator begins to bargain with management, he does so with a fund of experience, and implementing the experience are the well-thought-out tactics and strategy of the best minds in the union. These are often epitomized in printed instructions. His tactics are carefully planned to get the most out of any possible situation, and his strategy seeks continually to throw management off balance.
1/ Association Activities, Trade Association Department, Chamber of Commerce of the U. S., 1938.

*6* "Management generally not only does not employ these tactics (or any other tactics); it does not even know what kind of clauses are included in other indus trial contracts or the contracts of its own competitors. Generally the union has more facts on wages and working conditions in the area in which the bargaining occurs than does the management. Oftentimes these facts are carefully selected to make a case for the union.
"I have had the interesting experience of observing the same union bargaining with management that was uninformed and with a different management that was fully informed. A blind person would not have known that the same negotiator was engaged in both jobs, nor would anyone else know that the two resulting contracts were made by the same union negotiator." 1/ Where such circumstances prevail, it will undoubtedly be found that management lacked facts and figures because the members of the industry had failed to sense or had not been impressed with --the importance of accumulating the necessary information. Many trade associations make available to their members the facts and figures needed for efficient and intelligent handling of their own collective-bargaining negotiations. They provide, in addition, a variety of information and advisory services through which members may keep up to date on important developments of significance to them, to the end of advancing good employer-employee relations in the industry served by the association.
These trade association information services and related activities, such as those concerned with employment standards, apprentice training, education, etc., have proved to be of value to members in improving employer-employee relations. The resulting benefits of such services extend even beyond the members to their employees, and ultimately to the consuming public. Sound working conditions, stabilization of employment, and the many other improvements in employer-employee relations resulting from associationsponsored programs all contribute to greater and more efficient production, better earnings to employees, and more and better goods for consumers.
1/ Thomas Roy Jones, President, ATF, Incorporated, "The Scope of Collective Bargaining, "Personnel Series No. 81, American Management Association, 1944.

*7* As is characteristic of trade association activities, a program which has proved effective in one industry is not necessarily suitable to all others. Few, if any, associations, however, should fail to find among the wide variety of services which may be undertaken, particular activities which will be of value to their members. These include many types of activities which may be conducted by associations having a minimum staff, and at little expense. Typically the cost of services in this field, regardless of their range, however, is insignificant in relation to the resulting benefits.
In the following text the general considerations involved in the development of employer-employee relations information programs of trade associations are outlined and typical activities which have proved successful are illustrated.1/ Examples presented are given as illustrations of what can be done, rather than as recommended solutions for the problems of a particular association, and without attempting to generalize the conduct of activities in this field into any hard and fast rules of procedure.
This report deals only with activities of general-purpose trade associations. A current study of the Bureau of Labor Statistics, "Employers' Associations and Collective Bargaining," presents a detailed study of activities of employer associations primarily concerned with matters of employer-employee relacionships.

*8* ESTABLISHING THE PROGRAM This Often the trade association executive will have to take the initiative in developing a program of employer-employee relations information.
is, of course, particularly so in the case of an industry in which there has heretofore been little or no union-organizing activity. In such instances, the association executive must undertake to make his members understand the value to them of such services, and must be prepared to meet the problems and obstacles which can be expected to arise where a new activity of this character is proposed.
The logical first step in setting up the program is to make an appraisal of the status of employer-employee relationships among the members of the industry represented by the association, and to determine the interests and needs of the membership. In making this appraisal, as well as in formulating recommendations for a proposed program, the association executive should have the advice and guidance of members having special knowledge and experience in employer-employee relations.
Ordinarily it is advisable to set up a committee to develop a program and to have responsibility for its continued supervision. Insofar as practicable, such committee should be made up of members who have special experience or are otherwise particularly qualified to advise on this subject.
Occasionally for example, where the membership on its own initiait may be unnecessary to canvass tive proposes a specific service or program -- the membership to ascertain its particular needs or interests. In most cases, however, it will be desirable to secure the views of as many members as possible.
Unionized firms, ordinarily will have interests and needs different from those not unionized; similarly, differences in union representation and in geographical *9* location may give rise to varying viewpoints among members as to types of activities which should be given emphasis.
Depending on the character of the industry represented and the scope of the association program, a broad declaration of labor relations policy may be desirable in order to provide the association staff with a working charter.1/ In some instances, however, it is considered preferable that the association employer-employee relations program be operated on a strictly "service" basis without a formal declaration of purposes.
In any event, the association executive should have a set of "operating rules" consistent with the needs of efficient administration of the program.
While he should have readily available the advice and counsel of his members who are well-informed on particular phases of the program, he should not become committee-bound." An interesting outline of the development of a recently established program as well as some of the problems involved was furnished by the Midwest Feed Manufacturers Association: The feed industry is quite young in comparison with many other industries. During the War, the huge demand for food rapidly enlarged the feed manufacturing business to a size undreamed of in the records of years past.
"And, like all other industries, labor and employer-employee relations became a growing problem, presenting to small-town feed men, who make up the greater portion of our membership, a circumstance they had never met before. From a staff of a few old 'trusty neighbors,' many small-town feed manufacturers suddenly found the need of hiring twenty to fifty employees and, of course, this brought on greater responsibility in management and labor relations.
"Many of our members were confronted with the need of negotiating labor contracts for the first time. Unwittingly, the association was thrown into the gap opening up between labor and management. With greater and greater 1/ The policy statement of the Chamber of Commerce of the U. S. on "industrial Relations in America" will be of interest to associations undertaking the development of their own policy declarations.

 10 ⭑ frequency, the association was asked for opinions and factual information, not only by its own members but also by outside companies. On the surface, this might seem an easy program for an association to undertake, for there is considerable general information available on this subject. However, after going deeper and deeper into problems covering labor and employeremployee relationship, we found that circumstances varied greatly. We found that employer-employee relations started with labor contracts, signed in ink, and went beyond that just as far as a wise employer wanted to go.
-- "First, we had to get at the root of the problem the labor contract. In order to accomplish this end, we called together our membership and submitted our program of labor relations. This, in the beginning, received a mixed reception for we were now usurping our authority and delving into private matters. Most of our members, at the start, were extremely reluctant to divulge information covering their own plant operation. They believed that these contracts were secret arrangements and to give them out publicly might be in violation of the contract. Other members did not want their competitors to have this information. Yet, each member admitted unhesitantly that a program of this kind would be advantageous if only this information could be had on the overall picture.
"So, one by one, each member was contacted by our Labor Relations Committee. This required many hours of conference and study of agreements.
Not until we had broken the ice with a few of the more reluctant members were we able to amass enough information to give us a truly authentic starting point.
"Much to our surprise, once we got the membership together in a single meeting and the matter was outlined to them, approval was enthusiastic." In some instances members of an industry will object to any activity in this field by their trade association in the mistaken belief that it might deprive them of some of their autonomy, and possibly pave the way to industrywide bargaining. However, services which aid the individual members in the efficient and intelligent handling of their own employee relations may be the best insurance available against the development of industry-wide bargaining.
The Folding Paper Box Association of America, which maintains a broad employer-employee relations program, stated: "This Association does not enter into any negotiations with unions on behalf of its members. Our labor relations program is based on the concept that the interests of labor and management can best be served by contracts written and signed by men familiar with the individual plant situation.
But we recognize that in many instances union representatives have a big advantage in that they have access to the contracts and wage schedules of numerous companies. In the past, individual plant managers have had to accept the union's statements, or, at best, rely on rumor or hearsay information to counteract them.
"It was this state of affairs that we set out to remedy. We determined to supply members with the facts which they need to bargain intelligently 12 GENERAL INFORMATION SERVICES For purposes of this outline trade association services dealing purely with collection and dissemination of factual employer-employee information, such as wage surveys, summaries of contract provisions, etc., are treated separately from those services which are primarily directed toward correction of particular problems, as for example, programs for training and recruitment of apprentices. This chapter is devoted to the former class of Although job evaluation is dealt with under the latter category, job classification is included in this chapter as an "informational" program, because of its close relationship to certain statistical services.
services.
Statistics.--The most common type of trade association service in In a recent survey, the field of employer-employee relations is statistics.
86 per cent of the associations reporting some form of employer-employee relations activity indicated that they collected and disseminated information relative to wage rates, earnings and hours worked.1/ Data on wages and hours, and other personnel practices is, of course, of basic importance to the members of every industry. Often the statistical program of a trade association is the foundation of a broad employer-employee relations service, since most types of information services in this field either require statistics, or can be made most valuable, through the full use of appropriate related data.
Commenting on the importance and value to members of such data, one trade association executive said: 1/ Labor Relations Activities of Trade Associations, paper presented to Northwestern University School of Commerce, 1946, by James D. Eggers, Secretarytreasurer, American Institute of Bolt, Nut and Rivet Manufacturers.

13 • "Labor relations statistics are difficult to define. or limit; they are as unlimited as the imagination of the people who participate in bargaining conferences It is worth noting that of all the species of statistics, those touching labor relations come closest to an employer's heart because it is easier for him to relate them to his cash register than most other abstract data."1/ The National Screw Machine Products Association, referring to its compilations of wage and hour data, stated:2/ "Continued publication of these reports can tend to cause employers, paying less than the average, to raise wages, or may lead to the installation of incentive systems, which benefits both the wage earner and the general public by increasing purchasing power within the industry." This Association compiles statistics reporting the number of employees in the industry, subdivided into the type of work performed, number of hours worked, wages paid, profit sharing plans, pension trusts, insurance benefits, and vacation schedules. In connection with the analysis of collective-bargaining agreements, some associations prepare a statistical analysis of prevailing practices designed to show how frequently certain provisions are found in the industry and what is the most common type of arrangement.
In addition to the uses which members may make of employer-employee relations data, such data have an important value to various parts of the association program.
Statistics of this character are usually vital in industry appearances before congressional committees or government agencies.
Another by-product of a program in this field is the opportunity to use appropriate data for public relations purposes.

 15⭑ Reporting on its wage survey activities recently, one national association said: "A survey of wages being paid by firms in the industry was conducted early in the year for the purpose of securing vital operating information for members. The data obtained revealed wages being paid various types of employees. The results were compiled on a regional basis as well as on a national basis to provide members with a true perspective on wages in their own regions and in the nation as a whole. More than that, the figures enabled members to determine proper labor costs and to make equitable adjustments. In addition, the data proved of inestimable value to many members in reaching settlements of income tax questions by equipping them with indisputable facts about operating costs and practices in the industry." In commenting on the relationship between statistical programs of trade associations and those conducted by local employer associations or industrial bureaus of chambers of commerce, P. C. Gaffney, in his previously cited report on "Labor Relations Statistics," said: "The statistical program of the local employer association has a very definite value, as has that of the trade association. The two programs should not be competitive with but complementary to each other, and there is ample room for cooperation that will be mutually profitable.
Trade associations which disseminate labor statistics applicable to their industry will frequently find it helpful to learn more about the facilities and features of the local employers associations and industrial bureaus of chambers of commerce in the areas where their members are located.
The exchange of labor information on a reciprocal basis generally is arranged very easily." Job classification--Job classification programs are valuable and appropriate to most types of industries and may be undertaken readily by most associations. The purpose of job classification is to standardize terminology throughout an industry in order that a reliable comparison may be made of rates shown for a particular job. The Folding Paper Box Association of America described its set of job classifications as "the backbone of our entire Labor Statistical Program, since without it it would be impossible to make any intelligent wage rate comparison." 16 Describing the development of its job classification program, the Midwest Feed Manufacturers Association reported that there were some 500 job classifications in the industry at the time this effort was undertaken.
This was revised to less than 200, all of which were grouped under one of seven categories: (1) Unskilled male labor; (1) Unskilled male labor; (2) Semi-skilled male labor; (3) Skilled male labor; (4) Female help; (5) Maintenance; (6) Service employees; and (7) Distribution employees.
"This job reclassification required us to spend many days in the mills interviewing and carefully watching the actual work done by some seven hundred employees in order to determine exactly the actual work each employee did and the importance his particular operation played in the overall plant production," the Association Secretary stated, adding that "this reclassification was very advantageous to both employer and employee." Contract information--Many associations have undertaken to furnish their members with information relative to collective-bargaining agreements in effect in the industry. A variety of plans of this character are provided.
Some associations limit their activity to maintaining a central depository for agreements entered into by members. In other instances, the association makes a detailed compilation in which contract clauses are classified according to subject matter.
Reporting on its labor contract information service, the Society of the Plastics Industry said: "As a service to member companies confronted with or anticipating the necessity of executing agreements with groups of employees, the Society collected copies of existing contracts which members had executed. Excerpts from these were compiled in booklet form and distributed to members who found them to be highly valuable aids in handling their contract problems. In addition, the Society developed a file of contracts currently in force for reference purposes if occasion should arise that a member sought guidance 17 ⭑ in executing a contract. Word of this booklet reached firms and associations outside the industry and prompted many to request copies." While such compilations generally are confined to union contracts, some associations send non-unionized members complete questionnaires covering their policies on the subjects normally included in contracts of the unionized firms in the industry. Further, contract information services need not be confined to highly unionized industries. For example, the American Bankers Association said: "The collection and dissemination of labor organization agreements is a part of our work, although labor union activities have been comparatively minor in the banking business. We have, however, kept in close touch with situations where contracts between banks and unions have been negotiated, and copies of those contracts, together with appropriate comment, have been made available to our member banks." The Optical Wholesalers National Association, Inc., pointed out that its compilation of labor agreement clauses is of practical value to all members, whether unionized or not. In the case of nonunionized members, it was stated that the compilation provides an excellent indicator by means of which the firm's labor relations program may be compared with existing practices in unionized firms. Where a member firm was unionized, it was noted that this service would aid in the writing of a new labor agreement and, in the case of renewal of an existing agreement, would make possible the selection and use of the best clause pertaining to a given subject.
In the introduction to its compilation of "Union Contract Provisions," the National Cottonseed Products Association states: "This binder contains a series of clauses quoted verbatim from union contracts in the cottonseed industry. It is a report of conditions as they are and it should therefore not be construed as an endorsement by the association of any particular clause or contract, or as a recommendation to members of 'standard' contract provisions." 18* This compilation presents clauses arranged approximately in the order in which they appear in the majority of contracts. Clauses covering the same or related subjects are grouped together. Each clause is identified by location of the mill (by region), a key number for office use only, name of union, and date of expiration.
As is the usual practice, this Association makes its compilation available to members on a loan basis only. This practice is feasible, because only a few members will have need for it at a given time. By furnishing it only when needed in connection with negotiations, the expense of maintaining the service is greatly reduced and members are assured that copies furnished them are up to date.
The introduction to the compilation of collective bargaining contract clauses prepared by the Southern Pine Association shows that clauses included are "taken from collective bargaining contracts currently in effect (1) in the lumber and wood products industries in the South, and (2) in other more highly organized, national industries, for purposes of comparison." Purpose of the manual is stated to be to acquaint members of the association "with the types of clauses now in effect and to afford an opportunity for comprehensive study of what has been included in other manufacturers' collective bargaining contracts." Clauses contained in the Southern Pine Association's binder are classified according to the following outline, which also constitutes a table of contents: 19 ARBITRATION BONUS PAYMENTS BULLETIN BOARD CHECK-OFF CONTRACTS Agreement Revision Termination Separate Agreement DISCHARGE AND DISCIPLINE GRIEVANCES Arbitration HOLIDAYS HOURS OF WORK Call-In-Time Overtime Hours Guaranteed Minimum Hours' Pay Waiting Time INTIMIDATION, COERCION AND DISCRIMINATION JOB CLASSIFICATION JOB PROTECTION Work by Supervisors LEAVE OF ABSENCE MANAGEMENT RIGHTS RECOGNITION SAFETY AND HEALTH SENIORITY Effect of Military Service Handicapped Workers Lay-Off, Rehiring, Promotion and Transfer Supervisory Employees New Employees Union Representatives' Preference Veterans' Job Preference STRIKES AND LOCKOUTS UNION ACTIVITY UNION SECURITY Closed Shop Union Shop Maintenance of Membership Preferential Hiring No Membership Requirements VACATIONS WAGES Overtime Pay Premium Pay Pay Day Night Shift Differential Duration of Agreement Rates for Temporary Transfer Deductions Sex Differential Incentive Systems Probationary, Seasonal Workers and Learners A booklet entitled "Standard Clauses for Agreements Between Management and Labor" was prepared for members of the National Sand and Gravel Association and the National Ready Mixed Concrete Association. This booklet contains a discussion of factors to be taken into consideration in connection with each of the clauses commonly found in agreements used in these industries 20 and presents a study of standard proposals.
It is pointed out that this study is not a proposal for a uniform contract" and that "questions of company policy are matters for the individual employer to decide for himself." In further explaining the limitations of the study, it is added: "We are not recommending any of the standard clauses for inclusion in any contract which you may negotiate with a labor union; our objective has been to make language available to you which will limit and define your responsibility if you decide to make a certain type of agreement with the union, or if, where you have no union, you may wish voluntarily to adopt a specific company policy." As is pointed out in the discussion of statistical services, analysis of prevailing practices as disclosed by collective-bargaining agreements filed with the association is valuable to members in connection with contract clause information. One association executive stressed the importance of such data as follows: "The negotiation of each clause in an agreement has two distinct phases. The first involves bargaining over what settlement of the issue is to be made. For example, is a sick-leave clause to be included in the contract, and if so, which form thereof? The answer to this question is most frequently determined on the basis of prevailing industry or area practice.
"A manual of contract clauses signed by other firms contains the basic information to determine what industry practices are, but it is a long and tedious analytical job that none but the largest firms are equipped to do. Further, such an analysis made by each company needing the information is a tremendously wasteful process of duplication. The need here is filled much better by a statistical analysis of prevailing practices, drawn from the contracts, but with reference to their end result, not their phraseology.
"Such an analysis provides an immediate answer to the question: how frequently are such provisions found in the industry and what is the most common type of arrangement?" 21 Meetings and discussions--A number of associations have inaugurated regularly scheduled meetings and conferences devoted to discussions of employeremployee relations matters. The Radio Manufacturers Association holds a twoday industrial relations seminar every six months under the auspices of its Industrial Relations Committee. Experts on government conciliation, wage incentives, selection and training of personnel, the conference method of management, and other aspects of employer-employee relations were presented during a recent seminar. Informal discussions, with questions and answers, followed each talk.
Laws and rulings.--Up-to-date information covering current developments under laws affecting employer-employee relations is important to all businessmen, and trade associations maintain a variety of bulletin services to provide their members with such information. In connection with these services the association staff usually encourages and undertakes to answer questions from members, and to secure copies of regulations or orders affecting a particular member, and otherwise to give members all possible help on their individual problems. Care is exercised, of course, in handling individual inquiries, to avoid supplying legal advice or opinions under circumstances where this would constitute the practice of law.
Counsel is often aided in handling legal problems of a member client where the association is able to make available promptly all applicable official rulings and other information. Associations often follow the practice of putting counsel of member companies on the mailing list for employer-employee relations reports.
The laws relating to employer-employee matters with respect to which trade associations most often furnish information are the Labor-Management 22 Relations Act, the Fair Labor Standards Act, the Portal-to-Portal Act, the Walsh-Healey Public Contracts Act, and the Bacon-Davis Act. Reports cover changes proposed or actually made in such laws; rulings of administrative agencies, such as the National Labor Relations Board and the Wage and Hour Division of the Department of Labor, and important court decisions. A number of associations also report developments under state laws which involve matters of interest to the membership.
As is usual in association reporting services, effort is made to indicate to members the particular significance to their industry of any important new ruling.
Other types of related information published by some associations includes the results of elections conducted by the National Labor Relations Board to determine bargaining representatives, and arbitration awards.
Further details on typical reporting services are included in Appendix I of this report outlining the programs of the Southern Pine Association and the National Association of Motor Bus Operators.

 23 EMPLOYMENT STANDARDS AND RELATED SERVICES In addition to compiling and disseminating information of the character described in the preceding section, some trade associations are engaged in the promotion of particular employment standards.
A typical service in this field is the furnishing of information on methods used to promote better relations between management and employees.
Promotional activities relating to health and welfare have been carried on by trade associations for many years. The conduct of safety campaigns, the establishment of safety awards, and the collection of information with respect to frequency and severity of accidents are indicative of such activity.
There follows specific consideration of some of the typical activities of trade associations designed to improve employment standards.
Job evaluation--Both management and employees are interested in the elimination of inequities in the wage structure existing between companies in an industry or within individual companies. If conducted on a sound basis, job evaluation (also called "job rating" or "job analysis") affords a factual basis of determining the value of each job. Such a determination can be made a most effective instrument for improved personnel relations.
Job evaluation or job rating is defined in a bulletin of the National Electrical Manufacturers Association, one of the pioneers in this field, as: The process of analyzing a job to determine the degree of skill, effort, responsibility and working conditions in relation to other jobs in the same plant, in order to evaluate each job as a basis for fixing wage differentials. It represents an attempt to set occupational wages on the basis of detailed analysis of the job requirements." The National Electrical Manufacturers Association, furnished the following outline of the development of its job rating plan, and of benefits resulting from this plan: 24 "Almost from the formation of the NEMA Industrial Relations Committee in June 1935, consideration was given to evolving ways and means of providing a service by which facts and information could be provided to members confronted with employer-employee difficulties. This resulted in the setting up of the NEMA Industrial Relations Department.
"One of the first tasks was the compilation of adequate data regarding earnings by jobs or occupations in the industry. However, this information was considered of little value unless some method was used whereby jobs could be classified in participating company plants so that the resulting data would be comparable.
"In one of its bulletins to member companies, the Industrial Relations Committee discussed 'Wage Payment Policy.' Two points in particular were emphasized: 1.
2.
The necessity of keeping informed as to going wage rates currently being paid for comparable jobs in the community.
The importance of proper wage differentials between jobs within a plant.
"Inequalities in earnings differentials on different jobs within a plant, due to lack of proper job classification, may be just as much a source of dissatisfaction, and hence a cause of grievances, as failure to pay the going rates of wages. It was recognized that one of the reasons why wage differentials had not received more attention was lack of knowledge as to how such differentials could be determined, except on an arbitrary basis.
"Under these circumstances, the Industrial Relations Committee concluded to devise a plan for rating jobs which would be made available to member companies in order to promote interest in job classification and wage differentials.
"It was recognized that such a plan must meet certain basic requirements. It must be simple, readily understood by everyone involved, applicable to a wide range of processes or operations, and made so concrete as to insure uniformity of ratings. The purpose, of course, was to devise a method for rating a job and not the man on the job.
G "This resulted in the development of the NEMA Job Rating Plan which provides a highly effective method for the point rating and grading of jobs on the basis of the four general characteristics of the job Skill, Effort, Responsibility and Job Conditions. Within each of these characteristics there is a further breakdown into job factors or attributes, which permits a precise and objective means of analyzing and measuring job requirements. This breakdown consists of eleven factors, classified under the four general characteristics. Considerable care was used in developing a specification or explanation for each factor or attribute and for the degrees thereof and to make this simple and easily understood so that any job could be readily classified and rated.

 25 "The Plan furnishes both a method for evaluating jobs in terms of their relative requirements and a factual basis for determining wage differentials.
"The first edition of the NEMA Job Rating Manual was published in 1937, followed by two bulletins on Job Rating (No. 43 in 1937, No. 45 in 1938), all of which were distributed to members. These bulletins explained the purpose, nature and operation of job rating plans in general and the NEMA plan in particular. Thus, between the plan itself and the explanatory bulletins, members were supplied with full details as to the uses of job rating as a tool of management.
"Subsequently, two revised editions of the Manual were published, one in 1941 and the other in 1946. Changes were made without deviating from the basic principles of the original plan but to reflect refinements directed toward greater uniformity of understanding and therefore better uniformity of rating by individual raters.
"The typical examples which were shown in previous editions were omitted from the 1946 Edition. This information has been issued in a companion manual or supplement to the 1946 Edition, entitled 'Guide for Use of NEMA Job Rating Manual,' to serve as a guide for those using the 1946 Edition.
The 'Guide' undertakes to explain the various steps necessary in using the Plan and includes a group of sample illustrations.
"The earlier bulletins on 'Job Rating' (Bulletins No. 43 and No. 45) were revised following the publication of the 1946 Edition and the Guide, and reissued as Bulletin No. 72. It contains supplementary information to clarify some of the basic problems involved in introducing, operating and maintaining a job rating system.
"The Industrial Relations Committee originally decided on the policy of making the NEMA Job Rating Plan available to industry generally, not merely to electrical manufacturers, having in mind that while a well-conceived plan is of specific value to the company using it, the company receives additional value from the plan if other companies in its locality, even in different industries, also are using it. This recognizes the well-known fact that to a large degree the wage structure tends to be influenced by conditions and wage structure of a particular area even more than it does by what other plants in a particular industry are paying at other locations.
"As a result of this liberal policy, the NEMA Job Rating Plan is in very widespread use in the electrical manufacturing industry, the metal trades industries in general, aircraft and aircraft parts industries, certain branches of motor accessories manufacture, paper products, and many other miscellaneous industries.
"As a further step toward stimulating continued progress and to supplement the information already published, the Industrial Relations Committee sponsored a conference on Job Rating in March, 1947. Leading experts in the field discussed vital elements relating to the technique and operation 26 of a job rating system. The topics presented, which dealt with Establishment of Jobs, Measurement of Jobs, Pricing of Jobs, Introducing a Job Rating Program, Operating and Maintaining a Job Rating Program, provided a background for discussion of problems, procedures and developments in connection with a job rating program. The conference was well attended and there was definite evidence that the effect of each session was notably worthwhile." The National Screw Machine Products Association, National Shoe Manufacturers Association, American Institute of Laundering, the American Bottlers of Carbonated Beverages, and the National Metal Trades Association are among other trade associations which have performed outstanding services to their industries through the development of practical job-rating plans.
In announcing the publication of its "Manual of Job Evaluation in the Soft Drink Industry," an officer of the American Bottlers of Carbonated Beverages said: "Members who have examined the Manual have been unanimous in praise of it, explaining that if some such material had been available in the past, many of their troubles regarding job distinctions and wage differentials would have been solved."1/ Incentive wage plans--Information on incentive wage plans is provided by a number of associations. The National Association of Ice Industries prepared a bulletin entitled "Incentive Wage Plans for Route Servicemen," which outlined "principles underlying the over-all structure of incentive wage plans that will serve as a guide in tailoring a plan to fit each ice company's particular situation." program.
The American Institute of Laundering pointed out that: "The successful use of wage incentives requires a carefully prepared It is important that every factor involved be thoroughly evaluated, and that all factors be coordinated to provide a pattern which may be expected to permit the adjustment of every conceivable eventuality to fit that pattern.

 27 This must of necessity go far beyond consideration of the mere payment of incentives.
"There appears to be little question but that the most important factor to be considered is the attitude and ability of top management to conform to that pattern or to devise a program which is sound and at the same time conforms to management's ability to manage that program consistently.
"It is highly important that a wage incentive program be so designed and administered that it is thoroughly fair to both management and labor. It is not sufficient for management to evaluate that fairness only from management's point of view. It is equally important, and perhaps more so, that it be fair from labor's point of view. The difficulty of being able to see both sides at the same time indicates the advisability of including in the planning of a program a third party who can reliably and objectively prepare and install a wage incentive plan entirely satisfactory to both parties." 1/ Bestabilization of employment--Activities aimed at stabilization of employment offer a wide range of possibilities for trade associations.
cause of the growing attention being given to guaranteed annual wages, programs of this character are an important aspect of labor relations, although they ordinarily will present problems extending far beyond that field.
An outstanding activity in this field is the "Balanced Program for Shoes," developed by the National Shoe Manufacturers Association. One of the major prewar problems of the shoe industry was irregular production, caused in large measure by the buying habits of retailers. While production peaks caused higher manufacturing costs, delayed deliveries, and impaired workmanship, the dips in production resulted in unemployment, excess inventories, and costly markdowns. The Balanced Program for Shoes was developed with the objective of eliminating these uneconomic conditions and to provide a greater measure of continuous employment at the manufacturing level.
1/ The A. I. L. Member, No. 310, April 10, 1946; "Planning Wage Incentives." 28 The first announcement of the program was made in April 1945, after initial exploratory studies. In April of the following year the Association reported: "While the Balanced Program for Shoes is a long-range plan and has been in operation only a comparatively short time, tangible results already have been achieved. Many manufacturers have adopted the plan and are tying in their advertising and publicity promotions with the objectives hoped to be obtained by the Balanced Program for Shoes . . . . One of the most pleasing results of the Program is the great interest shown in its accumulated effect on our national economy by leaders in other industries. Executives representing industries far removed from the shoe industry have made intensive study of the data, approach, method and objectives of the Program. They have indicated that they can see many benefits to management, labor and consumers from such a stabilized year-round production and merchandising plan. Several are adopting for their own industries plans based on the Balanced Program for Shoes." 1/ This brief commentary emphasizes the important contribution toward the improvement of conditions affecting management, employees, and the consuming public, by a carefully planned association activity.
Education. 2/--Many types of employee educational and training activities are conducted by trade associations. These include instruction pamphlets, correspondence courses, short training courses, and cooperation in apprentice training programs. Some of these activities are carried on in cooperation with colleges and universities.
Programs of this character are an important contribution to improved efficiency and higher worker morale. They help to build good will for the association and the industry which it represents.
1/ "Balanced Program for Shoes Report," April 24, 1946 2/ For a detailed study of trade association educational programs see "Enlightened Self-Interest -- A Study of Educational Programs of Trade Associations," by Dorothy Bowden, American Association For Adult Education, New York. (1937) Price 75 cents.

 29 Recognizing the importance of capable supervisory personnel in promoting good relations with employees, increasing attention is being given to training programs for managers and foremen.
Education, information and research comprise the important phases of the Restaurant Administration Program of the National Restaurant Association at the University of Chicago. In addition to the long-range course, a three-day conference is conducted annually by the University in cooperacion with the Association.
The first short course was on Personnel Administration in the Restaurant Industry and dealt with such topics as: "Understanding your Employees"; "Human Relations in Supervision"; "Guiding Employee Adjustments in Customer Relations"; "Job Instruction." One session was devoted to the consideration of trade unions, covering conditions that make for unionization; new problems set before management by unions; and techniques in dealing with unions.
Another short course was on Modernization of Restaurant Operations In this the employees' comfort and working conditions were and Facilities.
given every consideration.
The last short course was on Management and Management Controls in the Restaurant Industry and dealt with such subjects as: "Policy Formulations"; "Organization"; "Effective Utilization of Personnel"; "Quality and Quantity Food Production"; "Sanitation and Food Handling"; "Measurement and Control of Customer Satisfaction." One of the leading research problems so far undertaken was Human Relations in the Restaurant Industry. The findings of this survey, conducted by Dr. William Foote Whyte, of the University of Chicago, are soon to be published in book form.

 30 Pointing to the importance of these phases of its program, the Association said: "Handling of personnel is the restaurant industry's number one problem. Studies in general industry have shown that absenteeism and labor turnover are lowest where the plant is set up for harmonious teamwork between supervisors and employees. Even high wages will not hold a worker on the job when he does not get along with fellow employees and supervisors. On the other hand, the worker will turn down opportunities for higher wages elsewhere when he feels he is part of the team where he works. Even efficiency in production is dependent upon human relations.
"It is recognized that every job in the restaurant industry can be done better when it is done in a harmoniously functioning human organization." The Ice Cream Merchandising Institute, affiliate of the International Association of Ice Cream Manufacturers, conducts a broad program of employee training through member wholesale ice cream companies which emphasizes service to the consumer, special attention being given to personal conduct and cleanliness of employees and presentation of the product.
Reporting on results from the use of sound slide films as a part of its employee-training program, the National Association of Ice Industries said: This series of films is basic and elementary in teaching ice people what they should know to render courteous, clean, professional ice service; and in aiding them better to perform their duties. Thousands of ice employees have been trained with these films. The profitable results achieved and enthusiastic praise of the hundreds of ice companies that have used them attest their effectiveness." The vocational training program of the American Institute of Laundering has been prominent for some years. Its training courses for owners, supervisors, and other personnel are furnished through a full-time training school conducted at the headquarters of the Institute. Short courses are also taught to laundry owners and managers both at the Institute and in the field.
The following associations are among the many which have conducted or sponsored educational programs for employees, and indicate the wide range of industries in which such programs have proved successful; American Meat 31* Institute, American Gas Association, American Bakers Association, Milk Industry Foundation, National Retail Lumber Dealers Association, National Association of Retail Grocers, American Savings and Loan Institute, and the American Bankers Association. In some instances, as in the case of the American Bakers Association and the American Bankers Association, an originally association-sponsored program has been carried forward by a separately organized Institute working in cooperation with, or under the auspices of the association.
Health and Safety.-Association programs designed to reduce accidents and to promote better health among workers have been of much benefit.
These programs are varied in scope, ranging from dissemination of information on activities of particular members, to an intensive program of engineering and medical research. Annual safety contests are sponsored by many associations.
For some years the American Trucking Associations, Inc. has conducted annually a National Truck Safety Contest for fleet operators and a National Truck Roadeo, in which drivers from all parts of the country compete for cash and other awards. Both contests are part of an intensive program of accident reduction. The National Lime Association, the Book Manufacturers Institute, Inc., the Ohio Dairy Products Association, the Portland Cement Association and the National Crushed Stone Association are among those trade associations conducting programs directed toward better worker health and safety.
Indicative of the value of such programs is the following comment of the Portland Cement Association: "In 1938, the year of lowest accident frequency by the member plants of the Portland Cement Association, the non-member group of plants which does 32 not participate in Association accident prevention work produced a frequency five times as great as that of the plants within Association membership.
In 1942, the year of highest frequency in fifteen years for the member group of Portland Cement Association, the non-member group produced a frequency 7-3/4 times that of the member group.
"In 1946, the frequency rate for the non-member group was eleven times that of the member group." Apprentice training--A number of trade associations have taken the leadership in encouraging increased apprentice training in their industries.
Our association believes that utilization of accelerated training wherever possible and practicable is the main key to increasing productivity of employees." 35 PUBLIC RELATIONS The trade association executive often has an excellent opportunity to contribute to the welfare of his industry, by keeping in mind the public relations aspect of employer-employee relations. Viewing the interests of his industry in the light of current developments and trends of opinion he may advise his members concerning attitudes and procedures which will promote harmonious management-employee relations. At the same time, members have much to gain by recognizing the public relations opportunities inherent in association-sponsored employer-employee relations programs.
Public relations can hardly be treated as a separate aspect of employer-employee relations. The promotion of sound employment standards, the furnishing of information which tends to make bargaining more equitable and harmonious, and other activities in this field contain in themselves important elements of public relations. Indeed, it may well be said that the field of employer-employee relations activities offers almost limitless opportunities for building goodwill for industry.
It can be seen readily that employer-employee relations activities can provide the basis for intra-industry public relations designed to advance further the cause of good relations with employees, as well as with the customers of an industry and the general public.
The many techniques of public relations which may be used in connection with employer-employee relations programs are outside the scope of this report. To cite but one of many examples, the Public Relations Committee of The Rubber Manufacturers Association, recognizing the need for improvement in 36* employer-employee relationships, as a means of facilitating and assisting in this improvement, publishes quarterly the "RMA Employee Relations Publicity Review." This Review is a pictorial digest of publicity ideas used by rubber manufacturing companies in their house organs, internal and external. The purpose of the publication is to disseminate worthwhile ideas or techniques, to encourage their use, and to give other companies material which they in turn may be able to adapt to their needs in their efforts to improve their own employer-employee relationships.
Another typical example of trade association public relations in the employer-employee relations field has been the preparation of simple, factual information for employees as well as employers on matters of mutual interest. One type of activity of this character was the preparation by trade associations of material relative to the Labor-Management Relations Act of 1947. Much misinformation, often of an emotional character, was circulated among employees relative to this law by its opponents, and the material prepared by trade associations was designed to help employees understand not only that the law could not have the dire effects predicted by its critics, but that it actually afforded the worker many important benefits.
Illustrative of an association undertaking designed to carry a message to all employees and others interested in a particular industry is the preparation by the National Screw Machine Products Association of an economic history of the screw machine products industry for the period of 1890 to 1944. The story of this industry is told in simple language, emphasis throughout being placed upon the relationship between production, investment in tools, and rise in wages.

 37 The purpose of this history was to have something concrete in the form of explanatory communications to offer industry employees, in order that they in turn could tell their neighbors what they do and where they work, and thus take pride in their industry because it is identified.
Distribution by the Association is in the form of eight four-page 8 x 11 folders to be mailed monthly to the employee, with supplementary mailings to be made to stockholders, schools and colleges, buyers from the industry, and other interested parties. The folders are in four colors; the few charts used are simple and contain no figures. Copies are sent to employees at their home addresses through a master mailing list submitted by member as well as non-member companies. Companies preferring to handle their own distribution are supplied copies for this purpose.
If the industry is to enjoy good public relations, it must have a good story to tell. Thus, the association executive may have to take the leadership not only in organizing an effective program, but in formulating management attitudes. The extent to which the association executive can do this will of course vary greatly as between different organizations. He must not go beyond the bounds of what his particular members may consider appropriate industry-wide activities into the realm of individual management prerogatives.
Nevertheless, the association executive has many opportunities to play a part in encouraging sound attitudes of his members on employee relations matters.

 38 ⭑ APPENDIX I TYPICAL ASSOCIATION EMPLOYER-EMPLOYEE RELATIONS PROGRAMS The following pages present a detailed explanation of typical employeremployee relations information services of associations. They illustrate some of the specific means by which associations render valuable service to their members and their industries.
SOUTHERN PINE ASSOCIATION LABOR INFORMATION SERVICE The primary purpose of the Labor Information Service is to furnish our subscribers up-to-date and useful information on labor relations trends and developments in the lumbering industry. This service, based on continuous study, provides a valuable guide for the handling of labor problems which are so vital to the industry today. Not only does it furnish information helpful to management of organized plants and to those negotiating a collective bargaining contract for the first time, but also includes warnings against common pitfalls that may bring employers in violation of federal labor laws.
Three methods are principally used to disseminate this information: (1) By making available an analysis of the clauses commonly used in union contracts currently in effect in the Southern lumber and wood products industries.
(2) By the publication of a periodic news sheet entitled "LABOR NEWS ITEMS" which features significant labor relations developments both as applied to industry generally and to the Southern lumber and allied industries in particular, and (3) By the distribution of a Labor Relations Bulletin Service containing the basic regulations and executive orders governing labor matters with appropriate comments, interpretations, rulings and decisions as they become available.

 39 ⭑ COLLECTIVE BARGAINING CONTRACT CLAUSES.
This manual is a compilation of clauses taken from between seventy and eighty current union contracts, representing agreements arrived at as the result of collective bargaining on the part of management and labor. It covers the great majority of the unionized plants in the Southern lumber and woodworking industries and is the only existing source of information of its kind available. The manual is kept constantly up-to-date by the addition of newly negotiated contracts as we receive them and by the recording of any changes that are made in existing contracts that comprise its contents. The clauses are classified according to subject and each clause is followed by information consisting of a confidential mill identification number, the expiration date of the contract and the identity of the union with whom the contract is made.
The contract manual is especially valuable to the manufacturer who is negotiating a collective bargaining contract with a union for the first time because it enables him to weigh demands made in the union's proposal against those that have already been agreed upon by management in similar operations and included in their union contracts.
It is also valuable to a sawmill operator re-negotiating his contract as it enables him to readily check what, if any, new demands are being made upon his company for matters not usually included in other collective-bargaining agreements made with other operators in Southern Pine territory.
LABOR NEWS ITEMS. A periodic news sheet featuring significant lab r relations developments and union activities in the Southern lumber and alliea industries, pertinent United States Court and National Labor Relations Board decisions, arbitration awards in labor disputes, and other matters dealing with labor relations of general interest to the lumber industry.
LABOR RELATIONS BULLETIN SERVICE. The service is issued in loose-leaf form so that it may be kept as a permanent record in one volume for ready reference.
Through it, we endeavor to help our subscribers intelligently meet the constantly changing conditions brought about by the government regulations and executive orders affecting employer-employee relations. The Bulletins which are issued from time-to-time include not only the basic regulations and executive orders but also appropriate comments, interpretations and rulings upon them as they become available. The text of federal laws dealing with labor relations is also included.
Labor Relations Service also makes surveys from time-to-time of major problems bearing upon employer-employee relations and furnishes individual informational service on labor relations matters to subscribers upon request.
A complete reference library of official decisions and opinions on laws and regulations dealing with labor-management relations, also of new developments in them, is maintained.

 40 NATIONAL ASSOCIATION OF MOTOR BUS OPERATORS LABOR RELATIONS DIVISION 1. Functions: The functions of the Labor Relations Division are to provide management with the following: The a. Systematic and detailed statistical data on wages and working conditions in the intercity bus industry for use in collective bargaining.
data are compiled from current labor agreements covering more than half the employees in the intercity bus industry and from field studies undertaken by questionnaire or by actual visits. The information thus made available provides management with data on prevailing industry practices comparable in scope with those heretofore available only to labor groups and thus assists in restoring some semblance of equality in the collective bargaining process so far as statistical data are concerned.
b. Information on economic and legislative developments. Trends in legislation (including administrative and judicial interpretation and application) and in the economy generally are analyzed and interpreted in relation to their impact on the intercity bus industry in order to assist management in its day-to-day operations.
c.
An information and consultation service. This service is provided in order to assist management in connection with individual or special problems not covered by the two preceding general functions. The services range from furnishing detailed data on legislation and its application and interpretation to the preparation of elaborate analyses of union demands for new concessions, intercompany wage comparisons, and detailed suggestions concerning the content and wording of contracts.
2.
Publications: The publications of the Division are of three types; each constitutes a separate series rotoprinted on stationery of a distinctive color. Reference to the contents of all publications is facilitated by a quarterly cumulative subject index.
a. Research Bulletins, each treating a single subject, are based on the analyses of existing labor agreements and on field studies. Typical are detailed reports on the industry pattern with respect to union recognition, premium payments for overtime, basic wage rates, and take-home earnings. Each of these analyses includes, in addition to a summary of prevailing industry practices, detailed suggestions as to matters that should be considered in contract negotiations on the individual issues. The statistical data supplied are far more extensive and conclusive than any formerly available to management in the industry.

 41 · b. A Monthly Bulletin contains brief items of current information on economic, labor, and legislative trends including important administrative and judicial decisions. All of this information is interpreted in the light of its importance to the bus industry.
C. Special Bulletins are issued from time to time as warranted by important developments such, for example, as the initiation of a new federal wage, price, labor, or other similar policy or procedure. These Bulletins ordinarily supply information on detailed procedures required of management in complying with regulations.
3. Scope of Operations The functions of the Division are concentrated upon developing a greater degree of cooperation and integration among individual managements in dealing with labor problems and upon providing management with effective and sound statistical data for use in negotiations. The Association does not participate in collective bargaining nor undertake in any way to represent management in this process.
Further information on policies, procedures, and publications may be had by addressing an inquiry to the Labor Relations Division, National Association of Motor Bus Operators, 839 - 17th Street, N. W., Washington 6, D. C.
